
Across the hallway, in this little house where she grew into
womanhood, sits my lost child. There has been no change since I
brought her home more than a month ago. During these days—
and nights-a certain routine has been established that gives the
house a superficial atmosphere of quiet and resignation; but I am
not resigned. When Arnie was a four-year-old and was lost in the
mountains where we spent our holidays, did I give up the search
until she was found? Neither will I give it up now. A mind is
"lost" but it is heartbreak I am concerned with. For I
know, from the fragments of letters (begun to me and never fin-
ished) I found in her wastebasket, it was her heart that first lost
direction and surrendered to despair. From members of the cell
came intimations of disaster, and the doctors said the trouble
might have started years ago; so I am not certain what should
come first. I know only that I must search until I find what hap-
pened; when, indeed, something was begun, that ended its cycle
in a wastepaper basket in that desolate little room in New York.
Everything put down must be factual and objective, and so far
as I can make it so, without emotion; though it is difficult not to
be overwhelmed by regret, remembering that all this might have
been prevented if I had remained in New York. Yet at that time
I was in no condition to help. Ironically enough it was my health,
not Arnie's, we were considering that Monday evening last Octo-
ber when I let my child persuade me to return to Bethel.
That Monday, when the others had left, and Arnie and I had
cleared away the cups and saucers and what remained of the tea
cakes I always served before the meeting began, I took from the
[1]refrigerator and set on the table a black-walnut cake with white
icing filled with walnut meats. The cake was Arnie's favorite, but
she did not exclaim enthusiastically over it as she always did when
I had made something she particularly liked; and with impa-
tience at my own weakness I felt a wave of self-pity engulf me.
"You haven't touched the cake,” I said, and though I had
meant to make the remark lightly, as if Arnie's lack of apprecia-
tion was not important (though it seemed terribly important to
me at the time), my voice wavered on the verge of tears.
"I love it," Arnie said, glancing at the cake with what seemed
to me polite inattention. "But just now I am not hungry."
I retired into myself, hurt and offended. It had been a tremen-
dous effort for me to make the cake, though formerly I could
have done it "with my eyes shut." I was conscious of my unrea-
soning attitude, and yet was unable to change it. And again, as I
had before, I thought of telling Arnie about the dream that had
waked me so often during the past months, and kept me awake
long after the dream was over; but could not bring myself to do
so, feeling, perhaps unjustifiably, that to Arnie and Ben (as well
as to Endahl), it would seem ridiculous. Even my motives, so
simple and clear before, had become confused. And I wondered
if I had become, what I had always said I would not be, a posses-
sive mother, who now resented Arnie's concentration on her
work and on Ben, and was using the dream and wakefulness as an
excuse for further possessiveness.
All this confusion, so foreign to me, was stirring in my mind as
we sat at the kitchen table and talked over the questions that had
come up at the meeting and the directives received from the CEC.
But these matters, so interesting before, so important and dis-
cussed with such enthusiasm, became secondary as my lack of at-
tention showed itself and Arnie became more and more thought-
ful. Finally we gave up all efforts to continue. An uneasy silence
settled between us, a frightening thing to me, for we had always
been open and spontaneous with each other. Then Arnie looked
straight at me, and this sudden look, so loving, so concerned,
warned me of what was coming.
"Mama," she said abruptly, “for some time you haven't been
yourself. What is wrong?"
[2]"Wrong?" I laughed, but even to myself the laughter sounded
forced. "Nothing is wrong."
"Mama," she said reproachfully, "you and I have been through
too much together not to be honest with each other. Are you ill?
Are you lonely because I am so busy? What is it?"
"Of course I'm not lonely,” I said emphatically; and spoke only
what was true. Since we had lived in New York I had been alone
months at a time in that small apartment on the Lower East Side,
and was quite content. From where I sat (across the table from
Arnie), I could see, at the other end of the large room, my shelves
of books, a radio and record player, the rug on the floor
surrounded by a couch and some chairs just as they had been left
from the meeting, making the room a place of hospitality—a
home. In my bedroom there were more books, my electric sew-
ing machine and the desk.
"How in the world could I be lonely?" I asked irritably.
"There's scarcely a day when I'm not called on to take part in
some demonstration or front or fraction meeting, or even help
picket during a strike. I like people. I am interested in them. You
know how members of the cell visit me here, and even when you
are away people come in for evenings of music and discussion
just as they do when you are here only perhaps not so many.
How could I be lonely?"
I did not add that I looked forward to her calls on the tele-
phone, or to the cell meetings, when, because it was the first cell
she and I had attended in New York, she always managed to be
present, or at least come in at the end; so that we might, as we
were doing that evening, have a visit together.
But Arnie kept shaking her head. "Something is wrong,"
she insisted. "You have never been sensitive before. It isn't like
you."
There she sat, my daughter, looking so earnest and concerned,
and scarcely a day over twenty. As she spoke to me a flush had
come in her cheeks, emphasizing even more the loveliness of her
enormous brown eyes, as well as the keen intelligence behind
them; the sober, reasoning mind that had grown through the
years from awkward flounderings, like a baby learning to walk,
[3]to conscious integration and concentration on a purpose. So dif-
ferent now. (But I must not dwell on that.)
“Arnie,” I said, “I think I've never told you what a beautiful
baby you were. Most newborn infants,” I chattered on in a falsely
cheerful voice, “are scrawny and red and ugly, not quite human.
But you were. When they put you in my arms for the first time
you were asleep so sweetly asleep—your long dark lashes against
the little round flushed cheeks, and your hair so blonde. Sud-
denly you opened your eyes and looked straight up into mine,
not unfocused as most new babies' eyes are, but straight at me
with those great brown eyes. The contrast with your hair. . .
With a sudden impatient movement Arnie lifted the percolator
from the table and took it over to the stove. "Don't try to change
the subject, Mama," she said, not looking at me; and still watch-
ing the flames she had lit under the coffee, she asked, “Have you
seen a doctor?"
"No," I said almost violently, "I am not sick." And then I
thought: How foolish of me to be so emotional when I know, I
know what is wrong. And as she stood there, waiting for the
coffee to heat, it was on the tip of my tongue to tell her about the
dream. The words even formed in my mind. The dream is like
this, Arnie: I walk alone through heavy sand between high,
sharp-pointed crags. There is no color anywhere; the pallid crags
and pale heavy sand add to my feeling of desolation. Then, grad-
ually, some invisible, inexorable Presence makes itself felt, judg-
ing me. I try to hide from that Presence, behind the crags, in
some dark place between them, but there is no place to hide; and
the dream ends in a frantic and frustrated struggle, from which I
wake sobbing and cannot go to sleep again.
I longed to tell her, to take her entirely into my confidence, as
I had always done. But when she brought the percolator back to
the table and sat down, the words would not come. Perhaps I
would have risked making myself ridiculous to Ben and to
Endahl, but I knew that if I acknowledged it had not been pos-
sible for me to reason away a foolish dream Arnie would be dis-
appointed in me. I had never disappointed her over the years,
but had gone with her every step of the way, not only because
[4]she wished me to, but because I had become convinced. I could
not disappoint her now.
"But something is wrong, Mama," she insisted. She leaned
across the table and took one of my hands in hers. "You were al-
ways thin, but now I can almost see your bones," she said, looking
ruefully at my hand.
Suddenly I felt tears pushing at my eyes and drew my hand
away. I must not cry. I had not cried in years—never, since that
early Christmas morning after Julie had left us. "The truth is,"
I said, trying to make a joke of it, "your Ma is getting old, and
can't sleep as well as she did. I . . . simply . . . can't sleep."
"You are fifty-two," Arnie smiled. “That isn't old. Do you
know what I think? I think you're tired. Nothing else. Or maybe
bored. How long have you lived in this same place, doing the
same things?"
I counted the time, and was surprised to find how long it had
been. "More than twelve years," I said.
"That's it, then. That's it," she said. "Don't you see, Mama, I
have had so much variety in my life—and now, going down to
Washington, and other places. You talk of meetings. But people
do get bored with meetings. I, myself, I'm glad I've been relieved
from some of them. No, you need a change," she said, frowning
down into her cup of coffee. "You should get away from New
York entirely."
“Ah,” I exclaimed, "now you're trying to get rid of me." And
at once, as I said it, wanted the words unsaid. But Arnie did not
take advantage of them. She could so easily have said, “Don't be
so sensitive," or condescendingly, "Now, Mama, of course I don't
want you to go; I'm only thinking of your health." She looked at
me indignantly, and then the sweetest, loveliest smile flooded her
whole face and overflowed from her expressive eyes. "Mama!"
she exclaimed, and that was all.
"Where in the world would I go?" I asked. "To a cheap board-
ing house somewhere? That wouldn't be a rest."
From the time she was a small child, just old enough to begin
showing her own personality, Arnie had a way of tilting her head
as she became intent on some idea or project, like a small wild
[5]animal that hears a noise and cocks its head to listen. Now in just
that way she tilted her head, intent on some idea.
"What is it?" I asked. "You have a plan for me. What are you
thinking?"
“Mama. . . ." she said, but her voice trailed off into nothing,
ending in a small embarrassed laugh.
"Why are you hesitating?" I asked impatiently.
She lifted her head and looked me full in the face. “You might
not like. . . ." She hesitated again, and then said slowly and
firmly, "I was . . . thinking of Bethel."
...
"Oh no!" I protested. "Never!" From somewhere in my mem-
ory the words "Outrageous fortune" leaped forward and repeated
themselves in my mind, as if Shakespeare had expressed once and
for all in those two words all the pain and bitterness evoked
when my daughter said, "I was thinking of Bethel." Then I heard
her say in a small persuasive voice, “You know, Mama, the little
house is there, just waiting for you."
I wanted to say: Not waiting for me! And could only regret
that I had showed her the letter I had received from Mr. Craw-
ford, the real estate agent in Bethel, informing me that the peo-
ple who had been renting my house were leaving the first of Oc-
tober. Yes, it was there; the house I had inherited from my father,
where Arnie and I had lived after my divorce until the crisis came
that precipitated us into another world. It was there, but except
as a possession from which I received, toward the beginning of
each month, a check from Mr. Crawford, the thought of coming
into closer contact with it, of returning to Bethel at all, was like
trying to pull a deeply imbedded splinter from under my own
nail. Yet I tried to consider it, as I had always tried to consider
Arnie's opinions and wishes, with reason and without prejudice.
"You could stay all winter and into the spring," she said in the
same persuasive voice. "Some of your flowers would still be there.
Think of the jonquils, the daffodils. Remember? Along the edges
of the garden and over in the field beyond. And then the wis-
teria, the yellow jessamine, and your roses. . . .”
"And then?" I questioned.
"Then you'll come back to New York the last of April in time
[6]for the May Day celebration—all made over and new," she an-
nounced triumphantly.
No doubt it was her suggestion of spring in the South to which
I responded. I believe there is not a place where spring comes so
graciously and beautifully as it does in our part of the country.
In the North it comes like a spoiled child: reluctant, sulking,
with unexpected storms. There, almost always, it comes smiling.
As I considered, a small, fresh hope, like spring, seemed to well
up in my heart, and suddenly what had seemed impossible, never
to be considered, became possible .
“But what would you do?” I asked, for since we had come to
New York I had given Arnie half the rent from the house as my
principal contribution to the Movement.
"I have thought of that," she said.
"Yes," I agreed dryly, "I am sure you have thought of every-
thing."
"But, Mama. . . ." she began to protest, then thought better
of it and went on. "You will have to keep the apartment here. So
if Ben and I could live in it while you are away that would even
up everything."
“Well, . . .” I said in an assenting voice. We looked at each
other and smiled.
"It's settled, then?" Arnie asked, still anxious, still not sure.
I nodded.
It was eleven o'clock, and we had been talking since nine. The
walnut cake, that neither of us had touched, gleamed white on
the table. Arnie leaned toward me and put her arm around my
shoulders. "Let us arise,'" she said laughing, "and go up to
Bethel.''
I laughed with her. "To think," I said, "that you should re-
member after all these years." It was a family expression. My
father and mother had used it when I was a child, and it became
more than an expression; it was a call to action when some deci-
sion had been made. I had carried it into my own family after
my marriage, and even before Arnie was born, her father and I,
when we were away for holidays, and it was time to return, said
laughingly to each other as we began to pack: "Let us arise and
go up to Bethel.' " It had been part of Arnie's childhood.
[7]Gaily she transferred our coffee cups and percolator to the sink
while I cut a wedge of cake and wrapped it in waxed paper, so
that she might take it with her to share with Ben. I washed the
dishes and she dried them and we began to chatter and make
plans. "Mama, you're a different person," Arnie said and gave me
a great hug. And I was.
That was Monday evening. On Tuesday I sent an air mail
letter to Mr. Crawford, asking him not to rent the house, but
to save it for me; and began to pack.
[8]Chapter II
Even in a small apartment, I discovered, over the years a person
can accumulate a surprising amount of extraneous matter. I had
made the decision to leave New York the first Monday in Octo-
ber, but it was the last week of that month before I had finished
getting my trash cleaned out, personal papers and other things
packed and sent by express, and the apartment ready for Arnie
and Ben to take over.
Arnie seemed happy and contented, though I noticed as she
stood with me in the Pennsylvania Station, before the gates
opened to let us go down to my train, that a previous habit she
had taken on, of moving her shoulders nervously as if trying to
shift a burden or pack, had come on her again. I said nothing, for
I had spoken to her about it when it had started some months
before. She had stopped it then, or it had stopped of itself. Then
the conductor came and began to manipulate the gates to open
them, and I forgot everything else in the sudden agonized reali-
zation that within the next ten or fifteen minutes I would be say-
ing good-by to my child, and with that good-by, and by the de-
liberate act of boarding a train, was preparing to put more than
a thousand miles between us. It would be the first time since
Arnie was born that we had been separated; for if we had lived
in different apartments in New York most of the years we had
been there, still there had been the reassuring knowledge that
she was in the same city.
As we walked down the long flight of steps to the train, all my
attention became centered in an effort to keep Arnie in ignorance
of the state of my emotions. We joked again about Jacob and
[9]Bethel and laughed together. Arnie said, "I have never known
what comes before and after that line." I said I could not remem-
ber either, but would send her a Bible from the "Bible Belt" if
she liked, so she might read it herself. She said no, she and Ben
could get along very nicely without such a present. Then she said
Ben and Endahl had a theory that church steeples were phallic
symbols, and we went into a discussion of that question. It was
interesting and served to hold my emotions in check, which was,
perhaps, what Arnie had intended. We had always enjoyed in-
tellectual discussions.
As we neared the train she said, "What will you do about Aunt
Julie?" I said rather sharply I did not know, and we did not speak
of Julie again. I reminded her about the large coffee percolator
to be used on Monday evenings, for the cell was to continue to
meet in my apartment while I was away; and I had arranged with
Rose to make the coffee.
"Mama," Arnie said gaily, "you mustn't expect your kind of
housekeeping from me." I laughed and said I knew enough about
it to expect nothing. But of course we both understood that Arnie
was far too busy with much more important things to think of
such details.
I suggested, rather hesitantly it is true, that if she became ill
while I was away Mum Hattie would be available. Arnie did not
answer, and looking at her face I saw a queer little expression of
either pain or derision pass over it; and which I interpreted to
mean that she had not recovered from the bitter discussion with
Mum Hattie that had occurred some time before. I was not cer-
tain Mum Hattie herself had forgotten Arnie's rudeness at that
time; but there was a certainty that if the child she had loved and
nursed from twenty months to the sixth year needed her, Mum
Hattie's generous heart would immediately respond to a call
for help. But I did not belabor the question.
Arnie went with me into the coach, for I was taking the day
coach to save money, and as we embraced I forced myself to be
the first to let go, though I was longing to keep her there. Outside,
on the platform, she stood and waved as the train pulled out of
the station. I strained to get a last glimpse of her. She was smil-
ing.
[10]That night on the train I experienced a strange variation of
the dream. Instead of the pallid crags of the other dreams I came
suddenly on a high mountain covered to the top with fir trees,
one row above another. But these trees were not green. They
were ash gray, as if a devastating fire had swept over them, con-
suming the trees-yet every branch and needle and cone was
intact. Again I experienced the desperate loneliness, the knowl-
edge of being separated from my body, and the awe-ful Presence
that haunted me. The heat was overpowering, as if the fire that
had devastated the mountain had left the ground and atmos-
phere like a furnace. Because of the heat I was astonished and
grateful when, just before me at the base of the mountain, I dis-
covered a bed of cool green moss. There were green trees around
the space and the loveliest flowers-bluets, bird-foot violets,
and small wild iris-blossomed in the moss. A clear spring welled
up below some rocks, and from it trickled a cool little stream.
The ecstasy this glimpse of freshness and color gave me was in-
expressible. I started forward to enter the refreshing place, but
as I did so the trees closed ranks like soldiers, and I was shut out
staring at the face of the mountain covered over with ash-gray,
regimented trees.
The depression brought on by this dream and the separation
from Arnie lasted until I reached Bethel, when the exigencies of
practical life took over. As I left the train that morning it seemed
to me the old brick station looked exactly as it had since my child-
hood. There was the same old smoke-begrimed brick building,
solid and substantial, and the slate roof that extended over a
wide paved outdoor waiting room. There were the signs that
said "White" and "Colored" over the waiting room doors, and
which took me back to January, 1938, when Arnie and I had sat,
exiles into the unknown, waiting for our train to the North. It
was Arnie, then just eighteen, who insisted, after we boarded the
train, that she must ride in the Jim Crow car to Washington.
"But, Mama," she said when I suggested that perhaps she would
make other people feel uncomfortable, “I must live what I be-
lieve. I must, Mama. You need not come with me. I'll join you
in Washington." And she did.
"Well, how was it?" I asked when we were on our train for
[11]New York. She was very subdued, her young face serious and ab-
sorbed. "You were right, Mama. People looked at me as if they
thought I was peculiar or as if they questioned me. I did not feel
comfortable. But I did not go in there to feel comfortable. I sat
there and tried to imagine what it would be like if I could not go
anywhere else. The important thing is not what other people
think, but what it did for me.”
As I stood on the platform a hundred other memories, both
painful and joyful, stirred in me, not in sequence of time but as
if each was crowding to take first place: the day Julie and I re-
turned for our mother's funeral; Julie and Mama and I leaving
for the mountains or the sea in the summer, and returning; and
when I was older going off to St. Ann's and coming back for joy-
ful holidays. Then Arnie skipping ahead of her father and my-
self across that very platform, a five-year-old anxious to get
home to the rectory and to Mum Hattie, who was always there to
greet her when she was small.
Weary from the journey I was glad to find a cab outside. The
driver, a young Negro, knew the building on the square where
Mr. Crawford had his office, and we drove there first. Mr. Craw-
ford was not in his office, and I was relieved, for I felt in no mood
for renewing old acquaintances. But the girl who was in the of-
fice, and whom I did not know, had the envelope with my keys
that Mr. Crawford had left for me.
Soon we passed South Main Street and the eight intervening
blocks and reached Eighth Avenue, South, and I could see the
little house showing through the two magnolia trees that stood
one on each side of the brick walk leading from the gate to the
front door. Just a small wooden house, painted white with green
blinds, a chimney at each end and a wide veranda extending
across the front and around some distance on one side; and a
white picket fence and a gate. What I could not see was the ex-
panse of green lawn at the back of the house and the two steps
leading from that up to the garden.
"There it is," I said to the driver, and impulsively I added, “I
haven't seen it in twelve years."
"My," he said, politely wondering. "My, that's a long time."
He was kindly and courteous and took my bags inside, into the
[12]little hallway, after I had unlocked the door. "Thank you, m'am,"
he said when I paid him and gave him a tip.
Then I was left alone; alone with other memories that crowded
on me from the walls of the house. As I had down at the station,
I pushed them away. For years Arnie and I had successfully ig-
nored the past, and used the present only as a preparation for
the future. So it had been easy in that other life to keep all mem-
ories in abeyance, indeed to forget that we had any past at all.
And I was determined to keep it so: to look on my months in
the little house only as an interlude, a time of rest when, concen-
trating on the garden, I would forget the dream and with
renewed strength look forward to that day in April when I would
return to New York in time for the May First celebration; and
take up my life there exactly where I had left it when I
said good-by to Arnie.
But it has not turned out that way. It is May now and I am in
the little house; I am in Bethel; and the memories must be given
freedom to assert themselves. I must let everything, every detail,
however painful, come, and hide nothing from myself. For some-
where, if I put it all down, I may find the clue to what it is that
tortures my child.
[13]Chapter III
It was the strangest thing: the effect the little house had on me.
At first I slept, and slept again, through each night and almost
through each day. This lasted for three days. Then an abounding
and seemingly inexhaustible energy and enthusiasm took hold of
me. Nothing was too difficult; and except for repairs I did every-
thing myself. And when, after many weeks, I knew the house was
clean, I sat down and rested. There is a particular kind of quiet-
ness about a house that has just been thoroughly cleaned, like
the stillness in the woods in early spring, when all life seems sus-
pended and yet everything clean and new and ready to welcome
the family home: the bursting buds and riotous colors that are
coming.
After the house was cleaned there remained only the task of
waxing, and I took a large pleasure in doing this, especially bring-
ing out the grain of light mahogany in the wood of the old piano
which had belonged to my grandmother. On those old yellow
ivory keys Julie and I had learned our notes and practiced. Arnie
had done the same. I had it tuned last December, and the piano-
tuner, one of the refugees from Hitler who had settled in our
town, told me something I had never known before: that a piano
is never tuned true, but always a bit off key. Then I was too busy
to ask him why. Some day I must ask.
It was a wonderful winter. I was interested every moment dur-
ing the day, and at night fell exhausted into my bed. My appe-
tite came back, and I slept. Occasionally the dream returned, but
not with the impact of the nightmare in New York. Some nights
when it came I was even able to go to sleep again.
[14]Since Bethel is a town of forty thousand people, and Julie lives
in our old home, Mars Hill, far to the north of the public square
in a spot that once had been quite out in the country, and my
small house is many blocks to the south, I had believed, when I
decided to return, it would be easy to remain isolated. Yet there
had been an anxiety, a dread, that remained until early in No-
vember, when I learned from Mr. Crawford that Julie was in
Europe and not expected back until September. Long before Sep-
tember I would be gone, and I was glad. The quarrel that had
separated us twelve years before had not been entirely Julie's
fault. But the bitter words about Arnie had been her own.
I believe even our mother was at times intimidated by Julie,
that is, afraid of her "tantrums" which were quite violent, when
she would say unpardonable things. Although it is supposition
on my part, it seems to me now that much of Julie's charm came
from the fact that even from childhood she was always acting a
part: the star of the play; and her tantrums resulted from her
frustration when the lesser actors did not respond to their cues,
or had not learned the parts that in her thoughts she had as-
signed to them.
Although we were twins, Julie and I were sent to separate
schools, for our father and, mother decided, wisely perhaps, that
it would be best if I were given a chance to develop without my
sister's strong personality to influence me. Yet just as soon as we
met again at once I fell under her domination, and again she was
able to make me feel that I was always in the wrong, and that
she was always right.
There was the time when our mother died suddenly and Julie,
who was at school near Washington, and I, from the nearer St.
Ann's, returned in response to the messages summoning us
home.
My mother was the dearest person in the world to me. Julie
was more our father's favorite, though I believe he tried not to
make any difference in his attitude toward us. Sometimes in his
letters, written to me while I was at boarding school, he expressed
his love and called me "My dear little daughter." But at home he
was singularly reserved in expressions of affection, though when
[15]we had guests and political questions were debated, particularly
those about the South, he was articulate enough.
My mother had received a rather unusual kind of education,
for from the time she was eight years of age until she was mar-
ried at eighteen, she was under the tutelage of a retired clergy-
man who lived in her parents' home; and who made her learn
Greek by reading the New Testament in that language, and read
with her books on philosophy such as Locke's On the Human
Understanding. But she was not an intellectual as we think of
intellectuals; and it was I who listened while she taught us about
cooking and keeping house (because my instincts were in that
direction), and Julie who was uninterested. I was the one who
loved to sit beside Mother while she gave me lessons in the fine
stitches she had been taught when she was a girl, and Julie who
rebelled at having to learn them. Not that this was any virtue
on my part, or lack of virtue in Julie, but that our separate na-
tures directed and controlled us.
On Sunday afternoons, although she was not "on duty" on
that day, Hattie would join us as our mother read aloud or
taught us the Catechism and the Collect for the Day. Once each
year when we were young children she read us her favorite, Pil-
grim's Progress, so that we knew parts of it by heart. In winter
we would sit before the drawing room fire, all about us the Sun-
day stillness, and listen to stories from the Old Testament, made
so real to us by our mother that Abraham and Isaac, Jacob and
Esau, David and Esther and Daniel, and many others were rooted
in us as part of our cultural and Christian heritage.
In summer and early spring the Sunday afternoon reading
would take place on the veranda. Then the reading became
somewhat lighter. Hattie and I always became absorbed in what-
ever my mother selected, but Julie almost always wore a frown
and would bounce on the old joggling board, announcing, with
every bounce, her rebellion against the discipline of listening. My
mother had a delightful sense of humor, and I can hear her now
chuckling or laughing aloud over the Pickwick Papers and Nicho-
las Nickleby. Even Julie could not help showing an interest in
the latter, and giggled unrestrainedly when the "wild man"
threw vegetables over the fence to Mrs. Nickleby and said,
[16]"Queen of my soul, be mine, be mine." And for a time I became
"Brother Charles," one of the twins in that novel, and Julie
"Brother Ned," a secret bond that produced giggles and some-
times explosions of laughter from us; our pleasure heightened
because others looked at us in wonder and did not understand.
Going home for our mother's funeral I joined Julie at Greens-
boro, and we sat in the train silent and uneasy. Not knowing
death, nor realizing what it might mean; not being able, because
of lack of experience, to visualize a mother who had been alive
and well when I had last seen her-dead; and yet knowing death
meant she would not be there at home to meet us, as she had
been from the time I could remember (when I came in from
school, or from a party, or from playing, or more recently home
from boarding school), I seemed to be suspended in a void, not
yet sorrowful, but as if I could see, down in the void, the dark-
ness, the anguish of sorrow that was coming.
It broke painfully upon this condition when Julie said harshly,
as the train was nearing Bethel, "I am not going to wear black.”
Julie had brought back many new ideas from the school she
attended, and from the girls she had met there, or with whom
she had spent one of the shorter holidays; and I had been tre-
mendously intrigued by them. But what she said then, as we sat
in the train, came as a shock. Yet, always ready to find a compro-
mise with my sister, I said, “Then you could wear white," for it
was April and warm enough. But Julie said impatiently, “Oh,
don't be so provincial!"
In my ignorance of life, and my superficial knowledge of peo-
ple, I could not then know that Julie was simply repeating what
had been said to her in criticism by some girl whom she
had visited in New York or Boston during the Easter holidays, or
by a teacher she admired—just as she came from school at Christ-
mas wearing, instead of the high-heeled pumps she had pre-
viously been so anxious to acquire, a pair of hand-sewn moccasins
from Maine that were the new fashion at her school. And when
she said, "Don't be so provincial," I felt, as usual, in the wrong
and could only sit, more miserable than ever, looking out of the
window as the familiar stations appeared and the train drew
nearer to Bethel.
[17]Sep, our colored chauffeur and butler was waiting on the plat-
form, and he told us that our Aunt Julia, Father's sister,
for whom Julie had been named, was waiting for us in the car.
Sep had been with us for many years, and as soon as he came to
us to take our bags, as if pity for our forlornness or grief for our
mother had touched him, his homely face twisted into a knot of
sorrow; and as he lifted our suitcases I heard from deep within
him a sort of heavy dry sob break through.
Aunt Julia, not a comfortable and appealing person like our
mother, kissed us solemnly, and as we drove through the streets
she told us about our mother's sudden death, the result of a
heart attack. As she spoke I was still unable to understand what
had happened, and even when Father met us in the hall of our
home, outside the closed door of the drawing room, realization
still did not come. As he came to meet us Julie rushed forward
and flung her arms about him. He held out his other arm, the
one that was not around Julie, to me. But before I reached him
I had time to see his face that was stern and yet strangely beau-
tiful and young; and in a moment of unusual perception, just
before his arm went about me, I realized with astonishment that
my
father had not always been the old man I had up to that time
thought him, but was once as young as Julie and myself. And as
he held me and I wept, a remembrance came of my mother. I was
sitting beside her on a stool. We were both sewing and my mother
said, "Childie, it is so wonderful to be forgiven." What she said
made no particular impression on me, but something implied in
her statement did. I looked up at her, saw the rather joyful, the
young look on her face and said in amazement, "Do you do
wrong things, too?" "Oh, yes," she answered, and it seemed for
that moment that our mother, whom I had always considered
old, was as young as I.
Aunt Julia, who had been hovering about, went with us to our
rooms and saw that we were comfortable. She had evidently
taken, or had been given, responsibility for everything, since my
mother had no living brothers or sisters; and when she had left
me alone in my room beside the bags Sep had placed on the floor,
I sat on a chair, listless, incapable of any action.
I don't know how long I had sat there when I heard the flip
[18]of Julie's nails against the door, a habit she had acquired at school
instead of the usual knock; and the door opened. Julie did not
come in, but beckoned me mysteriously to join her in the hall. I
went to her, and she took my hand and drew me down the hall
where a doorway opened on to the back stairs that led to the but-
ler's pantry. Halfway down these stairs she sat down and
motioned me to sit beside her.
"What is it?" I whispered. "Why are we here?"
"To get away from Aunt Julia," she said. "I told her I would
not wear black and she was shocked. So I settled for white for
both of us. She said we had no white hats and that she would send
someone to get them for us. But I will not wear a hat someone
else has bought for me."
Feeling, as Julie often made me feel, a sense of being honored
because she had included me in a sort of conspiratorial alliance
against someone or something, in this instance Aunt Julia, for
whom neither of us had any close affection, I whispered, "But
what can we do?”
"We will go uptown and get them ourselves," Julie said.
"But... people don't do that," I said, and then "Do they?"
For the look Julie gave me made me unsure, and I did not want
her to say again in that scornful voice, “Don't be so provincial.”
"We do," Julie answered, emphasizing the word “we,” and it
seemed to me those two words gave Julie immense stature, far
above my own; courageous, strong, unyielding to the demands
and customs of lesser folk, such as Aunt Julia was, and the others;
a condition that I, if I accepted what she said, could share.
Whether she had already persuaded Sep to take us, or did so
while I was washing my face and combing my hair, I do not
know. Trained by our mother, Sep had an unfailing sense of
what was right and wrong, or more particularly in this case,
what was correct; but after we had slipped down the back stairs
and to the driveway where the car was parked, we found him
waiting behind the wheel, and he said nothing to prevent us
(although to me the very set of his back seemed to express dis-
approval), but drove uptown to the hat shop.
We had known Miss Mamie Reed, who waited on us, all our
lives, and after Julie had explained to her what it was we wanted,
[19]she murmured words of sympathy as she searched for some sim-
ple unadorned white hats; and while we tried them on Julie
was quite matter-of-fact, but I was unable to speak. Suddenly, all
feeling of doing something courageous, something conspiratorial,
had left me, and I was utterly miserable. The shop, Miss Mamie
Reed, Julie and myself, all seemed unnatural, affected, putting
on an act, as we tried on the hats (a thing done so gaily at other
times), while our mother lay dead in that room behind the closed
door at home.
The hats were selected and charged, and we returned with
them to the house, again by the back stairs. When Aunt Julia
found out what we had done she did not fail to point out to us
that it showed disrespect for our mother, and that others would
know about it and we would be criticized and our love for our
mother questioned. When we were alone and Julie said, "She
doesn't really care about Mama, only about what people will say,”
I did not answer, but secretly I felt that Aunt Julia was right, not
because of what she had said, but because of the knowlwdge that
had come to me in the shop that all we were doing violated some
truth that was in me; and this feeling was confirmed when the
rector of our church came and we went with him into that
room where our mother lay in her coffin.
I had never before seen the interior of a coffin. Our mother's
head lay on the satin pillow as if she slept; yet so unlike sleep.
Her loved face, her whole body, seemed strange and removed,
like a distant mountain covered over with glacial ice. She, who
had always been so warm and responsive, now unresponsive and
cold—indifferent, almost (it seemed) contemptuous of us whom
she had left. And as I looked at her all the finality of death over-
whelmed me; not only that I was saying good-by for all time to
my mother whom I loved, and who was leaving us, who had al-
ready left us; but that her going signified to me a world in which
I had been abandoned, a world unresponsive, indifferent, con-
temptuous, cold, a world in which she would never be; and I sank
to my knees beside her coffin and wept uncontrollably.
It was then I felt two strong hands on my shoulders, gripping
them, and hot tears on my neck that was bent over and exposed.
I looked and saw through my tears long black fingers near my
[20]cheeks, and knew that Hattie had come from New York. The
warm hands on my shoulders loosened their grip, and as I rose to
my feet Hattie took my hand strongly in her own; and so
we stood together, weeping over the mother who had, from Hat-
tie's twelfth year, been a foster mother to the orphaned Negro
child who had come to Mars Hill thirteen years before.
Strangely enough, Julie and I had not come together in our
grief. But for the rest of the days we were at home, until we re-
turned to school, there was a marked simplicity about my sister,
quiet sincerity I had never seen in her and was never to see again,
even at our father's funeral which occurred five years later.
[21]Chapter IV
One of the first things I did when I returned to Bethel in Octo-
ber was to find and examine the scuppernong arbor at the foot of
the garden where, the September after the separation from my
husband, Arnie and I had eaten the clusters of golden scupper-
nongs that always seem to gleam with an inner radiance absorbed
from the sun.
Early one morning I came out of the kitchen and
my child was standing, so slim and lovely, balancing on the wob-
bling supports of the old arbor like a ballet dancer, stretching
out her arms to the early autumn sun and sky. “Mama,” she said,
her face glowing, "the earth is so beautiful, Mama."
Then, for the first time in long months I looked at the earth
and the sky. I saw them. I saw the fig bush that, in my preoccupa-
tion with pain, I had not even noticed before; the large beauti-
fully formed leaves still green, and the purple figs against them,
and my child up there on the arbor, her brown eyes shining, and
the happy look on her fifteen-year-old face. And I knew that
beauty was still a fact. It was my child who brought me
that knowledge; who made me change then, from a defeated old
woman of thirty-eight, without hope, to a woman who knew that
life was good; and that I could go on—and live.
It was my child who, last October, persuaded me to come to
Bethel and realize that beauty again. I owe her so much, and
especially I owe her this wonderful spring. Early in the year I
had the supports of the scuppernong arbor renewed, and I
pruned the vines and bushes. Then March came and the small
green tender leaves began to appear everywhere. Soon the wis-
teria that climbed over the side piazza was in bloom, heavy with
[22]sweetness. A yellow jessamine I had transplanted from the woods
years before mixed light, heavenly scent with the wisteria and
the exquisite blossoms hung like joyful small bells, gold against
the purple. Behind the house, in the garden and the field beyond
it, daffodils and jonquils and narcissuses with bright colors, and
gay movements as a breeze stirred them, celebrated their resur-
rection.
Long ago I had had a door cut through the wall of the dining
room so that we might eat on the side piazza if we wished. That
morning in March when the telephone rang I sat there at break-
fast feeling so peaceful.
In New York I had been very active in the Movement, and
took advantage of every opportunity to propagandize. I talked to
the milkman, to the man who came to repair my radio, to the
man who read the gas meter, to my neighbors. Here in Bethel I
had allowed myself to rest from that and had lapsed into a con-
dition of pure enjoyment.
Sitting at the table that morning I stretched with a real sense
of physical well-being, with only a small nagging worry because
the postman had not brought me the letter from Arnie I had
been expecting. I poured myself a second cup of coffee, and lifted
my head to smell the sweet odor of jessamine and wisteria, feel
the soft heavenly spring breeze on my face, and listen to the birds
chirping and singing.
It was then the telephone rang. Afterward I wondered whether
the pain that passed like a splinter through my heart, as if I knew
beforehand what was coming, was something I imagined or
really experienced. The telephone seemed to ring violently, but
perhaps that, too, I felt in retrospect.
Certainly I had no reason for dread. There had been some
anxiety and on my way to the telephone I remember thinking: it
is Arnie calling to reassure me. For I had written to her twice
and had received no answer, and a few days before had reluc-
tantly sent a special delivery asking why she had not written. Yet
I knew that at times she was called away on important business,
and then could not write. Also she had Ben to take up her time
and so far as I knew was happy with him, although I had thought
she might be mistaken about that step.
[23]"But, Mama," she had said, “you know I have always believed
in living what I think. That is my rock, Mama. Don't take that
from me."
And of course everyone in the Movement approved, or at least
appeared to do so, as well as those people on the fringes who
were part of us and yet would not become too obviously
involved: people like college professors, scientists, social workers,
ministers, people in government-a justice of the supreme court,
an assistant attorney general-all thought it a wonderful
thing, and put Arnie just a little higher on the pedestal that had
been made for her. For Arnie was important. Hitherto she had
been assistant to Agitprop, a position she still held. But there was
her secret work about which even members of the cell (except
perhaps Masha) knew nothing. I had not told anyone that Arnie
acted as liaison with a secret Apparatus. Certainly Arnie should
not have told me, but we had shared many secrets and she knew
I had never divulged one of them. But if few people knew it,
everyone understood that Arnie had become very important. The
Movement is, in a way, like a small town (a world within
a world), and everyone, from New York to Washington and out
to the coast, even to Hawaii, would know almost within minutes
of its happening when someone had been elevated to a high po-
sition. So now, within the Movement, Arnie had a particular
place, a high place. Now her name was known even across the
ocean, and in time who could fortell what honors might come
to her. She was even given a decoration, but that also, had to be
in secret, in a hotel room where only Arnie and two officials from
overseas (members of the Apparatus), one of whom presented
the decoration, were present. Even I was not allowed to be there.
But Arnie wanted me near, so I waited downstairs in the lobby
of the hotel for her, thinking: Now my child has security. She is
secure in the future and for all time.
As Arnie had come out from the elevator and met me in the
lobby her face was shining with a joy that inspired me. As we
hurried out to the bus she said, "Mama, now I am completely
trusted. They trust me in the same way that Washington trusted
Lafayette, or Jefferson trusted Mazzini." I looked at her dear face,
full of enthusiasm and faith, and knew that she was not thinking
[24]of security for herself (as her mother was), only for others. And
though she was grateful for the honors, they were not her goal.
She was as single-minded as Rosa Luxemburg.
When she spoke of Washington I thought: She is trying to
please me. For though I had, long ago, relegated Washington to
his Marxian place in history (the leader of a bourgeois revolu-
tion), nevertheless in our family he had always been held in
special affection and veneration because one of our direct ances-
tors, a friend of Tilghman, had been among those young ardent
officers on Washington's staff. From that association we had at
Mars Hill a very beautiful silver punch bowl, given to the ances-
tor by Washington himself and made by a silversmith in Phila-
delphia after General Washington's own design.
The telephone shrilled, one blast after another. I put down
my
coffee cup
and ran to the table in the hall where the tele-
phone always rested and lifted the receiver.
"New York calling Mrs. Braxton," a girl's voice said.
"Hello, Arnie?" I questioned, expecting to hear her clear,
warm voice. She had called me twice since I had been in Bethel,
once at Christmas time, and more recently on my birthday.
But it was not Arnie's voice that spoke to me. It was a man's
deep voice. "Is this Mrs. David Braxton?" he asked.
"Yes," I said. "Yes." And my heart gave a great leap of appre-
hension and dread.
One night in New York, at Arnie's request, I had put up a for-
eign member of the Apparatus in the small extra bedroom she
always occupied when she was with me in the apartment. The
comrade-I never did know his name-came late in the evening
and left before daylight. He was gone when I woke. Later when
I went to the front stoop of the tenement to get my mail I de-
cided to go across the street to the corner drugstore where I had
a charge account for some toilet articles I had been needing. As
I stepped from the sidewalk I began reading a letter I had re-
ceived. Suddenly someone took hold of my arm and a man's
voice said abruptly, "Where are you going?" I looked from the
letter to the street and saw the blue trousers of a policeman. For
a moment I was too frightened to move. I had not realized until
then that having the "Comrade" in the house had been a strain
[25]at all. Then I raised my eyes to the policeman's face and saw that
he was smiling. He released my arm and said admonishingly,
"Be careful, lady. You'll get run over."
When I told Arnie about it she laughed. "But, Mama," she
said. "Even if 'they' had found out it wouldn't be the police who
would come. It would be the FBI."
Yet when a man's voice asked over long distance from New
York, "Are you Mrs. David Braxton?", I had the same sick feel-
ing of shock and apprehension.
"This is Dr. Kaplan of Bellevue Hospital," he said, and when
I did not answer he again asked, "You are Mrs. David Braxton?"
I told him that I was, wanting that moment arrested in time
so that I need not hear what was coming; for I knew, with a
prescience I hated, that suffering waited for me.
"Mrs. Braxton, we have a young woman here," the business-
like voice continued. "Your name and address appear on an en-
velope found in her purse."
I know that he continued talking, but the telephone bell still
seemed to ring violently in my ears. I could not understand
anything he was saying, and wondered if the physical condition
that had come on me at the hearing for
my divorce years before
was being repeated. I sat down in the chair beside the table and
tried to become quiet and meet whatever was waiting for me at
the other end of the line.
"Hello,” I heard the doctor say as if he had become impatient.
I said, “I am sorry I did not hear well. What were you say-
ing?"
"The young woman is ill." The doctor raised his voice, almost
shouting, as if he thought I was deaf; and perhaps I had given
him that impression.
"What young woman?" I asked stupidly, trying to still the
trembling in my voice.
"Mrs. Braxton," he said loudly, and I could feel his impatience
mounting. "Do you have a young woman relative, or a young
woman friend living in New York?"
"I have a daughter," I said faintly.
"Will you describe your daughter?" he asked.
[26]"Brown eyes," I faltered. "Brown eyes
could not go on.
"Hair?" he asked in a businesslike tone.
99
I said again, but
"Blonde," I told him. “But a pale blonde—not really blonde,"
I repeated foolishly.
"How old is she, Mrs. Braxton?”
"Thirty," I answered.
"Is there anything you could tell me that would distinguish
her from others?" he asked.
I tried frantically to remember, at the same time refusing to
face the fact that the young woman in Bellevue Hospital-ill?
in an accident? dying?—might be my daughter. For I had written
to members of the cell since I had been in the South, and it could
be one of them. Margot, or Myra, or Rose, especially Rose. I
tried to be reasonable and remember. And then I thought of the
ring.
"My daughter," I said, "should be wearing a signet ring with
a design cut into the gold-a ring for making an impression in
wax. . ." It was a ring that had belonged to my father, cut
down to fit Arnie's finger for her twenty-first birthday.
The doctor said with what seemed to me to be cruel satisfac-
tion, "Yes, she is wearing such a ring. Or was."
"Was?" I asked, my heart stopping.
"When they found her," he said.
"Tell me what is wrong," I almost screamed.
And then he told me. I well know that now we look on this
kind of thing in a different way from that of our fathers and
grandfathers. We have been educated to consider it rationally.
Among some I have known there is a certain self-complacence in
having been psychoanalyzed or even in having spent some time
in a mental institution. It may be that I do not understand this
kind of pride. But that morning when the doctor said, "Your
daughter is in the psychiatric ward," I could only know it was
my dearest, my best, my loyal companion who had come to this.
The shock was like a blow on my heart, a physical blow that left
me without breath.
"No, no!" I gasped. I was not speaking to the doctor but to
[27]Life itself. Years ago when my husband and the girl had come
to me and said they wished to be married I had cried out "No,
no!" in the same way. And yet it had happened. There was noth-
ing, no negative in any language that could prevent the fact. I
had to face it. So now I must face this fact.
My hand was rigid on the telephone, and I tried to relax it.
The fingers were stiff and painful. I transferred the receiver to
my other hand.
"Now tell me," I said, and my voice was calm and controlled.
"Mrs. Braxton," the deep voice at the other end said quietly,
"would it be possible for you to come to New York?”
"Of course," I said quickly.
"Immediately?" he asked.
"I can leave at once." I tried hurriedly to remember the sched-
ule of trains that left Bethel for the North, and then suddenly,
although I had never been in an airplane, I said, “I will fly.”
"Very well, we will expect you. Come to the hospital and ask
for me-Dr. Kaplan," he said, and it seemed to me that the doc-
tor's voice which I had thought held some compassion had be-
come dry, unsympathetic, even hard. As I heard the receiver go
dead at the other end—and he had not said good-by-there was
a feeling, even over that long distance, of being left desperately
alone.
And then I thought how ashamed Arnie would be of me. We
had talked so much of the unimportance of the individual. We
had read Rosa Luxemburg's letters written from prison when she
said she felt herself only a drop of water in the great flood sweep-
ing over the earth; and that she was glad and happy to be lost
in such a mighty flood. I tried to remember: It is the mass of hu-
manity that is important, not the individual or what happens to
him. But I was not able to do so. I was concerned only with my
personal agony.
[28]Chapter V
I arrived at the hospital directly from the airport, and after ask-
ing for Dr. Kaplan, sat in the waiting room surrounded by my
hastily packed bags, and with a volume of Alexander Hertzen's
memoirs in my hand. Arnie's father had given me the six small
volumes years before at the time he returned from France.
Someone he met over there had recommended them to him.
Twelve years before I had left most of my books in Bethel,
packed away; and having found these when I was unpacking had
begun to read them again, appreciating the style and information
much more now than I had at the age of twenty-two, when I had
tried to read them to please my husband.
Hertzen gave a very clear picture of Russia in 1850 (he died
in 1870, the very year that Rosa Luxemburg was born), and
though he was a liberal I had been told in New York that he did
much to prepare the way in Russia for Socialism. The volume I
had in my hand was one which included some letters his wife had
written to their daughter Natalie, whom she called Tatochka,
and she begins a letter: "Tatochka, my darling, my little star,
my little flower, my joy. . . ." These letters were the only part
of the volumes that had gained my attention when I had first
read them, and when Arnie was a tiny baby I would look at her
as she lay in her crib and repeat those words in my mind,
ashamed to say them aloud, feeling they were too flowery, and
yet liking them because they expressed the joy I had in that small
bundle of humanity who was our child.
Dr. Kaplan was busy. I waited exactly an hour and twenty-five
minutes for him, for as I sat there I watched the hands of the
[29]clock opposite me on the wall. Somewhere in the great hospital
was my suffering child. Why was she there? And was it indeed
Arnie? The fact of the signet ring on her finger was not conclu-
sive, for I remembered at least one occasion when Arnie had
loaned the ring to one of the members of the cell for use as a
wedding ring. I had not slept all the night before, and if these
questions had beat at me during the night with the sound of the
airplane engines, then in the quiet of the hospital waiting
room they were not diminished, but continued with redoubled
vigor. Only the knowledge that I must try to be calm in prepara-
tion for the coming ordeal prevented me from screaming my
anxiety as the long minute hand moved and halted, halted and
moved, over the face of the clock.
At last the doctor came. He was a tall man, perhaps forty years
old. His brown hair had receded from the two sides of his high
forehead, leaving a peak of hair at the center which gave him
the appearance of a monk with a tonsure. His mouth was well-
formed, rather large, and above it a positive nose. These details
I did not notice at all during the first interview. I was not then
thinking of him as a person. I saw only a doctor. But what was
important was that as I looked at him and heard his voice and
saw the benevolent expression so many Jewish men have, espe-
cially the professional ones, I was suddenly filled with courage,
for everything about him contradicted the feeling I had had of
indifference at the termination of the long distance call.
"You are smaller than I had expected you to be," he said as
we shook hands, and again I was agreeably surprised-and com-
forted by the personal, interested tone.
"Perhaps," he added in a matter-of-fact voice, “I had expected
height because your daughter is taller."
“Like her father," I said and only realized as I spoke that by
accepting in my answer the words "your daughter" I had al-
lowed him to establish as a fact that the young woman in the psy-
chiatric ward was Arnie.
There was an awkward pause, made more awkward by my
poorly suppressed impatience. I could not understand why he did
not take me at once to the ward where the "young woman”
waited to be identified. Now I know it was just that anxiety, that
[30]emotional tension, he was trying to dissolve before taking me
with him, but then I did not understand. I was nervously and
unconsciously handling the book in my hand. "What are you
reading?" he asked. Inwardly protesting I silently gave him the
book.
"Ah," he said with satisfaction. "So you read Hertzen? Do you
remember where he says: 'Ignorance, ignorance alone is the
cause of pauperism and slavery . . . Science, nothing but science
can correct that now and give them bread and shelter. . . .' Do
you remember that?"
I answered that I had just been reading that section of the
memoirs that included the correspondence between Pitcherin
and Hertzen. Apparently delighted with my knowledge he went
on to speak of Hertzen and others and of the situation in Russia
in the 1850's. I tried to listen, but was thinking with a sort of an-
guished impatience: Oh, these Jewish intellectuals! They would
stop to have a literary or artistic or Socialist discussion in the
mouth of hell itself. Then it was impossible to keep silent any
longer as he left Hertzen and began on Kropotkin and interna-
tionalism. At another time I would have enjoyed such a discus-
sion and could have pointed out the difference between the Sec-
ond and Third Internationals, even quoting from Rosa Luxem-
burg's letters to Kautsky; but not then. I said, and my voice,
which I think is normally not unpleasant, came out as a hoarse,
demanding croak: "Dr. Kaplan, where is this young woman?"
For a second he looked startled-he must have become ab-
sorbed in what he was saying-and then he said quietly: "Sit
down, Mrs. Braxton." For all the time we had been standing. He
led me to a corner of the waiting room, away from the other peo-
ple, and we sat near each other.
"How did it happen?" I asked again, my voice still hoarse from
strain and loss of sleep, but controlled and not demanding any
longer. "What exactly is wrong?"
"Mrs. Braxton," he said, "we don't know what is wrong with
her. We are making tests, or rather beginning to do so, but it is
much too early to say. It might take months."
"But how did she get here?" I asked.
He hesitated. And then, looking away from me, and speak-
[31]ing in a cold, withdrawn voice, he said: "She was found on the
street by a patrolman at three in the morning."
"Alone?" I asked.
"Yes," he said. "The patrolman questioned her, but she would
not or could not answer. Frankly, he thought she was.
"He thought she was intoxicated?" I interrupted, and my
voice did not hesitate. At last, I thought, I am facing what is now,
and what is to come. But I did not dream of what was to come.
"Yes," he answered, and his voice was more sympathetic. “That
is what he thought."
"And the patrolman brought her here?”
"He called a squad car. They brought her. And . . . that is
all we know. Apparently she cannot rouse herself to answer any
questions. We looked in her purse and found the envelope with
your address on it. She had no other identification. . .
No, I thought, Arnie, if it were Arnie, would be very careful
not to have any papers at all in her bag. Of course a letter from
me would be innocuous, for I never mentioned her work when I
wrote to her except on rare occasions.
"Did you read the letter?" I asked him.
"There was no letter," he said, "only the envelope addressed
to her and with your return address."
"But why didn't you go to the New York address?" I asked,
thinking of Ben.
"We did send there, first thing," he assured me. "But the pa-
trolman reported that no one answered and the neighbors said
everyone had gone away. So we got in touch with you."
"May I see her now?" I asked.
He gave me a long look that said as plainly as if he had
spoken: Can I trust this woman to keep her emotions under con-
trol? Probably he was dreading the whole business. He sighed
and rose from his chair. Without speaking we walked down an
interminable corridor. I do not remember much of that long
journey to the ward. I know we took an elevator and then
seemed to pass from one building to another, and then to an-
other that appeared much older than the others, and took an
elevator again. In that building, as we came out, another eleva-
tor opened down the wide hall and I saw a drunken man, stag-
[32]gering, dirty and ragged, brought in by a policeman; and I
winced, thinking of Arnie coming into the hospital under such
an escort. One thing, though, was certain. Although a number
of people in the Movement drank, some of them to excess, Arnie
had been one of those so imbued with the inspired need to
keep herself physically fit that in this respect she was almost fanat-
ical.
“Here we are,” Dr. Kaplan said, standing before heavy old
double doors, with frosted glass tops.
"Doctor," I said, touching his arm, "this may or may not be
my daughter. . . .
“I am afraid it is," he interrupted.
“But if it is," I went on as if he had not spoken, “I cannot be-
lieve she was intoxicated."
“Oh,” he sounded surprised. "She wasn't. We found that out
almost immediately."
"Then why is she here, in this ward?" I demanded.
"Your daughter is ill, Mrs. Braxton," he said patiently. And
added, “Very ill.”
[33]Chapter VI
He opened one side of the heavy double doors and went before
me into the ward. I was conscious that a nurse had come to meet
him and that they talked, but my eyes and thoughts were con-
centrated on searching, among the separate heads belonging to
the patients in the ward-lying on a bed, sitting in a chair be-
side a bed, or moving about (there were two girls at the other
end of the long ward working over something at a table)—for
the unusual and unmistakable curls-the color of dry sand—that
would tell me I had found the head I was seeking.
During the month I remained in New York and visited the
ward every day that was allowed I came to know the nurse who
was on duty that morning very well. She had a slight accent
which I could not place at the time (I later learned that she
came from a French-speaking canton in Switzerland); but that
morning as she turned to me and smiled, I knew that hers was
one of the loveliest faces I had ever seen. Not lovely in physical
beauty; as a matter of fact her face in repose was careworn. But
the beauty came from an inner expression of peace that made it-
self felt by me even in that distressful time. This was in con-
trast to the feeling I received from one of the other nurses I later
came to know (and I realize with all those difficult patients the
strain must be great); the feeling that the nurse was thinking:
All right, this has to be done, let's get it over. For I received from
this nurse, the one with the accent, an impression that I was a
guest being welcomed into a home.
She and the doctor finished their consultation and she turned
to me as the doctor left the ward. "Your daughter's name is
[34]Arnie?" she asked; and as I nodded, unable to speak, and as she
led me along the wide space between the rows of beds, she said,
"It is an unusual name. I have never heard it before."
“It is a nickname," I explained. "My own name is Ann and
my daughter was given the same name, so in order to distin-
guish us from each other, we began to call her Arnie."
My knees were growing weaker as we walked slowly down the
center aisle, flanked by beds. A woman raised herself on an elbow
in bed and called out "Nurse!", and when the nurse did not an-
swer, shouted at her obscenely. The two girls who had been
bending over the table at the end of the ward looked up and one
of them laughed loudly enough to be heard over the ward. The
other one (neither could have been over sixteen) looked at Miss
Andrique, the nurse, and said "Hush!" to her companion in a
loud voice, and then began herself to make a disturbance by
singing one of the popular songs of the day and gyrating her
body to the music; and as she did so I saw that she was pregnant.
Suddenly there was the sound of scuffling at the entrance door
behind us. A woman's hoarse voice shrieked curses and a man's
voice spoke authoritatively, trying to calm her. The nurse said
"Excuse me" and left me hastily. I looked that way and saw a
stout, bedraggled woman, wearing a fur coat made of skins of
several different animals and colors, and who had on her arm a
large patched bag, struggling with a policeman. Wisps of gray
hair straggled from under a tight-fitting fur cap, as she flung her-
self about trying to get free.
I heard the policeman say, "Well, Miss Andrique, here's
our Mamie back again." I learned afterward that this poor
woman was generally harmless, but that at times she conceived
the idea that she must cut, probably as she had cut the furs from
castoff fur coats to make her singular garment. At the times
when this mania came on her she would slash at people's cloth-
ing, with a pair of scissors taken from her bag. Once she had in-
advertently wounded someone with the scissors. After a few days
in the ward she would become normal again and some months
might go by before she had another attack.
I turned away from her and the struggle she was making, sick
at heart, as I had been when I saw the drunken man being
[35]taken to the men's ward by the policeman; not only sick for those
two human beings, but because, here in this ward, if the doctor
was right and my daughter was there, Arnie also had been
brought, perhaps struggling like that poor woman.
While the nurse was quieting the woman and taking charge
of her, I turned back to look over the ward, searching each face
for the one I so longed not to meet in that place.
One of the young girls at the table, the one who was not preg-
nant, made an impudent gesture at me, and then toward the
floor near her. I decided she was trying to draw attention to her-
self and turned away, but still saw no one who looked familiar,
and again it came to me, with a little feeling of hope, that my
daughter might not be there. The young girl, the one who was
not pregnant, was again trying to attract my attention. She
grinned at me and pointed, jabbing her right forefinger toward
her right and my left, as if asking me to share a joke with her.
I took a few steps forward in the direction toward which she
seemed to be pointing and saw, at the end of the row on that
side, someone seated on the floor beside one of the beds. Halt-
ingly I went toward the seated figure. And though in place of the
inexpensive but dainty and fastidious clothes she usually wore,
she was clothed in a rough gown (evidently furnished by the hos-
pital), and though her hair, always carefully brushed, was now
a mass of disordered curls, and though the face was bent over
and hidden from me by the hair, I knew without any doubt that
the figure seated on the floor by the bed, not leaning against the
bed, but sitting apart from it, was my child.
Even now I do not know how I reached her, whether I ran or
walked, whether I cried out or was silent. For a time all con-
sciousness left me, and then I found myself kneeling on the floor
holding that head of disordered curls to my breast. It was the
worst thing I could have done for my child, and for the impres-
sion made on the doctors and nurses. Now I am ashamed of hav-
ing given way. But there had been no sleep for me since the day
before . . . but excuses do not help.
The tense body in my arms did not relax. I felt its singular
restraint, its resistance, clear through my own. From that body
[36]there came the impression as clearly as if the words had been
shouted: You are an enemy.
“Arnie,” I said, “Arnie, this is your mother. This is Mama.”
But there was no response. Her feet, in washable slippers of tow-
eling, showed from under the edge of the gown that must
have been used (by how many pitiful ones in the past) a hundred
times, and then washed a hundred times, before it had come to
her. Her knees were under her in an awkward position and I
tried with my other hand to straighten them out and make her
more comfortable; but they resisted me.
A touch came on my shoulder; a hand was pressed there as if
the owner wished to say by that pressure: I am here; and I know,
and I understand.
"Let me help you," the nurse said, and put her hands under
my arms to lift me. But I resisted.
"Why is she here?" I asked. "Why on the floor?"
"It is where she wishes to be," Miss Andrique answered.
I turned back to my child and pushed the curls that had been
covering her face from her forehead.
"Arnie," I said. "Arnie." But her bent head did not move,
though I was straining the hair from her face; and as soon as I
lifted my hand from her forehead the hair fell back.
"Could we get her into the bed?" I asked the nurse.
"Not now," she said. "We gave her an injection last night, and
then it was possible to put her to bed. But as soon as she waked
she was like this. Don't, don't despair," she said with the accent
that made what she said more particular, more meaningful. “She
will be all right, if God in His mercy. . . ."
I could have struck her. I was hideously angry. "Don't say
that!" I whispered hoarsely. "Don't talk to me of God and His
mercy."
With the strength desperation and anger had given me I
turned to my daughter and put my whole hand under her chin
and forcibly lifted it until her eyes were on a level with mine.
And then-then all anger left me and I regretted what I had
done. For then I saw her eyes. In them, in those eyes that had al-
ways expressed so clearly the firm intelligence, the personality
[37]behind them, there was no expression at all. They were rigid just
as her body was rigid. More than that, there seemed to be a film
over them. And then, as I held her chin firmly in my hand, while
I looked into her face, I saw that what I had thought was a film
over her eyes was in fact unshed tears. And these unshed tears re-
mained there. They did not spill over, ever. And under them the
color of her eyes that had been so warm, so clear a brown was
clouded. The pupil seemed to have dissolved and under the film
her eyes were pale and strange.
But the most terrible thing was that the eyes did not recognize
me. I said, looking into them, "Arnie, this is your mother. It's
Mama, Arnie."
I kept repeating this, as if repetition could reach her and bring
those eyes back to life and release the rigid muscles. But not by
one movement of her stark, rigid face, not by a single change in
the drowned eyes did she show that she was conscious of my pres-
ence, of Miss Andrique's presence, or of anything, or of anyone.
[38]Chapter VII
It was through Miss Andrique, the nurse, that I got in touch with
the officer who had found Arnie; but that was some time later.
That morning, after leaving the ward, I took my bags, which had
been brought to the psychiatric building from the main waiting
room, out to the gate, where the watchman on duty in the little
cubicle like a sentry's box clucked at me reproachfully for carry-
ing them, and very kindly left his station to find me a taxi.
I had my keys to the apartment, and as we drove downtown I
looked ahead, planning what I might do when I reached it. At
the same time I was wondering about Ben, since the doctor said
the patrolman they sent had found no one occupying the house.
Had he and Arnie separated? Was the quarrel I had witnessed
between them the night before I left New York and in which, to
my eternal regret, I had allowed myself to become involved, the
beginning of the end for them? I could not believe it. None of
Arnie's letters to me during the six months I had been away had
indicated any change in their relationship. She had mentioned
him in the most natural way as if he was constantly in the apart-
ment working at his typewriter. Then why, I asked myself as the
taxi passed along Second Avenue, had the neighbors said Ben
had gone away. Not that he "wasn't at home," which would have
indicated a temporary absence, but that everyone had "gone
away."
As I had directed him the taxi driver turned east on Eleventh
Street. Here I was reminded of our first years in New York as
we passed the hall where Arnie had gone to occasional dances
given for the benefit of one of our front organizations. I had at-
[39]tended one of these dances, but did not like what I saw there. Of
course it was different for Arnie, who was only nineteen then
and who loved to dance, and was enjoying herself on the floor.
Few of the Party officials ever attended these dances, and long
ago Arnie had stopped going to them. But that evening, because
she was on the floor, she was not able to see what I did as I sat
in one of the boxes upstairs. While I watched it seemed to me
these people, most of whose names and faces I knew from hear-
say and from having them pointed out to me at front meetings,
were using the dance only as an excuse for letting go all inhibi-
tions. I know that I sound puritanical (in reality a beautiful
word, but with us a term of reproach, even of condemnation), in
saying this now and in feeling it at the time, but it seemed to me
the cause was too important for any of us to lower the standards
of conduct as it was lowered then. The thought occurred to me
that evening that these men from the front organizations were
like boys who have an overwhelming desire for adventure, and
will irresponsibly dare each other to shoot a gun, or fight a duel
with loaded weapons, as two boys in our neighborhood did-and
that they enjoyed punishment, because by bringing it on them-
selves, and by being so much braver and so debonair, they were
showing up the "stodgy" people by whom they were punished
or accused. So these men (respected in the bourgeois world),
seemed through their irresponsibility, and under the influence of
liquor, to be showing an aspect of their natures that was also an
aspect of the attitude they carried into the front committees.
That evening (I was then forty years old), I was sitting in one
of the boxes beside a man who had a very prominent position in
the bourgeois world, and a wife and children as well; and under
the cover of the darkness, for the lights were dim, especially in
the boxes, he slipped his hand under my skirt and on to my
knee. By this time I had been thoroughly educated by comrades
and current "literature” into at least an academic freedom from
"prudishness" about sex in any of its manifestations, so it was
not shock at what happened but indignation that literally
brought me to my feet. At the same time I heard myself saying,
in a clear voice that carried above the music, one of the un-
printable exclamations of disgust our friends had often used; and
[40]which I had become accustomed to hearing made to each other
by the boys who played on the streets in front of the tenements
or in the courtyard of our house, and who used these expres-
sions naturally and frequently as if they were a necessary part
of the language. As I left the box I glanced at the man who had
been beside me and saw that he was gaping at me as if struck
dumb with amazement. I was amazed at myself, for until that
moment I had not realized that my mind had absorbed, so com-
pletely that I could use them impulsively and naturally, the un-
printable words I had heard.
Later I had told Arnie about the incident and expressed my
indignation and disappointment. "But think of the reactionar-
ies, Mama,” she said. (At the time Arnie was attending the
Workers' School and was using many new words and expressions
[at least new to us] that came from her studies there.) "They do
the same things, only worse. Think of some of the men at
home." "But," I said, "we should be better than they. We are
changing the world and making new people." But Arnie became
impatient with me. "People won't change, Mama, until we have
changed the whole world. We have inherited all the capitalist
faults and evils. You can't expect any real change until the whole
environment is changed. And besides," she added, "those people
are useful to us now, and we have to suffer them, but as soon as
we come to power that element will be liquidated." And of course
all this was so reasonable I had been silenced.
From Eleventh Street the taxi driver turned right and took us
into Tompkins Square. I had often gone to that square before
the East River parks were opened to the public, and in the
spring and summer had sat on the benches under the trees, and
had always thought it a beautiful square, though toward the
end of summer the leaves on the trees became dry and brittle and
dusty. But earlier it was pleasant to sit there and enjoy the ex-
panse of open sky made visible by the size of the square and be-
cause the buildings on the four sides were only three or four sto-
ries in height. I would sit there on a Sunday morning and watch
people going into the Eastern Orthodox Church at the far cor-
ner, or nearer to me, into the Roman Catholic and the Lutheran
Churches that were on the eastern side. On weekday afternoons,
[41]if I sat in my usual place I could hear the boys in the Jewish day
school chanting their lessons, for the Jewish Synagogue and
school was just behind one of the churches.
As we drove by, the square looked the same as it had when I
had left the October before; for, though it was March, the
trees still had no leaves on them, and I could not help but con-
trast their bareness with the luxurious promise of the spring I
