had left behind. Then we passed from the square into the dark-
ened tunnel made by the high tenements on each side of the
street.
Finally we arrived at the tenement behind which was my
apartment. I do not know why some buildings on the Lower East
Side have what are called rear houses, or for what purpose these
rear houses were designed many years ago when the tenements
were built. I know that one such building we looked at when we
first came to New York and were searching for a home that would
not cost too much, and that would also be among the "wretched
of the earth," had been used as a small factory some years be-
fore. There were even some rusted machines on trestles in the
two-story building, which the landlord said had been used for
making men's vests.
I persuaded the taxi driver to carry my two heavy bags
through the hallway of the tenement and across the courtyard.
The remodeled building in which we lived had two stories. My
apartment was on the first floor, shallow steps leading up to the
doorway. I noticed two full milk bottles standing just inside the
outer door and thought fleetingly but affectionately of Robert
Todd, the milkman I had persuaded to join our cell, while I
fumbled in my purse for my key ring. My attention partly on
what I was doing and partly trying to puzzle out the significance
of the two milk bottles, but almost wholly concerned with a sense
of desolation that had come on me, I was startled to hear some-
one call, "Mrs. Braxton." I turned and saw Mrs. Eagan, one of
our neighbors. Her head, leaning out of the second floor win-
dow, brought back the times when she and I had often ex-
changed neighborly talk across the courtyard. “Hello, Deedie,” I
called, but before I could ask any questions she said, "You look-
ing for your daughter?"
[42]I said, "No, my daughter is in the hospital."
Deedie Eagan leaned farther out of the window. Her habitu-
ally glum face lit up with the expression that always came on it
when she sensed that there was news. Each time this happened
her features, that were usually drab, came alive; her eyes spar-
kled, her ears seemed to spread out wider from her head; and
even her nose moved, the nostrils quivering sensitively.
"My, that's terrible!" she said with a groan of sympathy.
From former experience I knew that others would be listen-
ing behind the windows of the tenements, and shrank from hav-
ing Arnie "put on the air" so to speak. I did not trust my own
emotional control either and though I did not feel at all sleepy
there was an immense weariness throughout my body. Yet, para-
doxically enough, it was good to have Deedie recognize me, be-
cause the moment before, as I stood at the door, there had been
a strange feeling of unreality, of belonging nowhere, as if I were
a ghost come back to haunt the scenes of my
former life.
"Your daughter very sick?" Deedie asked.
"Oh," I said cheerfully, "she'll be out soon." And even mak-
ing that far from certain statement gave me a temporary feeling
of hope, as if the doctor had in fact said to me, “Your daughter
home in a week."
can go
“Why did the cop come?" Deedie called, her voice sounding
to me as loud as a trumpet.
I found my key ring and inserted the key into lock. "Deedie,"
I said, “I am so worn out I can't talk now. I was awake all night.
I'll see you later."
"Okay. You need any food let me know," Deedie yelled. I
turned and nodded and smiled and thought of all the canned
goods under Deedie's bed. She was on relief and often for one
reason or another received extra help. We had heard her tell of
all the tricks she used and had often had some hearty laughs
over them when we occasionally visited her in her cold-water
flat and she would lift up the edge of the counterpane that cov-
ered her bed and show us what was there. The cans and other
supplies were always stacked high even though she shared what
she received with two married daughters. We had encouraged
Deedie to feel that the government owed her the relief or wel-
[43]fare she got and that the more she demanded of the government
the more she would receive. But it was impossible to make her
conceive the idea and ideal of the State being all-important and
that it would bring security not only to her, but to all people,
especially those like herself. It seemed only that she got a "kick”
out of obtaining something for nothing; and we had had to come
to the conclusion that she was a backward worker and would
remain so. But she was good company, especially after a drink or
two of beer.
Now, however, I was not looking for company. More than any-
thing else I wished to be alone, or thought I did. And though I
was disappointed, there was also relief when I had opened
the door of the apartment and felt almost certain that no one
was there. As in many of these flats, the kitchen was the first room
entered, and the largest. So I entered first the room in which
Arnie and I had been sitting when she persuaded me to go back
to Bethel. All was as usual: that is, the bookcases, the couch, the
chairs were at one end, the stove, refrigerator, cabinets and table
at the other. But the room did not look lived in as it had when I
was there.
The doors of the two bedrooms that led off from the kitchen
were closed. Just to be sure, I knocked on the door of the bed-
room which had been mine, and which Arnie and Ben had been
occupying while I was away. No one answered, and I opened the
door. Even at first glance the room had an air of desolation
about it. The bed was unmade and the whole room, even more
than the other, seemed abandoned, left to its fate, like con-
demned tenements I had seen that waited for the wreckers.
There was no evidence of Ben about; no bathrobe, no bedroom
slippers, no ties on the rack. In the closet Arnie's clothes were on
the hangers at one side, leaving half the space on the rod empty.
I hurried to the smaller bedroom. Ben had intended to use
this room for working on his book and before I had left for
Bethel he had settled his books and papers and typewriter there.
But no papers, books, nor any other paraphernalia remained;
and the typewriter was gone.
The sense of unreality that had taken possession of me at the
door persisted, and became almost unbearable. I had not real-
[44]ized until then what a shock it had been to have my child look
at me as if I did not exist. And though Deedie's recognition had
helped, I needed desperately to relate myself more definitely to
those desolate rooms and the life I had been living in them for
the past twelve years. It was then the thought came that I might
get in touch with Ben if I called him at the magazine where he
worked.
In the larger bedroom where the telephone stood on my desk
I looked up the number of the magazine in the telephone book.
When the receptionist answered at the other end I asked for Ben.
"I am sorry, he is not here," the girl said.
"Can you tell me where I might find him?" I asked.
"Well.

she spoke to someone in the office. "I'm afraid

"And you don't know where I could reach him?”
"No," she said. "But you could leave a message. It is possible
he might get in touch with us again." But she sounded doubtful.
I thought for a moment, and it seemed to me it was best not to
leave my number and name; so I told the girl "never mind” and
put the receiver down. As I turned wearily from the telephone
I glanced over the surface of the desk where the lamp I had
turned on there, in order to see the number in the telephone
book, showed more clearly a pile of papers, on top of which I
saw a letter from myself, lying there as if Arnie had just finished
reading it. On taking it up I saw at once that it was the latest
letter I had written her, the special delivery letter, the envelope
of which had been found in her purse. I searched all the papers
that had been under the letter, but what I was searching for I do
not know. The papers were innocuous, notices of various front
meetings, and some clippings about meetings already held; mate-
rial that Arnie, as Assistant to Agitation and Propaganda would
keep for her files. Then I glanced down at the wastebasket be-
side the desk and saw that it held some crushed typewriting pa-
per, the same kind of paper (probably taken from the store of
it Ben had on hand), that Arnie had been using for her letters to
me while I was at Bethel. I reached into the basket and took up
the first crushed sheet, pushed the papers aside, smoothed the
[45]sheet out on the flat top of the desk, and read the words written
there in my child's handwriting:
"Dearest Mama:" (it said) "The reason I haven't written is
because there has been some trouble.
The last words were
crossed out.
""
I reached down and took up another sheet and smoothed it
out. On it were the words:
"Dearest Mama: Would it be asking too much of you to come
back to New York now instead of later? I know it is spring and
you are loving it down there. I would go down but if I stay here
I am certain. . .
""
There were no more crushed sheets, but down among some
discarded folders and mimeographed releases I saw several torn
fragments of typewriting paper. These, too, had Arnie's hand-
writing on them. I took them out from among the other contents
of the basket and one by one fitted them together on the surface
of the desk.
"Dearest Mama:" (the torn fragments said) "Something un-
believable has happened. I cannot understand. . . .
And then far below, toward the bottom of the paper, as if the
words had been added there as a last resort, a futile and pitiful
attempt to balance the scales that were becoming unbalanced,
she had written: “Do you think there might be a God who could
help me?"
I read them, and then my head went down against my arms on
the desk, and for the first time since I had received the telephone
call from the doctor, I wept.
[46]Chapter VIII
Perhaps the tears relaxed the tormented muscles of my mind
and body. Whatever the cause, when the paroxysm of bitter
weeping was over a great weariness overcame me and I staggered
to the bed and lay down upon it, though it was still unmade. I
did not wake until almost six o'clock in the evening.
The doctor had said I might visit Arnie only at the prescribed
hours, and I could not see her until the next day. The heavy
weight of the events of the last twenty-four hours came on me
soon after I awoke; but I knew that the everyday things must be
given preference over anxiety and that in this now desolate place
it would be necessary to establish some order so that I might, for
my child's sake if not for my own, function as a normal and rea-
sonable human being.
Having had no food since the day before my first thought was
for a sustaining meal and I opened the door of the refrigerator.
It was almost empty. I sighed, thinking of the way it had been
kept full of good things when I was there. On one shelf there was
a little cream in a half-measure carton, two eggs and a few slices
of bacon, and the bottles of milk I had brought in. That was all.
So it would be necessary for me to go out and find a delicatessen.
I washed my face (later there would be a refreshing bath),
and straightened my rumpled shantung suit, and taking up my
bag and coat, for in March it is cold in New York, I went
through the courtyard and the hall of the tenement to the street
outside.
It was only then I remembered that I needed money. In the
morning I could go to my own bank and have a check cashed,
[47]for I had kept my account there since I had expected to return
to New York the latter part of April. But even to buy a few
things at a delicatessen meant that a small check must be cashed.
I thought fleetingly of Deedie Eagan, for I knew she would share
her food with me, but I also knew with a very solid foundation
of experience that if I went to Deedie I would spend the rest of
the evening there. It was too high a price to pay. So, as I had
done in past years when I needed a small check cashed or a favor
done, I turned to the left toward the corner drugstore where the
owner, Mr. Friedman, who was "Doc" to the neighborhood,
had never, so far as I knew, refused to help anyone. Gentle, un-
assuming, he was always ready to cash a relief check, give advice
about a pain and recommend a medicine, take a speck out of an
eye, relieve a toothache, or recommend a doctor or a dentist or a
clinic. Sometimes in the evening the drugstore would be filled
with people of all races who needed help or advice. This had
been especially true just before the war, when he had helped
gnarled hands make out international money orders to be sent
to Europe or had given advice on how to bring relatives to this
country. And after the war they came to him with bundles of
clothes and food sewed up in clean flour sacks so he might ad-
dress them in the proper way and make out the declarations for
the customs.
I hurried to the corner, with a small lift to my heart, as I felt
in anticipation the warmth of his benevolence. And he was
there, behind the prescription counter. Short and stocky, but
well-proportioned; the kindly face with rather large gray eyes
looking puzzled as if life and its problems were too much for him,
but as if, though he could not understand, he accepted the puz-
zle that was life and did the best he could with it. He was just
as I had last seen him there the evening before I had left New
York, even to the beard that, since his morning shave, had be-
gun to show again on his chin and the lower part of his cheeks.
He must have felt my gaze for he looked up from what he was
doing, over and between the four people waiting at the counter,
saw me and gave me the child-like, pleased smile to which I had
become accustomed; only now there was a special lighting of his
eyes in welcome.
[48]I was pleased to see that he began to hurry a little with his
customers, not because I was impatient, but because the very
fact that he hurried, who was usually so deliberate and quiet in
his movements, gave an added warmth to that of which I had
had a taste when he had smiled at me over the head of the
woman to whom he was speaking. Finally the four customers
were gone, though some others had come into the store after I
had done so. I went to the counter.
"How are you, Mrs. Braxton?" he asked in his gentle, fatalistic
voice as we shook hands.
I answered that I was feeling very well physically.
"Yes," he agreed, "your visit South has done you good. I can
see that. You are not so thin.
•
I asked him about himself and his family (they lived in an
apartment above the store), and he said he, his wife and the two
children were well.
"This is a surprise," he said. "I understood from your daugh-
ter that she was not expecting you until May."
"Yes," I answered, “but. . . ." I looked behind me, saw the
others who were waiting and said, "I'd like to speak to you," for
it had occurred to me, when he spoke of Arnie, that perhaps he
could tell me something about her condition during the past
weeks.
"They will wait," he said. "I am expecting my assistant any
moment."
I started to say: Do you have an assistant now? but remem-
bered that it was Friday evening and that though he was not
religious, to please his wife he kept the Sabbath, and that a
young pharmaceutical student came in on Friday evening while
he went upstairs for dinner and the preparation for the next
day.
"Could you cash a small check?" I asked.
He said that of course he could, and I wrote the check and re-
ceived the money from him. Then I did not know how to go on;
but he helped me.
"I haven't seen your daughter in several days," he said. "In
fact, not since a few days after her husband went away."
I had meant to be very careful in my questions but could not
[49]restrain my anxiety and said, "When was that? I mean,” I
amended, "when did her husband go?"
Mr. Friedman wrinkled his forehead trying to remember. One
of his most engaging qualities was the manner in which he ac-
cepted a person and his need; no lifting of the brows or ques-
tioning. So now, as when he spoke of Arnie's "husband" in the
most natural way, he did not find my question unusual, but was
ready to give me the information asked for.
"He came in here," Mr. Friedman told me, “one morning to
get a check cashed and buy some toilet articles. I said it looked
as if he were stocking up for a long trip and he said he was that
he was leaving that day. Excuse me a moment," Mr. Friedman
added, for his assistant had come in and was beside him. I moved
away while they conferred; and then he came around the
counter and joined me while the people who had been waiting
gathered before the assistant. The Friedmans' apartment had a
separate door on the Avenue where stairs led up to the apart-
ment, and we walked slowly along the street toward that door.
"I remember now," he said. "It was a week ago, that is, tomor-
row it will be a week exactly since he came in.”
We reached his door and I stood there, holding to him with all
the concentration that was in me, keeping him from his dinner,
with nothing to say, and yet longing to ask questions to which
he might have the answer. At last I "found my tongue" and re-
minded him that he had spoken of Arnie and that she had come
in to see him.
"But how is she?" he asked.
"She is in a hospital; that is why I am here earlier than I had
planned to come," I said.
"Oh. . . ." he considered. "Then that is the reason.
she very ill?"
·
Is
"No," I lied. "But what did you mean by saying 'that is the
reason'?"
"Nothing really important," he seemed embarrassed.
I begged him to tell me, trying to keep my voice composed so
that he might not know how anxious, how desperate I was to re-
ceive from him even one small incident that would help me to
visualize what had taken place since I had received my last letter
[50]from Arnie. One fact had already been established-that Ben
had left the Saturday before. So now I knew that my child had
been alone in the apartment from that time until, on Wednes-
day evening, when she had begun the letters I had found in the
wastebasket, she had gone out into the city, where she was found
at three o'clock on Thursday morning.
He did not answer my question at once, and appeared to be
trying to come to some kind of decision. And as I waited I
thought of him; a wonderful man, so good, so kindly. Yet I knew
that, because of his nature, which was not unmoral, but amoral,
he had no place within himself where he could find the strength
to say no. People had told me that during prohibition he had
sold alcohol behind the counter. If he did, and I am not sure it
was true, he did it, not to make money, but because of kindliness.
He had told me himself that he had never denied anything to
his children and sighed and groaned over the way they were
turning out. I knew that whatever Arnie asked of him, he would
not question her need.
"It was not important," he said, and I saw that he was reluc-
tant to tell me because in his kindly way he did not wish to say
anything that might disturb me.
"But it is important," I urged.
“Well,” he said. “It was only that she came in on Monday, this
past Monday—and she looked very ill, Mrs. Braxton-not at all
like herself. Up to that time I don't believe she has looked a day
older than when you first came to live here-how many years
ago was that?”
"Twelve years,” I said. "And then?" I prompted.
"Twelve years," he mused. "And she hasn't looked a day older.
And all this winter strong and happy. But on Monday she was
older looking. She was thin with hollows under her eyes. Al-
most overnight she had lost as much as twenty pounds. She said
she had been unable to sleep and asked if I would give her some
sleeping pills."
"And she hadn't consulted a doctor?" I asked.
He shook his head sorrowfully. “I asked her, because she was
looking so ill, and she said all she needed was sleep. 'I have
never in my life used sleeping pills before,' she said and she
[51]laughed; not carefree laughter, Mrs. Braxton, as her laughter
had been before. And she said, 'I am almost afraid of them. I sup-
pose because they are something mysterious and unknown.'
Well, something like that."
"Oh, that is true," I agreed. “She never needed them."
"So I gave her a few," he went on. "She didn't come back
again and I thought everything must be all right. Afterward I
felt I should have insisted that she see a doctor. But now, I sup-
pose, everything is satisfactory. Now she is in a hospital there will
be plenty of doctors."
He did not ask what was wrong with her and I did not volun-
teer the information; not because I did not trust him, but be-
cause once begun, it would be impossible for me not to confide
in him all my sorrow and perplexity. I thanked him and said
good-night and hurried away.
Walking west, toward First Avenue, for the first time since I
had returned to the neighborhood I became aware of the famil-
iar tenements between whose high walls I had made my way so
often on errands for the house, or to a demonstration or meet-
ing. Lifting my eyes I could see, in some of the apartments, the
shine of the Sabbath Eve candles, already lit by the wife, the
mother of the house, who might be seen, with her head covered
by a kerchief, saying the Friday evening prayers over the candles.
And I thought of the time, now ten or more years ago, when the
Nazis were conquering Europe I had said to myself: It is people
like these, my neighbors, who are being tortured and killed-and
remembered my mother's disapproval if we repeated any cheap
expression of prejudice we had heard: "I forbid you to say that
about God's Ancient People."
"God's Ancient People," I repeated to myself. "Would it not
be curious if those women, lighting the candles for their fam-
ilies, were right and there is such a being as God; a Being
who chose them as His own, as my mother and father had be-
lieved."
Then I corrected these thoughts in the light of reason. In the
past hundred and fifty years it had been only those who denied
God who had brought social progress to the world: the people
of the French Revolution; then Marx and all who followed
[52]him; the makers of the revolutions of 1848, Social Democrats,
but all denying God. And in our own country Stevens, Sumner,
and Wade who had engineered Reconstruction in the South and
whom my father bitterly called "The Godless Three." Only these
had brought about progress for humanity.
Certainly the Nazis also denied the existence of a Creator. But,
as we all knew, they had imitated, in an exaggerated and dis-
torted way, much that was in the Marxian philosophy, just as
the Marxian philosophy (which is also a Way of Life), had taken
many of its ideals from the Christian religion, for most of the
old Bolshevists had been reared in Christian homes. The Nazis,
in denying God, were only following in a foolish and unthinking
way the philosophy of Marxism, which is deeply and meaning-
fully and in the best sense dedicated to the purpose of elimi-
nating superstition from the minds of men.
Thinking all these things I soon reached Second Avenue,
where I found a delicatessen open, and then returned to the
apartment with my bag of food. There I made an omelette and
after supper when I had put clean sheets on my bed I was ready
to lie down, though the long sleep during the day had rested me
greatly.
Lying in bed, with the light off, but not yet sleepy, I heard the
various sounds, different from the sounds outside my little house
in Bethel, from apartments so much closer than the houses of
my neighbors in the South: voices or instruments from radios, a
child crying, an occasional quarrel; voices muted now because
the windows were closed; and sounding quite far away and felt
more than heard, the rumble of trucks that shook our building
as they passed.
And into my mind came a plan of what I might do after I had
seen my child the next day. I would visit, one by one, certain
members of the cell. I made myself see them all, sitting in the
kitchen-living room, on chairs, on the couch and on the floor as
we held a meeting. I tried to feel back in my memory and select
from those I saw the members who had been closest to Arnie and
myself, who might be able to give me some hint of what had hap-
pened to her. And looking at them in my imagination and feel-
ing backward in memory, I saw Rose, who was my favorite, Mar-
[53]got, Miriam (or Myra), Bertha and Bob. Perhaps in the follow-
ing days I would remember others, or eliminate some of those
who had come particularly to my mind.
And then I remembered: The cell meets on Monday, here, in
this apartment! How I had forgotten I do not know, except that
there had been so much on my mind and heart. So I went to sleep
seeing the members coming into the apartment on Monday eve-
ning (only two days away) and hearing them say to me: "Com-
rade.
[54]Chapter IX
Dr. Kaplan was not at the hospital on Saturday and Sunday
when I visited the ward, but on Monday morning I had a few
words with him. He seemed impatient, and all the time I was
with him, anxious to get away. I suppose it was the lack of any
clear-cut diagnosis, as might enable him to say: "Your daughter
has appendicitis; we will operate tomorrow, and she can go
home in a few days," that made the situation embarrassing, even
irritating to him. But his reluctance only gave added confidence
in him because he was not glibly reassuring.
After seeing Dr. Kaplan I visited my bank and had a check
cashed. On Saturday I had cleaned the apartment thoroughly
and baked tea cakes in preparation for the cell meeting on Mon-
day evening, so there was little to do in the house on Monday.
There then remained of that day, after my check was cashed, the
time until seven-thirty in the evening when members of the cell
would come to my home and I could at last ease the gnawing
anxiety that was in me by asking them questions about Arnie.
As the hour of seven-thirty approached I found myself becom-
ing more and more nervous, like a hostess who is not sure of
herself because she is entertaining for the first time people whom,
for the sake of her husband's work, or for her own acceptance
into a certain group to which she wishes to belong, or for some
other reason, she longs to please. And with the same feeling of
expectation and dread, and with trembling hands, I set out the
cups and saucers and spoons in careful, symmetrical designs on
the kitchen table, my actions so different from those on the other
Monday evenings when, with warm expectancy, I had laid out
[55]the china and refreshments and prepared the coffee without any
thought of how they might seem to others. On those occasions I
had had in mind only the looked-forward-to pleasure of the com-
pany of people for whom I felt affection and with whom I shared
a great common purpose; and always an inner delight at the
thought that Arnie would soon be there.
I berated myself now for the feeling of nervousness and found
myself saying foolishly: "This is a free country," as we had when
we were children and were defiant toward each other or toward
our parents. But why I said it I do not know, for I was defiant
toward no one.
At last the hour arrived when the members of the cell were to
be expected; but I heard no voices in the courtyard, no knock at
the door. At first I was only puzzled by their tardiness, for often
some of them came early, and occasionally even at seven for a
talk over the coffee and cake about some problem either per-
sonal or concerning the Party. For some time I remained puzzled
and disappointed, yet still expectant. I glanced at the clock in-
termittently, but it only told me that the minutes were going by,
and no matter how often I glanced at it, longing to arrest the
hands, or listened for sounds outside that would tell me some of
them were arriving, neither of these things, so wished for, came
about; and at nine o'clock it was necessary for me to give up look.
ing at the clock, and stop listening for voices in the courtyard,
and say to myself, "They are not coming."
The words were necessary, so that I might face the fact, but
the very act of saying them and of accepting what, for more than
an hour, I had been trying to deny, gave me a feeling of the deep-
est depression, as if by a whole community of people I had been
rejected. Now I felt more alone than I had ever felt before, and
the loneliness was only increased as I viewed the tea cakes set out
so temptingly on the table, the cups and saucers and spoons and
the large percolator which I had bought especially for the unit
meetings.
Almost fifteen minutes went by before I roused myself from
the depression enough to know that I must do something in or-
der to keep myself on an even keel; and then resolved that I
[56]would immediately go and visit a member of the cell and find out
for myself why they had not come.
The first name that came into my mind was Rose's, doubtless
because she had been nearest and dearest to me of any in the
cell, almost another daughter, for she often walked over in the
evening or on Sunday and we would sit in the kitchen and talk
over glasses of tea in the Russian style, as Rose liked it. So I was
confident that I could depend upon her loyalty and affection.
Without giving myself time to think any longer I took up my
coat, and locking the door of the apartment, went out into the
street. Rose lived with her family on Tompkins Square; and it
was not necessary for me to consult my notebook (never left care-
lessly about) that had in it the initials and addresses of the vari-
ous members of the cell, to find her address, for I had visited in
her home before.
Rose had been a member of the cell for five years, since she
was eighteen. The youngest child of four, she was now twenty-
three. Her hair was red and curly (inherited from a great-grand-
father in the old country, that is, in Russia, who had been called
"Red Beard"), and her eyes large and brown. Her body was
stocky and rather unattractive, but her piquant features and ani-
mated, gamin-like manner toward everyone and everything (ex-
cept when she became serious and attentive over Party matters)
made those about her forget anything but her bubbling and en-
dearing personality. I think one of her characteristics that at-
tracted me first was that she was completely uninhibited. But this
attraction was more the fascination that a snake, suddenly en-
countered on a path, might have had for me. I was shocked at
the sophisticated expressions she used and comments she made.
Before I knew how young she was I had thought her much older.
Then, as we grew in affectionate regard and understanding I
realized that a number of the sophisticated remarks came from
her without her knowing in the least what they meant, but that
they had been made by someone she admired, and she repeated
them for that reason rather than because she understood their
meaning, just as a child will repeat what he has heard an adult
say without understanding, and be given credit for more wisdom
[57]than he possesses. She would make some profound statement
about a symphony or other piece of music played on my victrola,
without comprehending what she said, but because she had
heard that comment made by someone whom she looked up to
as smart. Yet my perception of this characteristic, which came
much later when I had known her for a longer time, did not de-
tract in the least from my pleasure in her company or my deep
affection for her.
It was Rose who introduced me to the stories of Shalom Ale-
'ichem, the great Yiddish-Russian writer, and though I came to
know later, when I talked to her about them, that she had not
read them herself (although very knowingly she had said to me
that many of the expressions could not be translated into English
without losing the vital spark of humor), nevertheless I was
grateful to her for giving me the opportunity of experiencing, in
the mind and heart of another, a great author, a humor so closely
connected with pain that laughter and tears mingle as the poign-
ant, beautiful words convey so exactly the character of the
"Dairyman" about whom the author is writing.
Once Rose had taken me to her home for the first Passover
Supper, the Seder, for her parents were orthodox, although her
mother had let her hair grow out (a concession to her children),
and did not wear the wig that many of the orthodox women of
the East Side did. It was when I was there for the Seder that I
saw how this youngest child was idolized by her parents; and
even by her brothers and sisters, who were present with their
wives and husbands.
I was astonished that evening at the way all lounged in their
chairs or on the table until Rose whispered to me that it was
the custom at this celebration to "recline." "We are supposed to
‘lie on each others' bosoms,' she said, laughing. She also ex-
plained the dishes that were on the table: the bone and egg,
parsley and salt water (as I remember it) symbolizing the bitter
hardships of slavery in Egypt, and the cakes representing the
bricks the Israelites were forced to make at that time. There was
also a plate of matzoth and glasses of wine which they tipped and
spilled at certain places in the service. In the center of the table
there was a single glass of wine, which Rose explained to me
[58]was there for Elijah, who would come in during the evening
as a guest.
Mr. Coplan, Rose's father, was a tall, handsome man and not
old, although he wore a beard, and I had to confess to myself that
when he had assumed his prayer shawl and stood at the head of
the table and told the story of the Jews, a great inner dignity and
pride that was in him lent a large impressiveness to the scene,
like one from the Old Testament; and the expression my mother
had used, "God's Ancient People," became for a moment very
real to me. And I noticed that while he was explaining, touching
each of the objects on the table and intoning the Yiddish or He-
brew words, the faces of the sons and daughters about the table
became serious and absorbed, though just before then they had
been laughing with Rose, whose voice as she explained it all to
me held a note of mockery; and when, as the youngest, she asked
the questions of her father, "Why do we do this . or that?",
her voice sounded almost impertinent. All were drinking wine,
and I believe Rose more than the others, and when it came time
to open the door for Elijah she ran to the door, opened it, and
called very loudly “Elijah!” and looked expectantly into the hall-
way, as if really believing that Elijah would appear. When noth-
ing happened she turned, gave a shrug, threw out her hands and
said, "See? No Elijah." It was a clever and dramatic comment
on the futility of religious belief, and her brothers and sisters
laughed, and then checked themselves, looking at the mother
and father. But the parents, so devoted to Rose, and thinking she
could do no wrong, although they did not laugh, looked on with
loving tolerance.
Rose had told me that they did not even suspect that she had
been a member of the Party for five years. That is why I knew,
as I made my way to her home that evening, it would be neces-
sary for me to see her alone, and I wondered how this could be
arranged in that hospitable home.
The Coplans lived in the second floor of one of the brownstone
houses on the square and I was glad I had only one flight of
stairs to climb, for the walk there had been tiring, mainly be-
cause of the tensions that were in me. Mr. Coplan came to the
door when I knocked. He invited me in at once, but I asked if
[59]Rose was at home. He said that on Monday evenings she had a
study club meeting, and I realized again that they had never sus-
pected where her activities were centered, and that he would be
astonished if he had known that for the past several years Rose
had spent those Monday evenings at her "study club" in my
home at a cell meeting.
He asked if I would come in and wait for her; that she usu-
ally returned about ten o'clock and he was sure she would be
glad to see me. I was undecided about going in to wait, for it
had occurred to me that it might be better to stay outside and
intercept Rose on the sidewalk so that we might talk freely of
what was in my mind.
As I hesitated we heard steps on the stairway below and Rose
appeared. When she saw me she halted as if she had seen a ghost,
and a curious expression came on her attractive face, an unusual
expression for Rose-of indecision.
I said impulsively, "Hello, Rose. I want to see you." Yet still
she did not move.
"What's the matter? What's the matter?" her father asked.
"Come and speak to your friend,” he added in an admonishing
voice, as to a child.
At that moment, when everything was at a standstill in time
(only for a moment, yet it seemed longer), a story about her fa-
ther that Rose had once told me came suddenly into my mind.
Her father had come to this country from Russia and though he
spoke English well he never really felt at home in that language
-so that he disliked to talk over the telephone and especially
over long distance. Occasionally one of the married daughters,
who lived in New Jersey, called him and he would listen to her
for a time as she spoke to him in English, and then having under-
stood little of what she said, because he obstinately would not lis-
ten, he would call out, "What's the matter? What's the matter?
Write me a letter."
So that evening, after the still minute of indecision or distress
or apprehension had come in Rose's face, I saw another look
come on it, of resolution and impudent challenge that had been
on her face that evening at the Seder; only now this look was
for me. She came running up the stairs and going to her father
[60]put her arms about his waist (he was much taller than she), and
hugged him. Then she looked back at me and said, as if teasing
her father, but making me understand by her glance that she
was speaking to me. "What's the matter? What's the matter?
Write me a letter."
Her father laughed (the expression had become a joke in the
family), and said to me over her head and over his arms that
held her, "What can you do with a child like this?" very
proudly.
They stood together with their arms each about the other, and
I almost faltered in my resolution when I saw the hardness in
Rose's face. I had never seen her like that before and it was a
shocking experience.
"Rose," I said, "will you come out and walk part of the way
home with me?”
She shook her head, so that the red curls which were her most
distinctive beauty shone with little flickering lights under the
hall lamp where they were standing. "It's late," she said, "and
I've got to get my sleep. Remember I have a job."
I turned to her father and deliberately, although hating it,
made an appeal to his sympathy. "You see," I said to him, "my
daughter is ill in the hospital, and I am lonely. If Rose
could walk just a little way with me, it would help.”
·
•
"Of course," Mr. Coplan said. "Go on, Rose, go on," and he
pushed her toward me. "Go and help your friend.”
She came, but most reluctantly, and what persuaded her to
do so I do not know. Perhaps it was the news of Arnie's illness
which I could see came as a surprise to her and she was curious;
but also Rose, as well as her father, possessed a warm human
sympathy that it was difficult for her to deny.
It was this sympathy upon which I was depending; but in spite
of all the questions that were churning in me, when we were on
the sidewalk and going slowly east toward my home, I could find
nothing to say. Rose, as well, and this was unusual with her, was
also silent. An atmosphere of caution seemed to envelop us both.
A panic came in me, for I knew that at any moment she could
say "good-night" and leave me. And I understood, with the
knowledge of that new hardness in her face, that she would be
[61]capable of doing exactly what I, in my anxiety, dreaded. And
then I thought of something I might say that could possibly lead
on to other and more important things.
"I was all prepared for the cell meeting tonight,” I said casu-
ally, “but no one came. I was surprised and disappointed."
“Oh," Rose said, “"didn't you know? We've gone further under-
ground, and all the cells have been broken up and divided,
and. . . ." suddenly she checked herself and then with surpris-
ing awkwardness, for she never lacked assurance, she asked, “Are
you still a member?"
"I have my red card," I said, astonished at her question.
"But we don't use cards any more," she told me with pride in
her voice that she knew something that I did not.
"Certainly I am still a member," I said indignantly. "Why do
you ask such a question?"
Again the atmosphere of caution enveloped us. “Have you
talked to Arnie?" she asked.
"No," I admitted. "I have not talked to Arnie. She is too ill for
me to ask such questions."
"What is wrong with her?" Rose asked curiously, and I felt just
then, as I saw a small, mean expression of triumph come on her
face, something I had sensed before, but would not acknowledge,
that from the very beginning Rose had felt a foolish sort of jeal-
ousy of Arnie, as she would perhaps have felt for anyone (be-
cause she was accustomed to be first with her family), who came
before her in the love which she demanded from all of those to
whom she gave her affection.
And so when she asked "What is wrong with her?” in a curi-
ously flat voice, I said impatiently "Wrong? Nothing is wrong
with her. She is ill."
"But how is she ill?" Rose persisted.
At any other time I would have shared this terrible thing with
her. Formerly I would not have hesitated. Formerly I would have
been certain of her sympathy and loyalty. But now there was the
fact, just acknowledged by me, of her lack of sympathy for my
child, her jealousy of Arnie, her possessiveness toward me and my
affections. And then there was the frightening atmosphere of
caution that had come between us.
[62]Without answering her question about Arnie I said, "Rose,
where is Ben? I came back from the South on Friday, and I have
not heard from him, nor do I see any sign of him about the
house, and he has left the magazine. Where is he?"
I was surprised that she seemed vastly relieved at this question.
Her voice was quite carefree and gay as she answered: "You know
as much about that as I do." Then she added uneasily, “I must
go back. Pop will be waiting up for me."
I halted under a street light and looked at her, and her gaze,
as she met my eyes with her own, was open and frank. “Rose,"
I said earnestly, "when was the last cell meeting held at my
home?"
"Don't call it a cell," Rose admonished. She was always "up"
on new words to be used and every new slogan, and in spite of
her lack of respect for bourgeois convention, within the Party she
insisted that everyone else, as well as herself, be rigidly correct
and conventional about these.
"Whatever you call it,” I said impatiently. "When was the last
meeting there?"
"Oh, about two weeks ago," she spoke carelessly, as if there
was no importance in what she was saying. "That was the meet-
ing when we received the new directives."
I felt suddenly that we were closer than we had been at any
time during the evening, and laid my hand on her arm. "And
nothing about Arnie?" I asked. "Was there nothing about
Arnie?"
"That was later," she said impulsively, and then, as if she re-
gretted what she had said, she drew away from my hand and
stood apart and I saw the hardness come into her face again.
"What was it that happened later?" I asked quickly, trying to
hold her in that moment and in that mood when she was about
to tell me the thing I wanted to know.
But now she was entirely withdrawn, not only physically, hav-
ing rejected my touch on her arm, but also in her mind. I could
feel the withdrawal, and the caution. She took several slow steps
away from me. I knew that she was preparing for flight, and said
very loudly, so that she must stop and listen, "Rose, where do we
meet now? Where did you go tonight?"
[63]"Ask Arnie," she said with a spiteful, mischievous glance at
me. "Ask Arnie." And then she fled across the street and I heard
her light quick steps going on toward her home. But I could not
ask Arnie.
[64]Chapter X
When Arnie and I took the flat on the Lower East Side, inadvert-
ently we set a fashion among the comrades. One of those who
moved to the tenement in front of us was Margot Ward, though
that was not her real name. She, her husband, Diego, and their
five-year-old daughter lived in the flat on the first floor of the ten-
ement just opposite us. She was a college graduate, daughter of a
New England minister, who had cut herself off entirely from her
family, and at the time was on the staff of our daily newspaper.
I always felt that Margot "did not have her feet on the ground”
as my father would say, but perhaps because her background was
important, she seemed to have the confidence of those higher up.
Tall and angular, she possessed a certain elegance, and when she
went out she looked as if she had stepped from a Madison Ave-
nue shop, though the few clothes she had came from Fourteenth
Street. At home she was generally sloppy in her dress and in her
housekeeping. But she was often away.
Her husband worked at night, so he was able to stay with the
child in the afternoons and early evenings when Margot was at
the newspaper. She was a source of gossip and sometimes indigna-
tion to people in the tenement; for one of the reasons, she said,
that she had left bourgeois life was that she had found Americans
prudish and puritanical; that so much evil was underneath the
surface of bourgeois rigid propriety she was determined to be
perfectly open and honest in whatever she did.
I believe one of the crises that came about was not her fault,
for she said the child was perfectly well when she had left it alone
that evening. At the same time she was always vehemently assert-
[65]ing that the individual life did not count, set against Party
needs, and in this individual life she included her child and her
home. Theoretically of course she was right, but as it worked out
practically, in a neighborhood of people, and with a small child,
the theory seemed to have a flaw in it.
The first I knew of anything wrong that evening was when I
heard little May (Margot had named her child for May Day, one
of the great annual holidays of our year), crying pitifully. I
thought the father was at home, or that Margot had not gone
out, and paid little attention, at first, to the child's crying. But
when it continued and increased in volume, sounding so pitiful,
so miserable, I could bear it no longer and crossed the court-
yard. The door was partly open and I went in and found Deedie
and another neighbor bending over the child's bed.
Deedie was exclaiming angrily. "It's a shame!" she said.
"Night after night she leaves the kid alone. What kind of mother
does she think she is?"
I went to the crib and felt the child's head. It was hot and
dry. I was certain she had fever, and added to this, she was naked
and her bed was wet.
"I'm going to call Child Welfare," Deedie said determinedly.
"No, Deedie," I said. "Wait.”
In a moment I was back and lifting the child (who was a dear
little thing with her father's large brown eyes and curly hair). I
sat in a chair with her in my arms and soothed her until she had
quieted enough for me to take her temperature. It was 104 de-
grees.
I looked about the room which was large but in a terrible state
of confusion, and felt I could not remain there, much less try to
do something for the child in that place. Deedie and the other
neighbor helped me take the child over to my flat. Before leaving
I wrote a note for Margot saying that May was with me, and
looked in the drawers of a dresser and chiffonier for some night-
clothes for the child. I found none and scarcely expected to do
so. When we had settled little May on Arnie's bed and I had
given her a sponge bath, I cut down an old cotton nightgown of
Arnie's and slipped it on her. Deedie and the other neighbor left
me sitting beside the bed, the child now quiet and sleeping, and
[66]clear across the courtyard I could hear Deedie's voice, threaten-
ing to call the Welfare-although she did not at that time.
Sitting there I became more and more indignant at Margot,
not only because of the child, but because of the harm she was
doing the Movement by her behavior. Also she had majored in
psychology at college and her talk was always filled with terms
that came from those studies, and she was especially intrigued
by Pavlov's dog. Impatiently I demanded of her, in my thoughts,
why Pavlov's dog was more important to her than her child. But
when she came in (smelling of beer, for most of the comrades
would stop after a meeting to discuss it over a beer or two), and
took the child in her arms and lovingly thanked me for taking
charge, there was nothing I could find to say.
It seemed to me Margot herself, in her former life, must have
been starved for love, for along with psychiatry she emphasized
love, and I suppose one of the visions that had attracted her to
the Movement was one in which she saw that all people, having
education and justice and the best environment, with no bar-
riers of race or religion or class, would be able to love each other.
To me her attitude sometimes took on the form of sentimental-
ity, especially when she explained her predilection for sleeping
with other men than her husband as an expression of that love
which to her was "above all."
And what happened a month or so later was, I suppose, a natu-
ral consequence of her beliefs, although it seemed rather unnatu-
ral, also. For included in her rebellions against the society in
which she had been reared was one against inhibitions concern-
ing sex. Often she and Diego could be seen through their cur-
tainless windows, as we left our house at night for a meeting, go-
ing about naked in their flat. Both of them being thin, almost
scrawny, there was nothing lovely in the sight; and I said to
Arnie they reminded me of a sketch by Matisse called "The Danc-
ers."
That year when May was six years of age Margot insisted that
her husband bathe at the same time and in the same tub with
the child, so that the little girl, from the very beginning of her
conscious life, might become accustomed to sex. Margot was torn
with anguish and surprise when, the following year, the neigh-
[67]bors called in the police and swore that Diego (who I had never
thought had much character, but was dominated by his wife), had
been having intimate relations with his child. Margot insisted
that it was because she was a Party member, and did not hide
it, that the neighbors had made the accusation. And perhaps it
was. After a time during which she separated from Diego and he
was expelled from the Party-for everyone blamed him and was
sympathetic with Margot-she got her child back from the Wel-
fare and went to live in Greenwich Village on Perry Street.
At about the same time Myra Steward (her real name was
Miriam Cohen) left the tenement in front after a stay of a few
years.
Sometimes it happened that, like Margot, members of the
Party had one particular concern which had brought them into
the Movement; and without studying the basic reasons for their
choice so that horizons were widened each day and the intellect
stimulated and centered on the underlying purpose, but with lit-
tle reason and much emotion, they entered the Movement; and
it must be admitted, some of them made fairly steady and in-
tensely loyal members.
Just as Margot's particular slant was love, Myra's was inter-
racial problems. At that time in two apartments in the tenement
in front of us there were twelve young Chinese men living coop-
eratively, so that they could save money which they were putting
into a bank for the purpose of paying their way to China to help
with the Cause there. All were members of the Party and while
they lived there were part of our cell. They had a mimeograph
machine and their special Party duty was to mimeograph and
distribute, under the name "Committee for a Free China," infor-
mation about Chiang Kai-shek and the Kuomintang govern-
ment that would make it impossible for the people of this coun-
try to have any confidence in them.
Myra had talked to us, and to me, personally, about her convic-
tion that interracial marriage would solve the race problem. She
was a little thing, not particularly attractive, and yet with the
engaging awkwardness of a boy who has just arrived at a party
and has decided that he must turn cartwheels or stand on his
head in order to make others-and himself-feel at home. Such
[68]a boy is not a show-off in the usual sense of that word, and
neither was Myra. And like the boy who stands on his head or
turns cartwheels, she embarrassed us rather than made us feel
at home when she would come into the apartment and immedi-
ately begin to make a speech on some question that interested
her.
So when she announced in a loud voice to Arnie and myself,
giving her reasons, that she felt she must marry one of the Chi-
nese comrades, Arnie said to me later that if all members of the
Cooperative stood together as one they might resist her, but not
otherwise.
Myra had selected one of them who was called Lee, although
he had a longer and more formal name, as her future bride-
groom, but she courted all of them and was up in the Coopera-
tive whenever she could get away from her job, which was with
one of the unions.
We did not often see the young Chinese, but once a week at the
cell meeting we had an opportunity to watch the progress of the
"romance." It was always Myra who went over to sit beside Lee,
not vice versa. Myra, always talkative, kept up her side of the
conversation easily; but Lee would sit in what might be called a
lugubriously courteous silence. And the rest of the Cooperative
took the romance very seriously. Once Chang, who by character
and talent was the leader of the group, although by conviction
they had no designated leader, came over very early one Monday
evening. I gave him coffee, which he had learned to like, and
some cakes, and with a suddenness that was quite unlike his usual
reticence, he plunged deep into their problem. He said that Myra
wished to marry Lee and at first they had all opposed it, for they
did not believe in interracial marriage. But Myra, who was very
convincing in an argument, had persuaded them that they were
not being true revolutionists if they did not believe as she did,
and that it was their Marxian duty to encourage Lee to take that
step. And one evening, Chang said, with awe in his voice, Myra
had wept and accused them of being cruel and unfeeling in their
treatment of her.
Chang was much distressed and wanted my opinion about the
whole matter. I said I felt it was a situation that involved only
[69]Myra and Lee and it might be best to let them work it out them-
selves. But he could not see it in that light. He felt very strongly
that it was a problem for the whole Cooperative.
Later he told me they had held a formal meeting and had said
to each other and to Lee that even an American Imperialist
would not be so cruel as they had been to Myra. So they made a
decision at that meeting that Lee must marry Myra. They did it
democratically, he said, by vote on small pieces of paper. There
was only one negative; and from that time on Lee and Myra were
looked upon as an engaged couple.
And then, some weeks afterward, Lee disappeared and none
of the members of the Cooperative were able to tell Myra where
he had gone. Soon afterwards the whole group moved away, and
we never saw them again. It was rumored that they had at last
saved up enough money and had all gone to China.
This happened at about the same time that Margot separated
from her husband; and she and Myra took a small basement
apartment in Greenwich Village. But they continued to be mem-
bers of our cell.
During the war years many things happened to them both, so
that, sometimes for several weeks at a time, they were absent
from cell meetings. Myra, who was determined to go to China,
got as far as San Francisco, and then came back-disappointed.
Margot was sent to Washington for a time (but I believe that
was after the war), as a representative of our newspaper, and was
often present at the President's sessions with the Press where she
was able to insert questions that made good propaganda, and
especially on the race question (for at that time one of our goals
was to plant a deep sense of guilt about that question in the
minds of the bourgeoisie, so that the whole country would be on
the defensive); and she did, in spite of what I looked upon as
faults, an excellent job while she was there. In every contest or
argument with others we were taught that if we could put the
other person on the defensive it would give us the advantage that
attack always has in any war. For we were constantly being re-
minded at cell meetings, even during the war years when out-
wardly we were cooperating with the current war effort, that at
every moment of our lives we were fighting that greater and
[70]more important class war which would not cease until we had
accomplished our aim in the world.
I had thought that Margot should not have been sent to
Washington on such an important mission. But Arnie reminded
me that it was only through experience people could learn to be-
come responsible professional revolutionists, such as Lenin had
predicted that the Movement, to be successful, must have. And
if Margot had made some mistakes, Arnie said, even one of our
most mature leaders had done so when he was younger. He had
been sent to China with several thousand dollars in thousand-
dollar-bills for the comrades in China. While on his way, and
stopping over in Manila, he had run out of small money and
with his false passport and other documents had felt that he was
safe; and so he had gone to a bank to have one of the thousand-
dollar bills changed. It was an almost unforgivable mistake. He
had neglected to throw off any possible-and probable—surveil-
lance, and because he was seen cashing the thousand-dollar bill,
all through his stay in the Philippines and in China he involved
many comrades, so that a number of centers had been broken up.
Arnie was told this in confidence by a member of the CEC and
she told it to me.
These were the two, Margot and Myra, who, on Tuesday eve-
ning after I had made the abortive attempt to get some informa-
tion from Rose, I wished to see; not because they had been very
close to me, but because I knew they admired Arnie excessively,
or had appeared to do so. Also, I told myself as I took the bus at
Fourteenth Street, both of them were people who liked to talk,
and even in ordinary conversation something might emerge that
would clarify the situation.
There was a bell on the door of their basement apartment. I
had climbed down a short flight of steps to reach it, and as
I pressed the bell I looked through the soiled panes of glass on
each side of the door, but could see no light inside.
Finally, to my great relief, I heard someone coming through
the hallway. A light was turned on. Still with my face to the pane
of glass, I recognized Myra; who saw me at the same time. Then
a curious thing happened. I know that Myra saw and recognized
me, even through the soiled pane of glass, for my face was pressed
[71]against it and I rapped on the glass. I saw recognition come on
her face. And yet suddenly she turned; the light was switched off,
and all was darkness again. I rang the bell several times, hoping
to bring her back. But she never came.
Later I learned from Bertha that while I was in the South
Margot had been transferred to Chicago, and that Myra had at
last succeeded in getting her wish. She had married a Chinese
and she and her husband, at the very time when I visited the
apartment on Perry Street, were even then preparing to go to
Hongkong and from Hongkong into China.
[72]Chapter XI
It has been said that when a person is deeply concerned about
one subject or one purpose others are involuntarily drawn into
the atmosphere and activities of that concern, so that coincidences
happen which otherwise would not. Whether this is a basic law
or not I do not know. But the fact is that I did meet Bertha Ma-
son (a member of the cell about whom I had been thinking in
connection with Arnie), by accident one day in a cafeteria on
Fortieth Street. But before that came about there was an encoun-
ter with Robert Todd; only this was the result of my own en-
gineering.
I had first met Bob one morning several years before, not long
after the war was over, when I had risen early for some reason
and he was there at the door leaving my milk. He was a fine look-
ing young man, not handsome, but sturdy and reddish blond and
freckled. Before the war (during which he had fought in the
Pacific), he had worked near his birthplace in Vermont in the
marble quarries. I was drawn to him at once, and as usual when
a possible convert came along I talked to him that morning about
the Movement. He was uneasy and I wondered if I were making
a mistake when he said, "Look, Mrs. Braxton, I'm interested, but
haven't time to talk now. I've got to deliver this milk. But to-
morrow is my free day. I'll stop by then. Okay?" I agreed and
from that time, on his free day, Bob stopped in and I gave him
pamphlets to read and talked to him. I found that two things
interested him particularly: elimination of war, and the explana-
tion of poverty. His people had been farmers in Vermont for
generations and his father had lost the farm. "It's so clear now,"
[73]Bob said. "I could never understand why, though my father
worked so hard, he lost the farm. Now I know it wasn't
my father's fault. It was the system under which we live that
caused his failure." This conviction, that his father was not at
fault, seemed to comfort him greatly.
Eventually Bob became a member of our cell and I was very
proud of him. But, about a year and a half before, he had mar-
ried a childhood sweetheart from Vermont; and his wife, ap-
parently, would have nothing to do with us, so I had never met
her.
For this reason, his wife's antagonism, or indifference toward
the Movement (he was always vague about her attitude), I did
not want to go to his home, and therefore the best way I could
get in touch with him was to rise at dawn and meet him as he
brought the milk.
It was still dark when my alarm woke me. After dressing I went
outside to the courtyard to wait, hoping that I had not hit upon
Bob's free day, for I had long ago forgotten which it was. All the
windows of the tenement before me were dark, and there was
a quiet sense of people sleeping. I walked across the courtyard,
treading lightly, and through the hallway to the stoop outside.
Here there was no hint of dawn and the dark canyon of the street
stretched silent and empty. The street lights were far away and
dim, and I seemed to be the only person on earth. I looked up
at the sky, a thing I had not done since leaving the South. It was
possible, after a moment or two, to make out a star that was as
alone up there between the black overhanging cliffs of tenements,
as I was in the shadowy canyon of the street. A deep silence came
on me and for a short time I felt at peace, the kind of peace
I
had experienced only a few times in my life; that is, under and
deeper than silence, and more positive than silence, for what we
usually accept as silence is only an absence of accustomed sound.
Then the moment was broken in pieces as I heard the unmis-
takable noise of the milk truck, the scrape and squeal of brakes
as it stopped and after a time came on. Finally it was possible to
make out the truck and soon it halted before our tenement. Bob,
for I was relieved to see it was Bob, reached behind him, took
[74]out his tray of bottles and swung himself from the wagon to the
ground.
"Bob," I said.
He was standing just below me ready to mount the steps and
he looked up, startled.
"Why, it's Mrs. Braxton," he exclaimed. He switched the tray
to his left hand and ran up the steps to shake hands. "I thought
you were still down there," he said.
Knowing he could not stay long I began abruptly. "Arnie is
ill,” I told him, making my voice low, for no one was yet awake
in the tenement behind us. "I know you have so little time. . .
But before I could go on he interrupted me. "Did you know,
Mrs. Braxton," he asked in haste, also whispering, yet the whisper
managed to convey to me, standing beside him on the stoop, a
very excellence of joy. "Did you know we have a child?"
I said I did not, and asked him when the baby had been born.
Two months before, he said. "It's a wonderful thing, Mrs. Brax-
ton," he said, speaking low, "a wonderful thing." He was not
given to enthusiasm, and I knew that he could not have said
those words to me, in that Vermont voice with the twang in it,
that was usually so reticent about expressing emotion, except that
we were alone, in the deserted street, and that it was dawn at the
beginning of a new day.
But there was my child. I hastily congratulated him and then
asked him hurriedly if he knew what had happened to Arnie and
Ben. I found my voice unsteady as I begged him to tell me what
he knew about them and about the cell.
He appeared to be puzzled and at the same time embarrassed.
"But, Mrs. Braxton, didn't you know?" he asked.
I laid my hand lightly on his arm and asked, “What is it I
should know?"
"I left the Party before Christmas last year," he said.
My hand dropped heavily to my side. I was stunned into si-
lence.
"Didn't Arnie write you?" he asked, and it was almost as if,
very awkwardly, he was trying to comfort me.
I could not tell him that Arnie made it a rule, both of us made
[75]it a rule, never to mention Party matters in letters, because, as
Arnie said, some day we might mention some one thing that
should not be mentioned at all. But she might have written me
this news in a roundabout way and I was surprised that she had
not.
"But why, Bob," I asked. "Why did you leave? What made you
do it?" I could not keep the heavy disappointment, the sense of
loss, from my voice.
Slowly he shook his head. "I don't know, Mrs. Braxton. Even
before you left I was thinking about it, but put it off because I
think a lot of you and didn't want to disappoint you. But when
you went away-then I could do it."
"But I was coming back. You knew that. I am back. I am here."
"Yes'm," he said. “But it's too late now. We're moving to Ver-
mont. . . . I'd better go," he said uneasily, glancing about him
at the light that had come into the dark street while we talked.
We looked at each other. I know that my disappointment in him
must have showed in my face, and in his there was regret, even
sorrow. "Well," I said, “good-by then,” and am glad that I held
out my hand and was the first to do so. He gave my hand a great
squeeze with his own and then, before he disappeared swiftly
into the house, with real sincerity in his voice, he said, "God bless
you."
I remained on the stoop, astounded and hurt. After all the
talks we had had together, the logic and reason I had presented
to him, what he had said was as grievous to me as Rose's attitude
had been; for if Rose had been like a daughter to me, he had
been like a son.
Presently I moved and went inside. As I passed through the
hallway I heard his steps above me, and the bottles clinking
against each other; and at my door I lifted the bottle of milk he
had left there for me, and walked slowly into the house.
Perhaps as a result of my early rising and the disappointment
about Bob, Arnie seemed worse to me that day, even more rigid
and withheld, if that could be possible. As I told the doctor, with
whom I had a talk, it was not her physical condition that had
deteriorated, but a feeling I had that she was even farther away
mentally. He said it was natural for her illness to take that course.
[76]"Whether voluntarily or involuntarily," he said, “she has with-
drawn from life into a world of her own. Unless we can arrest
that withdrawal, her condition will become progressively worse."
His words depressed me. At the same time there was a grain of
hope in them, if, as he implied, her withdrawal from the world
might be arrested. And I resolved to continue to do everything
in my power to discover the reason for the withdrawal.
After my interview with Doctor Kaplan I asked to see Miss
Andrique, who was not on duty that morning. She came to the
reception room and I asked her if she could tell me the name and
address of the patrolman who, that Thursday morning at three
o'clock, had found Arnie. She said she did not know, but perhaps
she might be able to find out from the records. I waited for some
time and she returned with a slip of paper in her hand with the
name "Patrolman McGuire" written on it, and his address. I
went out at once and telephoned him and made an appointment
to see him at his home that same morning.
He lived on the West Side in midtown Manhattan, far over
near Tenth Avenue. Probably because I was tired as a result of
my early rising I took the wrong subway and suddenly came to
myself at 149th Street on the East Side; so that I had to return to
Grand Central and shuttle to Times Square where, at last, I
found the right train.
The McGuires lived in a brownstone house. They were on the
first floor (above the basement), and it was a pleasure to go into
the old high-ceilinged hallway and living room with wide win-
dow sills showing the thickness of the walls, for they reminded
me of Mars Hill, the home in which I had lived my early life,
the home my great-grandfather had built. I said this to Mr.
McGuire after he met me at the door and we were walking to-
gether into the living room. He was a big man, with dark blue
eyes and black hair and a very Irish upper lip; and I smiled when
we went into the living room and found two little girls playing
there and I saw that they were exact replicas of the father-as
well as of each other-with the same gentian blue eyes and black
hair and the same Irish upper lip.
He introduced them to me in a very serious and formal manner
and we spoke of twins. I said that my sister and I were twins, but
[77]not identical, as his were, for my twin was dark and I fair-haired
and with blue eyes. I questioned him about their age (he said
they were five years old), whether they were similar in personality
and character (he said indeed they were not), and told him some-
thing of Julie and myself when we were children.
The twins were making a disturbance over some toys, and he
took them out to the kitchen to their mother. Returning, he in-
vited me to sit down, and when we had both found chairs he
asked rather stiffly and formally what it was I wished to find out.
I said I was not sure, since I did not know anything. “I don't even
know," I said, "where it was you found my daughter."
That, he said, he could easily tell me, but he knew very little
more. He had called a cruise car and the officers had taken her to
Bellevue.
Here I interrupted to say the doctors had found that Arnie
was not intoxicated as they had first thought. He was surprised
to find this was so, and I was glad I had established this fact. I
then went on to explain her condition and to tell him what the
doctor had implied, that it was possible to "arrest" the progress
of her illness and that I was determined to discover the reason
for it and do everything in my power to bring her back from that
world in which she was, for the present, lost.
Naturally I did not speak of our affiliations to him, any more
than I had to the doctors. Those secrets were not mine to expose.
In spite of what Rose had said, “Are you still a member?”, a ques-
tion that made me indignant each time I thought of it, and in
spite of the fact that I had not been notified, and Rose had con-
sciously refrained from telling me, where the cell was now meet-
ing, I knew in my heart that I was as devoted to our Cause, as
firmly loyal as any of them could be. So, especially because of the
work that Arnie had been doing, and might still do again, I
could not say anything to the doctors about it, nor could I men-
tion it to the officer to whom I was talking.
"You found her at three in the morning?" I asked.
He considered. "Well, it was some time before that," he said.
"Then I called the cruise car."
"Did she say anything to you?" I asked, "while you waited for
it?"
[78]He shook his head. "Not a thing."
I then asked him where he had found her, for I wished to visu-
alize as clearly as possible every step of the way my child had
gone. Between the time when she sat at the desk beginning the
letters to me and the moment when she was found by Patrolman
McGuire there was as little, so far, to enlighten me, as there was
between the time I had received her last cheerful, normal letter
and the evening when she had gone to Mr. Friedman and asked
for the sleeping pills.
"She was just lying there," Mr. McGuire said, “like as if she
was frozen, hunched up, I mean, like a person gets in bed when
it has come on cold in the night, and they don't know yet what it
is making them uncomfortable. You know what I mean?"
I said that I did.
Mr. McGuire passed his large hand over his face as if by doing
so he could bring back his memory of that early morning. "The
steps were shallow," he went on, "and she just lay there on them,
like I said."
"She was lying on some steps?" I asked.
"Yes'm, steps of some church." He seemed surprised that I had
not known this, though he had not told me. I asked if he remem-
bered which church it was, and he said he did not, but would
try to check as he went on his rounds; and I had to be satisfied
with his promise to let Miss Andrique at Bellevue know.
I had done all I could there, and though I knew it might
not be important for me to learn the name of the church, still
from an accumulation of small bits of knowledge some fact
might emerge that would lighten the darkness in which I lived.
[79]Chapter XII
And the darkness seemed to be lightening a little when I crossed
by shuttle from Times Square to Grand Central; and when, going
into the street to find a cafeteria where an inexpensive lunch
could be bought, I saw Bertha Mason seated at a table in the
place on Fortieth Street.
With the experience with other members of the cell during the
past two weeks in mind, and especially the experience with
Myra, who had refused to open her door to me, I thought it best
to make certain that Bertha would not leave before I had spoken
to her, so I went immeditely to her table. It was with surprise and
great relief I saw her smiling at me even before I got there. She
was unaffectedly glad to see me, and we arranged that I would
bring my tray and join her at lunch.
When I reached her a woman had sat down there to eat, and
while that person was present I was unable to ask Bertha any of
the questions that were burning to be asked of this one member
of the cell who seemed to be receptive.
Bertha was secretary to an editor of a peace society, and had
been able to do much to influence the policy there, especially
when the Party line had been pacifistic. The members of the
peace society believed in living their ideals here and now and
had, in their offices, a variety of all races and creeds and political
affiliations. Therefore it was natural that the editor should have
as his secretary a Negro girl. And Bertha had not been taken on
merely because of her race, as had happened in some places, but
because she was an excellent secretary and had a delightful per-
[80]sonality. Up to the time when Ben had turned to Arnie she had
been one of the brightest members of our cell, full of fun and
joking with all of us. If sometimes her wit held a spice of malice
it was always humorous enough to make even the person, at
whom the malice was aimed, laugh with her. For she was never
spiteful. She had met Ben at the office of the peace society when
he had just come down from Harvard, and after they had become
fast friends (and I believe lovers) she brought him to our home
one evening with Endahl. I was happy for her that she had found
an intelligent and highly educated man, who was also extremely
good looking, and cordially invited him to return, not dreaming
that this invitation, so lightly given, would bring suffering to her.
At that time Arnie had just freed herself from a two-year in-
volvement that had become irksome and to my great joy was, at
least temporarily, making our apartment her home. Ben and
Endahl came often and finally came Arnie's decision to join Ben
in his apartment in the Village.
Among the various and confused speculations about what had
happened to Ben, one had struck me as possible: that he had
gone back to Bertha. And when the woman who had been sitting
at the table had gone I turned immediately to her; but as I
looked into her face and saw the expression of faint malice on it,
and the look of satiric amusement in her eyes, as I had seen it
when she was about to make use of her sharp wit on someone, I
could not ask her the question "Where is Ben?” that had been on
the tip of my tongue. In some foolish way I felt that I would be
betraying Arnie if I did so. And to forestall any biting thing that
Bertha was about to say, that might alienate us from each other,
I told her that Arnie was ill. "I know," I said, "there is no rea-
son why you should be interested in what happens to Arnie;
but
you do know how much I love her, and I have always thought
you and I were friends."
As I said this her whole expression changed. She answered in a
troubled voice, not at all like the bright malice I had been
expecting. "No," she said, "I don't care, very much, except as it
affects you, about . . about your daughter. And Ben. . . ." She
halted and did not seem to wish to go on.
:
[81]"What of Ben?" I asked.
"I haven't cared what happened to him—not for a long time,"
she said. But with what effort she said it.
"Yes, yes, I know," I stammered unhappily. "Arnie. . . ."
"Oh," she said, "it wasn't Arnie. It was Ben himself who dis-
appointed me."
"When was this?" I asked.
"Do you remember about a year ago, Ben and Arnie and I
were sent to Boston for a front meeting?"
I did remember.
"It was during that trip to Boston I found out Ben was a snob."
Bertha said.
For the first time I felt a pity for this child. She had always
been so self-sufficient, so, it seemed, able to take care of herself,
even during the time she was losing Ben, I had never thought of
her as needing pity or compassion. But when she said that Ben
was a snob I knew that in some way she had been made by him
to feel inferior. For it had been my experience that only those
who have been made to feel inferior, or have allowed themselves
to be made to feel so, or, for one reason or another, to feel an
envy of another—they are the only ones that use the word snob.
It does not seem to be a word that can be used objectively by any-
one, as a person might say, "she has red hair," or "he has blue
eyes."
I had in my life become acquainted with many "snobs"-with
spiritual snobs in the church, with social snobs, with intellectual
and political snobs within the Movement and on its fringes; with
snobs within unions, who look down on the rank and file, and
those within the rank and file who look down on the ones below
them; with neighbors living in our tenement who "turned up
their noses" at other neighbors; with those who are snobbish
about wealth and property and those who are snobbish about
being poor; with Jews who look down on other Jews and Negroes
who assert their superiority over other Negroes. An accumulated
experience of "snobbery” in all its manifestations had brought
me to the conclusion that it is a trait so common to humanity that
it must be accepted like hunger, as a need that is not hurtful to
others unless taken to the extreme that greed is taken; and that
[82]the greatest snob of all is the person who asserts self-righteously,
“I am not a snob.”
