Certainly I could not say this to Bertha, but listened to her as
she told me about her experience with Ben on the train to New
Haven. "Before we got on at Grand Central," the child went on,
"I knew he was trying to ignore me. He bought tickets for himself
and Arnie and left me to buy my own." In the same grieved voice
she continued the story of their journey.
It seemed that they had gone into the smoking compartment of
one of the cars on the New Haven and Hartford Railroad, where
there are comfortable seats, facing each other for those who wish
to smoke, and which are separated from the regular car seats by
partitions of glass. Ben had hurried Arnie into one of these com-
partments, carrying her overnight bag and his own, and they had
left Bertha behind. She had arrived in the car, into which she
had seen them go, after they were seated. Already feeling that
Ben was trying to ignore her, Bertha had seated herself opposite
them. And then, her natural pride asserting itself, she grew more
and more angry. They were to stop over at New Haven for a
meeting and go on to Boston next day.
"And Arnie paid no attention to you?" I asked Bertha.
"No, Mrs. Braxton, she didn't."
"But I am ashamed of Arnie,” I said. "Never before, so far as
I know, has she failed in courtesy to anyone."
The conductor came through the car, Bertha said, and Ben
had given up his own and Arnie's tickets, continuing to ignore
Bertha. It was after that, when Ben so definitely left her out, that
Bertha determined she would not be ignored. She took her bag
and crossed the aisle and sat beside Ben and smiled at him. Im-
mediately, and without acknowledging her presence beside him,
Ben had turned his back upon her. For a time she tried to force
his attention by making an occasional remark, but nothing
came of it, and she began to see herself as others in the car might
see her: a girl who was forcing her attentions on a stranger.
Overcome by shame as she saw the eyes of people in the car
turned curiously upon her she returned to her former place
across the aisle.
By the time she had finished her story Bertha was crying si-
[83]lently, and angrily wiping away the soft, slow tears with her paper
napkin. My heart ached for her, but at the same time I under-
stood what an uncomfortable position Ben had been forced into,
between two women, both of whom cared for him; and I realized
too that Bertha's hurt feelings might easily have caused her,
when she was sitting beside him, to aim her wit with deadly ac-
curacy at Ben, and that whatever she said might have alienated
him. It was Arnie of whom I felt ashamed, although there was
something to be said for her own embarrassment in that situa-
tion. I could only wonder at the stupidity or the ignorance of
the Party functionary who had ordered those three to make the
trip to Boston together.
Nevertheless I was fond of Bertha, and though, because of
Arnie, I was trying to be objective about Ben I felt a resentment
toward him because of his treatment of this child. "That was very
ugly of Ben," I said heatedly, “and when I see him I shall tell
him so."
She looked at me in surprise, her lashes still wet and clinging
together. "How can you tell him?" she asked.
There was so much meaning in that question that all my anx-
iety about Ben's mysterious absence returned, only stronger
than it had been before. Bertha must have seen the confusion
and anxiety on my face and understood that I was ignorant of
what had happened to Ben. "He is in Europe,” she said, “on his
way to the Workers' Fatherland. They will subsidize him while
he finishes his book. It is a great honor. But first he is to visit
Endahl. They sailed together."
"But what of Arnie?" I asked in anguish. "What of Arnie?"
If there was a triumphant gleam in Bertha's eyes, it has not
been possible for me to blame her. "I don't know," she said.
"Who does know!" I exclaimed hopelessly, but Bertha ac-
cepted the exclamation as a question.
"You could ask Masha," she suggested.
"Why Masha?" I asked uneasily. For a second the whole un-
mistakable sequence of events appeared before me, like the ter-
rain seen from the window of an airplane; but I swept it from
my mind, not willing to face it.
[84]"Well," Bertha said reluctantly, "Masha has been made
Assistant to Agitprop."
"But that is impossible!" I exclaimed. For Arnie had been se-
cure in her high position. If there was security anywhere it had
been hers.
"If this has happened. . . ." I said trembling.
"It has happened, Mrs. Braxton," Bertha said sorrowfully-
and I knew she was sorrowful for me and not for Arnie. "Masha
told us."
I must have wavered as we rose from the table, for Bertha gave
me a quick, anxious look and then took my arm and held it as we
threaded our way to the door. Outside, after she had signaled
a taxi for me, and while we waited for it to turn and come even
with us, I asked Bertha to visit me soon, and she promised she
would, but I thought she would not. She stood on the sidewalk
and waved to me as the taxi drew away, and I lifted my hand in
farewell. Only then I remembered I had not asked her about
the next meeting of the cell.
As the taxi carried me along the familiar streets I did not see
them at all. I was looking inward, trying to gain the resolution
I would need. For it was clear to me that it would be necessary
to find Masha, who would be a victorious Masha, before whom I
must humble myself (and in doing so humble my child) —
knowing that only in this way I might persuade Masha to tell me
what it was that had come between Arnie and the Movement to
which she had devoted, without any thought for herself, twelve
years of her young life; and which now, whatever it was that had
happened, had sentenced her, perhaps for the rest of that life, to
mental darkness and pain.
[85]Chapter XIII
Even in the beginning, when I first knew Masha (who became
a member of our cell in 1939), I was not drawn to her in affection
as I was to so many others. Then, when her antagonism toward
Arnie developed, which she tried to conceal, but was unable to,
my lack of affection became active dislike. But there was some-
thing else, a very unreasonable attitude on my part, that made
me distrust her from the first: that she reminded me of Greta
Moseley. I remember saying this to Arnie, and Arnie exclaiming,
"Oh no, Mama! Greta was almost beautiful."
"I don't mean in appearance," I said. "It is something I feel;
a love of power that could become cruel. I felt it in Greta, and I
feel it in Masha." "But Greta's aim in life was to help other peo-
ple-the underdog," Arnie protested. "Nevertheless," I told her,
"when Greta first came to Bethel, she said to me, 'I could not live
if I did not feel myself superior to everyone else.'"
Arnie looked at me reproachfully and I realized that my voice
had become sharp and censorious-and immediately controlled
it.
We said no more about Greta, and even what we had said was
unusual, for we did not often speak of that unhappy time, or of
the past at all. That is what makes it so difficult to do so now
when I must, for the sake of my child, face those memories of
our life in Bethel, even going back to Mars Hill-and to Julie.
Mars Hill was not the first home my great-grandfather built
when he came from Virginia to our state. In what was then a
wilderness he built a large log house, and this house (now rented
out as a farm), is still standing; but years ago the logs were
[86]covered over with white clapboard so that it does not appear on
the outside as a log house at all. In this home my mother and
father lived when they were first married until the death of my
grandfather when they moved into the old large sprawling brick
home my great-grandfather had built. Here my father laid out
the lovely gardens with box hedges and all manner of flowering
shrubs: with wisteria that climbed on the trees and in the spring
hung down from the branches, presenting to Julie and myself a
lovely background for our various romances as we grew up. We
loved Mars Hill, but I think Julie more than I; or perhaps she
felt more possessive about it than I did.
I do not know whether it was the presence of our Aunt Julia,
who remained at Mars Hill after our mother's death, or Julie's
devotion to Father and to the house that made her decide, when
we returned for our summer holidays, that she would not go back
to school in the fall, but keep house for Father. I went back for
another year to St. Ann's and after my graduation returned to
find Julie mistress at Mars Hill. It was a satisfactory arrange-
ment and seemed to me quite natural and right. Just as I had
accepted her leadership almost from the time we were born, so I
accepted it in our home, as well as in our relationships with
others.
Though Julie was more popular than I, she had a curious at-
titude toward those young men with whom I had "engagements.”
And until David came, probably without consciously doing so,
she ruined my natural attitude of open friendliness with every
man who, up to that time, wished to marry me. Whether he was
someone who lived in Bethel, or someone I had met at a Uni-
versity dance or on a visit to a friend from St. Ann's, at some time
Julie would say to me in the confident way she had, and which
made me believe her: “He doesn't really care for you, Ann. He
tried to kiss me, too.” Or “He put his arm around me right there
on the stairs, while you were getting your evening wrap. . . ."
She had an unusual way of saying these things, and I believe I
was not as frightened of her tantrums as I was of the gentle, al-
most caressing manner she used at such times. Even as far back
as when we were fifteen and were beginning to select friends who
were congenial to our different personalities; and when I had,
[87]with our mother's delighted approval, planned a luncheon for
some of my particular cronies, and for which I painted the place
cards with verses I had composed; Julie said to me in that soft,
caressing manner, when the guests had gone after expressing
their ecstatic enjoyment of the luncheon: "Those girls don't
really like you, Ann. They were just pretending."
So now, facing it, I see that all along some powerful force was
at work in my twin which prevented her from wanting me to
have a life separate from her own. Yet when I allowed myself to
give in to her and, having given in, to repeat the process, an ir-
ritation toward me showed itself, as if I were neither important
nor interesting; in fact as if I bored her, which I probably did,
as we are bored by anyone who hasn't a will of her own.
That this process was not repeated when David began showing
me attention was only because at the time when he came to be
the rector of our church in Bethel Julie was completely engrossed
in her first real love, James Guthrie, a young lawyer. Jim had
volunteered with the Red Cross as an ambulance driver before
they had fallen in love, and both of them knew he would be
leaving as soon as his application came through. When it did,
and he was ordered to go for training on a certain date, they de-
cided to be married. It was a lovely wedding with eight brides-
maids for Julie; but the most significant event of that wedding
for me was when I, as Julie's maid of honor, walked up the aisle
toward David Braxton, the young rector, and reaching the foot
of the chancel, looked up and saw that his eyes were on me, their
expression assuring me that he (who heretofore had been almost
wary in his attentions as if he felt he must not make a favorite of
anyone in the congregation), had singled me out. And this feeling
was confirmed at the wedding reception when he suddenly be-
came as ardent as before he had been careful-an attitude that
continued when I was left at home with my father while Julie
was away in the North with her husband during his period of
training.
When Julie returned after Jim had gone overseas I was ready
to announce my engagement to David and to ask Julie to be my
matron of honor; inadvertently inviting her to take over all the
wedding preparations, which she did beautifully and well, ar-
[88]ranging thoughtfully for David's uncle and aunt who came from
Virginia to represent his family at the wedding-for his mother
and father had died even before David left the seminary. I was
especially grateful for the attention paid his relatives for I knew
of David's sincere and deep regret (he was an only child), that
his mother, who had longed for him to be a clergyman as his
father was, had not lived to see him ordained; and that his father,
who had looked forward some day to performing the marriage
ceremony for his son, was lying beside his wife in the cemetery
of that little church in Virginia which we visited after our wed-
ding.
I am convinced even now that our marriage, in the beginning,
was a truly happy one. We were completely absorbed in each
other of course, and yet not so completely that we were unable
to look beyond ourselves and see, as David said, that our love was
God's love made particular in us; and that it must overflow to
others.
It was all the more inexplicable to me when David made a
decision to go overseas. At times I thought the reason he became
a chaplain in the army was because of something Julie said after
her husband (who had joined the Lafayette Escadrille when our
country entered the war in 1917) was killed near Fer-en-Tarde-
nois in France. Julie said then in David's presence that clergy-
men's wives had no need to be worried about their husbands, nor
clergymen about their own lives; and as she said it I saw a look
come on David's face as if he had made a resolution. Perhaps I
only imagined that Julie had influenced him, but resentment
came in my heart on the day my husband left in May, 1918, to go
overseas, for I felt that Julie should not have allowed bitterness
over her husband's death to make her critical of David.
And again I blamed Julie, but secretly, when my husband re-
turned in February, 1919, and I saw that a change had come in
him. It is possible that the change did not come at all from the
war experiences, but that over a period of time I had given in
to him, just as formerly I had given in to Julie; and that just as
I had bored Julie when I allowed her, on the surface, to domi-
nate my life, over a period of time I also became boring to my
husband. From my childhood there had been in me a very rich
[89]inner life of the imagination. Often in the evening during ado-
lescence, after I had made myself ready for bed, I would kneel at
the window looking out on the stars and feel my heart expanding,
reaching toward them; and then as the beauty of the night came
in on me there would be a magnificent feeling of growth, as if
my expanding heart had room not only for my family but for all
the world. So in minor ways all beauty and color affected me,
but I was never able to be articulate about it, except at first to
David. In the beginning he had shared this inner life with me
and it was this sharing that had made me so happy. But after he
returned from Europe he seemed unable to meet me on that basis
again. The change was neither sudden nor obvious. With me
he continued to be tender and loving, and at night, lying in our
bed together, we were as close physically as we had ever been.
But there was not any more the still moments between us, when
we could stop on a walk in the country, looking at a sunset and
without speaking, turn and smile into each other's eyes; and this
was what I missed and could never bring back-that deeper
understanding, needing no words, that indeed, could not be
expressed in words-so that I was left dumb and frustrated, but
happy enough.
In November, 1919, when my father died, David was a great
support and strength to me and to Julie, and it was then that
Julie began really to respect David and to say, "I shall never for-
get how wonderful David was when Father died.”
From that time she became a "pillar of the church," and when
Arnie was born, in December of that year, she was a pillar of
strength to me; because I was very ill. And yet, contrarily, as I
became convalescent I almost resented her taking over, as
Aunt Julia had done at my mother's death; for it seemed to me
that this taking over prevented my husband from feeling any re-
sponsibility, and made him seem, when he came to see me in the
hospital, not the person most concerned, the father of our child,
but rather the rector calling on one of his parishioners.
Along with the church work Julie was active in a number of
organizations in our town, and she inherited my mother's place
as president of the board of the public library, which included
[90]the public library for Negroes that my mother had organized
many years before.
Julie often traveled to the North for conventions or confer-
ences with committees. After 1925 she became deeply interested
in a peace society (the same society that later on, employed
Bertha Mason), and it was at one of their conventions or confer-
ences she met Greta Moseley and invited her down to Bethel for
a visit to Mars Hill. And while Greta was there Julie suggested
to David that he take her on as his secretary.
That was in September, 1933. Arnie was then thirteen and had
just been confirmed. All her young life Arnie had been a joy to
my husband and me. She was not spoiled even with all the at-
tention she received from members of the congregation. (And I
have often thought that much of her even disposition came from
the training she received from Mum Hattie up to her sixth year.)
As she grew older, occasionally a stubborn streak appeared and
she became unmanageable; a condition that did not last long,
but was unhappily real while it lasted. Once, when she was six,
she said to me, when in one of these moods, "But, Mama, I want
to be bad." It was impossible, at such times, not to become im-
patient with her. At the same time it was impossible not to respect
the honesty, the concentration with which she went about being
bad; just as, when she was her more "natural" self, she concen-
trated on other aspects of life, such as her confirmation.
I had never seen her, up to that time, so intent, so absorbed,
as she was during the period of instruction before her confirma-
tion, and she hung about her father with more than her usual
devotion. It must have been because for the first time she saw
him, not as "Papa" or "the rector," but as the person looked up
to and listened to seriously and with respectful attention by her
contemporaries, both boys and girls.
Also her father was a charming person, and though he was not
handsome, in a very masculine way he was one of the most at-
tractive men I have ever known. And when he stood at the door
of the church and spoke to members of the congregation as they
came out from a service, the smile he gave them, like Arnie's, was
brilliantly magnetic; not in a superficial way, but one that was
[91]sincere and came, at that time I am certain, from a loving heart
in which there was no guile.
Dr. Kaplan asked me, one day at the hospital, about Arnie's
early life and her knowledge of sex at that time, and I tried to
tell him. No one, I believe, has ever really solved the problem
of how a parent may speak to a child about sex, and to me (in
spite of what Lenin said, that sex is no more than a glass of water
taken by someone who is thirsty) there still remains a mysteri-
ous element I cannot explain. At least it was so with my husband
and myself. Lying together, or sleeping wrapped in each other's
arms, we touched a mystery; something of the feeling that comes
in earliest spring when, on a walk in the woods, are seen the new
fresh shoots coming out of the ground in the midst of dead leaves
or mold, and there is a sense of the miracle and mystery of re-
newed life. So is love renewed between a man and woman who
in a very true sense cherish each other, as my husband and I did
until the time that Greta Moseley came.
I gave Arnie books to read, and spoke to her, explaining the
facts. But in spite of my determination to be modern and tell her
everything involved, what I said could no more give her any valid
interpretation than if a person had said to me, before my mother
died, “This is the way death is," giving the physical facts of death
before I had experienced the unsuspected and deep emotions
roused in me when I saw my mother whom I loved lying dead in
her coffin.
It was when Greta Moseley had been my husband's secretary
for about a year that Arnie came to me one day and asked,
“Mama, what is a street walker?" and I said, "Why do you want
to know?"
She answered that she had heard some people talking about
Mr. Tate and Mrs. Boone, and one of them had said Mrs. Boone
was no better than a street walker. This time I was not at all pa-
tient with her questions and curtly forbade her to speak of Mr.
Tate and Mrs. Boone again. I could not trust myself to discuss
them with her at all, because at that time they had become a
source of argument and misunderstanding between my husband
and myself.
Mr. Tate was one of the vestrymen of the church. He lived
[92]alone except for an unmarried daughter, Mary, who looked after
his large home and made him comfortable. He was a few years
younger than my father and before my father's death had been
associated with him in a law firm and in the church.
It happened that a very attractive widow, a Mrs. Boone, came
to live in Bethel; and because she attended our church she be-
came acquainted with Mr. Tate. Soon people began to notice
that Mr. Tate's car was parked almost daily, or nightly, outside
Mrs. Boone's home, and people began to talk and to sympathize
with Mary Tate. Mrs. Boone was a woman who was not careful
about the friends she made and she was seen with two other
women in the town who did not have the best kind of reputa-
tion. Not that they were what were called bad women, but that
they did exactly as they pleased without considering the opinions
of others (very much in the same way that Margot Ward had
behaved in our neighborhood among the people who lived in the
tenements in New York). All three, including Mrs. Boone, were
women of ample curves (though well-corseted), so that some wit
gave their little group a name which clung to them, “The Three
Bustqueteers." None of this increased Mary Tate's composure of
mind about her father, though she dutifully entertained Mrs.
Boone at dinner whenever her father wished her to do so―other
members of the community never being invited at the same time.
I did not understand, then, why it was I felt so strongly about
Mr. Tate. He had a perfect right, as my husband insisted, to miti-
gate his loneliness (his wife had died three years before) by hav-
ing a woman friend, and no one without being certain, had any
business to speculate about their relationship. But I insisted
vehemently that Mr. Tate should either resign as a vestryman of
the church or else give up the appearance of evil. Of course there
was another solution—that he marry Mrs. Boone-to which solu-
tion, because of the inheritance involved, his children were un-
alterably opposed; and perhaps, he, himself, had never considered
it. To be fair to his children it must be admitted that, so far as
anyone could see, only lust was involved, stimulated by the bour-
bon that was the elderly man's source of energy and desire.
As the days went on I became more vehement in my condem-
nation of Mr. Tate and my husband more stubborn in his de-
[93]fense. But under it all, my condemnation of Mr. Tate and my
husband's defense of him, there was the other question (as I see
it now), that in protesting about Mr. Tate and the woman who
was supposed to be his mistress, I was actually expressing the in-
security and fear that the presence of Greta Moseley aroused in
me; and my indignation against Mr. Tate was not primarily con-
cerned with him but with the developing relationship between
my husband and his secretary.
Greta Moseley was an attractive young woman, twenty-five
years old, very dark, very boyish, with great assurance. She and
Julie were warm friends, their friendship stemming from the
ideals for peace of that society whose conference Julie had at-
tended, and at which she had first met Greta.
Greta called herself a member of the Society of Friends, but
I often wondered then, and have wondered since, whether she
was in fact a member. She was a very persuasive talker, as I had
plenty of opportunity to observe, for often my husband brought
her over to supper at the rectory.
For months I felt no conscious resentment. But in small ways
I began to feel a great difference in my husband, both in his re-
lationship to me as we were together at night in our room, and
the almost flippant and cynical attitude he began to show toward
the very beliefs upon which the church was founded. At the same
time he began to develop a great concern for his fellow-man—a
concern which he called the “Social Gospel."
He brought over to the house, and left in his study there, a
book Greta had lent him about George Fox, the originator of the
Society of Friends, written by a contemporary. When she was at
supper in our home, especially in the beginning, Greta often
spoke to us about this group and said to us that "Society
of Friends" meant friends of all the world, and that George Fox
had made humanity his first concern. I was determined to dis-
cover if this was so, and going into my husband's study found the
book Greta had lent him; and though it was a long book, I read
it all. After that I was able to point out (and I wonder if, before
lending it to David, Greta had actually read it through) that the
original name had been "Society of the Friends of Truth,” and
that George Fox, while emphasizing the fact that all are children
[94]of God, nevertheless had not put humanity first, but had put God
first; and that he had, when in prison, sung hymns aggressively in
a loud voice, praising God in no uncertain terms.
So the struggle about ideas, as we expressed them and argued
over them at the supper table, became a struggle between Greta
and myself over my husband. And as he became more and more
filled with enthusiasm for her point of view, I became more
deeply concerned over those he had formerly held. Quotations
and convictions which I had not, up to that time, dreamed
were a part of me, sprang to life during our arguments when we
were alone. I pointed out to him that his Lord had said: "This
is the first and great commandment: thou shalt love the Lord thy
God with all thy heart and soul and mind," and that "to love thy
neighbor as thyself" was put second. When I argued that the
command of his Lord was: “Seek ye first the kingdom of God and
all these things shall be added unto you," he answered that he
believed in making heaven on this earth, and this was what our
Lord had meant by the "kingdom of God." I asked him how it
was possible for us to make a heaven on earth when we are all
"miserable offenders." He then astonished me by saying he did
not believe we are sinners. I was the more astonished because he
had preached in the past more than once on the text: "If we say
that we have no sin we deceive ourselves and the truth is not in
us," and I reminded him of this fact, but he ignored it. I then
declared that he had abandoned his belief in God altogether. But
this he denied. "I shall never abandon belief in God," he said.
"But we must be progressive and open to all the new ideas that
are pressing on us. A great social economic, political and religious
revolution is taking place in the world and I mean to be a part of
it. How can we help humanity," he added, “if we insist that our
religion is the only one-that Jesus Christ was God. I love my
fellowman too much to impose such a stricture on his thinking
and belief."
I looked at him in amazement, but by this time he did not seem
aware of me at all. He seemed caught up in some vision of his
own. “There is a feeling of freedom about all this that I can't
explain," he said, "a sense of adventure, of burning bridges, of
kicking over the traces, fascinating and attractive. It is intoxicat-
[95]ing. We are making a new world, reaching out to all people, and
if doing this involves sacrificing some of our dearly held beliefs
the sacrifice is worth it."
I was the more cast down by all of this because at that time
Arnie (who was fifteen) had taken a great liking to Greta; and
since Julie, also, was friendly with her, I felt myself moving over
quicksand, often sinking and reaching out my hands for human
help and finding none. Formerly I had been able to pray to
a Higher Power sincerely, but now that my husband had for some
time given up the custom we had always had of saying our prayers
together (from which I had gained assurance and peace, strength
and comfort), I had allowed myself to relinquish this practice.
And though the situation was forcing itself upon me, as if I were
being sucked down into the quicksand, I refused to acknowledge
it, and still struggling, thinking at any moment I could pull my-
self to safety, I continued to believe that somehow all former re-
lationships and responsibilities, my husband's to the church and
our love for each other and for Arnie, would be restored. It was
a surprise to me, then, when my husband and Greta came one
morning when Arnie was at school and I was busy with house-
work (we had a cook, but no maid, and I always did the upstairs
cleaning), and said the words that changed my life so drastically.
I was not particularly concerned when I heard David come in
below the stairs in the middle of the morning and call out to me.
He was in and out of the house at all hours, coming in from a
call, or to get something from his study in the rectory to take over
to his study and office in the church.
When he called me I went down just as I was in a house dress
and with a kerchief on my head to see what was wanted; but was
surprised and embarrassed to find Greta there also. He asked me
very solemnly to come into his study and I followed them there.
He asked us to be seated, and it was then I felt a tightening in
my throat and my heart began to beat against me so that I won-
dered at the sound it made and that they did not hear it. I put
my hands on the chair in front of me and leaned upon them, for
my knees had become so weak I could scarcely stand. And yet I
could not sit down. An atmosphere about them, with all that had
gone before, made me aware that some hideous knowledge was
[96]waiting for me and that I must stand in order to receive its impact
without faltering too much.
Since I did not sit down, neither did the other two and we
stood there in silence, myself staring at my husband, Greta also
looking at him and he with his eyes cast down. I had forgotten
completely that I had been cleaning the house; that my face and
hands were by no means immaculate, and that my hair was tied
up in a kerchief. My appearance must have contrasted strangely
with the other two who were impeccably dressed; my husband in
his clericals and Greta in the simple but expensive style of dress
she always wore to her work at the church. My huband was eight-
een years older than Greta but that morning the difference in
their ages was not apparent, for he looked almost as young as
when we were married. Although he was of medium height and
weight his appearance varied at times. While in the chancel and
the pulpit he always seemed to take on stature as if he were more
than six feet tall, and in the room that morning he appeared in
the same way. His features, each of which was more familiar to
me than my own; the expressive brown eyes, noble forehead,
abundant straight black hair, only a little touched with gray, his
mouth so like Arnie's (or hers like his) that stated both the power
of the senses and the forcefulness suggested by his determined
chin; all now were brightened as if by reflection of a youthful
light from the girl, short, dark, and visibly sensual, who stood
triumphantly beside him. (But even now I cannot be fair to
Greta. I know that she was lovely, almost beautiful, as Arnie said
she was.)
Then my husband spoke and said that he and Greta loved each
other and that they felt the only right thing, the only honest
thing, was for them to be married.
"No! No!" I cried out. And again. For even if I had been pre-
pared for what he said, nothing could have prevented the sudden
and hideous sense of loss, the despair that came in me when my
husband said, holding the girl's hand in his, "We wish to be mar-
ried." And then, as I saw the proud look on her face and my hus-
band's smile, for the first time in my life, which had scarcely
known even a mild anger before, a consuming overpowering
rage came in me, strange and frightening. I longed to do some-
[97]thing physical to express it, smash the girl's face, or tear her apart.
In the days that followed, again I discovered in myself new
and frightening emotions, with difficulty controlled. I reminded
my husband that a divorce meant he must leave the ministry and
he said he had faced that fact and decided that henceforth he
would give his life to helping humanity and that this was what
God wanted him to do. I said some bitter things at first (I who
had never been bitter). I said he should not bring God into the
affair; that no doubt Mr. Tate had also felt the leading of God.
But all this only alienated my husband and made him more de-
termined in the course he had chosen.
There is no doubt there was suffering for him in the situation
as well as desperation for myself, but I believe the person who
suffered most at that time was our child. I know now that she
had been feeling the tension in the house some time before, and
this was not surprising. At the table, at meals, we three sat, none
of us eating with our accustomed appetites, and with that awful
tense silence among us, or polite words shouting that something
was desperately wrong. It was even a relief when my husband told
me that he had notified his bishop and the vestry that he was
leaving the ministry.
Before he went to New York to wait until the time (according
to the laws of our state) when we might get a divorce, one more
terrible decision had to be made; and if I had thought I had suf-
fered before, the knowledge that we must face our child and
say, "Choose between us," was almost more than I could bear. My
husband and Greta had urged me to take the whole thing lightly
—to be, as Greta said, “civilized." But it was impossible for me
to do so. I felt that the situation could not be taken lightly by
any of us because real love was involved and that any other at-
titude would imply a flippancy, a cynicism about life and honor
and decency which I was not able to feel. I said to my husband,
who wished for an amicable arrangement whereby Arnie would
spend half the year with him and half with me, that I could not
agree to such a plan; that such a division of her allegiance would
only bring her confusion, and misery to us all. Then, he said, I
would be forcing even a more cruel decision upon Arnie. Never-
theless I would not give in, but adhered to what my instinct told
[98]me, that the wounds we were then making in each other and in
our child could be healed only if she chose one of us. And if I was
wrong—if what has happened now is the result of my decision
then I can only know that at the time I was making the wisest
decision that I, as one of her parents who was forcing this sorrow
upon her, and with my limited understanding, could make. Pain-
fully I had come to this decision, for it was my sincere belief that
because of Arnie's evident affection for Greta and her devotion
to her father she would put him first, and that not only was I los-
ing my husband, but would also lose my child.
We called Arnie into the study one evening after supper and
it was in almost every respect like the morning when my hus-
band and Greta had announced to me their intention, except
that now it was our child who, when I had sat down and my
husband had taken his seat behind his desk, stood holding to the
back of a chair. And I knew that she, as I had weeks before, was
standing to receive a blow she knew was coming. I looked at her
and noted again how thin and pale she had become, and how the
painful awareness of something she could not wholly understand
had come into her young face; and that she swayed as if her knees
bent under her weight; and how she looked at me, and then at
her father, and then at me again in silence.
I waited for my husband to speak, but before he could do so,
she said in a loud, clear voice, "You are getting a divorce, aren't
you?"
I believe my husband was as stunned as I by her unexpected
words. Neither of us answered, so astonished were we; and in the
silence I had the feeling that, as she stood there before us, our
child had become our judge. Although I know she did not
mean it in that way, but she was trying in her own manner, and
according to her own lights, to be fair, to be reasonable, and
above all, not to allow herself to be completely submerged by the
“waves and billows" pouring over her in this storm, it was just
as if we two were the accused and she was the judge who must
make a decision about our innocence or our guilt.
She turned to her father then and asked in a quieter voice:
"Why, Papa? Why must you?"
[99]Her father, now finding his voice, explained that he loved
someone else and she said “Greta?" and he said yes.
She said, "And you won't be a clergyman any more?"
"No," he answered. "I won't be a clergyman. I am planning
to devote my life to humanity, something I was not able to do in
the church with all its forms and traditions."
A light came into her eyes and she said sweetly, "Yes, Papa,
I understand that."
A sound—a sudden impulsive movement-made me look at
him, and I saw that he started up from his seat to go to her, a
flush coming in his face and an immense relief suffusing it. Then
he saw that I was looking at him and forced himself back into his
chair, gripping the arms. For we had agreed on one thing,
and that was that in this contest for our child neither of us would
take unfair advantage of the other.
Perhaps he was justified in what he said next. I am not sure it
was fair. He explained to Arnie that Greta had returned to New
York, but that as soon as our divorce was granted they would be
married and Arnie could live with them and help them in their
struggle to make the world better by helping humanity. "Your
mother wishes to remain here in Bethel," he said, and it seemed
to me there was a hint of contempt in his voice as if by wishing
to remain in Bethel I was proving myself in his eyes to be the
same kind of person to whom Julie had once said, “Don't be so
provincial."
"Do you, Mama?" Arnie turned her lovely candid child's eyes
on me, and they seemed to pierce my heart, like a sword.
"For the present, yes," I answered and my voice did not trem-
ble, as I had thought it must, for my whole body was trembling.
Her father went on to say in a voice that, evidently by force
of will, he was making expressionless, that she could choose to
live with him or with me, or divide her time between us. "Your
mother," he said, "thinks you should make a definite choice. . .
He was going on when she interrupted.
""
"Is that so, Mama?" she asked, turning to me. "Do you think I
should choose between you?"
"Yes," I assented, and could not trust my voice for further ex-
planation.
[100]"I believe," her father said, still in that expressionless voice,
"that there is no reason for such a choice, and that you could
spend part of the year with me and part with your mother. Of
course," he added, "all this could be left until the matter reaches
the courts. You are a minor and the judge usually decides such
questions. Though I think, considering your advanced age,” and
he smiled, "the judge would allow you to make the decision your-
self. I do agree with your mother that this is the best way, to talk
it over now when we can, in the privacy of the . . . the. ." he
hesitated.
“Of the family?” Arnie asked coolly, and he nodded.
“Only it isn't a family any more, is it?" she asked.
•
There was a terrible silence. I don't know what my husband
was thinking. He accused me later of using a woman's weapon.
But as I sat there, waiting for my child to choose her father (for
I was so sure that would be the outcome), I thought of the joy
she had been to us—the first time I had held her in my arms, and
the evenings when we had hung so proudly over her crib as she
slept. Now she was fifteen, beginning to be a woman. And as I
thought these things and realized that, at the very moment, I was
saying good-by to my child, certain that I had lost her, tears
poured down my face, and I could not even raise my hand to wipe
them away.
I heard my husband say, “It isn't necessary for you to decide
now, you can wait."
"Only if I know-if I know now—is there any reason for wait-
ing? Do you want me to wait?” she asked.
"I want you to do whatever is in your mind to do," he said.
"It is your decision."
Then I heard her clear young voice say, "Oh, then, I choose
Mama."
I wiped the tears from my cheeks and looked up in astonish-
ment. At first I could not believe what I had heard. It was my
husband's face that had gone suddenly stark naked as if all super-
ficial pleasures and hopes he had been enjoying had been torn
away leaving him exposed, that convinced me; and at the same
time robbed me of any feeling of triumph. But a deep and real
[101]sense of gratitude toward my child took hold of me, and has never
left me.
...
"You are
you are certain about this?" her father asked.
"You are certain you do not wish to divide your time between
us?"
"I am certain," she answered. "Not because I don't love you,
Papa," she hastened to add. “I love you and want to help people
just as you do." And then she swallowed painfully, arching her
thin little throat. "But it isn't a choice between you and Mama.
It's a choice between Mama and Greta."
[102]Chapter XIV
And when the time came for us to appear before the judge, in
the same apparently calm decisive manner, when the judge asked
her which parent she chose to live with, she answered, "With
my mother."
These are the only words I heard during the hour that we
were present at the private hearing. My husband and his lawyer
sat at one side, and my lawyer, Julie and myself on the other.
Arnie sat a little apart from us all and did not once look at her
father or me, but kept her eyes fixed intently on the judge as if
only by keeping them there she could preserve that calm which
she so evidently desired to maintain before us. And though she
had no tears in her eyes, the calm which seemed so effortless as
she sat there did not seem effortless to me, who had heard late
the previous night, when I was lying awake, stifled weeping in
this very house and in the room where she now waits. And this
weeping seemed to echo in my ears, and then became harsh
music, dissonant and unbeautiful, assaulting my ear drums and
preventing me from hearing any of the words that were said (as
if I had been deaf), until I saw the judge turn to Arnie and ask
her a question, and the sounds that had stopped my ears quieted
and I heard her answer, "With my mother."
At that time we had been living for more than a year in this
house. When my husband left the rectory and Bethel it had been
necessary for me to make a decision about a home for Arnie and
myself; but I could not do so. During those first weeks I felt when
I waked at night, as often happened, that the woman who lay
there so singularly alone and cold with more than a physical
[103]coldness, was a stranger; and it was impossible for this stranger
to make any decision about a future that concerned someone
else. A dozen times a day at first I would say to myself “I must tell
David" as I had so many times before, and the knowledge that
he was not there robbed me of the former zest in living that had
made waking not a grim acceptance of another day, but a joyful
plunging into the continued rhythm of life.
Julie had urged us to live with her at Mars Hill and I was
indifferent and acquiescent. It was Arnie who, surprisingly
enough, refused and made the decision that we move to the little
house I owned. For a time she was the "executive" and with de-
termined energy and superficial gaiety, that carried us both in its
strong current, got us moved from the rectory. I understood even
then that her gaiety was only on the surface. For after the di-
vorce when she received a letter from her father who, with his
wife, with Greta, had gone to England to be a representative of
the peace society there (letters she did not share with me, and
which I did not wish to share), she became quiet and remote;
and I saw the same painful awareness of sorrow come on her
young face that I had known when the situation was developing
between her father and myself, and we were being carried, against
our wills, toward disaster.
Eventually I roused from my lethargy and began to enjoy the
life that had come to us. I returned to my love of flowers, to my
books and to pleasure in music, and was even able to help Arnie
when her music teacher began her on the "Well-Tempered Clavi-
cord." I would sit beside her at the old square piano with the
ivory keys and the beautiful mahogany grain that I can see now
(if I turn my head) in the soft light of the lamp, as I sit here at
the desk in a corner of the living room-so still now, with the
stillness of late evening on a quiet street-filled with shadows.
During those years I forced myself to go to church, after the
new rector came, but Arnie often would not go, and gave the
excuse that she needed to study; and slowly I began to lose heart
and to stay at home on Sundays. It was not that I did not feel
welcome at the church, for all my old friends were sympathetic
toward me. But it was this very sympathy that made it more diffi-
cult. Each time one of them called me on the telephone or came
[104]to visit me my pride was lacerated by their attitude which
(though I might have imagined it in my sensitive state), was
overly cordial and had an element of uneasy curiosity about it
that seemed to say, "How is she taking it?" They felt my lack of
response, I am sure, and yet they continued for a long time, only
gradually giving up a relationship that must have seemed to them
to lead to nothing; and more and more I fell into a pattern of
life that included only occasional visits with Julie, and more cen-
trally Arnie and her studies, my books and music and my garden.
I even tried to write a little, going back years to the time when
I was at St. Ann's and our English Composition teacher had dis-
covered in me a talent for writing. Miss Hale was the member of
the faculty whom I regarded with most affection and admiration.
She was not at all good looking, for her red hair and many
freckles on a large-featured face were not pretty; and yet there
was an indescribable sweetness that shone from her eyes and was
evidently so much a part of her nature that her features and even
her voice took on the beauty of her disposition. She was very
strict in her demand for attention and quiet concentration in the
classroom, but surprisingly she did not antagonize us, even when
she read our compositions or stories aloud and criticized them
with mercy. She was English, or of recent English extraction, and
expecially the pronunciation she gave to the word been, not
"bin" as we said it, but as if it were "bean," made me long to
imitate her and I would have done so and said "I have Bean,"
except that I felt it would be "putting on"; and even when I
tried it out in my room alone, saying "I have bean," "You must
have bean," "She has bean away," out loud, still it seemed un-
natural and affected, so that I had to relinquish that ambition.
But another and more fascinating ambition soon took its place.
One day when Miss Hale had severely criticized, before the
class, a story I had written, and I felt dazed with disappointment
and hurt pride, she asked me to stay when the others had left
the classroom, and when I did so she said to me that my story
showed a great deal of talent. "Only," she said, "you must be
very careful of the words you use, to be sure they say exactly what
you mean to say, and also that a word is the best, the most apt
that you can use in that particular place.”
[105]It would be difficult to express the delighted amazement with
which I viewed myself after she had said to me "You have a tal-
ent for writing." It was just as if I had been a nautical explorer
and had suddenly and unexpectedly come upon a wonderful and
beautiful island that had not been shown on the map by which I
had heretofore set my course. The discovery of this talent roused
in me an ambition to follow Miss Hale's directions, and receive
humbly her criticism and joyfully her rare words of praise, so
that before my senior year ended I had had two stories published
in the school magazine, one of which was reprinted in an ex-
change, the magazine of the State University. But my marriage
put all of that out of my mind. And it was only when Arnie and
I were living in the little house, and I was much alone that I re-
membered, after more than twenty years, what Miss Hale had
said to me, and began, but without much continued or concen-
trated effort, to write again.
To me the saddest effect of the divorce on Arnie was the
strange and unnatural attitude she developed toward the oppo-
site sex. Up to the time when the trouble began, even from her
ninth year, it had been difficult for me to keep Arnie to her stud-
ies because they were interrupted by the boys who were so evi-
dently attracted to her and made choir practice, or other church
activities, an excuse to hang about the rectory. And when Arnie
was fourteen and several members of the congregation gave
dances for children of that age, and invited the mothers as well,
I would sit with the other mothers and see, with enormous pride,
that my daughter never lacked a partner; and that when the
music began for another dance she had more than one invita-
tion. She never seemed to make any effort to attract, except that
she would look at some boy, and in the most natural, warmly
confiding manner, give him an effortless smile that seemed to
say: "How nice you are, and how nice I am, and how lovely it is
when you and I are dancing together." But after the separation
between her father and me her manner with boys and men be-
came rigid and self-conscious, even awkward, as if she had never
been thrown with them at all.
The only exception was a Jewish boy, son of the Rabbi in
Bethel, who was a senior in the high school where Arnie was a
[106]junior. He came to our home perhaps twice a week to help her
with mathematics in which Arnie had not been well grounded in
her former school, and without his help I am not certain she
would have graduated the following year. With him she seemed
to feel at ease, but only as a pupil might feel toward a tutor who
is preparing her for an examination. His attitude toward her
appeared, at least on the surface to be based on the same founda-
tion, but warmed by a kindliness and courtesy that the gangling,
shy, awkward boy of seventeen or eighteen showed not only to
Arnie, but to me when, grateful for his help to my child, each
evening he was there I prepared and served to them on the din-
ing-room table, where they had been working, hot chocolate
and cake or sandwiches. His eyes were a most beautiful shade of
dark blue, and with his contrasting black hair, gave him a look
of the Irish, which made me remember a belief our Aunt Julia
had clung to all her life that the Irish were the lost tribes of the
House of Israel. But soon I forgot him, for after his graduation
that year Saul de Leon went on to Columbia University.
After Arnie's graduation when she was seventeen Julie asked
me what I intended to do about her debut; for Julie and I had
been given a coming out dance by our parents two years before
our mother's death. I was quite certain, considering Arnie's new
attitude, such an affair would be less than useless. But I allowed
Julie's energy and enthusiasm to overcome my reluctance. And
since Arnie passively agreed, Julie went ahead with plans for
the debut and sent out invitations.
I should have been grateful for this, and for Julie's offer to
give Arnie her dress, and I was grateful; only I could not, I
would not, express to Julie the knowledge so painfully forced
upon me day by day that Arnie was not the same child for whom
Julie had seen so happily relaxed and smiling her shy joy at the
boys of fifteen and sixteen and seventeen, and who never,
for one
moment, allowed us to wonder whether or not she would have a
partner.
It was arranged that on the evening of the dance, held in De-
cember, 1936, just before Arnie's eighteenth birthday, she and
I would spend the night in my old home, and Julie gave us one
of the guest rooms, with twin beds. Arnie's dress, made under
[107]Julie's direction, was perfect for Arnie. It was not pure white but
a warm oyster shade of rich brocade made very simply, with a
touch of old lace around the neckline that left Arnie's shoulders
bare and with small puff sleeves. A full skirt gathered in at the
waist against the tight little bodice gave the child an appearance
of more mature development, yet emphasized the slimness of her
tiny waist. The rich off-color white did more to bring out the
beauty of Arnie's coloring than pure white would have done,
and as she stood before Julie and me in the guest room just be-
fore we went downstairs I thought, with pride: My daughter is
beautiful. At the same time I saw, with some apprehension, that
Arnie was tense, and not relaxed and happily expectant as a girl
of seventeen should have been.
Julie had invited both the young crowd and older friends, and
Arnie had received many flowers that were arranged in vases
all over the house. Sep had charge downstairs, and as he also had
impeccable taste everything was managed beautifully. He met us
at the foot of the stairs and presented to Arnie her lovely bou-
quet of cream roses with red hearts and to Julie and myself our
corsages of orchids which he had just taken from the refrigerator;
and as he exclaimed enthusiastically over Arnie's appearance I
saw that she gave him the beautiful smile that lit up her whole
face. And I was cheered, thinking that when the time came and
the music began she would fall into her old, natural and be-
guiling ways.
But unfortunately this did not happen. At first everything went
smoothly, for each of the young men, as well as the older ones,
did their duty and danced with her, but I noticed with chagrin
that Arnie, who had known how to dance almost since baby-
hood, and was full of natural grace and a sure sense of rhythm,
was dancing badly and stiffly as if she had never in her life
danced before. Also her face became set in an unnatural and
tense smile, not at all like herself, the self I had known for six-
teen or more years. And as the evening went on, and after the
men had done their duty, I noticed that Arnie stood bitterly
alone against the wall, or that when she danced it was with one
of the older men. And I realized for the first time the misery a
mother feels when she sees her child unpopular and knows that
[108]she is helpless, and that only the child herself can change the bit-
ter loneliness felt as she stands forsaken and ignored against
the wall.
Julie came to me and said, “What is wrong with Arnie? Why
isn't she dancing?" and I could only shake my head and regret
that I had not said to Julie in the beginning about the dance,
"All that would be useless." But it was too late.
"I'll see about this," Julie said with determination. She left
me and I saw her go to the stag line and taking the arms of two
young men there, laughing and flirting with them, but at the
same time firmly propelling them, she made her way toward the
corner where my
child was standing alone. At that moment Arnie
saw her aunt approaching with the two young men, but before
Julie could reach her she turned and rushed from the room. I
went into the wide hall (for I had been in the drawing room)
and to the library, and into the dining room where supper
would be served later and where Sep was dispensing punch from
the Washington bowl. I looked everywhere but could not find
her. Then it occurred to me, with a sickening heaviness, that my
child must have left the dance and gone to our room. I hurried
upstairs and without knocking opened the door and went in.
There I saw the roses Arnie had been carrying lying on the dress-
ing table, the beautiful dress flung carelessly over a chair and my
child lying in her bed staring at me with unhappy eyes. Then a
dreadful anger came up
in me, of which even now I am ashamed.
It was the anger felt when someone from whom much has been
expected fails to support the pride that has said to the world, this
is mine, and because she is mine, she must not humble me before
the others. I did not then understand what it was that made me,
as I stood beside the bed, and as Arnie stared at me in horror,
lash out at her with words that hurt and bewildered. "Why did
you do this?" I demanded. "What will Julie think? What will
everyone say? I am ashamed of you. Ashamed." And again I de-
manded, "Why did you do it?"
"Mama, don't," she said, sitting up in the bed. Taking my
hand and holding it rigidly in her own she begged, “Let's go
home. Back to the little house."
"Now?" I asked incredulously. "Now?”
[109]"Yes, now," she insisted. "We can go down the back stairs. No
one will see us, Mama. I will die if we don't go. Please, Mama.”
I should not have let her persuade me. I should have been
adult enough to make her face the situation. But I, also, did not
wish to face it. I, also, was a coward. And yet it seems to me now
that our cowardice had begun some time before, when we had
not had the courage to face our community with stout hearts.
At the same time the suffering we had experienced and the way
we had taken it, not lightly, but with sore and troubled hearts,
had changed us, so that in entering a life of which we were not
any more an organic part, we were not following our true na-
tures, as they had developed, but had become involved in a situa-
tion which, while real and legitimate for all the others, neverthe-
less had become false for both Arnie and myself.
I left a note for Julie saying that Arnie was ill and it had been
necessary for me to take her home. We slipped down the back
stairs and found a bus that went near our street.
From that time on, even on that very evening, began the sepa-
ration from Julie and our old life, and the centering of our lives
in another direction; a direction that, in progressive stages, each
of which was the product of what had gone before and of our
own personalities, naturally and (as it appeared) inevitably led
us on to the situation that finally exiled us from our former life,
and which after twelve years has ended again, here in the little
house where we came that evening in misery and defeat.
[110]Chapter XV
Together with the "International" there was a song used at
meetings at Madison Square Garden-such as the celebration of
the October Revolution and Lenin's birthday-that had touched
Arnie and myself deeply when we first lived in New York. The
music was in marching, thrusting tempo and the words no less
imperious and sure:
Forward, ye workers, freedom awaits you
O'er all the world and the land and the sea.
On with the fight for the cause of humanity
Forward, ye workers, and the world shall be free.
And I believe it was the meaning and purpose given to life by
the words "for the cause of humanity" (the very words, though
neither had heard the song at that time, used by Arnie's father
when he was explaining to her his reasons for leaving the
Church), that caused, Arnie, one day in August, 1937, to enter
our county courthouse; and by that act, by entering that door at
that particular time, to set in motion all the subsequent events
that resulted in such violent changes in her life and mine.
That summer Arnie was eighteen. After the debacle of her
coming out dance she had rallied, and during the seven months
since then she had continued her studies through a correspond-
ence course maintained by the State University. Also she gave
time to her music lessons and practiced on an average of two
hours a day. All this satisfied a need in her nature for concen-
trated work, but it was mental activity; and look as I might, I
could find no evidence of her heart being concerned about any-
thing at all. Yet she seemed content, as she went on from day to
[111]day, sometimes helping me with the housework and occasionally
in the garden.
I could have been most happy having her with me in that
constant companionship, but it was disturbing to see her so in-
sulated, so emotionally indifferent. It was almost, I felt at times,
as if all those enchanting qualities I had seen in her from her
babyhood, and which had developed so naturally with her
growth, had become paralyzed on that day in the judge's cham-
bers when she had watched her father and myself formally and
legally break up the family of which she had been a part. I was
certain that those qualities had not died, but were merely in a
state of temporary paralysis. And as a mother or father or nurse
might urge a child who has had infantile paralysis to take one
step and then another, I urged Arnie to find young people her
own age and go out with them; but she responded indifferently
and sometimes with irritation.
It was then I conceived a plan to mortgage the little house,
thereby making it possible for Arnie to go to England and visit
her father. For a long time I debated the question in my own
mind, postponing a decision; for it was necessary for me to face
the possibility that, once in England with her father and Greta,
Arnie might never return. So it was with difficulty and with pain-
ful misgivings that I finally presented the plan to my child.
At first I saw her eyes light up with interest; then, to my sur-
prise-for I had believed that the thought of traveling, of seeing
her father whom she loved, would conquer her indifference and
give her that eagerness and joy in life she had possessed before—
she refused to go. For a whole evening she argued with me, pre-
senting all sorts of reasons why it would not be possible for her
to go away, or to allow me to mortgage the house. Not once did
she say, "But, Mama, I don't want to leave you." Her arguments
were all purely intellectual. As she argued I puzzled over her
attitude, so vehemently rational, until I saw (or thought I saw)
that same dread which had been in my own mind was also in
hers; and that some of her vehemence came from the fear that
she would not, if she went to England, be able to preserve her
loyalty to me (thus disappointing herself), and that perhaps once
there she would remain with her father.
[112]I had followed Arnie's lead and kept all emotion controlled,
even as I tried to persuade her that she should go. Then all at
once I began to see that each of us was trying to outdo the other
in expressing the nobility of our motives. True nobility is a won-
derful quality, but I have observed that there is a great simplic-
ity about it, and in its unconscious manifestations in a human
being, almost imperceptible to those who are not sensitive to its
greatness. But when two people are consciously determined to be
noble and the determination develops into a contest of wills
over who shall show most of that quality to the other, the strug-
gle eventually becomes ridiculous and rather hypocritical.
Finally I laughed, because our rational arguments had become
irrational as we went on and on, neither of us allowing the truth
of real and deep emotion to thrust itself up into the self-con-
scious mental atmosphere that had been created between us.
As I laughed Arnie halted in her argument and looked at me
in surprise; and then, for the first time in months, her face and
eyes were illuminated by that smile which I had come to think
was lost forever, and she laughed with me: a blessed release for
both of us.
It was perhaps this release of emotion, in laughter, that made
it easier for Arnie, a few days later, to find release in tears. We
always had our dinner, as had been the custom in our part of
the world, around two o'clock in the afternoon. Supper was a
simpler meal, very much like the "High tea" of the English, and
which we did often call “tea” as my mother and father had done.
One day, during the early part of August, soon after our discus-
sion about England, and Arnie's firm decision not to go there, I
came hurrying home from marketing to prepare our dinner and
found my child lying on her bed, weeping. For a long time I
could not get from her the reason for her sorrow. But when she
was more composed and had wiped away the tears with a wash-
cloth wrung out in ice water which I brought her, she told me
that in the morning, on her way to the public library, which is
on Main Street at the western end of the Square, she had seen
people going into the courthouse; and she had followed them
inside to the room where the trial of two Negro boys was being
conducted.
[113]We had, of course, known about this trial for some time. The
two boys had been arrested in April and had been in prison
since. At first their arrest had been reported only in our local
newspaper. Then, as the date for the trial came nearer, Northern
had made the case headline news.
papers
The events that led to the arrest of the two Negro boys were
simple. One day in April they had met two white girls on a
freight train on which all were stealing a ride. The girls (they
were eighteen and twenty-one) were acknowledged prostitutes,
though the younger, Posie said to me later, which I met her in
New York while she was under the protection of the Party, that
the older girl, Lilly, had persuaded her into the life she had been
living.
I believe no one has ever been able to distinguish the truth
amidst the sordid details given, and the terror and hate and bit-
terness which the trial aroused in our town and in our part of
the country.
To me the immediate blame for all that happened should be
put upon the sheriff and the other men who took the four young
people from the train, accused the boys of raping the girls, and
arrested them. Knowing well the reputation of the girls, they
must surely have understood that women of that sort made no
discrimination as to color, any more than had many white men
of the South, or the Carpetbaggers and soldiers who came from
the North and occupied the South during Reconstruction, when
they desired someone of the other race.
But though I put the immediate blame on the sheriff, neverthe-
less, since I am trying to be factual and objective, it is necessary
to realize that there was much truth in the often ignored facts of
Reconstruction, which made even the second generation after that
time fearful of control by Negroes and Carpetbaggers—just as the
Jewish refugee who tuned my piano found it difficult to believe
that the Nazi regime would not have a resurgence of power in
Europe.
This fear or dread was expressed by the sheriff one day during
the trial when I had gone with Arnie to the courthouse. Coming
out of the courtroom after the noon recess had been declared we
met the sheriff in the corridor. There he angrily berated Arnie
[114]for her stand about the Negro boys. People were still leaving the
courtroom and they gathered about us as the sheriff, a large man
with a powerful voice, in vivid language reminded Arnie of the
Southern women who had been raped by Negroes during Recon-
struction. (His accusation was more personal because it was known
in some underground way that the sheriff's aunt had been one of
those young girls, all of whom were kept nameless by their fami-
lies—for it was only a man with unusual character or chivalry or
love who would have married a girl who had gone through such
an experience.)
Standing in the wide hallway, surrounded by curious people,
and with Arnie facing the indignant sheriff, I was overcome by
shame for him and for ourselves; and I looked, not on the sheriff
or the persons about us, but at my child to whom he was speak-
ing. Arnie's face turned perfectly white, and then was flushed
with indignation as she said contemptuously, "Sheriff, you still
believe in that old movie, The Birth of a Nation. Wake up, Sher-
iff." And he answered, "I am awake. And this is the death
of a nation."
Arnie was furious, but I, having heard my father speak of
those days (when he was a boy in his teens), knew that there
was some truth in what the sheriff had said; and that my grand-
father had been forced to hide in a certain closet at Mars Hill,
which was called "Grandfather's Closet," or in the swamp near
our home, each time the militia had come to arrest him because
he was a member of the "Resistance." And once when Julie and
I were quite young and were in town with our father, he stopped
us suddenly on a corner of North Main just opposite the court-
house and very solemnly pointed to a spot on the sidewalk. He
told us that our grandmother, who scarcely left her home during
all the time of Reconstruction, had found it necessary to go in
town one day to take him, then a boy of twelve, to the dentist,
since my grandfather was at that time hiding from the militia
and could not accompany her. He told us that as they came out
of the building (the same building in which Mr. Crawford, the
real estate agent, now has his offices), he and his mother had
met there, on that corner, a Negro member of the legislature,
just coming from the courthouse across the way; that this man
[115]had stopped before my mother and had spit on her, not once but
several times; and that my grandmother had quietly taken the
angry young man who was to become my father firmly by his
shoulders, and forced him to enter their carriage which was
waiting at the curb and to drive hastily away.
As our father told of this happening, he became excited, so
that Julie and I were embarrassed by his vehemence; and we
wished only to forget as soon as possible our father's behavior
and the fact that our grandmother had once been treated in
such a manner. But such memories were a part of the feeling that
had been engendered during Reconstruction; and it was this
dread of power and persecution, coming down from the North,
which must have had something to do with the manner in which
the sheriff arrested the Negro boys and with the accusation made
against them.
In New York Posie told me that she and Lilly had given them-
selves freely to the boys after drinking with them-for the boys
had a bottle of cheap whiskey. At the first trial Posie had not
been allowed to testify about this, and the Party was protecting
her so that at the second trial she might make this testimony
before the court. On the other hand Lilly, under the protection
of the State's attorney, denied that they had given themselves,
and swore that the boys had raped them.
The morning session of court was just beginning that August
day when Arnie followed the crowd into the courtroom. She saw
the young Negro boys brought in handcuffed and heard the
State's attorney lash out at them with what seemed to her prej-
udiced hatred, while the boys sat there young, frightened, and
confused. Arnie remained to the end of the morning session, and
her pity for the boys was so great, her sympathy for their situa-
tion so profound, that she had come home to sob out her pity
and sorrow.
But Arnie was not the kind of person who allowed emotion to
be spent in futile tears. Always it was necessary for her to do
something, to make a decision, just as she had made a decision
about remaining with me when her father and I had separated.
So I was not surprised that from then on it was impossible to
[116]keep her away from the trial (even if I had wished to do so), and
that the courthouse became the center of her whole life.
It had been rumored early that Communists had come into
the community to stir up trouble over the case, but I paid little
attention to the rumor, knowing that each side was making un-
warranted statements about the other, and that a Northern law-
yer had been brought down to defend the boys-which was an
added irritation to the Southern whites, even those who were
originally in favor of writing the trial off as a mistake of the sher-
iff and his deputies, and letting the Negro boys go free.
People who remember the case will also remember the eight-
een-year-old girl, her brown eyes blazing, who, at meetings
held at night on the public square and in the Negro district and
in the poorer sections of town, stood on an improvised platform
and asserted the innocence of the two Negro boys to the crowds
who gathered about her. That girl was Arnie. But neither the
Northern press, which in pictures and releases made a heroine
of Arnie, nor the Southern press which, in general, pointedly
ignored her, ever guessed at all the underlying currents that were
sweeping Arnie and myself into another life, and another world.
Late one afternoon I was at home preparing supper when I
heard Arnie's voice accompanied by deeper voices, and into the
house came my child, followed by three young men. They
were all talking at once and in a most animated manner and
Arnie scarcely had time to introduce them before they had
plunged into discussion again. I left them and went into the
kitchen, where Arnie followed me and asked if the young men
might remain for supper. She said they were representatives of a
New York newspaper who had come South to cover the trial. I
was pleased to see my child so animated and with young people
near her own age, and entered joyfully into the task of changing
our very simple supper, which we usually ate on trays, into some-
thing more appropriate to our guests, and served in the dining
room.
Subsequently I had many opportunities of observing all three
of the young men who came that evening, for they returned
again and again. Arthur Monroe (his real name, as we later
[117]learned, was Isidore Gerson), was the most interesting and the
most attractive, for he was tall, with black eyes and black hair
and a most engaging charm of manner when he was not being
rude. Later I understood that his rudeness was a conscious rude-
ness and based upon principle; and if he ridiculed Arnie and my-
self and our manner of thinking and our customs, there was al-
ways a reason behind his doing so.
Abe Mackan, the cartoonist, was slight in stature, but with a
lively wit, when he was not complaining about his health. Al-
though he appeared to be sound of body, he was continually tak-
ing some kind of pill; and always at a meal, and with profound
gravity, almost reverence, he set out his three bottles of pills be-
fore his place at the table. He was red-haired, with tan eyes and a
puckish face, and for the most part good-humored, thorough in
his sketches of the people taking part in the trial the good humor
did not prevent him from giving savage wolf-like expressions or
masks to the sheriff, the prosecuting attorney, the judge, and all
others who represented the South in the courtroom.
Even on that short acquaintance I discovered that their atti-
tude about the trial was little more reasonable than the attitude
of the sheriff and his men, only at the opposite extreme. And this
fact was especially brought to my attention that first evening by
Joseph Raymond, the third young man. Joe had rather a lugu-
brious countenance, sometimes lightened by a child-like smile.
As they came in to supper, which was late because of the change
of menu, and I gave them their places, I saw him look at the
glass of iced tea at his place and frown. I had a mint bed in the
garden and always put some crushed mint leaves in the bottom of
our iced tea glasses and topped them with sprays of mint, both for
the taste and for the contrast of color which the amber tea, the
yellow rind of the lemon, and the green mint gave to our table.
As soon as he had sat down Joe lifted the spray of mint from his
glass and threw it on the floor; then, taking up the long spoon, he
began unhappily to pick out the crushed mint leaves from the bot-
tom of his glass.
I said, "Don't you like mint?"
Continuing to bring the crushed leaves to the surface, and with
[118]an air of disgust, he said, "I don't want even a taste of the foul
stuff the Lords of the Lash use in their juleps."
That evening his behavior prejudiced me against him, and I
thought him rather a "light weight" intellectually and emo-
tionally. But later, when he was selected by the Party to initiate
and carry on, over a period of time, an exposé of a popular na-
tional magazine (with Arnie helping him in the research), and
when he made a smashing success of it so that thousands of peo-
ple became permanently disgusted with the magazine, I gained a
real respect for his ability.
At the supper table that evening I managed, for Arnie's sake,
to control my irritation at Joe's behavior. And when we were
all seated I followed my usual custom and, bowing my head
(never thinking of any other way of beginning a meal), said the
Grace that had always been used in our home.
I was considerably surprised as I did so to hear an exclama-
tion, then a low concerted yelp of laughter; and as soon as I had
finished the Grace and lifted my head, I looked about the table
at the faces of the young people. I saw that Arnie had a shamed
and embarrassed look on her own, but whether for me or for
the young men, who were now smiling at each other, I did not
know.
But as soon as they had left Arnie said, looking uncomfort-
able, “Mama, if they come again, please don't ask a blessing.”
"Why not?" I demanded, for the young men's rude manners
had antagonized me.
"It's embarrassing," Arnie protested. "They don't believe in
it. To them it is ridiculous."
"But after all," I replied, "this is my house. If they cannot be
courteous enough to accept the customs of the home in which
they are guests they should not come."
"Oh, Mama!" Arnie exclaimed, exasperated.
"But why?" I asked, still irritated by their behavior. "What
harm does it do?"
"Lots of harm, Mama," Arnie said with a seriousness that
made me give her all my attention. "And besides," she added,
smiling at me persuasively, "you don't believe in it yourself."
[119]I considered that and asked her why she felt that I did not.
"You don't go to church," Arnie said. "You know you don't
believe. Asking a blessing is just an outworn habit. Why should
you keep it up?"
As I centered my attention on this aspect of the matter, taking
it with the seriousness with which she was speaking to me, I
could think of no reason why I should continue a practice that
was, as Arnie had said, only a habit. And so whenever the young
men were there for a meal, and even when Arnie and I were
alone, I began from that time to leave off saying Grace, and
eventually discontinued it altogether.
[120]Chapter XVI
During that August other young men from New York joined the
group that gathered about Arnie and made our home their head-
quarters. The others came and went, but those three, Art and
Abe and Joe, remained and made a nucleus. Ours was, perhaps,
the only home in Bethel that was hospitable to the "Northern
influence," and the young men took advantage of, and in most
instances appreciated, our sympathy and understanding—and
our food.
And there was one aspect of this "invasion" that brought me
particular joy. For just as a mother might be enormously relieved
and pleased to see her child who had been paralyzed at last re-
gaining the natural use of his limbs, so I felt as I saw Arnie be-
coming again her "natural" self. It was as if her former attitude
toward the opposite sex, her coming out party, had never been.
As if, in a curious way, she had become a popular debutante,
