surrounded by young men, all of whom were striving to gain her
favor. And she, who had so recently been tense and awkward,
blossomed under the healing rays of this sun, the attention of the
young men, which contained a meaning that for a long time I
did not understand, and which Arnie did not share with me.
Only, whenever I came into the room, I saw that because I was
there they all, including Arnie, became quiet-as if they resented
my presence, which prevented them from being at ease and spon-
taneous with each other.
At first I accepted this attitude as something that was natural
and to be expected from young people toward an older person,
especially the mother of one of them; and I felt no apprehension,
[121]but only a great joy, as I saw my child regaining her former per-
sonality.
But gradually an anxiety, vague at first, began to present itself
to me. To my dismay Arnie became distant and uncommunica-
tive. When I lay awake in my bed at night I would hear her open
the front door with her latch key and come in tiptoeing, and qui-
etly (almost guiltily, it seemed to me) go to bed without even
putting on her light. As the days went on, and as she was more
and more with the others, at the trial, at the outdoor meetings
in the evening, in their discussions in our home, I watched my
child's face with that perception known only to one who loves,
and which is not curiosity but concern. And I saw that a change
had come in her, especially in her attitude toward the young
men, and in theirs toward her: an intimacy was indicated that
heightened the vague anxiety I had been feeling into something
more like certainty. In my child's face I began to see (and be-
lieved it was not imagination), the unmistakable appearance of
physical repletion; and though for some time I would not face
it, at last I acknowledged to myself that at eighteen my child had
become a woman, in the sense that she had experienced what I,
as a married woman, had experienced with the man I loved.
This knowledge was more painful to me because I unhappily
suspected that there was not one within the group of young men
who surrounded her about whom I could say, "She loves him.”
One evening I determined to wait up for her and sat in the
living room reading and playing some of my records until one
o'clock, when I heard, outside on the porch, the sound of steps,
some stifled laughter, and Arnie came in alone.
“Why, Mama!" she exclaimed, halting abruptly at the door
of the living room. "Why are you up so late?"
I turned off the record player and sat facing my child as she
stood before me with a secretive smile on her lovely mouth, try-
ing to rouse in myself the courage to express some of the thoughts
that, all evening, had repeated themselves in my mind under the
sound of the records I had been playing; or which had crossed
out, as if a hand had marked through them with a crayon, the
lines on each page of the book I had been trying to read. But it
[122]was the one conviction that had come to me-that she did not
love-which gave me the courage to say without any preliminar-
ies and with a simplicity that astonished myself: "Arnie, with
which of the boys are you 'sleeping"?"
And just as I was amazed at my own temerity, so my child was
equally astonished. A shocked look came on her face, as if I had
been guilty of a serious offense. But even as it came into her face
the shocked look vanished, and as she sat down on the footstool
near me I saw that look of pure honesty, the straight-forwardness
that had always been part of her, appear in her eyes; the honesty
that would not allow her, when she was directly questioned, to
rationalize or excuse or hide anything; and she said, 'quite simply
and in the same way in which I had asked the question: "Not
with any one, Mama,” emphasizing the word "one" in a signifi-
cant way.
"Do you mean," I asked, "with more than one of them?" There
must have been in my voice some of the pain I felt as my child
said this to me, looking at me with clear, honest eyes; for as I
asked that question she turned her head away from me (as she
sat near me on the square stool, her favorite spot since she had
been a child, when she and I were talking), and said uncomfort-
ably in a low voice, "You don't understand, Mama.” And as she
said it, probably for the first time allowing herself to see it all as
others might—as formerly she herself might have thought of a
girl who had become promiscuous-a flush of red, a blush, came
into her face, spreading from her neck to her cheeks and to her
forehead.
It was this blush, so childlike, that gave me the courage to go
on speaking about this matter; and if there was pain in my heart
because of the way she had chosen, I hid it, for I desired above
all else to keep her confidence, and the open relationship that up
to that time had existed between us. Also it was impossible for
me to forget that in the bitter time of my loneliness and despair
she had chosen me rather than her father and Greta.
"What is it,” I asked in a calm voice, “that I do not under-
stand?"
She looked at me then, and I could see that she was trying to
[123]gain confidence from somewhere within herself, and her lovely
mouth became set in a defiant firmness I had never seen on it
before.
"Well, Mama," she said in a voice she tried to make easy and
natural, but was unnaturally hard, "it's like this. I have applied
for membership in the Communist Party."
Then it was indeed only by the greatest self-control that I was
able to keep my voice and my expression from revealing the
depth of misery I had been hiding since my daughter had said,
"Not with any one, Mama." For up to that moment I had not
believed any
of the accusations of the sheriff and others in our
town that the Communists were "behind all the trouble," and
had discounted it.
"So Art and Joe and Abe are Communists?" I asked, but it was
more a sure statement than a question, for now all that had
been happening, the whispers, Arnie's withdrawal of confidence
in me, all was explained. She nodded and I, struggling with a
vast sorrow that threatened to overwhelm me, nevertheless ral-
lied my forces to face this second revelation which at the mo-
ment did not, in my eyes, bear any relation to the first. Now it
became necessary for me to see not only Art and Joe and Abe
and the others as if they had been wearing masks and had sud-
denly removed them, showing other and different faces, but it
was also my child whose face had become strange and unarticu-
lated, as if seen through a mist.
I heard her say, "Didn't you suspect at all, Mama?” in a low
voice.
"No," I stammered awkwardly. "No. I hadn't. . . .”
There was a silence. "But what has that to do
and again could not go on.
· ·
?" I began,
She laid her hand on my knee. "Wait, Mama," she said. "Wait.
I have something to show you." And with a quick movement she
rose from the stool and rushed from the room.
Presently she came back with a pamphlet and sat down on the
stool again, pulling it nearer to me. I looked over her shoulder
and saw on the front of the pamphlet its title, "The Communist
Manifesto"; and as she turned the pages looking for some partic-
[124]ular passage she had in mind I saw that she, or someone, had un-
derlined certain places on each page as if they were especially
important.
"This is the beginning, Mama,” she said, bending over the
pamphlet, and began to read: ""The history of all hitherto ex-
isting society is the history of class struggles. Freeman and slave,
patrician and plebeian, lord and serf, guildmaster and journey-
man, in a word oppressor and oppressed, stood in constant oppo-
sition to one another, carried on an uninterrupted, now hidden,
now open fight, a fight that each time ended, either in a revolu-
tionary reconstitution of society at large, or in the common ruin
of the contending classes. . .
She glanced over the page and went on reading: ""Our epoch,
the epoch of the bourgeoisie . . . has simplified class antago-
nisms. Society as a whole is more and more splitting up into two
great hostile camps, into two great classes directly facing each
other-bourgeoisie and proletariat. . . .'”
I started to speak, but she laid her hand on my arm. “Before
you say anything," she begged, "please listen, Mama.”
She turned a page and read: ""The bourgeoisie has torn away
from the family its sentimental veil and has reduced the family
relation to a mere money relation.'
999
Again she put her hand on my arm, holding me still, and with
the other turned several pages until she came to a place she had,
evidently, been trying to find.
“‘In bourgeoisie society,'" she read, "the past dominates the
present.' Just as it does here in the South, Mama." She looked up
at me for a moment, then hurriedly returned to her reading.
" "In Communist society, the present dominates the past," she
read, emphasizing the two words "present," and "past."
66
"""But you Communists would introduce community of
women," screams the whole bourgeoisie in chorus. Our
bourgeoisie, not content with having the wives and daughters of
their proletarians at their disposal, not to speak of common pros-
titutes, take the greatest pleasure in seducing each other's wives.
Bourgeois marriage is in reality a system of wives in common,
and thus, at the most, what the Communists might possibly be
[125]reproached with is that they desire to introduce in substitution
for a hypocritically concealed, an openly legalized community of
women.''
"What does that mean?" I asked sharply.
She looked up, surprised at the sound of my voice, for she had
been completely absorbed in what she was reading.
"What does it mean? It means, Mama, that marriage, the mar-
riage ceremony simply doesn't exist for a Communist, except as
a convenience. I know," she said, "I know you think it wrong of
me to sleep with all of them. But they have showed me that I
cannot be the right kind of member unless I make myself abso-
lutely free of all bourgeois moralities and hypocrisies. It is so
wonderful, Mama," she said, leaning toward me in her enthusi-
asm, "to be free-to know there is no sin at all, no guilt, no fear,
only freedom. And one of our duties," she said, "is to do all we
can to break up family life. That is a revolutionary duty," she
said solemnly. "Papa understood that, not consciously, but he
understood that marriage doesn't mean a thing. It is humanity
we must work for."
"I don't know," I said. "I don't know. . . ." Perhaps to give
myself time to recover from the blows that had come on me that
evening I rose abruptly and went into the kitchen, saying, “I
must have some coffee."
Alone in the kitchen, making the coffee, I thought of all Arnie
had said to me, and every word she had spoken seemed to take
on a sinister meaning. I was angry and sore and dismayed. “What
have you done?" I wanted to shout at her. "You have become a
tramp a whore!" Only this aspect of what she had confided to
me beat upon my consciousness. "No," I whispered, “No!” and
thought of my mother and father and their standards of right
and wrong. I saw my mother kneeling in the pew at my side
(when I was a child), making her confession, "We have erred
and strayed from thy ways like lost sheep . . ." and the gentle
tears that came on her face and that she wiped away from her
cheeks with the knuckle of her right hand, trying to hide them,
as she said: “But thou, O Lord, have mercy upon us, miserable
offenders. . . Restore thou those who are penitent ac-
cording to thy promises declared unto mankind. . . ." And the
...
[126]words, "unto mankind," seemed to repeat themselves over in my
mind: "To all mankind."
"No," I told myself, "I cannot accept this. I will not.” And yet
I did. And when in years to come Arnie changed from one com-
rade to another—and finally to Ben-always she had the answer
that silenced me: "I must live what I believe, Mama."
Looking back to that time, here in the same house, the same
room in which this happened, and where I now sit writing, a
thought comes to torture me: Is it possible that weakness may be
more destructive than positive evil? Has my weakness then be-
come the instrument of my child's destruction? I am not foolish
enough to wallow in remorse. It was Arnie's decision. Yet I must
face the unhappy fact that from the evening when Art and Joe
and Abe laughed at me and Arnie said, "Why ask a blessing at
meals when in your heart you have given it up?" I submitted to
the combined disapproval of the three young men and of Arnie
herself; and that thereafter I looked to them for approval, like a
child. Was it because I wished to remain a child? I do not know.
I do know that on that occasion as well as on the evening when I
was in the kitchen struggling to make a decision, I had seen on
my child's face, in her resolute conviction about what she be-
lieved, a warning, a threat of the anguish of a separation to come;
a sentence of death (for to be left alone with emptiness is a kind
of death), if I did not accept and make myself a part of the future
that she had embraced with all the enthusiasm of her generous
nature. I could not bear it.
Presently I returned to the living room with a tray and both of
us sat drinking the strong coffee I had made.
Then Arnie set down her cup and said, “Mama, they have the
answer to everything everything."
"How do you mean?" I asked, still with a feeling as if my whole
world had been shattered.
"You know," Arnie pointed out, "everyone is disturbed about
wars and predicting another one. In a world Soviet State there
will be no wars."
"How do you know that?" I demanded. "How can you fore-
tell?"
“Because, Mama, wars are fought for colonies and raw mate-
[127]rial and for power. But in a world Soviet State the power will be
centralized; there will be no colonies, only countries owing alle-
giance and obedience to the central economy. There will be a
central control commission, a world economy, to distribute all
things needed, where they are needed. There won't be any more
want or people going hungry, because if there is a famine in one
part of the world the central economic planning commission will
take food from one part of the world and give it to the other. And
there won't be any more racial discrimination, for there will be a
Supreme Court and it will put anyone who shows racial prejudice
in prison. It will be a crime against the State. Racial prejudice
will be much more of a crime than murder or theft or all those
minor 'crimes' that the bourgeois economy has been so con-
cerned with. See, Mama. Listen . . ." she said and began to read
again.
""The charges against Communism made from a religious, a
philosophical, and generally, from an idealogical standpoint, are
not deserving of serious examination. Does it require deep intui-
tion to comprehend that man's ideas, views, and conceptions, in
one word, man's consciousness, changes with every change in the
conditions of his material existence, and in his social life. . . .'
"You see, Mama," she explained, "we must make the present
dominate the past. All the conditions resulting from modern com-
merce, from airplanes, all the inventions of our age have brought
about a new condition that demands that we change our every
thought. Here," she said, opening the pamphlet again. “Listen.
'Undoubtedly it will be said that religion, morality, philos-
ophy, political science, the law, constantly survived this change.
There are besides eternal truths, such as Freedom, Justice, etc.,
that are common to all states of society. But Communisn abol-
ishes eternal truths, it abolishes all religion, and all morality, in-
stead of instituting them on a new basis; it therefore acts in
contradiction to all past historical experience." "
"And is that what Communism does?" I asked.
"Exactly, Mama. In that paragraph Marx and Engels are quot-
ing a supposed champion of the bourgeoisie, but Art says that in
fact they are saying exactly what Communism does. It does not
[128]constitute all these things (freedom, morality, justice, religion),
on a new basis. It simply abolishes them all. Everything is new,
"The earth shall rise on new foundations.'
999
"What is that?" I asked. "Is that from your pamphlet?"
"No," Arnie said, and her face took on a look of joy and inspi-
ration. "That is from the 'International.' Would you like to
hear the words?"
"Yes," I agreed. "Say them for me."
And she did. Quietly the meaningful words that I was destined
to hear sung so many times came from my daughter, spread
through the room, and centered themselves in my mind and
heart:
Arise ye prisoners of starvation
Arise ye wretched of the earth
For justice thunders condemnation
A newer world's in birth.
No more tradition's chains shall bind us
Arise ye slaves, no more in thrall,
The earth shall rise on new foundations,
We have been naught, we shall be all.
Tis the final conflict, let each rise in his place,
The International Party shall be the human race.
And the second verse beginning:
We want no condescending savior
To rule us from a judgment throne.
•
She looked up at me smiling. I saw that lovely smile and the
tears that made her eyes more luminous. "You see, Mama,” she
said more quietly than she had yet spoken, “it is inevitable—the
final conflict. And it has already begun," she added, opening the
Manifesto again.
"Marx and Engels made a list of what could be done even be-
fore a revolution in bourgeois countries, and here some of it has
already been done. The list is on this page and in number six it
says: Centralization of the means of communication and trans-
port in the hands of the State. Even in our country we have done
that to an extent, and in many countries in Europe centraliza-
[129]tion of communication and even industries are already in the
hands of the State." She read again from the pamphlet. "A
heavy progressive or graduated income tax.'
"And that, Mama, as Art says, is our greatest triumph to date.
For we have forced this heavy tax on people who were rich, so
that they have been made comparatively poor. And even the
white collar workers and the richer industrial workers have had
heavy taxes laid on them. And as the workers demand more pay
and the government demands more taxes all will become poorer
-inflation will come and discontent-and then these richer
workers and white collar workers will become real proletarians
and be willing to fight to make the revolution a fact. For the one
foundation of a stable economy which the bourgeois had is gone
-the gold standard. And then," she said, reading from the
Manifesto, " 'In place of the old bourgeois society, with its classes
and class antagonisms, we shall have an association, in which the
free development of each is the condition for the free develop-
ment of all.'"
"But that means a paradise on earth," I objected. “And how
can we have a perfect world when human beings are so imper-
fect?"
“Oh, Mama,” she said impatiently, "they will become perfect
if we make the environment right. If that is perfect, then all the
good that is in human beings will be given freedom to grow,
and with freedom will come perfection—the true brotherhood of
man."
"How," I then asked, "do you account for great men, men of
great goodness, men of talent and genius, who have developed
from bad environment and unhappy surroundings?"
"They prove it," my daughter said. "They prove the very point
I am making that all people are potentially great, all are equal,
and if they're given the right environment we will have a world
in which all people, like those who are the exceptions now, will
be great and good."
We did not go to sleep at all but sat talking until dawn. And
when the first cold, dim light came in the window I stirred in my
chair, wearily, stretching taut muscles. But my mind was not
weary. I was stimulated; stimulated and convinced by all I had
[130]heard, and as Arnie and I parted to go to our beds we looked at
each other with new understanding.
After that evening I was no more shut out from the group
when they gathered in the living room after supper. I had passed
the test. I was approved. The young men answered my questions,
and in September, when the trial to which they had been giving
so much of their time was over, and the lawyers were preparing
an appeal for the two young Negroes whom the jury had con-
victed; before all the young men (except Art, who remained to
establish cells in that part of the country), had returned to New
York; by that time I also had made my application for member-
ship in the Party. And that fall, one November evening, we at-
tended our first meeting of a cell.
Several blocks to the east of our home begins the Negro section.
I had visited this section many times in past years, especially
when my mother was living, to carry to someone whom my
mother loved, and who was ill, food and medicine; or with my
father, when he was having a house repaired for a family in
which he and my mother took a special interest, or when he was
defending in court a young Negro boy who had "got into trou-
ble." Also I had driven Mum Hattie there when her husband
had come from New York and was living in Bethel. But that
night in November I saw that section with entirely new eyes. As
we neared the houses and cabins we moved quietly, staying in the
shadows, for though the trial was over much hard feeling still
existed and there was danger in this meeting-but more for the
Negroes than for ourselves.
The moon was not full and gave very little light, but by that
light I saw with new eyes the crooked lanes, the small lots, each
with a cabin, and behind these cabins rows of collards which, in
my imagination, appeared like rows of thin, emaciated children
with heads too large for their bodies. We were in the poorest sec-
tion of that part of town and, thinking of the cabins about me,
walls covered with newspapers to keep out the cold damp air of
winter, I felt a burning in me, and the words of the "Interna-
tional" that I had learned repeated themselves, as they had on
that memorable night when my child and I had stayed awake
until dawn.
[131]We met in one of the poorest cabins. There were eleven Ne-
groes, men and women; Art and Arnie and myself being the only
white people. Two of the young sons of the house were there,
urging us hospitably close to the wood fire that burned in the
large fieldstone fireplace, and on which they cooked their meals. I
thought of those two other young Negro boys, still in prison, and
that by coming here, to this house, this cell, we were helping to
make such injustice impossible.
When the business of the meeting was over Art asked the two
boys to sing for us. Leaning toward the fire and lowering their
voices, knowing they must not be heard outside the cabin, they
began to sing, and as they did so others joined in softly, gently,
but with great meaning, to sing the first verse of the "Interna-
tional." Dark faces shone in the firelight; eyes looked into ours
with confidence and hope; voices harmonized as they began an-
other song which I recognized as a favorite of Mum Hattie's. Only
instead of the name of Jesus they used the name of Stalin.
Stalin is our captain
We shall not be moved.
Stalin is our leader
We shall not be moved
Just like a tree that grows beside the water,
We shall, we shall, we shall not be moved.
The meeting broke up quietly, those who did not live in the
house slipping out one by one—for safety's sake—to return to
their homes. Art remained to the last and Arnie and I went out
ahead of him into the dark, stumbling a little over the unfamil-
iar terrain, for the moon had gone behind a heavy cloud. As we
came out into the road that led to the paved street, Arnie slipped
her arm beneath mine.
"Mama," she whispered, "did you feel it? Did you feel it?" The
moon pierced the cloud for a moment, lighting her face, but not
more than it was lit by a deeper, warmer glow that came from her
heart. "It is just as if we were God, Mama," she whispered. "We
are the Creators."
"Yes," I said, because I had felt it while I sat there in the
cabin—the thrill of creation. We were changing the world. Even
[132]in that poor cabin we were putting ourselves into a mighty stream
of power-irresistible, inevitable, carrying us toward the final
conflict. The thrill of creation. We were making a new world,
creating new people. We were God.
[133]Chapter XVII
All these things had not happened in a vacuum, in which Arnie
and I were sealed away, protected from our former life. We were
still a part of that community; there were people there who had
known me from childhood, and Julie, my own twin sister, still
lived in that large house which had been my home; and which
now, in the first flight of my enthusiasm, seemed only an unim-
portant speck on the surface of the world.
Certain unpleasant and distressing events gave us temporary
discomfort or pain, such as the sheriff's attack on Arnie in the cor-
ridor of the courthouse. Also we received anonymous letters. And
over the telephone, or by personal visits, my old friends expressed
their hurt or annoyance or disapproval of what Arnie was doing.
Late in August Arnie was arrested with some others at an out-
door meeting for "disturbing the peace." The next morning in
the municipal court I was astonished to see Julie enter the court-
room with her lawyer. She came, and as if it were the most natu-
ral thing in the world to meet her niece on that basis—that is, as
a prisoner-she sat down beside Arnie and myself and whispered
to me that her lawyer was there to post bail for Arnie. The judge
had not yet entered the court and I turned to Arnie, who was on
my other side, and told her what Julie wished to do. My child
became agitated and said she would not be separated from the
others who had been arrested the evening before. As it turned
out, however, all were given a suspended sentence. Julie took us
home in her car and tried by every means she could (especially
by reminding me of our mother), to persuade us to change our
way of thinking; and if not our way of thinking, at least to re-
[134]strain ourselves from demonstrating our beliefs in such a public
manner. I am certain that Julie did not suspect, any more than I
had suspected it of Arnie, that we were already deeply involved
with the Party; but she did recognize the fact that she was mak-
ing no impression on either of us; and as Arnie and I left the
car she leaned from it before Sep closed the door on her and said
bitterly, “I give up." From that time she did not call me on the
telephone, as she had often called before, and her bitterness and
disappointment must have been relayed to our friends, for when
I met any of them on the street-if I went uptown to shop, or to
the dentist, or for some other necessary purpose-they turned
their heads away, or else looked through me as if I did not exist.
An exception to this attitude toward us by the community was
a most surprising visit we received early in October. That eve-
ning Arnie and I were in the kitchen, almost through with the
supper dishes, and Art was in the living room.
I had become very fond of Art. Since the end of the trial, the
others having returned to New York, and Art only left of the
group that had been in our home so often, a situation developed
gradually, day by day, in which, to all outward appearances, he
and Arnie were husband and wife. Though he was out in the
country most of the week, organizing cells, when he was in town
he and Arnie slept in her room, and he treated me as if I were,
indeed, a mother-in-law, but one with whom he had also that
deeper bond, the bond of our mutual purpose which was taking
us all toward the inevitable destiny planned for the world. Origi-
nally from Chicago, Art had gained his reputation in the Party
when he was only twenty-two, five years before, as a brilliant
leader (although of course with his revolutionary affiliations
concealed), in the farm strikes in the Middle West in 1932. He
was brought to New York in 1934 and up to the time he was sent
to Bethel had almost commuted between Washington and New
York. He told us (and whether he was correct or not I do not
venture to say except that Arnie confirmed what he said from her
experience during our first year in New York) that in Washing-
ton they "were shown without any trouble into the presence of
the highest officials"; and that whenever the President, during
the years between 1934 and 1938, wished to persuade Congress to
[135]have some particular bill passed, he would send word to the Party
to put on a demonstration so that he could say to Congress "the
people are demanding it." When he told us about these successful
maneuvers Art's attractive mouth would widen into a mischie-
vous grin, his clever face would shine with triumph, and one of
his Tatar eyes would become elongated into a conspiratorial
wink, as he said: "The President thinks he is using us. He doesn't
even suspect it is we who are using him."
By this time Art felt himself a member of the household, but
he left it to me or to Arnie to answer the door. So that evening
when the old-fashioned bell twanged its complaining way into
the kitchen Arnie laid down her towel and said, "I'll answer it."
I stopped washing the dishes so that the clatter would not inter-
fere with sounds coming from the front of the house; for I was
curious, since by this time, with the trial over and everyone from
that part of the country returned to New York, we had few
visitors.
Listening closely I heard a man's voice which seemed familiar,
then a woman's and then I heard Arnie say (and I could tell by
the tone of her voice, all its cadences known so well to me, that
she was wondering and questioning), "Come in, won't you."
There were some murmurs, among which I could make out Art's
voice. Then Arnie appeared at the kitchen door, her eyebrows
lifted in astonishment. "Mama," she whispered, closing the door
behind her, "It's Mr. Tate! . . . and Mrs. Boone!" Her voice,
even though she whispered, made each name a separate exclama-
tion.
I laid down the mop, wiped my hands, and untied my apron.
But it was impossible for me to move. I stood there staring at
Arnie remembering those days, only three years before, when my
husband and I were so painfully at odds with each other about
Mr. Tate and Mrs. Boone.
"What do they want?" I asked.
"I don't know, Mama," Arnie whispered; and then, “Are you
coming out? They asked for you. At least, Mr. Tate asked for
you...
""
Then, as stupid, insincere or superficial remarks will come on
our tongues at a time when an unexpected event has temporarily
[136]confused or paralyzed the ordered sequence of thought, I said,
"We have no bourbon." For my earliest recollection of Mr. Tate
was of the times (perhaps every two weeks), when he and his wife
and Mary, their only unmarried daughter, had come to Sunday
dinner at Mars Hill. And of the hour before dinner, when the
women were settled in my mother's small sitting room or in the
drawing room, and when through the open door into the hallway
we would see Sep bearing in his hands a tray on which he was
carrying to the library, where my father and Mr. Tate always
secluded themselves for that time, the bourbon that was Mr.
Tate's preference. And we always spoke of the decanter that held
his favorite as "Mr. Tate's decanter."
Arnie laughed, but not loudly enough for those in the other
room to hear. "If he must have a drink," she said, "he may have
some of Art's whiskey." For though Art was in no way a heavy
drinker, he liked a glass of whiskey occasionally.
"Are you coming, Mama?" Arnie asked, for I still hesitated.
"Yes," I said. "I will join you in a moment."
When she had gone I stood in the same place, trying to control
the confused and disordered emotions, brought so vividly to the
surface of my consciousness by the presence in the other room of
those two. Also there was a question involved, which Arnie would
probably have laughed at, but was most real to me so that my
pride was up in arms not only on my own account, but on Arnie's.
For I resented the fact that Mr. Tate had not brought his daugh-
ter Mary whom, though she was older than I was, I had known
so well for many years; but had brought Mrs. Boone who had
never been a friend, and who, if what people said was true, was
his mistress.
Then I reminded myself that we had put all such moralities
behind us, and that it was most hypocritical of me (considering
the situation in my own home to which I had given my tacit
blessing) to hesitate for one moment to receive Mrs. Boone. I was
even able to persuade myself that Mr. Tate's visit might easily
be concerned with demonstrating his friendliness; that is, that he
was trying by this visit (knowing that Arnie and myself had be-
come isolated from Julie and the others) to assert a loyalty and
friendship for the sake of my father.
[137]Having reached this conclusion I went into the living room
and greeted Mr. Tate and Mrs. Boone with hospitable friendli-
ness. But I was not prepared for the way in which she greeted
me as if we had been intimate friends for a long time-nor for
the manner in which she seemed to make herself at home. For
when I offered Mr. Tate his choice of whiskey or sherry and he
declined both, and went into the kitchen to prepare coffee for us
all, Mrs. Boone followed me there and after I had prepared the
tray took it from my hands and carried it into the living room
and served the coffee as if she were, indeed, the hostess. I was
still sensitive about Arnie's situation, which I was certain must
be known in the town, and it seemed to me that Mrs. Boone's
confidence came from the fact that she felt herself and Arnie now
on the same level. I berated myself inwardly, for allowing these
bourgeois moralities to creep into my thoughts again, and was
able to smile and to thank Mrs. Boone for her help.
Mr. Tate was not much older in appearance than when I had
last seen him, just before my divorce. He was perhaps a little
more bald, slightly more rounded, but he was a tall man and
could carry the added pounds. I had never, even in my childhood,
been drawn to him in affection, for he always had a caustic
tongue. Now, as I looked at his face, while he continued his con-
versation with Arnie and Art, I seemed to see in his features-his
small blue eyes, the straight nose above thin yet sensual lips, but
more in the expression behind them-a self-satisfaction, an in-
ward and perhaps perpetual self-complacence, which I had never
noted before. Yet his very next words proved that he was, indeed,
a kindly and thoughtful person.
He turned to me and explained that he would have come
before to offer his services to Arnie (as a lawyer) in the struggles
that had brought her before the public the past summer, but that
he had been in Europe until only a week before. But, he said, he
wished to assure us that he believed the stand taken by Arnie and
myself was a very brave one, and that we should be supported by
all people whose minds were not closed to progress. He ridiculed
the sheriff, and the State's attorney, and repeated with caustic
comments some of the statements made about us in the town and
that he had heard in the church made by church members.
[138]"They even say," he told us, "that you are Communists."
Arnie gave me a warning glance, but it was entirely unneces-
sary. Fortunately Mr. Tate did not see the glance Arnie had given
me and went on, "They see Communists everywhere. I am cer-
tain they look under their beds at night.
." He then went on
to speak cynically-or skeptically-about some of the beliefs of
the church, just as my husband had done; and I asked if he were
still a vestryman, for I thought that perhaps, from the manner in
which he talked, he had resigned from that position.
"Certainly I am," he answered. "And we have quite a group
now in the national church, all of whom are working to bring
about social changes. For," he said, and I was astonished and
pleased to hear him using almost exactly the words in the Com-
munist Manifesto, "many of us now realize that new means of
communication, new inventions, and social changes have made
new conditions; and unless we wish to be left behind by progress
the church must adjust itself, and its ministers must take the lead
in making a new world. Of course," he added, laughing, "there
are some old fogies in the church who still believe in original
sin."
Every moment, as he talked, my heart was warming to him
more and more, and I even looked on Mrs. Boone with new eyes,
seeing in her not the voluptuous "woman of the town" as we had
vizualized her before, but a person with an attractive, kindly
face, a person of real intelligence; and I told myself: If they are
happy together, as they seem to be . . . why not?
Mr. Tate and Art developed a real affinity for each other and
when Art went into the dining room and brought out a tray with
the whiskey bottle and some glasses and a pitcher of water and
served the drinks to Mr. Tate and Mrs. Boone and himself, we
became even more a closely knit little group; and among us (led
by Mr. Tate) there developed a feeling of righteous indignation
against all the others in town who were against us, a common
assumption of superiority to them all, expressed in the ridicule
of their foibles at which Mr. Tate was a master.
Though I did not join in all of this, my feeling was one of
pleasure that we had a champion in Mr. Tate. Then, all uncon-
sciously, he did a thing that ruined this feeling for me, though I
[139]realized afterward that I was entirely unreasonable in taking
offense.
Increasingly during the evening he had made it evident to us
that he and Mrs. Boone were more than friends, and it was as if
he said to us: I understand you and the situation here. And so,
in the same way, because we are the same, you understand us.
Art, also, had let be known more than once (by raising Arnie's
chin and kissing her on the mouth, or by giving her an affection-
ate slap as she passed him) the relationship that existed between
them. And now Mr. Tate, placing his hand on Mrs. Boone's thigh
in a most intimate and suggestive gesture, looked at Arnie
(though as I said later to Arnie it was more a "leer" than a look)
and said to her, "It's wonderful, eh, Arnie?" Thus placing Mrs.
Boone and Arnie in the same category.
After they had gone and Arnie and I were talking in my room,
and I protested about this, Arnie suggested that perhaps I felt a
resentment because I was left out; because there were two couples
and I was the odd person. Later I did acknowledge that it might
well have been one reason for my objection. But at the time I
answered Arnie sharply. “On the contrary," I retorted, "I felt
more like the Madam of a house of assignation.” And then could
have bitten my tongue because I had allowed it to say a thing
of that kind about my own child.
Arnie then said that Art felt Americans were too prudish and
puritanical, and that Europeans were far ahead of us in tolerance.
"Look at Europe, Mama," she said.
"Yes," I exclaimed, “Look at it!" For we all knew and under-
stood the confusion and desperation there.
However, from that evening Mr. Tate and Mrs. Boone came
often to us; and allowing myself to become one of them, approv-
ing their way of life, just as I had Arnie's there was no longer
any barrier to friendship. And with the attitude and mood Mr.
Tate's visits always engendered in us, the attitude of young peo-
ple (though only Arnie and Art were young), who were in rebel-
lion against the stodgy customs of their elders, we became at times
quite hilarious.
Once a week Arnie and I stole from our home and attended
the meeting of the cell. At that time we had no right to be pres-
[140]ent, since we had not yet received our red cards. But finally they
did arrive and Art presented them to us at a meeting of the cell
when there was a special celebration with coffee and cake in the
little cabin-which was crowded that evening, for the cell was
growing.
Art was now reaching out to establish contact with share crop-
pers in the country. He was having some difficulty because the
Socialist group for share croppers had begun their organizing
first, and he was very bitter about them.
"Why do we hate the Socialists so much?" I asked him. “They,
also, are followers of Marx."
"We don't hate them," he said with a laugh. "It is they who
hate us."
"But why can't we work together?" I demanded. "We have the
same aims."
Art had a way of talking that I found, later, was characteristic
of many officials in the Movement at that time, but which was
new and impressive to me. He would draw up his chair, spread
wide the fingers of his left hand, and with his right hand press
them down one by one as he made his points. And often he used
the word "categorical."
"First," he said, drawing up his chair close to me and spreading
his hand and pushing down his first finger, “acknowledge that we
do have the same aims, that is to make the State supreme, to eli-
minate class distinctions, to abolish poverty and racial prejudice;
in other words, to make all men equal, as the forefathers of this
country stated so hypocritically that they are.
"Second," he bent down the next finger of his left hand: "We
do not hate the Socialists. We are in fact glad when they come to
power because they prepare the way for us. That is their histori-
cal role. That is what is happening in Germany."
(At that time, in the year 1937, and even later the Party, curi-
ously blind to Hitler's increasing power, was confident that Ger-
many would be taken over and made into a Soviet State. But on
the evening when Art spoke to me we did not dream that the
Weimar Republic's "historical role" had been to prepare the
way, not for Communism, but for Hitler.)
"And why is this, you ask," Art continued his explanation,
[141]leaning toward me with elbows on his knees and fingers thrust
before my face, and always with the charming smile making what
he said so easily acceptable. "Why is it their historical role to
prepare the way for us? Because under the Socialists people be-
come accustomed to looking to the State for Security; the bour-
geoisie are made to feel guilty for their social sins so that, by the
weight of their guilt, they are transformed into an inert mass
incapable of healthy action. Also the Socialists, as we do, be-
lieve that only by eliminating God and the divisions that re-
ligions make can all people of the world be brought together in
a real brotherhood of man. So the Socialists prepare the way for
us. That is categorical.
"And why do we struggle, why do we fight against each other
as you put it?" he asked, bending down his third finger. "Because
they wish to be the owners of the earth, and they know, as we
know, that it is we who are destined to rule. That is categorical.
It is, therefore, not we who struggle against them, but they who
are jealous of us, who struggle against us, for they know that
when we come to power, they go out. That is why," he said dis-
gustedly in a sort of anti-climax, "I am having trouble with these
petty Socialist politicians out in the sticks."
Both Arnie and I listened with profound admiration to his
exposition. And it was not only in my talks with Art that I was
learning more each day, and each day becoming more firmly
fixed in my convictions. But as I had formerly read my Bible,
now, along with various pamphlets Art had given us, I read the
Communist Manifesto until I almost knew it by heart. And
often Arnie and I, when we were alone in the house, discussed
with enthusiasm every new idea that had been presented to us.
We were truly learning (but Arnie more easily than I), to live
by the statement in the Manifesto: "In Communist society, the
present dominates the past."
[142]Chapter XVIII
Yet when that Christmas, the Christmas of 1937, came, I found
that many remnants of the past still clung to me and dominated
my thoughts and actions.
We had begun, under Art's tutelage, to speak of Christmas as
a "commercial holiday" with no meaning except that it gave the
merchants an excuse for increasing their profits. In spite of this,
however, I found as the season approached that Christmas still re-
mained for me a special, a most particular day, and that it was
impossible for me to relinquish at least the outward forms that
had always marked it. Art looked on with smiling tolerance as I
baked a large fruit cake, cleaned the house, put up fresh curtains
at the windows and made all the other preparations that had
characterized the season.
For years I had bought our Christmas greens from an old
Negro man who came in from his farm in the country with his
wagon loaded with cedar and holly and mistletoe. On the first
Christmas after we had moved from the rectory, in some way he
found our new address and appeared at the little house, present-
ing to me, with that manner he had of a rich man showing his
treasures, the greens piled high in his one-horse wagon; and min-
gled among the cedar and smilax the bright red of the holly ber-
ries and great clusters of mistletoe that his sons and then his
grandsons had let down carefully on a rope (so as not to injure
the delicate branches or lose one white berry), from high in the
old oaks they had climbed. So that year when he appeared as
usual I could not resist his treasures and bought a cedar as well
as holly and mistletoe for our home. Also, unknown to Arnie and
[143]Art, I had presents for them hidden away, along with the paper
in which I intended to wrap each gift.
Early on Christmas Eve I brought out the box of ornaments
and tinsel (some of which had been renewed and added to over
the years), that had been on Arnie's first Christmas tree when she
was less than a month old. Art was in the house that morning,
and as I struggled to set up the cedar tree in a corner of the living
room, very amiably he came and helped me to get it fixed in the
keg I always used for that purpose.
Arnie had seemed indifferent to my preparations, but when
she saw that Art was helping she began, after he had set up the
tree, to unpack the decorations.
"Mama,” she whispered, as Art left us to wash the stain of the
cedar from his hands, “you haven't bought presents, have you?”
"Yes," I answered firmly, though at once I began to feel
guilty, "I have presents for you and Art."
He came into the room at that moment and Arnie turned to
him. "What do you think, Art. Mama has bought us presents."
"Well," he said laughing and thrusting his hands deep into
his pockets, "what's wrong with that?”
"But after all the things you have said," she objected.
It is necessary to remind myself even now that all this was not
as simple as it seems. More and more Arnie and I had come to
understand that we were an integral part of a great Interna-
tional Movement whose authority extended from the center in
the Workers' Fatherland on down to each member; and that
when we had become members we had put ourselves, of our own
volition, under that authority; and that Art, who was the official
representative of the Movement in that section of the country, as
well as being a person who had gained our affection, was also the
authority to whom we owed obedience and respect. So that, as he
stood before me looking at me quizzically from his slanting Tater
eyes (as a parent might look on a child, not certain whether to
say no or accede to a request), I found myself smiling at him
placatingly, as a child might smile up at a parent who has the
power to give or withhold.
He swept one hand from his pocket and his arm went out into
a wide arc to include the tree, in one corner, the holly on the
[144]mantel and over the pictures, and the mistletoe hung in tradi-
tional style above the doorways. "If your mother wants to keep
up these sentimental trappings for a time," he said to Arnie, "we
don't object. She'll soon learn better. Just so," and the laughter
went from his face, as his expression became serious, almost stern,
"it's taken for granted all this is nothing but an expression of
bourgeois sentimentality."
"Of course," I agreed, happy that I was being allowed to ex-
press that "sentimentality" for this holiday which in years past
had been, except for Easter, the most important, the most joyful
event of the year. "And I promise," I said, speaking like the
"good child" I was becoming, "I won't do it again." And I never
did. In the years to come we paid no attention to Christmas, ex-
cept to regard it as a legal holiday, a time when we had a special
dinner and perhaps allowed ourselves the luxury of an expensive
movie in the evening.
After supper that Christmas Eve Art and Arnie retired to their
room and I heard them talking in low voices and laughing to-
gether, and they came to the door of the kitchen, where I was
stuffing the turkey for our Christmas dinner, with top coats on,
evidently prepared to go out.
"Mama," Arnie said laughing, and I could see that she looked
quite happy and that on her coat there was pinned a sprig of
holly, "we're going uptown for a while."
"Oh!" I exclaimed, "I thought you were to be at home this
evening.”
"Don't worry," she said reassuringly, glancing mischievously
at Art, "we'll come back early in time to drink the sherry."
For already I had set out in the living room, on a silver tray,
a decanter of sherry and glasses for the little ceremony that it
was our custom to carry out after the tree had been decorated
and all the presents put beneath it. Like most customs and tradi-
tions this one had derived from a very simple human need. In
our family the grandparents who had inaugurated it, after they
had decorated the tree and set out the presents for their young
children on Christmas Eve, and, were tired from their exertions,
had sat down with a glass of sherry and a slice of fruit cake, to
rest and admire the tree before they went wearily (and I believe
[145]happily) to bed. My father and mother had carried on this tradi-
tion, and in the rectory my husband and I had continued it. For
though we kept no wine or spirits in the rectory, as my parents
did in their home, each Christmas Julie had never forgotten
Father's custom of sending my husband and myself a bottle of
Amontillado sherry; and when Arnie was in her teens she had
joined us, on Christmas Eve, in trimming the tree and in the
little ceremony of "drinking the sherry" after it was done.
As they said goodnight, laughing together (and I thought with
a warm thrust of affection for him, that Art must have agreed to
let Arnie go out and buy presents for the tree), I saw from his
heightened color that he must have taken another drink of the
whiskey kept in the sideboard in our dining room. And I believe
it was this second drink and the unusual third he allowed himself
later, on which can be put most of the blame for his subsequent
behavior, though I am not certain he would not have done what
he did and said what he did when he found Julie there, even
without the liquor.
For Julie came that evening. I had trussed up the turkey and
set it in the refrigerator ready for the morning, and was sitting
quietly in the living room, allowing myself to enjoy the holiday
aspect of the room: the wood fire burning brightly in the fire-
place; the tree now decorated with tinsel and colored balls shin-
ing in the light from the fire; the holly, red and green on the
mantel, red-bowed wreaths at the windows and large bunches of
mistletoe like inverted candelabra, also topped by red bows,
hanging above the doorways; when I heard a car stop outside and
the old-fashioned bell at the front door send its warning through
the house.
Unknown to Arnie I had also bought a present for Julie and
one for Sep, not knowing even whether I should give them, un-
able under the circumstances to make a decision. But with all
that had gone before, with Julie's disapproval of Arnie and my-
self, I had not expected in the least that she would come to us.
So that when I opened the door and saw her there, and behind
her Sep with his arms laden with Christmas packages, I was as-
tonished.
"Merry Christmas, Ann," Julie said and, leaning down, kissed
[146]me on the cheek. As she did so to my amazement and dismay I
felt a sob tear at my throat, and it was only with the greatest diffi-
culty I was able to control it.
"Merry Christmas," I said to her, and then "Christmas gift,
Sep. I caught you." And I smiled at him, remembering our joyful
shouts when we were children, and Sep's equally joyful response.
He grinned in answer to my smile and said, “Christmas gift, Miss
Ann.” I opened the door wide and Julie stepped inside. "Won't
you come in, Sep?" I asked, but he declined and Julie, taking the
packages from him said, “In about an hour, Sep," and he left
us.
I helped Julie remove her coat and placed the presents she had
brought under the tree with appropriate exclamations. Julie and
I were now almost forty (we would have a birthday the follow-
ing March); and looking at my sister as she settled herself in one
of the wing chairs and took her hat off, fluffing her hair, I thought
that she looked infinitely younger than I, although we were twins.
There was not a gray hair that I could see in her brown, short
bob so beautifully set in the fashion of that time. And I, I had
only to look in the mirror to realize that my hair, during the past
three years, had turned almost white. As we sat opposite each
other I saw on my sister's face a look of innocent and benign
friendliness, of affection, and again I experienced the joy that
(though I realized its superficial, temporary quality), had been
in me since I had brought in the greens, without consulting
Art, and set them up in our home. Some of this feeling of re-
membered joy and laughter overflowed as I gave Julie her glass
of sherry and said, smiling, “ 'Queen of my soul, this goblet sip.'
Julie accepted the glass, looking at me with laughter in her
brown eyes. "Oh, Brother Charles!" she exclaimed as she had
done a hundred times when we were children and had for a while
called each other by the names of the twins in Nicholas Nickleby.
"Do you remember," she went on, "Mama saying 'Dra' it mild,
dra' it mild,' like Mr. Browdie when you and Hattie and I quar-
reled?"
"Yes, and for weeks if anything out of the ordinary happened
we would lay our hands on our hearts and say as if about to faint.
'I do so palpitate.'
[147]"Here's to Miss Fanny Squeers," Julie said, lifting her glass.
Opposite her I lifted my own. "Love, bliss, rapture. Rap-
ture, love, bliss,' " I quoted from the wild man's proposal to Mrs.
Nickleby.
All this time I could see that Julie's eyes were taking approv-
ing note of the Christmas tree and the decorations. But suddenly,
as she set her glass down she said, "Where is the beautiful crèche?”
and my heart sank low.
After the First World War my husband had visited Bavaria,
where he had found and brought home to Bethel a most lovely
crèche, all of it, the stable, Joseph and Mary, the shepherds and
sheep and the three wise men carved lovingly and carefully from
wood by a Bavarian craftsman, and painted in old colors that
made them look as if they had stepped out of an ancient stained
glass window. Even the manger was carved and the figure of the
Christ Child not fastened to the manger, but separate from it.
So unique, so beautiful, so different from all others, the crèche
had always been the center of admiration for all who saw it, and
each year seemed to present a new and fresh loveliness to their
eyes, as it did to ours. And it was for this reason, because it had
been the center of Christmas in our home, that Julie had par-
ticularly noticed that it was missing, because she, too, had loved
it, as we all had.
From the time when she was old enough it had been Arnie's
responsibility to set up the crèche on the old square piano, and
before putting up any other decorations we had always made a
special ceremony of bringing it out. Even after we had moved
from the rectory to the little house Arnie had continued this
custom. This year, however, she had said nothing about it, and
with all the discussion concerning decorations I had hesitated,
when I was getting out the ornaments for the Christmas tree and
came on the foreign wicker basket in which the figures were
packed, to take it out at all. So when Julie said, "Where is the
crèche?", it was difficult, in fact impossible, for me to explain why
it was not there; and I said, trying to make light of it, "Oh, it was
too much trouble to unpack.'
"You've never found it too much trouble before," Julie said,
looking at me curiously, and suddenly I understood, with a
[148]heightened sense of awareness, that Julie was speaking about the
crèche not only because she missed its loveliness, but because, in
a subtle way she was making it an issue between us and was try-
ing to discover whether or not there was a hidden reason why the
crèche was not there. And just because there was a hidden rea-
son, and because (as I later understood) I had not yet taken
a firm position on that question so that all "bourgeois senti-
mentality” had been put behind me as if it had never been, under
Julie's curious and demanding gaze I felt confused and made the
first excuse that came into my mind.
"Arnie has always set it up," I said.
"Then why hasn't she done so?" Julie asked, raising her brows.
"Or perhaps she intends to do it later...."
"Perhaps," I answered in a voice I well knew was not con-
vincing.
"Where is Arnie now?" she asked.
"Doing some last minute Christmas shopping," and as I an-
swered I was glad and relieved that this statement, so familiar,
so normal, was just as it appeared to be and was based on my
own inner assurance that this was, indeed, exactly what Arnie
was doing.
“Then," Julie said, raising herself from her chair, "let's get it
set up before she comes home. . . . Now, Ann," she said per-
suasively as I hesitated, “you know you want to. Come," she
said, holding out her hand. Recklessly and without for one mo-
ment thinking of consequences, I put my hand in hers, and so to-
gether, we, Julie and I, went into the storeroom. As I set a stool
below the shelf so that I could reach the wicker basket in which
the crèche was packed, Julie, three inches taller than I pushed
me aside and lifted it down. As she did so a small black suitcase
that was lying on the wicker basket came down with it.
"What is this?" Julie asked curiously.
"Never mind," I said. “Just lay it back on the shelf.”
She started to do as I had asked, but turned it over and looked
at it again.
"Oh, Ann!" she exclaimed reproachfully. And I understood
that she knew I had kept (against all common sense, against
all reason) that one very personal thing that had belonged to my
[149]husband and to his life in the church, and which she had seen so
often during my father's last illness when David had gone to
Mars Hill to give my father Communion. That small black suit-
case contained a silk cassock, a surplice, a white stole, a square of
linen for covering an improvised altar, and a beautifully fitted
leather container within which rested the tiny silver ciborium,
the pattern, and the chalice. Out of all David's possessions that
he had left behind him I had held on to this one thing only.
"Please, Julie," I said. "If we are going to get the crèche set up
we must hurry."
That drew her attention to the wicker basket. She laid the
suitcase on the shelf and lifted the basket in her arms. In the
living room as we unpacked the beautiful figures that had once
roused in me a feeling of artistic appreciation, as well as of joy
because of what they represented, I continued to feel an urge to
get it set up quickly. At the same time a sense of uneasiness, of
guilt, of dread, as if already I knew what was coming, almost
overwhelmed me. With a constriction at my heart that took away
any pleasure I might have had in placing the beautiful figures in
and about the stable, I went about helping Julie; only when she
began setting up the stable on the corner of the old square piano,
the place where we had always had it, so that it would be the first
and most important of the Christmas decorations to catch the at-
tention of a person entering the room, I did insist that we place
it, not on the piano, but on a small, inconspicuous table.
All my discomfort and apprehension seemed entirely unjusti-
fied when the door opened and Arnie and Art came in, just as
any other young people might return after a bout of Christmas
shopping, with smiling faces and cheeks reddened by the cold
outside air, eyes bright and happy, and with their arms full of
packages. It was so normal, so usual, so welcome that with a long-
ing to become, at least for a time, a person who was not taking
thought but could be relaxed in mind and body, I forgot to be
cautious and allowed myself to "wallow in sentimentality" as Art
expressed it later, as I saw my child whom I loved, and my twin
sister, so dear to me, meet and kiss each other-and smile.
Joyfully I hurried to the kitchen to cut the fruit cake so that
we might have it when we drank the sherry together. But when I
[150]came out I saw that Art was seated beside the table on which we
had set the crèche; and that with his long legs spread out before
him, and with one hand holding a glass of whiskey and water,
with the other he was fingering the figures we had set about the
stable, taking up first one and then another and examining them
curiously.
Julie was congratulating me on the cake, which had been
made, as usual, after our mother's recipe, and I was feeling a de-
light in this added evidence of good will, when I saw Arnie glance
uneasily at Art. Taking her cake and wine, she moved nearer to
him. Then, as Arnie changed her position Julie glanced that way
and in the midst of a sentence she halted, her mouth remaining
open, her short upper lip lifting as her face took on an expression
of astonishment.
Suddenly all became silent. It seemed to me I could still hear
the echoes of our voices in the room, but even these echoes
dwindled away and the silence became more heavily apparent.
And just as people will stand looking on the victim of an acci-
dent, a body on the sidewalk without speaking a word, and
though they might wish with one part of their minds to go about
their regular business, but are held by the fascination of horror,
so did Julie and Arnie and I gaze in fascinated silence at that side
of the room where Art sat beside the crèche.
He had found somewhere in his pocket, or had taken it from
one of the packages they had brought, a length of cord. As we
looked he lifted the figure of the Christ Child from the manger
and with deep concentration, completely oblivious of the three
women who watched (a concentration I could not help but ad-
mire), he slowly and carefully tied the cord about the neck of the
carved wooden infant.
At each end of the roof of the stable the eaves extended in
single carved overhangs. And as we watched, and with the same
concentration, bending his head over his "task," moving his
clever fingers in and out, he twisted and tied the ends of cord to
the end of the overhanging eaves so that the infant, with the cord
about its neck, dangled as if it had been hung on a gibbet.
I heard Julie gasp, but could not take my eyes from the spec-
tacle that was going on before us. Still in that heavy si-
[151]lence, which we could not break, and with the same utterly con-
centrated attention, Art took up the figure of Mary and laid it
down in the straw. I do not know whether the others knew what
he intended to do next, but I understood and would have cried
out, only that I was dumb. With great deliberation he then lifted
the figure of Joseph and placed it face down upon the figure of
Mary. Then, apparently satisfied with what he had set out to do,
he looked up at Arnie with a gleeful smile, left his chair near
the crèche, and in two easy strides came to the table beside which
Julie and I were sitting. Taking a glass of sherry from the tray,
and a plate with a slice of fruit cake on it, he sat on the couch
near Julie and myself and began to eat the cake and drink the
wine.
Still there was silence. I glanced at Julie and saw that though
she was, for the moment, speechless, her eyes glittered with anger,
and her mouth, so lovely in its natural shape, was straightened
into an angry line. Suddenly she stood and looked down at Art
as he sat smiling nonchalantly on the sofa, his legs stretched out
before him, apparently relaxed and contented. Then she looked
at Arnie, now gazing helplessly at the mutilated crèche, at the
Christ Child, hanging suspended from the rafter of the stable,
swinging to and fro.
"Julie!" I exclaimed, “Julie!”, my whole mind willing her to
keep the words I saw were struggling to be said from being
voiced in that room. She shook her head impatiently as if shaking
off my appeal, and I knew in despair that the storm that had been
threatening all evening, but which Julie and I had, with some
skill, kept from wrecking the ship of our family feeling, was now
about to break on us and the ship we had tried so valiantly to
keep afloat would founder and sink.
"I came here tonight,” she said, “at a time of good will and love
to show my love for my family—what is left of it. During the past
months nothing you have done has led me to expect any love and
good will in return. But I wanted to give it. Yes, Arnie, and don't
shake your head, I did come in that spirit. But I did not expect,"
and she looked at Art, who was now smiling broadly, "to have
everything I consider sacred, everything holy, trampled on by
by a Communist bastard and a cheap.
•
""
[152]I rose from my chair. "Stop it, Julie," I cried out. "Stop it."
"No," she said, her eyes blazing at me, "I will not.”
"Yes," I insisted. "Go, Julie. And be quiet
•
and go!"
As we stood facing each other crying out these words, under-
neath our emotional staccato exclamations quite clearly and dis-
tinctly I heard Art's voice, easy and mocking and utterly sardonic
singing,
"Silent night, holy night
All is calm, all is bright. . .
"
and both our voices hushed; but Julie's only for an instant, for
she merely glanced at Art and continued: "I thought you were
clean, Ann. I thought your daughter was clean, but both of you
have become filthy, no better than those girls who enticed two
ignorant Negro boys. Do you hear? You are no better than they.
You think, Ann, that people don't know. But everyone knows;
all know that your daughter has become a prostitute, a whore.
People laugh about it. Your daughter is a joke, a town joke,
an object of laughter to every coarse-mouthed, street-corner
hanger-on."
I saw a look of horror come on my child's face. It paled and
then a great flush of red spread over it, and she stood as if
stricken; but also there came, finally, a proud look as if she re-
membered the great cause for which we had begun to fight. I
could feel my cheeks reddening and my heart pumping heavily
as if it could not bear the burden of my sister's words; found
them too heavy, too immense for it to carry. And yet I longed to
find a place where we could come together, to show her why . .
why.
...
"Never mind about Arnie," I said. "Think of yourself. While
you are complacently enjoying your life, we are fighting the ter-
rible scourge that is in Europe, the shocking persecutions, the
tyranny, getting ready to destroy us all."
"Go on-go on and fight the Nazis," she shrieked, and though
I knew from other times that Julie possessed a temper that in her
young days was uncontrollable, I had never heard her, since we
had become older, give way. “But I hope,” and she raised her
hand, "I hope before God they will come to this country and that
[153]they will destroy you and your whore and your Jews. I hope to
God they do."
That was not all; but I cannot go on. Even now my heart be-
gins to beat heavily, as if, though more than twelve years have
passed since that night, it cannot bear the burden of the words
that were said in anger and bitterness and that built a wall, stone
by heavy stone, between us.
"So that's it," Art spoke for the first time. "So that's your good
will and love. You talk about the Jews," he said. "In another
twenty years those Jews you despise so will be ideological rulers
of this country. The Jews, the only progressive people in the
world will have influenced every mind . . . The Jews.
99
""
"Hush, Art," Arnie said. "Hush!" But he did not heed her.
"You talk about filth," he stammered. "We are the only ethical
people in the world. Why the Jews you despise so can drag a Goy
into the gutter and never get even a little finger stained with
filth. Those Jews you despise so. but Arnie put her hand
over his mouth. As she did so he looked at her wonderingly, as if
only at that moment he realized that he had been speaking. It
was the one time in all my knowledge of him that I ever saw Art
lose his poise.
·
We were all standing, looking at each other, panting with the
words that had been spoken-—and with those that had not—
when the doorbell rang.
"Go now, Julie," I said, knowing it must be Sep. “Go now.”
"Yes," she panted, taking her coat from the chair and fling-
ing it over her shoulders, "I shall go, and I will never come
back."
"I know," I whispered, "I know. That will be best. Go-and
never come back." Saying this I urged her toward the door,
though she needed little urging, and as she left us without a back-
ward glance, I closed the door upon her.
Without speaking-for what was there then about which we
could speak-Arnie came to me and kissed me on the forehead,
and in the heavy silence that had settled upon us she and Art
left me alone.
Slowly I began to straighten the room, taking the tray and
glasses and plates to the kitchen, and with a feeling as if doom
[154]had come upon us all, I untied the cord about the Christ Child's
neck, packed the crèche with all its figures in the wicker basket
and set it away in the storeroom.
Later Arnie came to my room and sat down beside me on the
bed, laying her arm across my shoulders. Speaking in a low voice
I carried on the thoughts that had been churning in my mind.
"What did Art mean?" I asked, bewildered, "by saying what
he did? Is he dragging you down? Is that what he meant?"
"Oh, Mama," she said with a little exasperated laugh, "he
didn't mean a thing. All the boys talk like that among themselves.
And it's our fault, because we make them feel inferior, just as
Aunt Julie did tonight. They have to invent something, some-
thing big and exaggerated, so they can feel superior again.”
"But why did he mutilate the créche?" I asked in an agonized
whisper. "What harm was it doing there? It was harming no one."
“Oh yes, Mama," she said. "Oh yes, it was doing harm. Just
by having it there we were preserving in ourselves the bourgeois
decadence. Mama, we've got to clean all that out of ourselves or
we'll never be any good to the Movement. It has got to be cleaned
out once and for all. We should thank Art because he fearlessly
helped us tonight, to get all that superstition out of us. We
should thank him for what he did."
"And for alienating Julie?" I asked.
"Yes, Mama, that more than anything. For it means we have
made a clean break with the past. A clean break," she repeated
as if the words gave her particular satisfaction.
When she had left me I sat in a chair beside my bed, not yet
undressed, thinking, or trying to think-to set my thoughts in
order-trying to be fair to all of us. Was Arnie right and should
I accept the words Art had said lightly as if "they did not mean a
thing?" Yet there was my grandfather's diary. During the Civil
War he had lived in Richmond, a member of the Confederate
Headquarters there. Scattered through his diary from the begin-
ning to the end of the war, on different pages, there was written
down the animosity felt by all toward Judah P. Benjamin be-
cause of his influence over Jefferson Davis. And while I knew
that some of the animosity must have come from prejudice and
jealousy, there was still what had seemed a "legitimate" bitter-
[155]ness because Benjamin had ridiculed General Lee's belief in God,
and had exercised his caustic wit on Stonewall Jackson whose
chief fault, in Benjamin's eyes, was that, before a battle, the gen-
eral knelt down and prayed. Was there, then, something that
cynically and consciously defiled? Then I remembered the un-
forgivable words Julie had spoken to Art and about my child.
Over and over they repeated themselves so that it seemed I had
received wounds in my body that would never heal. I sat there
clenching my hands to keep myself from crying. I sat there until
dawn. And then, when dawn came, I slipped on my coat and
quietly let myself out of the back door, and followed a path (after
leaving the garden) that I had often followed during the three
years we had lived in the little house.
The path led across a field to some woods. In the spring these
woods (thick with hickory trees, oaks, tulip trees and dogwood),
were covered on the ground below with violets, wild iris and
other spring flowers. Now in December the trees were bare and
there were no flowers. Always these woods had comforted me
when I needed to be sustained, as I had especially during my first
year in that home. So, I suppose, it was with the thought of that
comfort, formerly experienced, that I wandered distractedly
across the field and into the woods. But when I had got among
the trees, the sense of desolation, almost as strong as I had felt it
when our mother died, the sense of having made a break, not a
"clean" break as Arnie had said, but a break with jagged wounds
that tortured and that could not be eased, overcame me and I
flung myself on the ground and wept aloud.
Art and Arnie had not yet wakened when I returned to the
house, and I was able to make myself presentable and to go about
the usual preparations for breakfast. But underneath there was
the heaviness of death. Here was an end and I could not see be-
yond it.
Presently Arnie came in and sat down at the table where I
had set three places, one for her, one for Art, and another for
myself. I stood with my head averted, pretending to concentrate
on the eggs I was cooking, for though I had bathed my eyes, I
knew that evidence of the tears I had shed still remained.
[156]"Mama," Arnie said in a practical voice, "what would you
think of the idea of our moving to New York?"
I continued to stir the eggs, amazed at the simplicity of this
solution to all our difficulties.
"Art thinks," Arnie continued, "that my work here is done.
He thinks I can be of much more use in New York."
She took my hand as I served the eggs on her plate and put
it to her cheek. She looked up and smiled at me and I saw hope
in her face, even gaiety and joyful anticipation. As I considered
what she had said a fresh wind seemed to blow in the room, clear-
ing my head, giving my eyes a new and clearer vision. I laid my
hand on her shoulder and smiled down at her. "I think it would
be wonderful," I said. "I think it would be wonderful."
And so one day in January, after we had made the little house
ready for renting and packed all the things we would need in the
new life, Arnie and I rode down to the station and sat there alone
(for Art had gone on a week before), with no one of those who
had formerly been close to us there to say good-by.
But now all that did not concern me. Now I was ready, reso-
lutely turning my thoughts toward the new life. And beside me
sat my child, serious and absorbed, already concentrated on her
purpose, and who, when the train arrived that would take us to
New York, left me, and went to ride, as far as Washington, in
the Jim Crow car.
[157]Chapter XIX
The roses Arnie helped me set out in the garden when she was
in her seventeenth year are having a second blooming, and the
large crepe myrtle bushes, one on each side of the garden gate,
are still a delight; for even in late August their leaves keep the
tender green of spring, and the clusters of delicate transparent
crepe flowers, the color of red cotton blossoms, or, more exactly,
the shade of rose that comes in the heart of a watermelon when
it is ripe, stimulates in me a tingling response that no other
color arouses.
I have bought watermelons (sold at the door from a wagon),
and they have brought to mind summer evenings when our family
--and often young friends of Julie and myself-gathered on the
large back porch at Mars Hill around the watermelon Sep had
brought up; a melon which my father himself had selected early
that morning from the patch and which, with the dew still on it,
he had let down into the old well (for he insisted that a refriger-
ator spoiled the taste), to remain in a bucket with the rim just
above the water all day. In the evening after supper, on a moon-
lit night, my father would stand before the melon that rested
in a large black tin tray on an old table kept there for such pur-
poses, holding a long kitchen knife while all stood about him
waiting, with salt cellars ready, for our slices. But waiting also
for that first moment when, with a flourish, my father plunged
the knife into the frosted green rind, and in a sudden hush we
listened for the sound, like taffeta tearing (as the melon split
down its length), assuring us that the one my father had selected
was just ripe, but not too ripe-was, indeed, at its perfect best.
[158]This morning on the arbor at the foot of the garden I found
some scuppernongs already ripe, and with a pang I remembered
that early morning in September when my fifteen-year-old Arnie
stood on the wobbling supports (which had not then been re-
placed with new ones), and said to me: "The world is so beauti-
ful, Mama." I picked one cluster, gleaming like transparent old
gold, as if the rays of the sun, absorbed during the ripen-
ing period, glowed from within, and brought them into the
house. My first impulse had been to bring them to my child, but
at her closed door I hesitated, overcome by a feeling of inade-
quacy, of frustration, of despair. I longed again for Mum Hattie,
for there had been some quality in her-or perhaps because
two of us were together—that seemed to counteract the dead, un-
creative silence in the room.
As I hesitated outside the door, dreading to open it and meet
the heavy silence that seemed to push me out with live hands,
indignantly I asked, I demanded of Life an answer for pain, for
cruelty, for injustice. Then almost in the same moment (though
it seemed much longer), a strange feeling of humility came in me
as if Life, from whom I was demanding an answer, said: It is I
who ask the questions, not you. I am. What answers do you give
me? And I did not know. I had thought I knew all the an-
swers. Only a year ago I would have said with arrogant certainty:
I am making a new world and new people. I am making a heaven
on earth. That is my answer to Life. Now I am confused and un-
certain.
I opened the door and went to my child, who, as always, sat
with her head bowed over her hands, and laid the cluster of scup-
pernongs in her fingers. She allowed them to lie there and did
not look up. In a sudden rush of hope I said to that bent head,
"Come in the garden, Arnie. The earth is so beautiful." There
was no response.
And the thought came to me (as if it had been placed in my
mind), that in my haste to leave New York after the frightening
interview with Masha, I did not carry out one remaining con-
cern: to visit the church on the steps of which Arnie had been
found that early morning in March. One day I must go back to
[159]New York to that church; to the address Patrolman McGuire
gave to Miss Andrique, who sent it on to me.
But there is a more immediate task I have postponed again and
again: the account of those days and weeks and months in New
York (that accumulated into twelve years), after Arnie and I
had left Bethel. Each time I sit at my desk to begin that account
a barrier seems to be raised, just as when driving at night red
