lights are seen in the distance; and as the car slows down its head-
lights pick out a sign that says "Danger," and I long to go back.
Yet I know that I must face those years and whatever comes to
me about them, to the end-to Ben and to Masha; for in doing
so I may find some light on this dark road.
Perhaps one reason the barrier is there is because, as I endeavor
to face those twelve years, I begin to see that during that whole
period I must have been a constant impediment to my child. And
though in justice to myself I must acknowledge that, so far as I
can know, all the powers of my heart and mind were centered
on making myself an integral part of the future, at the same time
it was I who was censured for bourgeois deviations and lack of
political consciousness. And yet I believe that in accusing me be-
fore the cell Masha was only trying, through me, to express her
unreasoning hatred of Arnie. It is all the more difficult to under-
stand Masha's attitude as I realize more clearly than I did even
then how humble my child was before her. For to Arnie Masha
represented the dedicated worker, the advance guard of the pro-
letariat, to whom Arnie gave a kind of reverence that was closely
akin to worship, because collectively they were the almost holy
instrument to be used by history for making a heaven on earth.
It was to do our part in bringing about that heaven (the "King-
dom of God on earth" about which my husband had spoken),
that we left Bethel in late January, 1938, and arrived next day
at Pennsylvania Station in New York and were met by Art, who
had gone on a week before.
As usual Art was debonair, confident and cheerful; and im-
mediately, as we reached the top of the stairs, coming up from
the train, took Arnie in his arms and kissed her. He put his arm
around me affectionately, and led us out of the station.
I had visited New York before. Several times when we were
[160]very young our father and mother had brought Julie and myself
to the city; I had come with my husband, and we had taken Arnie
when she was thirteen; and again after my divorce Arnie and I
had come with Julie. But that was in another life where we stayed
in a luxurious hotel, rode only in taxis, and were waited upon by
others.
This arrival was like coming to a different and strange city;
so that I hesitated before diving into the unknown, not knowing
whether the water would be warm or freezing, deep enough or
too shallow for my inexperience. But Arnie, by the expression on
her face, by her laughter as she clung to Art, was not thinking
whether the water might be comfortable or uncomfortable,
whether it might be deep or shallow. She stood on the spring-
board poised, expectant and confident.
Art had offered his flat for our use until we could find a home,
while he stayed with a friend. Soon after leaving the station we
received our first introduction to the Lower East Side of the
city and to the ugly square building, near the gas tanks on the
river, where Art had his temporary dwelling. That building, as
well as others around it, has been demolished to make way for
the East River parks, but I shall never forget it, since that cold,
institution-like place was, for two weeks, our first home in New
York.
Inside the building our heels echoed on the cement floors and
the iron stairways until we reached the third floor and entered
Art's flat. In one corner of the large, square room a double
spring with a mattress on it was set on four wooden supports and
made up, in a fashion, with sheets and blankets. Opposite it was
a cheap chest of drawers and near this, behind a sleazy curtain,
a rod with hangers. In another corner was a metal kitchen table
with an electric plate at one end and beside it two plain chairs.
A few dishes, some "silver," and a pan or two were on rough
shelves above the table. A washtub (in which we took our
scanty baths) and a sink completed the furnishings of the place;
and warmth was supplied by an open gas heater. The toilet was
in the hallway, and this was shared with the family in the other
flat on that landing.
As we stood for a short time near the door looking about us I
[161]glanced at Arnie and saw that even her bright and expectant
look had faded, and a depressed, troubled expression had taken
its place. But in the next moment she made me ashamed; for I
had been thinking of the dirt and ugliness as it affected our com-
fort, but Arnie said: "And there are hundreds, thousands, who
live like this, Mama, in sordidness and hopelessness."
The next morning Arnie went off with Art to see the city, but
I could not; for there was something I felt it necessary for me to
do. As soon as they had gone I went out into the neighborhood
and bought ammonia, soap, scouring powder, cloths and mops
for cleaning, as well as a pair of sheets and pillowcases; for those
on Art's bed apparently had not been changed for some time.
Having brought in my paraphernalia I set about scrubbing the
floor, the walls as far as I could reach, the sink and tub in which
I washed the sheets and pillowcases that had been on the bed.
Outside the window I found a clothesline and pinned the sheets
and pillowcases to the line with safety pins, for I had forgotten
to buy clothes pins. Then, with a certain joy and relief I made up
the bed with the linen I had bought. It was all slow work for this
was a cold water flat and water had to be heated in the kettle and
pan on an electric plate that had only two rings. But that night
I slept well, as Arnie did, between the clean sheets, for both of us
had been restless the night before.
During that first week we trudged over that part of the city,
only farther downtown, searching for a place that would not be
too expensive, and that would have steam heat and hot water.
Though I almost abandoned what Arnie called our "basic need"
when, one evening, Art took us to visit a writer who also lived on
the East Side. We went with him joyfully and expectantly, for
we had already read a novel written by the young woman and
understood that it had made a name for her not only in
the Movement but outside in the bourgeois world.
She and her husband lived in a rear house behind a tenement,
a small one-story brick house they had made very attractive and
comfortable. The young woman and her husband were expect-
ing us and soon we were settled before the fireplace in which
burned a large, bright fire of cannel coal, and were served coffee
from a round table, brought up near the fire. I looked about and
[162]saw the cupboards and bookcases (the cupboards filled with
bright china and the shelves crowded with books), and it seemed
to me this was exactly what Arnie and I needed. And when they
showed us about the house that had two bedrooms, and another
room that had been divided into a bath and kitchen, with a gas
heater for hot water, I was convinced that we could ask for noth-
ing better, if a similar place could be found. But Arnie pointed
out that I would not have a man to bring in heavy scuttles of
coal, and she insisted that I must have a place with steam heat, as
we had originally planned.
The young woman writer offered to look for a place for us and
in a few days found, in a rear house, an apartment on the ground
floor which suited us in every way; for in addition to steam heat
and a bath there was a large kitchen-living room and, though
small, two bedrooms, one for Arnie and the other for myself.
Then, for a week or so I went about to second-hand furniture
dealers, from whom I bought an old-fashioned roll-top desk that
filled my need for plenty of space for papers and supplies; a couch
for the living room on which guests could sit, and which could
be made into a bed for anyone remaining with us overnight. I
found a good solid table and some chairs that were not expensive
and two chests of drawers for our bedrooms. The only new pieces
of furniture were the single beds for Arnie and myself, purchased
on the installment plan from a shop on Second Avenue.
Having set all these pieces up in our new home, we were then
ready to bring our more personal belongings, sent by express
from Bethel, and held for us at the station in New York. Then it
was indeed a pleasure to unpack our own china and silver and
the few books I had brought; the bed linen, and the towels and
blankets that only waited until the carpenter (whom the young
woman writer and her husband had recommended to us) had
finished the shelves and cupboards-which I had planned care-
fully so that they were not only useful but added to the homelike
appearance of the rooms.
But making our home was not the only activity that absorbed
our attention during those first weeks. For one day we met Art
at the Center; and with wonder in our eyes and hope and joy in
our hearts, and with a feeling of reverence, we entered that place.
[163]Outwardly, like the tenement in which Art lived, there was
nothing about the building to which we went that could attract.
It was what it meant to us, to the whole world, that gave the di-
lapidated place significance; that made it seem beautiful to us,
and stirred us deeply.
As he had promised, Art was waiting for us at one o'clock in a
small square lobby on the main floor. Immediately he led us to
the elevator which could be heard wheezing and creaking its
way down from the upper floors. The door was flung open with
a clang of steel, and we entered to begin the painful and arduous
ascent to the upper regions. The elevator seemed to be lifted from
one floor to another only by the might of the operator, who ma-
nipulated the old-fashioned lever with so much effort that I
became tense in my desire to help him. The operator was an
elderly man, thin, with an olive complexion and a deeply lined
face. As we were going up Art said cheerily, "How are you, Com-
rade?"
"I'd feel better," the man said, "if I'd got paid for the past
two weeks."
"You and me, both, Comrade," Art said slangily, and laughing.
But I could see he was embarrassed by this airing of "family"
difficulties, and he added sharply, "These are new comrades—
from the South," emphasizing the word "new."
He then introduced us to Tony and said, "Tony's father
fought with Garibaldi in Italy. He has his father's red shirt in a
trunk at home.” We made appropriate sounds and Tony said
nothing, but my curiosity and interest were aroused; and at a
later date Arnie and I went to Tony's home one evening, where
he served us wine, and at our request took the red shirt (though
it was not a shirt, but a red field jacket) from a trunk and proudly
paraded in it before us.
At the information booth on the third floor (the floor that
contained the general offices and, as I afterward learned, the
office of the official representative of the Workers' Fatherland
and generally known, whoever he happened to be—for there
were changes over the years—as "Comrade Brown") we met
Margot Ward for the first time, not knowing then that she would
become a member of our cell.
[164]But it was on another floor that we received an unexpected
and enthusiastic welcome. Here were the offices of the daily news-
paper to which we had been subscribing since November. And
here, in the room belonging to the reporters, we found Joseph
Raymond and Abe Mackan, both of whom had been in our home
so often during the time of the trial in Bethel the summer before.
It was a wonderful reunion. Even Joe's lugubrious countenance
lit up with pleasure at the sight of Arnie. Abe showed us his
original drawings for cartoons he had made for the newspaper-
some of which had already been published and seen by us, others
that were to come. At the same time he made "wisecracks" (and
this word expresses better than any other his brand of humor)
that kept us all laughing.
I have not often seen Arnie so lovely as she was that day. Word
of our visit must have gotten about and presently other people
came in. Soon there was quite a company, most of them gathered
about Arnie and Art, who stood together with Art's arm about
my child's shoulders. Apparently there was no embarrassment or
self-consciousness about Joe and Abe's former relationship with
Arnie, and no indication that it had been; though I searched
their faces to see if they resented in any way Art's possessive atti-
tude toward her.
It had always seemed to me that Joe cared more for Arnie
than he had admitted at the time in Bethel, and perhaps this
was the reason, later on, when he was given the assignment by
the Party to make an exposé of a national magazine that he asked
for Arnie's help in the necessary research, and so started her on
her way to work in Agitprop.
But for me the highest point of our day was the bookshop,
which was reached through a passageway on the main floor of the
same building, but was on another street. Here were riches we
had not dreamed of in books and pamphlets. Here we found the
"Letters from Prison" which Rosa Luxemburg had written to
Sophy, Karl Liebknecht's wife, and her "Letters to Kautsky,”
both of which had been recommended to me by the young
woman writer who had helped us find our apartment; and which
I bought at once, as well as some of Lenin's books and pamphlets.
Arnie was more enthusiastic about the pamphlets, an indication
[165]of her liking for the agitational and propaganda aspects of the
Movement and which eventually led her to the high position she
was given three years later. When we left the bookshop that day
our arms were stacked high with our precious purchases, the
contents of which we devoured as people devour a meal when
they have been starved.
That very evening, as Arnie and Art and I sat around the liv-
ing room after dinner, I began to read Rosa Luxemburg's "Let-
ters from Prison."
"She is so utterly human," I said to Art and Arnie. “Listen:
'I feel more at home in the meadows where the grass is humming
with bees than at one of our party congresses. I can say that to you
[to Sophy Liebknecht] for you will not promptly suspect me of
treason to Socialism. You know that I really hope to die at my
post, in a street fight or in prison.
"And she did," Art said, looking up from Lenin's "What Is
To Be Done?", in which he and Arnie had been absorbed. "She
died in prison-she and Karl Liebknecht—or were murdered by
the Socialists who were jealous of their power and influence."
I laid my book down and made coffee while Art told us about
the death of Rosa Luxemburg and Karl Liebknecht, who were
shot in the prison "while trying to escape."
As we drank the coffee Art looked about the room and for the
first time seemed to see all that had been arranged since we had
moved in. "Why," he said in surprise, "this is like a home. We
could have the cell meetings here. Why not?" For the cell he had
organized in that part of the city had been meeting at his flat.
It would be difficult to say how much pleasure his suggestion
gave me. When I was a child (I think twelve or thirteen), my
father bought a barrel of apples and had it set in the "coolroom"
in the basement-for we had no cellar. One day, without consult-
ing anyone, I filled my school bag with apples from this barrel
and at recess distributed them to the other children. Julie was
ashamed of what I had done and convinced me that the other
children had ridiculed me at the same time that they accepted
the apples with enthusiasm; and for a long time I felt that pain-
ful stab in the mind that comes from remembering an act which,
however sincere, appears foolish to others, and that remains in
[166]the mind of the "actor" long after it has been entirely forgotten
by others. But later I became convinced that my wish to share the
apples represented a good, human and universal instinct-the
instinct of hospitality. And when Art made the suggestion that
the cell meet in our home I was grateful, because in that large
city, in an aspect of it that was so strange to me, again I could,
so to speak, "share my apples."
So, from that time on, each Monday evening I became accus-
tomed to hearing voices in the courtyard between the front tene-
ment and our own; then the knock at our door that meant Rose,
Bertha, Masha, Bob, Margot and many others were arriving. For
the cell grew and flourished.
[167]Chapter XX
Soon, however, I was "sharing my apples" not only on Monday
evenings when the cell met in our home, but on other evenings
as well.
During our first years in New York, that is, in 1938 and 1939,
Art was not always in the city, for he was often sent on special
missions over the country. And though Arnie was busy during
the day with various kinds of work at the Center (filling enve-
lopes, running errands), or spending weary hours walking up and
down, picketing in a strike—for she was ready and eager to take
on the most menial "Jimmie Higgins" tasks for the cause—when
Art was in the city he almost always came to our home for dinner,
and Arnie stayed with him in his flat at night.
Abe and Joe also accepted our hospitality and, as had hap-
pened in Bethel, gradually our home became the center for a
group of "intellectuals." These included class conscious mem-
bers of unions, who enjoyed discussing-with a freedom they
naturally did not have in a cell or fraction or at Party headquar-
ters-national and international questions, causing endless argu-
ments that sometimes carried us into the early hours of the morn-
ing. For, unless they had come for dinner, on an "off" evening,
most of them arrived at ten or even after eleven o'clock, when
they had done with their work.
There was a romantic atmosphere about those gatherings; for
the people who came made our home a place for music and love
as well as for discussion. I believe it was this very atmosphere
which, in those later crucial years, brought Arnie and Ben to-
gether.
[168]Only one aspect of those sessions was objectionable; and, ex-
cept for one astonishing interlude, it was always the same. Right
from the beginning Art and Joe and Abe, with enthusiasm and
confidence, told the others, as well as Arnie and myself, exactly
what was wrong with the South and Southern white people and
what should be done about them. And they continued (as if we
were not pesent) to ridicule our customs, so that we became al-
most ashamed to acknowledge our origin. It was not that Arnie
and I felt that these comrades who spoke so knowingly of our
part of the country were incorrect ideologically. We were far too
humble to be so presumptuous. It was that we resented their con-
ceited assumption of their own omniscience and of our ignorance
about the very place in which we had, up to the time we came to
New York, spent our lives.
A German comrade, short, sturdy and opinionated, who came
during our third year (he had left his country secretly and would
return in the same way), was most overbearing in his opinion of
Southern whites and of the lack of initiative the Party had
shown in arousing the Negro population. Soon after France was
liberated from the Nazi occupation there came a French com-
rade. With an unfortunate predilection that had developed in
me for seeing a wry humor in a situation, I called him Comrade
Agénoux. For this little man, whom I truly revered (since he
had, after all, fought bravely with the Maquis and had influ-
enced many of them toward becoming members of the Party in
that country), would sit in our living room and bring his fist
hard upon the table so that our coffee cups rattled; and some-
times speaking of groups in Europe, but more often of the South-
ern whites, he would say, "We must bring them à génoux! à
génoux! to their knees! to their knees!"
But the person who disturbed me most on the question of the
South was Endahl, a comrade from one of the Scandinavian
countries; only this occurred some years later. More than any of
the others had done he sneered at everything about this country
and especially the Southern whites. He insisted that the Consti-
tution was outmoded and boasted that he was in touch with the
Supreme Court and was already influencing its members in
their decisions. And sometimes in moments of retrospect since
[169]Arnie's illness, I have wondered if my resentment toward En-
dahl for his opinions of this country was not in some degree also
a resentment toward him because during the whole period when
he was coming to our home he was promoting (whether from
principle or simply a desire to make mischief I do not know), the
affair between Arnie and Ben. There seemed some bitter core
within him that expressed itself in a love of mischief and even
obscenity, as when he circulated a book of short essays and
verses, written by an assistant professor at Harvard and pub-
lished by one of the pornographic firms, that ridiculed and
turned obscene every statement made by the young English
princess who was to be her father's successor. I could not see that
this book in any way advanced our cause. Nor could I see how
we were helped when Endahl promoted the defense of the por-
nographic publishing company (owned by a wealthy couple
whose money had been made through pornography and whose
daughter was a member of the Party), and which had been
charged by the Postal authorities with sending obscene matter
through the mails. With the support of the American Associa-
tion for the Preservation of Civil Liberties the case was taken to
the Supreme Court. And it did seem that Endahl was correct
when he boasted of his influence there, for on the basis of free-
dom of the press the Supreme Court made a decision that the
pornographic magazines and their contents and advertisements
must be allowed to go through the mails, though a judge of a
lower court had stated, "We must not confuse liberty with
license."
I blamed Endahl for getting me into trouble; though at the
same time I recognized the fact that my disgrace was the result
of
my own lack of control. I believe it was not so much what he
said as the superior manner in which he criticized the South and
the Constitution of this country that made me angry enough to
defend both the Southern whites and the Constitution, for which
"bourgeois deviation," when Masha heard about it, I was cen-
sured before the whole cell at our next meeting.
It was especially difficult for me to accept a wholesale condem-
nation of the whites of our part of the country, for my father,
whatever his faults, was a man of integrity; and my mother,
[170]though she possessed certain of the limitations of her generation,
was a woman of tolerance in the best sense of that often misused
word.
I have never been able to become interested in the writings of
Voltaire. Although he was supposed to represent in himself the
very spirit of tolerance, I felt this was not so, and that Voltaire
was enamored of Voltaire and of a certain excellence he had
learned in the manipulation of words; while my mother's toler-
ance was not an intellectual exercise, but spilled over from some
pure crystal spring within her. And just as a spring that has been
contaminated can spill only impure water, so from her pure
spring there welled up purity of motive that refreshed and invig-
orated. And while she was impelled to allow another to be "him-
self" or "herself," nevertheless through the pool of clear water
could be seen and felt the rock that formed its basin, the rock of
her principles and standards, which she did not compromise or
abandon (a cheap form of "tolerance"), but rather to which,
without spite or malice, she steadily held or lifted others.
My father, a lawyer, had more interest in the processes of law
and government. But he and my mother agreed in their trust in
God. And when I was a child in my teens, on a day my father re-
turned from attending a Circumcision (for he was sometimes
invited to these ceremonies by our Jewish citizens), and I had
expressed some thoughtless criticism, my mother became stern
and indignant (something that rarely happened), and ordered
me not to speak so of "God's Ancient People,” who, she said,
were part of us, and we of them.
On another occasion, after hearing some criticism of Abra-
ham Lincoln at a friend's home, as soon as I returned to Mars
Hill I went to a bookcase in my father's study where I had seen
on a shelf some books concerning that President. I was about to
remove them when my mother came into the room. She asked
me what I intended to do with the books. "Throw them out, I
suppose," I answered uneasily. Taking the books from me she
firmly placed them on the shelves again. She then explained that
my father was planning to write a life of Lincoln-to prove
that if he had lived Reconstruction could never have been. And
she pointed out to me a passage in my grandfather's diary
[171](which I have verified since), where my grandfather stated that
early in the year 1865, after Lincoln's return to Washington from
the Hampton Roads Peace Conference (held with representa-
tives of the Confederate States), he had proposed to his cabinet
that on the Confederate States surrendering to the North the
cabinet should decide upon compensated emancipation, pardons
for all "political offenders," and the return of all confiscated
property-but the cabinet rejected his proposals. "Only Lincoln's
proposals," my mother said, "were five years too late."
Knowing all this, and more of good about my father and
mother and numbers like them, it was difficult for me to keep si-
lent when Endahl spoke of them as if they were completely evil,
and of the Constitution as a piece of paper which, in Endahl's
words, should be "scrapped."
The resulting censure before the cell came as a surprise to me,
for everyone had always felt free, during the evenings in our
home, to say whatever they wished without any fear of having it
reported to authority. It was the first time I had ever expressed
any disagreement with others' opinions on the subject except in
private to Arnie. Even with Art and Joe and Abe I had been si-
lent except once, during that interlude when they made their
extraordinary about-face.
It was in the fall of 1939 when I began to perceive a change in
their attitude toward the Southern whites. "Began to perceive"?
No. The change came suddenly one evening during an informal
political discussion in our living room when, to my astonish-
ment, Art said: "Reconstruction in the South should never have
been allowed to happen."
I expected others to disagree violently with his unusual state-
ment, and when they did not, but seemed to agree, I looked at
them all, but especially at Art, at Joe, at Abe, wondering if by
some magic they had suddenly become different persons. And I
could only gaze at them in dumb amazement. Art must have seen
the expression on my face for he went on to explain his “posi-
tion."
In an almost impassioned manner he said he now understood
the attitude of the Southern white people toward Reconstruc-
tion, because there was an exact parallel in the situation of the
[172]German people after the First World War. In both cases, he said,
armies of occupation had taken over the land and war indemni-
ties (which made them poor and prevented them from becoming
industrialized) had been imposed upon them.
"Yes," I agreed. Finding my voice at last, I explained what my
father had often told us (and what I had feared to say before),
that our state was still, so far as I knew, paying thousands of
dollars each year to the federal government for "war indemni-
ties."
"More than seventy years after Lee's surrender at Appoma-
tox," Art added in an angry voice, as if he had become one of
those Southerners whom before he had held up to ridicule, con-
tempt, and condemnation. By this time Art had left off using the
word "categorical," but this was in his tone when he added:
"Can we blame Hitler?”
This question or tacit statement—was an offense to me, and
I said, “I do. And when you come down to facts those who pro-
moted Reconstruction were like the Nazis for they wished to
superimpose one race upon another and give it power over the
others just as Hitler does."
But if I expected their attitude about the South to continue
and had thought it a sincere search for truth I became disillu-
sioned about it when, in June 1941 (at the time of Hitler's inva-
sion of Russia), their attitude changed overnight, and they re-
turned to their former opinions about the Southern whites and
about Reconstruction.
Just as suddenly our slogans and directives changed and I said
to Arnie laughingly (but almost weeping, so distressed I felt),
that my confusion could be compared with the condition of the
pig whose pictures we had seen in an illustrated weekly. The
animal had been given sudden and inexplicable changes in the
stimuli that prepared it for the arrival of its food, and as a con-
sequence had became a psychopath.
Arnie could not help laughing with me, for she had a sense
of humor. At the same time she recognized the fact that though
I was laughing I was also genuinely distressed over the sudden
changes that had come, and for the first time she explained to me
the theory and practice of the tactic. She asked me if I remem-
[173]bered what Lenin had written about propaganda and agitation.
I said I had read the pamphlet but had forgotten the details.
She then reminded me that Lenin had said propaganda is the
overall explanation or exposition of principles of basic truth;
while agitation is for the moment, to rouse people to action on
some specific and immediate question.
Just so, she explained, on the whole political (national and
international) front the class war (the basic truth) is carried on
unceasingly. But there is also (like agitation on the economic
front) the immediate political tactic that is for the moment only.
It is not necessarily concerned with truth, and should have no
meaning to us except as a move to promote the great truth for
which we are fighting; the protection of the Workers' Fatherland
and the socialization of the whole earth.
"You remember," she said, "in 1940, before the world we for-
mally severed relations with the Comintern. But we know this
was never done and that statement was merely a clever tactic,
necessary at a given moment in history to mislead the bour-
geoisie, but never to be taken seriously by ourselves."
"But," I protested, "it was Lenin who said: 'Because we have
suffered we will understand how to make a world in which all
will have the good things of life.' And though I know that 'bread'
and all it connotes is the most important, good things of life
also include truth and honor.
...
""
“Oh, no, Mama," Arnie interrupted. "Don't you remember
the Manifesto: 'But Communism abolishes eternal truths, it
abolishes all religion, and all morality. . . .' Don't you under-
stand, Mama? Lenin meant all will have the good things of life
after the world has been made to rise on new foundations. Then
we will have a race of human beings so perfect in their under-
standing and sense of justice that there will be no class distinc-
tions, no race distinctions, no distinctions of wealth, and no one
having power over another. So the state will wither away."
"But in the meantime.
I began.
"Yes, in the meantime," Arnie said hurriedly, “when the time
comes that we take power in this country we must be willing to
see people we've known put in prison. We must be willing in our
minds and with resolution in our hearts to sit as people's judges
[174]and condemn even members of our own family to death if that is
necessary, though it may not be. If we aren't ready for that, then
we are not worthy of the trust that has been given us. We are
merely sentimental weaklings."
Then I understood how ignorant, how visionary, I had been.
Up to that time I had looked only to the end for which we were
fighting. And though I had read Lenin's pamphlets, citing the
means that must be used to bring about a revolution, and knew
from history that he had used the "Terror," all this had no con-
nection at all in my mind with this country or with the people I
knew, nor had I applied them to myself, as one who must use
those means; but I had looked only to the glorious future of the
world, when all would be happy, and all would have the good
things of life.
But as Arnie spoke so bravely about those means, facing them
so honestly, I knew that I must take my stand so that it would
be impossible for any other consideration, any "sentimental
weakness," to corrupt the pure revolutionary purpose that must
possess us all.
[175]Chapter XXI
And yet, even now, the face of Sol Auer, our laundryman, comes
before me. I wonder if it is true (as I was told in the union), that
he died. He never came back to us after that morning when I
smiled so coldly at his distress. But it may be he was transferred
to some other district.
Sol was a young middle age, with a short, slim, wiry and ener-
getic body. Every week he appeared proudly at our home with
a package of freshly washed and ironed laundry, and left with a
bundle of soiled pieces (the accumulation of the week before),
that I had ready for him. I looked forward to his weekly visits,
for he was so cheerfully alive. Even his knock at the door had a
quality of cheerfulness about it, as if he considered himself a
bringer of good tidings (the fresh, clean clothes done up in
brown paper), and the knock was a triumphant heralding of his
approach. As he entered the apartment after I had said “come
in," his eyes, that were dark brown, twinkled with good will; and
as he jauntily laid the fresh package of laundry on the table, he
would say in a voice full of innocent pride, "Well, here we are
again!"
After several abortive attempts to "convert" him I gave up
trying, for I could see he was too backward, too religious, for me
to be able to touch him with reason. But I became very fond of
that man.
He had a wife, a son and a daughter, and through his reports
about them I learned to know them all. His daughter, the elder
of his children, was nineteen at that time, and each week I was
brought up to date on the progress of her romance with a young
[176]man who had studied accountancy and was beginning his career
in the office of a CPA.
As the time approached for the wedding Sol grew more and
more excited over the preparations, and the week after the date
for that important event I looked forward to hearing his lively
knock on the door, and his cheerful "Well, here we are again!"
For I knew that he would give me the details; and I was not dis-
appointed.
"You understand," he said to me that morning, "this is our
only daughter. She must have a wedding to remember. My fa-
ther, Olav Hasholem, may he rest in peace, started an insurance
for these children at their birth. We add pennies every week, not
much, but enough. Now for our daughter's wedding it comes to
something for her. So we say, give her a wedding, a caterer, a
hall, a band-everything.
"But by 'everything' Poppa did not mean a wedding dress. My
daughter can sew, you understand. Her Momma can sew. But
what do you think? They must have a bought wedding dress.
Poppa says: Do you think I am Rockefeller? Sew a wedding dress
on the good machine Poppa bought you. Your Momma had no
store wedding dress and just as much married, and happy-more
than twenty years and not a cross word between us. Not a cross
word? You're dreaming, Poppa, my daughter says. For that im-
pudence I tell her, maybe we just have a rabbi in the parlor.
"Well it comes to compromises: Poppa makes a compromise.
On my day off we will go to Grand Street and rent a wedding
dress. So-o-o-o, we go to Grand Street. Such lovely satins and
laces! But no, this one is too so and so, that one too this and that.
Then, like a little crooning dove Momma says: There is a cer-
tain place, Poppa. So Poppa, dying on his feet, goes-UP-
TOWN. So my daughter has a wedding dress-paid for. No
rental. . . . But to see her! Fifty people crying 'How beautiful!'
Dark eyes, glowing, happy under the canopy. Dark hair under
the veil. My daughter. . .
At that time our fractions in all the unions had been directed
to agitate for the closed shop. We were the first, so I believe, to
agitate on this question, for it was reasoned by those above us
that if every worker was forced to join a union it would be
[177]simpler (by taking over a union whose members were disciplined
to obey), to conquer when our time should come.
During the very period when Sol Auer was planning his
daughter's wedding there were calls for volunteers at the cell
meeting who would take part in a strike for the closed shop in
the laundries; and in response to the call I was picketing one of
them, though not the one where Sol worked.
Through such activities I learned much about conditions, and
though I realized it was impossible to make him class conscious,
I did my best to point out all that should be changed in his place
that would be changed if the union had a closed shop. But he
would not listen.
About three weeks after his daughter's wedding and on the
morning of the day when the laundry was expected, I heard his
footsteps in the courtyard. But they were not quick and eager as
they had always been, and his hesitating knock at my door did
not herald the approach of a bringer of good tidings.
I said "come in" in answer to his knock, and he entered slowly,
reluctantly, and as he set the package of laundry on the table he
said, "Well, here we are again." I could see that he was endeav-
oring to maintain his former jaunty voice and attitude, and to
an extent he succeeded. But to me how despondently were the
words said, how without life they were spoken.
"What is wrong?" I asked, for his right arm was in a cast and
his head swathed in bandages. Only his mouth, one brown eye,
and the tip of his nose were visible.
"What has happened to you?” I repeated when he did not an-
swer, although I suspected the truth. For I knew about the
"strong-arm squads" hired by the unions, except in those unions
where the squads of "gorillas" were actual members.
"Oh-o-o-o," he said, as if surprised I had noticed anything un-
usual about him. “You mean this?" and he touched the bandages
about his head. “Nothing," he said. “Just an accident.”
"What kind of accident?" I persisted, for I wished to know
whether his opinions had been changed by such an experience.
He waited so long I thought he would not go on. All the while
he looked down in a shamefaced way at his feet. Then in a low
[178]voice he began to speak. "Why should I hide it?" he said. "It is
all over. What of it? What can I say? Yes, I have joined the
union. And maybe this is best for all, everywhere, as you told
me. Maybe this closed shop," he stopped for a long second and
then said almost savagely, "this closed shop that closed on me
... is the best. Only. . . ." He paused, still gazing at the floor,
and I thought he had done. But he continued hurriedly, as if
forced to speak and yet wishing to be finished. "I was almost
home . . . coming from work at night, you understand, think-
ing of dinner, the radio, Momma, when from behind a corner
men came and dragged me into the alley. 'You going to join the
union?' they say. 'You going to join the union?' And well, you
understand .. my arm broken, blood running into my eyes
from the head, I say yes. So I have my union card; I am a union
man." He lifted his head and I saw a single tear, followed by an-
other, leave the eye that was visible and roll down to be absorbed
by the bandage on his cheek.
Saying sternly to myself: "Think of the tears of the oppressed
masses," I smiled coldly at him. And hardening my heart (know-
ing this was my opportunity for proving to Arnie, to myself, as
well as to others, that I was cured of "sentimental weakness" and
"bourgeois deviations”), I said nothing.
He must have felt the lack of warmth with which I viewed
him, for before my eyes he seemed to shrink within the bandages
and become smaller. Then with his left hand (the one that
was not broken), he lifted the bundle of soiled laundry I had
ready for him and silently made his way toward the door. At the
door he paused, and without turning to look at me and in a puz-
zled voice as if he did not understand, but which sounded to me
as if it contained a reproach, he said: "I came here a boy, a young
boy, but when I get to age twenty-one nobody breaks my head
to make me a citizen of this country. I choose it. I choose it.
Because I believe. . . ." And then he was gone.
As the door closed behind him a curious, unexpected and flam-
ing anger against him came in me. I even followed him to the
door to call him back, so that I might express my anger
in some
telling words that had come to my mind. But when I flung open
[179]the door I saw that he had already crossed the courtyard and
was passing through the hallway of the tenement. I did not see
him again.
For a long time, for years, I had a self-righteous and self-com-
placent feeling whenever he came to mind. I congratulated my-
self on not giving way to weakness and was able to speak of
him (and others like him who had opposed the closed shop) hu-
morously, as if his experience with the strong-arm squad had
been a great joke. And as the closed shop became more and more
a part of the life of this country (an institution that, as one of
our leaders said, made ours a “government of the unions, by the
unions, and for the unions"), I felt a pride that I had taken part
in the beginnings of such a significant victory for freedom. Now
I wonder.
[180]Chapter XXII
Wherever I go, whatever I do, a single sentence spoken by Sol
Auer repeats itself in my mind. "Because I believe," he said. I
ask myself: Does that sentence haunt me because, miserably, I
must acknowledge to myself that I now have no belief-no hope
-no purpose?
But there is a purpose for my life-my child's restoration to
health. Soon she will be thirty-one. But that is young, young!
She has years of life before her. Yet as winter comes on and I
cannot work in the garden I become impatient with the weary
hours of drudgery, and diminishing hope, the gradual realiza-
tion of my loss of belief in anything except the resolution that
I will never abandon the struggle to bring her back.
And I search my heart and mind to discover if, by my seem-
ing indifference (which was not indifference at all, but a desire
not to interfere in her life and with her convictions), I neg-
lected to state my apprehensions more positively when Arnie
announced to me one evening that she was planning to live with
Ben in the Village, where Endahl also had an apartment.
Ben and Endahl had met at Harvard where the latter had
been an exchange professor while Ben was taking his Master's
degree. Ben was already in the process of breaking up his
"friendship" with Bertha Mason and was centering his attention
on Arnie, when, one evening, he brought Endahl to our home. I
believe it was that very evening that Arnie began to look on Ben
with particular interest. For I saw her eyes go to him, as he stood
in the doorway with Endahl, in surprise and delight. I was, my-
self (along with all the others), impressed by the appearance of
[181]those two as they stood together. They appeared to have the same
number of years. Endahl was a great handsome “Nordic,” blond
and blue-eyed, and Ben (almost as black as night), was just as
handsome a physical specimen as his friend.
Yet, as weeks went on and they came again and again when
Arnie was at home, I wondered about them. For it seemed to me
that there was little to recommend either of them except the fact
of extreme physical attraction. For their thinking, their intellect
(and they seemed to be of one mind), appeared to be quite shal-
low; and though both of them were more highly educated than
Art or Joe or Abe, no more than Art or Joe or Abe did they
seem concerned with the search for truth with which I had al-
ways connected education. According to the dictionary, educa-
tion means to "lead forth," and Miss Hale (my teacher at St.
Ann's) added to this her own interpretation: that the purpose of
education is not only discipline and knowledge, it is to lead the
spirit toward the search for truth, and that the truly educated
will never be satisfied with shallow or baseless conclusions.
All this was in my mind as I questioned the wisdom of the ar-
rangement Arnie and Ben were making. For when my child an-
swered, "I must live what I believe, Mama. That is my rock.
Don't take that from me," suddenly there came in me a forebod-
ing of disaster, so that I felt I must go on-not in disapproval,
but only to make her realize the meaning of the step she was so
blithely taking.
"Are you certain of your love for Ben?" I asked.
"What do you mean, Mama?" she demanded; and in that
question, with its overtones of irritation, of criticism, I seemed to
feel (although it might have been imagination), the influence
of Endahl, for she and Ben and Endahl were together most of
the time when Arnie was free.
So I hesitated, almost stammering, as I tried to explain that
the deepest respect and confidence and love would be needed
between them.
"Respect!" my child exclaimed in the same irritated voice.
"Why shouldn't I respect Ben? I only graduated from high
school, and he has his Master's degree. He knows much, much
more than I ever will."
[182]"Yes," I agreed. "I understand all that. But," I argued, "I am
thinking of the way he has treated Bertha, as if, after their close
friendship, she means nothing to him."
But Arnie insisted that these relationships were not to be taken
with the sense of responsibility and duty that I seemed to asso-
ciate with them. Ben owed nothing to Bertha, she said, any more
than she herself owed any responsibility to the comrade with
whom she had terminated her relationship several weeks before
that time.
"But even Lenin," I said, "remained with Krupskaya all his
life."
“And any number of people," Arnie retorted, "have told me
Lenin had other relationships frankly and openly—and isn't that
more honest than the bourgeois way of hypocrisy?” And she re-
peated the quotation from Lenin that sex is no more than drink-
ing a glass of water.
"And is that all it is to you?" I asked.
We were sitting in my bedroom in the apartment. Arnie had
just arrived from Washington where she had met someone from
the government who was cooperating on certain questions with
us. Usually she shared the purposes and events of such journeys
with me, but that evening she was too concerned with breaking
the news of her projected move to do so.
She had pulled up the solid square stool to my side-the same
stool she had sat on that memorable evening when we stayed up
all night in Bethel, and that she had used from the time she was
a child when we were talking together. As I asked that question
I saw a flush come up in her cheeks. For a time she said noth-
ing, and I asked myself fearfully: Have I said too much? Will I
lose her confidence? But to my relief she began speaking, her
head bent thoughtfully above her knees which were held
clasped in her hands.
"I'll tell you this, Mama," she said. “Ben and I are more con-
genial in that way-in that way," she repeated, "than I ever
thought possible." She hesitated as if trying to find words in
which to express what she was trying to say. Then she gave a
little excited laugh and shook her head. "But it's much more
than taking a glass of water, Mama. I have never . . ." and then
[183]she checked herself as if already she had confided too much of
something so personal, so belonging to herself and to Ben, that
it could not be my affair; and in this she was right.
"To him it is like a religion. You can see his whole attitude,"
she continued, now looking up into my face, "in the book he is
writing."
For Arnie had given me the first draft of Ben's manuscript to
read, and I had admired the scholarliness with which he had
presented the subject, a development of his Master's Thesis
which had been suggested to him by one of his professors at Har-
vard. What Arnie meant by his "attitude" was the theme of his
book-that all religions since man first began to worship have
been based on sex.
With apparent scientific detachment and objectivity and many
quotations he developed his contentions. And though I was
somewhat disturbed because he made such statements as "It is
accepted by many scholars," or "There is reason to believe," fol-
lowed by conclusions it was difficult for my reason to accept, I
had to acknowledge that on the whole the book was convinc-
ing. In this manner (but not to my complete satisfaction) he
"proved" that the Israelites in the Old Testament had worshiped
the bull (because the bull represented sex and fertility), and that
Jehovah was only another name for that animal.
I was disturbed by the effect all of this seemed to be having
on Arnie, and her absorption in Ben, the depth of which I was
able to note because such a thing had never happened to her be-
fore. As the months went on, while she lived with Ben and was
with Endahl, I seemed to see in her a change, a hardening of
thought, a resistance to openness toward beauty, toward truth,
toward compassion, which she had possessed from childhood.
And gradually (as we observe an old person whose arteries are
hardening slowly day by day, and watch with apprehension the
process of deterioration, of inability to connect what is at that
moment happening in his life with anything that has gone be-
fore), I felt in her an increasing loss of resiliency, of those inner
resources that had always before reassured me and given me the
knowledge that within her there was room and a happy and en-
thusiastic desire, a reaching out, for growth.
[184]Her absorption in Ben was definite and his attraction power-
ful, and it was this that produced in me a self-consciousness about
her relationship with him, so that I was careful about expressing
an opinion concerning his manuscript; and I did not say either
to Ben or to Arnie that parts of it made me uneasy, but spoke of
those things only that were excellent and about which I could
be sincerely enthusiastic.
That it was a book many people would read I was certain, for
though on the whole scientifically and sociologically accurate (so
far as I knew), it was also written in a popular style, and portions
were devoted to detailed descriptions of fertility rites and cere-
monies.
But as Arnie said, even if it did not become popular never-
theless its meaning, its purpose, its "lesson" or "moral" to use
outdated terms, would inevitably filter down through the intel-
lectuals who read it (professors and teachers and ministers) to
those below, to the masses of people, and would raise in all minds
doubts of what had been taught them about God.
Arnie had given me Lenin's writings on the subject of agita-
tion against religion in bourgeois countries, and felt that those
who preached atheism were actually doing harm. He advocated
the indirect or propaganda approach. "The Marxist" (he wrote)
"must be a materialist—that is, one who takes up the fight
against religion on the basis of the class struggle. The dialectical
materialism of Marx and Engels. applies the materialistic
philosophy to all things-to religion, to history, to the social
sciences. We must fight religion. That is the A B C of all material-
ism, consequently also of Marxism. But Marxism goes further. It
says: We must know how to explain on materialistic lines the ori-
gin of faith and religion.
•
•
And this, I felt (explaining on materialistic lines the origin
of faith and religion), was exactly what Ben had so successfully
done.
I was fond of Ben; at the same time there was always in my
mind the fear that with him my child had reached a place where
she was no more mistress of her own thoughts or desires; and
that she could be hurt if she should suddenly and unexpectedly
[185]be deprived of that upon which she seemed more and more to
depend-sexual pleasure and gratification.
I believe it was this very fear, unacknowledged at the time,
that had brought on the dreams, the feeling of confusion and dis-
trust of myself, the "illness" which had made Arnie insist that I
return to Bethel.
And one evening, when both of them had come over in a taxi,
bringing some of their paraphernalia (for they were to live in
the apartment while I was away), and I was in my bedroom pack-
ing for that return to Bethel, I heard voices raised and put my
head through the door. By the distressed look on Arnie's face,
and on Ben's an expression of sullen anger, I knew at once that it
was no ordinary discussion they were having.
Up to that time, after our initial talk two years before about
Arnie's relationship with Ben, I had never interfered in any way
with them, or discussed anything concerning Ben with Arnie. But
that evening I was nervous, excited, and yet, in a manner, relaxed
knowing that soon I would be leaving New York and it would be
months before I would be with them again.
Not realizing that simply by being present I would help to
bring to the surface other and more significant facets of their
relationship, I went farther into the living room where they were
sitting on the couch and stood tentatively beside the table, not
wholly committed to the discussion, and still feeling that at any
moment I could disassociate myself from it and retreat to my
room.
"It's because you're afraid," Ben said to Arnie, and though he
spoke the words lightly, still the sullen glance he gave her showed
me what he was really feeling.
"What is the trouble?" I asked impulsively.
"Oh," Arnie said with a shrug, "Ben wants to go to the Wal-
dorf for dinner." She turned to Ben. "You know I'm not afraid,"
she protested. "You know it is because I gave all that up long
ago. Why should I begin it now? You and I are dedicated to
something else. We have no time to live a bourgeois life."
As she spoke I could see that Ben was becoming more and more
impatient with her. And then a strange thing happened. He
[186]leaned toward her and with a short, quick, contemptuous gesture,
and reaching out his tongue, he licked her cheek. Then he rose
from the couch, and lighting a cigarette, walked across the room
and stood against the wall by the window, looking down on
her.
An amazed, hurt, shamed look came on my child's face. She
put her hand to her cheek, against the spot his tongue had
touched, and turning her head away from us, hid her burning
face with her hand.
"Let's get this settled," Ben said to her. "Why are you here?"
...
She turned slowly, and still with that shamed look on her face,
and with what I felt was a lack of confidence, of sureness, "You
know why," she said. “To make a better world. Not just for you
and me. For all people . . . a fairer and juster world for all hu-
manity. I know," she said, "you and Endahl think it sounds like
affectation. But it is true. I left my world with its ease and all it
meant so I could live what I believe what I am living; to make
a new world—to give hope to the hopeless and freedom to the
oppressed."
"And yet," Ben said, giving me a smiling glance, as if he took
me into his confidence, “that world you left is exactly what we
are fighting for. I and millions of others want what you have, the
manners and the silver and the polish. And we're going to get it
-ease and power and the good things of life."
"Then why didn't you get them long ago?" I demanded heat-
edly, going farther into the room; not realizing at all that by do-
ing so, by entering the room and taking part in the argument, I
had cut myself off from any retreat.
He laughed. "That is exactly what I am thinking,” he said and
laughed again.
I believe it was his cynical laughter coupled with his treatment
of my child that made me say an impulsive thing, a thing I had
never consciously thought and that astonished me. "Because," I
went on, "so far as I know your people have been on this earth
just as long as my people have been. We have built our culture,
the culture you so greedily covet. Where is yours?”
What followed has remained in me as a reproach. And though
[187]I spoke in anger and in distress because my child seemed inca-
pable of speaking in her own defense, I know now that all I said
must have helped give him and others a weapon against her.
Without waiting for him to answer I went on. "You talk like
Endahl," I said indignantly. “I am ashamed of you. We left bour-
geois materialism to make a world where respect for human per-
sonality and human dignity would be a primary consideration.
But bourgeois materialism is more to be respected than yours.
That, at least, is spontaneous, even innocent, because not delib-
erately and consciously cynical. Every moment of the time we
have spent with you, Ben, we have given you the respect in full
measure and running over that should be the basis of the new
world we are trying to make. But I have an idea that in giving
respect to those who do not deserve it we have lost our own self-
respect. If what you state is the philosophy of Materialism-then
I want none of it. I am sick of your cynicism, and Endahl's cyni-
cism. I am sick of it."
"Mama!" Arnie exclaimed.
"Don't listen to her,” Ben said, glaring angrily at me. “And be-
sides," he added, and speaking directly to Arnie, "fundamen-
tally you are in agreement with your mother. You," he said sar-
castically, "like to say you are living what you believe. That is
your 'rock.' Far from it. It is I who live and think and believe the
philosophy of Materialism. I who am the true Socialist. Who
wants respect if we have the power? All you people," he said with
a wave of his arm and including Arnie and myself with the oth-
ers, with "all you people"-"feel very self-complacent as you con-
descend to help us—to give us 'respect.' You think you'll always
keep your place as topdog. You were never more mistaken. We
won't be satisfied with the crumbs. We want it all. And that is
why," and in a swift, graceful movement he covered the space
between himself and Arnie, and sitting beside her on the couch,
put his arms triumphantly about her, "that is why you'd better
listen to me, yes, and to Endahl-not to that woman you call
'Mama.'"
Arnie pushed herself from his arms and stared at him. "What
is the matter with you?" she asked painfully. "What has Mama
done to you?"
[188]"She has respected me," he said laughing. "I don't want re-
spect. I want you and what you can give me. Materialism-Marx-
ism is the philosophy of things—of use. If you are not useful you
do not belong in the new world we are building. I want you to
acknowledge that. You are very useful to me—in many ways, just
as I am useful to you-in many ways." He reached toward her
and, laughing so that his teeth gleamed against his dark skin in
the light of the shaded lamp beside that end of the couch, he
lightly brushed the palm of his hand over her breasts. "No one
forced you to take me on. No one forced you to take on the phi-
losophy of materialism. You chose it. Now live it."
"But I do. I do," she pleaded.
"Oh b-
s!" he exclaimed. "I'm going. I'm fin-
ished." And he reached for his topcoat that was flung over the
back of the couch behind her.
“No, Ben," she said, clutching his arm. “No. I'll do anything.
Don't leave me. You're right. I know you're right."
I saw her arms go about his neck in a gesture of abandon and
surrender, and as I went swiftly into the other room I heard, even
through the door I had closed behind me, the sound of her weep-
ing.
Although I did not realize it then, I know and understand now,
eight months later, I was hearing through that closed door (as I
hear it clearly now again; as I heard it years ago, thin and dis-
tant on the side of the mountain ridge, through the early morn-
ing mist, after we had searched all night for our four-year-old
Arnie), the abandoned, the hopeless, the desolate wailing of a lost
child.
[189]Chapter XXIII
And yet it seemed to me that long before that discussion between
Ben and Arnie at which I was present (and which was the last
time I saw them together), and in spite of what Ben said at that
time, Arnie had faced all the implications of the direction she
had taken and was willing to abide by them. Especially was this
true concerning the "Terror." For though we had been assured
when we first came that the Terror would not be needed in this
country, when Arnie was told that it would be needed, and along
with the secret police would be a necessary adjunct of our coming
to power, she accepted this fact. And when I protested to her be-
cause I felt she allowed Masha to bully her, she said that Masha
came from the working class, and that I must realize that the
working class was the new aristocracy.
"Oh," I found myself saying, “I never dreamed you would ever
become a social climber." Arnie laughed, thinking I was being
facetious. And in a manner of speaking I was, though there was
some truth, and not a little dismay, contained in what I had said.
Masha had been a member of the Party several years before
Arnie and I had come to New York, and in 1939 she was trans-
ferred from her old cell to ours, though she lived in a housing
development a great distance from our part of the city. Arnie had
told me in confidence that Masha was the Party representative in
our cell; that is, she was responsible for discipline and the correct
interpretation of directives. One of the first uses she made of her
authority was to object to the custom I had begun of serving (on
Mondays when the cell met in our home) coffee and cakes from
six-thirty to seven-thirty, the hour when the formal meeting be-
[190]gan. I was so certain her objection was personal I refused to pay
any attention to it, and just before Art was transferred to Hawaii
(and months after he and Arnie had broken their relationship),
I appealed to him over Masha's head and got his permission to
continue the informal hour of which he had approved in the be-
ginning. I felt that Masha's objection was only another indication
of her growing antagonism toward Arnie, which might be ex-
plained by the fact that Arnie, a comparative newcomer, had
been promoted over her to posts of greater responsibility and pres-
tige than any she had yet reached.
No other reason appeared to me to be valid, for Arnie was al-
ways cooperative in her relations with Masha. And during the
war; that is, after 1941, when this country had entered the war
and we had received directives (as a temporary tactic) to coop-
erate with the war effort, and Masha had organized in the hous-
ing development a branch of one of the national patriotic
women's organizations, it was Arnie who made the initial contact
for her. It was Arnie who attended some of the meetings of the
Party fraction that planned the way in which propaganda could
be injected into the meetings, while at the same time serving the
outward semblance of patriotism and love of country that would
satisfy the women's organization of which they were a branch.
And it was Arnie who suggested the solution of a problem that
plagued Masha over a period of months in 1942 and 1943.
In every section of the city at that time (and even before we
entered the war), the Air Wardens had been organized on a pre-
cinct basis; and in the district in which Masha lived they held
their meetings in a settlement house, in the same building in
which the branch of the women's patriotic organization met. As
well as being head of this group Masha also became an air war-
den and attended their meetings.
One of the settlement secretaries had been detailed by the head
of the settlement to help with the Air Warden's organization. I
never met Miss Marshall, the secretary, but all that happened
was given to me in detail by Arnie, who was closely connected
with it in her position as Assistant to Agitprop.
Right in the beginning Masha had told us (while she sat in
the living room with Arnie and myself discussing her problem),
[191]that Miss Marshall was one of those "God and Country" people
without any conception of the progressive national and interna-
tional view possessed by all other members of the settlement staff
and which could always be used by us to such advantage.
Miss Marshall had been asked by the head of the wardens in
that precinct to put out a mimeographed bulletin once a month
with information about the wardens, and the young men and
women of the community who had gone into the armed forces.
Copies of the bulletin were to be distributed to the wardens in
that precinct and also mailed to the men and women in camps
here and overseas.
Instead of putting out regular sheets of mimeographed infor-
mation Miss Marshall cut the sheets down and formed them into
an attractive small "magazine” of thirty or forty pages, which to
Masha's dismay was received by almost everyone with interest
and applause.
The reason for Masha's dismay was this. At the Air Warden
meeting, when the first copy of the "magazine" was distributed,
Miss Marshall, who was considered its editor, explained its main
purpose. She then added that on one page they would find quota-
tions from the Declaration of Independence, from Lincoln, from
Washington and other founding fathers of this country-and es-
pecially concerning their belief in God. She said frankly that she
herself believed in God and felt that during the time this coun-
try was at war the Faith upon which this country was founded
should be emphasized. So each month, with the permission of the
precinct head, and using her authority as editor, she was dedicat-
ing one page of the bulletin to that Faith.
She must have seen the bitter disapproval on Masha's face as
she said this (but I am using my imagination and perhaps she did
not), and went on to say that anyone who did not agree with this
editorial policy, which she felt was the only true basis on which
to speak of patriotism in this country, should feel perfectly free
to express his or her difference of opinion in a letter which would
be "published" in the next issue.
Under the circumstances Masha could not make any written
protest, and for months was unable to counteract that influence,
except by expressing her discontent to individuals. It was Arnie
[192]who, being present at a meeting of the fraction some months later
when the fraction discussed this question, came up with a solu-
tion. "By this time," she said to Masha, “everyone will have for-
gotten that any
offer was made that others were free to express a
difference of opinion on that question. All you need do is put the
whole thing on the basis of democratic procedure. In your agita-
tion emphasize the fact that the rank and file of the Air Wardens
have been excluded from directing the magazine's policy."
Masha always objected at first to anything suggested by Arnie.
This was routine and something Arnie had accepted as part of
Masha's personality. And here Masha followed her usual proce-
dure. She asked Arnie what could be said if Miss Marshall as-
serted her right as editor to establish the policy of the bulletin,
and especially since she had the permission of the head of the
precinct; or what could she answer if Miss Marshall accused her
of not being patriotic because she did not believe in having God
handed out to the organization as the basis upon which this
country was founded.
"All that is true," Arnie said laughing. "But don't worry. That
kind of person is too polite to assert or accuse. I know; I was once
that kind myself. Just keep hammering away at the lack of democ-
racy and you'll win."
And so it happened. For after she had made the accusation of
lack of democracy Masha received the hearty support of the So-
cialist members (who had also expressed in private their dissatis-
faction with that editorial page); so they were able to work to-
gether to rouse the others until it was possible (the fraction hav-
ing urged all members to be present), at a regular meeting of the
wardens, suddenly to confront Miss Marshall with the accusation
that she was using Fascist methods, and was undemocratic. Miss
Marshall, never having suspected that the accusation was coming
(for the effectiveness of all such tactics is the result of surprise),
had no defense, and was democratically voted out as editor of the
bulletin. Masha was voted in as editor; and though there was
some talk, after we had taken it over, of using the bulletin as an
instrument of propaganda and agitation, Masha was far to busy
to do anything about it and it was discontinued.
The curious result of the whole affair was that Masha took all
[193]the credit for this maneuver, although Arnie, in my presence and
in the presence of others, had made the suggestion to her. And it
was another tactic (also suggested by Arnie in my presence),
which made it possible for Masha, with the help of Socialists and
Liberals after the war was over, by emphasizing the lack of democ-
racy and that the custom was not according to the Constitution,
to abolish prayers or reading of the Bible in the public schools
of this country, by a decree of the Supreme Court; a maneuver
that brought Masha to the attention of the CEC. And though
this decision of the Supreme Court was not accepted in every
state, it was still a law of the country and could be enforced at
any time when we gained enough power.
And this was by no means all. For Arnie told me it was planned
in the Agitprop Department that when the historic moment for
such a tactic should arrive and on the same premise and with the
expected Socialist and Liberal help), the goal had been set to
abolish all subservience to religious forms. For if it was possible
to take God out of the schools by law, then on the same basis—
that is, by agitation for separation of church and state-we
could also abolish the custom of having the oath, "So help me
God," sworn on the Bible in the courts. Also, on the same basis,
the custom of having the President and other officers of the coun-
try swear allegiance on the Bible when they were inducted into
office must be abolished, as well as the absurd and meaningless
practice of appointing chaplains for the armed forces and in both
houses of Congress.
And just as I had so easily given up the habit of having grace
said before meals when Arnie had shown me that I had already
given it up in my heart, so it would be possible to persuade the
people of this country to follow the same pattern with regard to
the sentence "In God We Trust," which we would persuade them
was as outmoded as woman's virtue, and had no more meaning
for progressives in this new day, this new time. We had found
that people in general, and especially "intellectuals," like to feel
that they are modern and progressive; and that if we accused
them on this one point (of not being progressive and up to date),
they would go to any extreme to escape the reproach. And Masha
[194]was peculiarly fitted by personality and temperament to promote
such maneuvers as had been planned.
For one thing, once begun on some project, she was inexorable
and never let go until she had accomplished it. For another, if
and when she wished to win anyone over, she could assume (and
I say assume because in the cell and with Arnie and myself she
was quite a different person), a warm, bluff, good-natured man-
ner that convinced outsiders that she was filled with the milk of
human kindness.
Her ability to make people like her was all the more remark-
able because in appearance she was not a very attractive person.
She was about medium height, solidly built, with large bones
covered by ample flesh. And she was so ugly that there was a fas-
cination in her features, as if they had been thrown together, as a
child might place dabs of unformed clay for a nose, a mouth, a
chin, on an oval base set up on an armiture by a sculptor. Only
her eyes, usually, gave her face life, for she was often quiet, almost
sullen with us. But her eyes, large and greenish, between heavy
lids, were always alive, and with a piercing gleam that at times
almost made me afraid, although until my last interview I never
had anything to fear from her. Her voice was naturally harsh, but
volatile, so that she could make it pleasant when she wished to do
so; and she understood the art of flinging off sentences and timing
a phrase in a careless, almost indifferent manner, that often
brought us to laughter; with the expression of secret amusement
and spite on her face, and that played about her mouth as she
spoke, only adding to the general hilarity.
On one occasion she came to our home just after her mother
had died, and she had attended the funeral. That evening, with
our living room crowded, as it usually was at that time, she had
an appreciative audience and gave us a vivid and at the same time.
sardonically humorous account of what had happened.
First she had gone to her parents' apartment on the East Side
to be with her family. She said her mother lay in the room before
she was placed in the coffin; and the coins put on her eyes to keep
them closed occasionally fell off, so that her eyes would spring
open. Then one of the relatives would have to rise from the floor
[195]to replace the coins; and this act would be accompanied by a fresh
outburst of wailing from the assembled family. Masha refused on
principle to take off her shoes or to sit on the floor as the others
had done, and which was the custom in a house of mourning.
And once when the coins had fallen from her mother's eyes, and
because Masha was in a chair and could reach the body more
easily than anybody else, to push down her mother's eyelids and
lay the coins
upon them again, she had risen to do so; but one of
her aunts had pulled her back forcibly from the body.
It was this same aunt who, at the cemetery, as they were lower-
ing the coffin into the grave, burst into loud sobs and, getting on
her knees before Masha, pleaded with her in the name of her
dead mother to give up Communism. (For, unlike Rose, Masha
had never hidden her membership in the Party from her family.)
Once the aunt had begun, others crowded about Masha, demand-
ing that she swear on her mother's grave that she would return to
God.
But a most ridiculous climax came, Masha told us, when one
of her uncles, while the grave was being filled, began a chant in
a mixture of Hebrew, Yiddish and English, which told of all the
trials and persecutions of the Jews. It ended with a detailed de-
scription of the persecutions in Europe, including the gas cham-
bers; winding up with the "Song of the Three Young Men," sung
by the "Holy Children" while they stood, unhurt, in the fiery fur-
nace which song began "Blessed art thou, Lord God of our fa-
thers, praised and exalted above all forever. . .
." And though
Masha did not use the names I had been accustomed to hearing
in my own childhood, I recognized-as she chanted for us, imi-
tating her uncle—the story of Shadrach, Meshach and Abednego,
who were cast into the fiery furnace by Nebuchadnezzar and yet
were not burned.
As Masha continued she seemed to enter the part like an actor
and her voice took on the cadences and rhythm of a Hebrew can-
tor. Her uncle, she said, repeated the prayer of Azarius; and when
he came to the part that went, "And thou didst deliver us into the
hands of lawless enemies, most hateful forsakers of God," he
pointed directly to her, as if she had been the one responsible for
[196]the gas chambers. And continuing Azarius's prayer he sang or
chanted:
"For according to truth and judgment didst thou bring all these things
upon us because of our sins.
For we have sinned and committed iniquity, departing from thee.
In all things have we trespassed, and not obeyed thy commandments,
Nor kept them, neither done as thou hast commanded us, that it might
go well with us.
Wherefore all that Thou has brought upon us, everything that thou
has done to us
Thou hast done in true judgment
•
"Why that's anti-Semitic!" Abe Mackan's voice cried out from
the corner where he was sitting. I believe he was not then trying
to be amusing, or that he had meant what he said as one of his
"wisecracks," but am convinced that he was truly indignant, even
hurt. For he added in a plaintive voice, "The Jews never do any-
thing wrong." Whatever he meant his voice was a shock to us all
for in spite of ourselves we had been caught up in the rhythm
and sound of the prayer, and his interruption, come at that mo-
ment, caused an explosion of laughter-almost hysterical.
When it had subsided Masha said dryly that not one of her
relatives had thought to ask why, if God had saved the three
"Holy Children" from the fiery furnace, He had not saved the
Jews from the gas chambers in Europe.
Before that evening, for some reason, I had never thought of
Masha as a member of a family with a mother and father, aunts
and uncles, sisters and brothers. And even as I realized that this
was so, it seemed very difficult to imagine her as a helpless infant
or as a child with adoring relatives. And it was possible that, be-
hind this façade of ridicule of her people, Masha was trying to
hide the sorrow she must have felt at her mother's death; a sor-
row which she could not acknowledge without at the same time
denying the implacable resoluteness, that had become so much a
part of her nature and her reputation within the Party, and of
which she was very proud.
Looking then at her heavy-lidded eyes, the mouth that smiled
only when she was at work—that is, when she was presenting to
[197]the world outside the Party the warm, persuasive front that made
her so valuable as a member—I remembered something she had
once said. It had been on an occasion when several had remained
after a meeting and were speaking, half-jokingly, about their am-
bitious dreams of what they would be when we had taken power
in this country; and one chose Commissar of Education; another
member of the Economic Council; another, member of the Su-
preme Court and Masha told us in a burst of confidence, unu-
sual with her, that she wished to be an ambassador from this
country to another. Then I understood why at times her actions
and words seemed to be affected or "put on," as when she intro-
duced two people and said smilingly and yet with an air of supe-
riority and consequence, “May I present. . . .'
"9
[198]Chapter XXIV
With all this in mind and with great trepidation I took the sub-
way to go to Masha's apartment. I had telephoned to make the
appointment, for I knew that most of her evenings would be
taken; and I wished, after making that long journey, to find her
at home.
My dread of the coming interview was somewhat ameliorated
by a hope I had been given that day about my child. It was Sun-
day, and I had spent an hour with Arnie, and had learned from
the nurse on duty that as a result of certain treatments (not dis-
closed to me) she was slightly more normal. That is, instead of re-
maining on the floor during the day she had consented to lie in
the bed or to sit in a chair beside it, and had even been per-
suaded to eat. Up to that time it had been necessary for them to
feed her artificially. And so, following my talk with the nurse I
did, indeed, find her lying in the bed. But as soon as I laid my
hand on her forehead, as I had done when she was a child and
ill with fever, she opened her eyes and looked at me with the same
rigid stare. She did not draw back from my caress, but as if she
did not see me or feel my hand at all, she sat up straight in the
bed and let my hand slip from her face. And so she was during all
the hour I was with her; pitifully rigid, her eyes still dark
drowned pools, and without any expression. Nevertheless there
was hope in the fact that she was in her bed without the necessity
of a quieting injection, which had up to that time been given her
every night in order to get her into the bed at all.
The small hope sustaining me, I made my way, after leaving
the subway station, toward the housing development where
[199]Masha lived. It was late evening and though April days in New
York are often as cold as some days of winter, that evening the
air was mild, presaging spring. My thoughts concentrated on
Arnie, and perhaps because I was postponing as long as possible
