the coming interview, I walked almost to the end of the street
toward the East River, always fascinating to me from whatever
angle or spot I viewed it.
A hazy, golden light, a reflection from the sun that was then
setting in the west, lit up the cables of the bridge over the river,
so that they appeared like fragile, lacy webs suspended in space.
And presently, as I stood there at the end of the street, the golden
light began to fade; the outlines of the bridge became vague,
merging with the darkening sky until all before me was night.
Then suddenly many lights, each one at that distance almost as
small as a pinpoint, appeared one after another, outlining the
shape of the bridge and its cables, as if the lights had been stars
that in some kind of celestial ballet had joined together to form
the graceful design which hung in the dark before my wonder-
ing eyes.
I would have remained longer, holding to this peaceful mo-
ment, but the lights reminded me that it was late. I hurried back
up the street to the housing development and to the building in
which Masha lived. The self-service elevator took me to the
fourth floor and soon I was pressing the bell of her apartment.
I had never been in Masha's home, and had not seen her in al-
most eight months, so that both were strange to me. I knew that
she shared the apartment with someone, but that person was not
present. The living room was furnished with modernistic pieces,
the kind that are in show windows all over the Lower East Side
along Second Avenue; and so, from that point of view were famil-
iar to me; and I found myself thinking, perhaps unjustly and
with some snobbery: Just the style of furniture Masha would
have.
As she took my hat and coat, and we shook hands and looked
at each other, I saw the same Masha, with the nose that seemed
to have been placed there like a piece of clay, the heavy eyebrows,
the heavy-lidded eyes. But on her face, on her mouth that smiled
at me so graciously, and in her manner that welcomed me as if I
[200]were an honored guest, I did not recognize at all the Masha whose
way with Arnie and myself had been so ungracious, so sullenly
spiteful over the years.
For a time, after we had sat down (I in one of the upholstered
chairs whose back slanted at an uncomfortable angle so that I was
obliged to sit on its edge, and Masha on the couch), we talked in
a desultory way about the weather-the mild day—and I made
some remark about the comfort of her apartment with which she
smilingly agreed. But even as we spoke I wondered at her
friendly, gracious attitude; the same attitude she had toward those
whom she wished to impress, formal but warm as when she would
say, introducing two people, "May I present. . . ." It was as if
already she imagined herself an ambassador, as she had once
stated it was her ambition to be. And yet, as we went on talking,
I noticed that she did not call me comrade as she had always cor-
rectly done before, and for some reason this frightened me.
All my pride rose up in protest against allowing Masha to wit-
ness any anxiety in me—any pain. But I could see that if our po-
lite words continued, and we kept up the dreadful pretense that
had come on us, like two wooden figures, manipulated by strings,
bowing to each other, nothing would come of this visit which in
prospect had given me so many painful hours; and that I would
leave her place without discovering any of the reasons why my
child had begun a letter to me in which she said, "Something un-
believable has happened. . . .”
Finally I broke abruptly into what we were saying, and looking
straight into Masha's face as she sat so solidly on the couch, smil-
ing so graciously, I said, "Did you know, Masha, that Arnie has
been ill?"
"Yes, Mrs. Braxton," she answered. "And I was so sorry to hear
about her illness. How is she?"
Suddenly I spoke what was in my mind, and because of the
pressure of the false atmosphere, my voice was harsh as it came
painfully from a throat that was so constricted it almost prevented
me from speaking at all. "I understand," I said, "that you have
become Assistant to Agitprop."
"Yes," she agreed. "Yes, I have." And as she said this, as I
looked into her eyes and recognized the gleam of triumph and
[201]satisfaction between the heavy lids; as I saw the sardonic smile
that came for a second on her mouth (though it was quickly sup-
pressed), an anger came in me which I found it difficult to con-
trol.
I had faced the fact that I would find a victorious Masha, one
who would not hide her triumph, and that I would find it neces-
sary to humble myself, as well as my child, before her, in order
to discover the reason for that victory. But the task, as I sat there
looking into her eyes, gleaming with triumph she could not hide,
was more difficult, more painful than I had imagined.
“But why, Masha, why?” I exclaimed. “What has happened?
Arnie and I are still loyal members. We are still fighting.
"But, Mrs. Braxton," Masha answered in a calm, quiet voice,
so different from my own which had become agitated and louder
than I had meant it to be, “Arnie is not a member. Arnie has been
expelled from the Party."
"What do you mean?” I demanded. "That is not so. That can't
be true."
"But it is so, Mrs. Braxton," she said in the same quiet, formal
manner. And still in this same quiet, formal, unagitated manner,
she reached to the low table in front of the couch and took up a
box of cigarettes and offered it to me; and when I shook my head
impatiently, she lit one for herself from a silver lighter that rested
beside the silver box.
"But why?" I asked, and in spite of my desire not to do so, I
found myself begging. “What did they say? What is the reason?"
And when Masha said nothing but looked at me with cold eyes
over the smoke of her cigarette, I asked again, "What is the rea-
son?"
"Racial prejudice," Masha answered, and her chin lifted and
she looked at me with contempt. And I knew that, looking at me
in such a way, she was also looking at Arnie through me, with
that contemptuous smirk which condemned at the same time
that it triumphed.
"Are you not ashamed," I said. "After knowing Arnie all these
years, knowing Ben. . . ."
"The accusation came from Ben," she interrupted.
"From Ben?" I asked.
[202]"From Ben and Endahl," she said complacently. "But it was
not an accusation against Arnie. It was you, Mrs. Braxton,
who were accused of racial chauvinism. At first Arnie was not in-
cluded, except that a very simple thing was demanded of her:
that she cut herself off from you—entirely.
“I felt sorry for her, Mrs. Braxton," Masha continued in a hy-
pocritically unctious voice. “She was so astonished at first, so con-
fused at the accusation, just as you are today. Then she became
abject and humble, excusing herself and excusing you, but will-
ing to obey the Party and cut herself off from the influence of
your bourgeois deviations.
"I flattered myself,” Masha said, "that my influence had some-
thing to do with her decision-and I congratulated her. When I
did that she looked at me strangely-a wild expression came on
her face and she exclaimed, 'No, I will not. Not because
my
mother,' she stammered. 'But what is freedom, what is liberty?
Must I live in fear . . . You wish me to, because But I
cannot the innocent millions . . . I will not give my life
to fear.'
.. ·
"She turned to Ben, to Endahl, to members of the CEC who
were present, as if she expected them, after that outburst, to give
her understanding. 'Comrades!' she said, 'Comrades!' I could have
smiled, Mrs. Braxton, at her naïveté, if I hadn't felt sorry for
her.
"Because as she said that, looking at each separate face, and
they turned from her in disgust, she exclaimed, 'Oh my God!'
and began to weep. A silly weakness, Mrs. Braxton, at which I
was not surprised. She was weeping when we left her. And not
one, Mrs. Braxton, not one turned back to speak to her. She was
left alone-disgraced, abandoned."
Never had Masha's talent for telling a story, for presenting a
living account of what had happened, served her so perfectly. I
could see my child before them, as if I had been present at that
grievous ordeal. There was no doubt in my mind that Masha
had told me the story in order to give me pain. And this purpose
she had accomplished. But without intending to do so she had
given me the assurance I had come to get from her. Now I was
certain: It was my child's heart that had broken—not her mind.
[203]At that moment of disaster her mind had spoken clearly: "I will
not give my life to fear," she had said.
I leaned toward Masha from the edge of the uncomfortable
slanting chair. "And you never once spoke for justice," I stated
indignantly, "never once said what you know is true. For you
knew in your heart, and know now, there is no truth in that
accusation, either about me or about Arnie."
She had a fair trial, Mrs. Braxton; a democratic trial," Masha
answered, stamping out her cigarette. "A bourgeois deviation,"
she said, still stamping the cigarette, "was to be expected." Then
suddenly dropping the cigarette into the ahstray she looked
straight at me, and I saw the hate in her eyes, the bitter hate
that was not only for Arnie and for me but for others. "And
what," she asked pitilessly, "do you know about justice? What do
you know of the rage in our hearts when we see our own poverty
and watch others, not worth my little finger, wallowing in wealth.
What do you know of suffering who have always had your com-
fortable place in life? We have a right to that kind of life, too,
and we're going to get it. . . ."
"Not by lies," I said.
"Oh yes," she answered. "We will lie and steal and murder and
we will deceive, just as Lenin said we would. And that also is our
right, to use any means to bring about that end for which we are
fighting; and who is there to say we should not, or we cannot. Not
you and your pious Arnie.
"And I'll tell you something else," she continued, as if having
once begun and the dam having broken she could not contain the
flood of her hate, but it must pour in all its devastating power
over the land. "If this had been after our day had come, if we
were in power, your daughter would not be resting safe in a hos-
pital. She would be dead. And even now," she said in her harsh
voice which seemed to express all the hate and jealousy that had
accumulated over the years, “even now she is a danger to us all.
For she knows too much, Mrs. Braxton. And if I were you," she
said, and it was evident she was trying to make her voice sound
kindly as if she were giving me, a neighbor or comrade, advice
about a doctor to whom I might take my daughter, "I would take
her away, as far away as you can. For you know, Mrs. Braxton,
[204]that even here we have power. We can't put her in prison, but we
have power here, in this country. You should know that. And I
advise you.
99
But I could bear no more. "No," I exclaimed loudly, "do
do not advise me." I lifted myself from the chair, my coat and
hat from the couch on which she had placed them and hurried to
leave the room. But opening the wrong door, a door that led into
a bedroom, in my confusion I struck my head so that I cried out.
Calmly and quietly (and I felt she was smiling), Masha said,
“This way, Mrs. Braxton,” and opened the door into the corri-
dor.
Without waiting to bring the elevator to that floor I rushed
down the four flights of stairs and out into the street. For a great
fear had come in me; and as I hurried along the street and down
into the subway the fear, instead of diminishing, became greater.
For into my mind had come the name of a woman.
I had often felt skeptical about some of the stories Arnie related
to me which had been told to her by foreign members of the se-
cret Apparatus. After all, these men were human beings and
might enjoy "showing off" to a young woman like Arnie; and in
the process might exaggerate here and there, or even invent de-
tails with which to impress her. Or so I had thought. Now all
these stories came back to me, and one of them especially was
hideously real. The facts were there, for at the time I heard it
from Arnie I had looked up newspapers of that day and verified
it.
Few details had appeared in the newspapers on the front pages
of which, at the time she vanished, the picture of Juliet Stuart
Poyntz had appeared. A former teacher in the public schools, this
woman had become a mysterious figure, a nine days wonder to
the public, particularly since it was hinted that she had been
involved in the Movement. These facts had been told to Arnie
by a member of the CEC who had known Juliet Stuart Poyntz
well. Like Arnie she had held a high place within the Party and
had also, like Arnie, been a liaison with an Apparatus, in fact
with the branch of the Secret Police detail in this country. When
she became dissatisfied or disillusioned and wished to leave the
Party they let her go. This much only of her history the member
[205]of the CEC had known. Her disappearance was as much a mystery
to him as it was to the public. It was, then, a foreign member of
the Apparatus to which Arnie had been assigned who cleared up
the mystery. Juliet Stuart Poyntz had found a position in the
bourgeois world and was living quietly in a pleasant apartment
when, one day, there was summoned from the Workers' Father-
land a man with whom she had at one time, lived. Reluctantly
he agreed to what was demanded of him. He telephoned her say-
ing that he, too, had become disillusioned, and asked her to meet
him at a certain restaurant for dinner and a sentimental reunion.
In front of the restaurant as she stepped from her taxi, two men
met her. They led her to a car and from that time she was seen
no more. Out in New York Harbor waited a steamship, the Sacco
and Vanzetti. In the basement kitchen of a private "home" in
Greenwich Village the woman's body was hacked in pieces, and
packed in brine. The cask was then taken to the Sacco and Van-
zetti, and shipped to the country which had been the goal of her
former hopes for mankind.
All this had made me physically ill when I heard it. And then,
like the others, I accepted it as a just and necessary precaution,
and merely an incident in the great war in which we were in-
volved. And as we speak of the death of another without truly
accepting the fact that this same end will one day be our own, so,
as we talked of that woman, I could not in the least imagine that
my daughter, so honored, so praised, so secure, so much a part of
the Cause, might ever be in the same position.
Arnie had shared too many secrets with me for me to feel for
a moment that Masha's threats were empty ones. There was the
wooded spot on the cliff which Arnie had pointed out to me when
we were traveling to Croton. "This," she whispered, "is where
our most important radio station is located, ready for the next
war."
Other secrets Arnie had shared with me came into my mind.
And I remembered a suicide that might not have been a suicide,
and another. Certainly, as Masha had said, Arnie knew too much.
Fear had taken such possession of me that when I reached the
tenement behind which I lived I hesitated at the door of the dark
hallway through which I must go; and passing across the court-
[206]yard I peered into each shadowy corner. But everything was si-
lent. Only in my heart and mind there was a turbulence that did
not leave me, and as I lay in bed I planned what might be said
to the doctor to persuade him to let me take Arnie to Bethel.
Then, from far back in the years came the memory of black
hands gripping my shoulders and hot tears falling on me as I
knelt before my mother's coffin. And of the years when a generous
heart overflowed out of sorrow and pain into love for my
child. I
rose from the bed and dialed Mum Hattie's number.
[207]Chapter XXV
Julie and I first saw Hattie when, one morning in March just
after our fifth birthday, she came trailing behind M'am Ame, her
grandmother, across the back yard of Mars Hill. She was then
twelve years old, a scrawny, big-boned girl in a red calico dress,
hair done up in "ropes" tight against her magnificent head. She
swaggered along, never condescending to let her eyes stray to
the two little girls (one with olive complexion, short upper lip
and large brown eyes-the other, the smaller one, fair-haired
and blue-eyed), who hung over the railing of the back porch
watching her with grave and critical attention.
Whatever Hattie's attitude toward us was that first morning,
Julie and I were anything but indifferent. We were intensely
aware that M'am Ame was bringing her granddaughter to see our
mother, and that if they came to an understanding Hattie would,
in her after school hours, and then full time in the summer, be-
come our "nurse."
We were still regretting Mary, who, for almost a year after our
old nurse died, had made life a long, exciting, Scheherazade-like
story, continued from day to day-the story of Henry and Mary
and Lilah. And we loved Henry, though we had never seen him,
and hated Lilah, Mary's bitter rival.
Moving her plump shoulders and even her whole body to the
rhythm Mary sang:
"When a man gets blue, when a man gets blue,
He takes the train and rides;
When a woman gets blue, when a woman gets blue,
She hangs her little head and cri-i-i-i-es.
[208]"But me," Mary said boastingly, "I don't hang my little head
and cries. Ain' no blue-gum nigger like that Lilah goin' t'git
me down." And her broad, goodnatured face, which often held a
sunny expression of complacent self-approval, changed in an in-
stant to sternness; and her usually warm, friendly hands became
hard weapons ready for vengeance.
Mary's duty was to keep us in the outdoors as often as the
weather permitted. We played in the back yard under her super-
vision; hunted flowers in the spring and nuts and autumn leaves
in the fall, in the woods about our home. And though we were
not supposed to go on the big road (the country road that ran
near our home), unless we had our mother's permission, never-
theless we often found ourselves there without it.
"Yes'm, Miss Annie," Mary would say to our mother. "No'm,
Miss Annie, I wouldn't take these chillen on the big road. No
ma'm!"
One fall day Mary led us between the box hedge of the garden,
over the solid rustic oak bridge my grandfather had designed and
had built at the foot of the lowest terrace (where the same creek
that flowed quietly on the flat surface of our pasture lands ran
tumbling here between the walls of a small ravine), out through
a gateway, through a grove of trees and on to the big road.
At a spot near the crossroads where the Negro church stood she
halted and would not go on. We waited and complained, but
Mary refused to move. Finally Julie said to me “Oh, come on,”
and we went into the field where tall purple spikes of ironweed
waved in an autumn wind and clusters of orange butterfly weed
invited us. Even now I remember (though the impression might
have come at a later time), a realization I had of the beauty of
that day, and of the surroundings. A few cedars, conical in shape,
stood lone and lonely at spots in the field, black against the sky
that was as blue as the bluest morning glories. Small white puffy
clouds (as if on important errands) scudded low just above the
maple trees in the woods, some distance across the fields that
splashed their brilliant red and yellow leaves against the dark
pines. Nearer still, small yellow butterflies, and the larger orange
ones (the exact color of the butterfly weed), undulated above the
flowers as if moving to some secret rhythm. I seemed to feel their
[209]rhythm in myself, to enter the life of the butterflies, hovering over
a flower or sailing away on weightless wings.
Julie and I had gone some distance from the road on which
Mary stood lonely, but as if waiting, and we had our arms full of
the purple and orange flowers, when we saw a wagon come rat-
tling along, driven by an old Negro man. We heard the old man
shout "Whoa!" to his horse and heard him call "Mary." Instantly
Julie dropped her flowers and began to run toward the wagon.
And I, equally curious, but with the flowers still clasped in my
arms, followed.
As we came up the old man, with grizzled hair, a long nose and
thin mouth, was offering Mary an apple. I could not understand
why she put her hands behind her, and why, in the voice with
which she refused, there was agitation; or why a disturbed, anx-
ious look was on her face. The old man's expression seemed bene-
volent and kind. Also it was mid-morning and I was hungry.
At last, though very reluctantly, Mary took the apple in her
hand. As he leaned over the side of the wagon the old man saw
Julie and myself standing behind Mary, almost hidden by her
skirts. He reached back in the wagon and brought out two more
apples which he gave to Julie and me; and which we accepted
greedily. But the wagon had scarcely rattled away when, to our
astonishment, Mary slapped them out of our hands. Picking them
up from the ground she then hurled them, along with her own,
far
away into the field at the side of the road.
Julie gave a scream of anger and frustration and started to run
out into the field, but Mary took us both firmly by our arms and
propelled us along the road toward home.
"Stop yo' cryin'," she said to Julie. "Don' you know I jus' saved
yo' life?"
"What do you mean, saved my life?" Julie panted, hurried
along by Mary's grip on her arm.
"Don't you know them apples was conju'd?" Mary demanded.
Julie halted, and a thrill of exquisite fear took possession of
me. For the word "conju'd" was prohibited at home. Yet we
heard it often in the kitchen, and it meant to us something fear-
ful, to be dreaded, and at the same time mysteriously alluring.
[210]"Who conju'd them apples?" I asked breathlessly.
"Don' you know a juju man when you see one? Didn' you hear
that ol' man tell me Henry couldn' come. Didn' you hear him say
Henry sen' me a love apple?"
"An' he didn'?" I asked in wonder.
"What you think? I got mo' sense than that. It was that Lilah
sen' me word to meet Henry here. She pay that juju man to bring
me apples she poison. Dead Mary ain' goin' make no stir in
Henry. Dead Mary cain' make no trouble for Lilah. But Mary
she ain' dead yit!" she said triumphantly.
That night I had a dream about the juju man who was trying
to make Julie and myself eat poisoned apples and woke scream-
ing. My mother hobbled into the nursery. I can still hear the
sharp sound of her cane (for she was having one of her occasional
attacks of rheumatism) falling on the floor as she took me in her
arms. Neither of us, then, could explain to her what it was that
had terrified me. But my mother was troubled; and when, some
months later, there was a repetition of the nightmare, for the first
time she learned something of our life with Mary.
It was winter when, this time with our mother's permission,
Mary led us down the road to a country store where a man had
brought a performing bear. We stayed watching until the man
who owned the bear collected the nickels we had brought for him
and went away, the bear (now on all fours) following obediently
behind him.
The winter day had been warm and soft. Benign twilight made
the quiet road a place where footsteps sounded unearthly, though
planted on the earth, as if they were the footsteps of others, not
our own. It was almost dark when we reached the Negro church
(originally built of logs and later covered over with clapboards
painted white), that my great-grandfather had built for the slaves,
and which stood at the crossroads. The church had a large “bury-
ing ground," unenclosed, flanking it on one side and surrounding
it in the back. The graves, some with tombstones, but all covered
with brightly-colored vases and broken bits of red and blue and
green glass, in the daytime had never seemed objects of fear. But
that evening as we passed, and as Mary stopped before the
[211]church, in the dusky, eerie twilight all appeared entirely differ-
ent. Even Mary seemed different. In a hushed voice she told us
that at night the ghosts of those who had been buried came out
from the graves. As she spoke the tombstones seemed to quiver in
the evening light as if they were indeed the spirits of those who
had died, and the bits of colored glass glittered like eyes.
"Why do they come out?" Julie demanded. “What they want?"
"They's seekin'," Mary answered in that new, smothered voice.
"What they seekin'?" I whispered.
"Seekin' them that did 'em wrong," Mary said. "They's the
res'less ones."
"What they res'less about?" Julie asked impatiently, as if angry
at the ghosts because they frightened her.
"Cain' fi'give and cain' f'get. That's what they res'less about,"
Mary said.
Suddenly the shuddering, tingling fear of ghosts was gone and
the pain, the sorrow, the loneliness of the "res'less" ones over-
whelmed me and I began to cry dismally.
My weeping startled Mary from her concentration and she
grabbed our hands. “Come on, you chillen'," she said as if we had
been keeping her there. "Yo' ma'll give me what fer you stay out
so late."
That night my mother was again awakened by my screams.
Sitting on a chair between Julie's bed and my own, with her
quiet, comforting hand smoothing my forehead, she began to ask
us questions. I babbled incoherently, and it was Julie, the articu-
late one, who told about the juju man and about the ghostly
graves.
A month later we returned with our mother, but in full day-
light, to the same cemetery, to pay our respects to two new-made
graves. Some years before Ma'm Ame's son Wash had gone away
to Baltimore to work in the Johns Hopkins Hospital, and had
married there. Both he and his wife had been killed in the wreck
of an excursion train to New York. Now the "burying ground,"
that Mary had made such a fearful place, assumed once more for
us its everyday, its natural aspect, as we placed the flowers brought
for that purpose on the graves of M'am Ame's son and daughter-
in-law.
[212]My father had gone to Baltimore to bring the bodies home,
but it was M'am Ame herself who went later for her granddaugh-
ter, Hattie, who was injured in the wreck, and had been cared for
at the same hospital where her father had worked.
[213]Chapter XXVI
Apparently the conference between my mother, M'am Ame and
Hattie was settled by an agreement satisfactory to all. For on the
following Monday afternoon Hattie came officially to be our
nurse. At that time, I believe, our mother considered her only
another makeshift—a child, who, while six years older than Julie
and myself, was nevertheless a child. None of us had any intima-
tion that her life and the life of our family (especially after the
death of her grandmother, M'am Ame), would become a close
and unbreakable union.
She came into the yard that sunny, mild winter afternoon with
her bookbag over her shoulder. Julie was in the swing that was
fastened to the huge limb of a large oak tree at the edge of the
bare yard in back of our home, and I was pushing her. Without a
word Hattie slung her bookbag on to a bench under the tree and
took my place behind the swing. Julie screamed with delight
when Hattie's long arms made the swing go higher and higher,
so that Julie could even look over the brick wall into the kitchen
garden.
It was on that very first day that Hattie gained my trust if not
my love. For when it was my turn to swing and Julie refused to
"let the old cat die," Hattie caught the swing and ordered Julie
to get out of it.
"Who are you?" Julie demanded, holding on, "giving me or-
ders?"
"I'm yo' nurse, that's who," Hattie said. “Go ask your ma, she'll
tell you. Now you git down and let yo' sister swing." She tipped
[214]the board seat so that Julie slipped to the ground. But my sister
did not easily let go. She clung tightly to the
ropes.
"You're just a nigger," she said spitefully, looking angrily up
into Hattie's face. "Your grandpa was my grandfather's slave."
"So he was. So he was," Hattie agreed. “But in Africa my
gran'pa had more slaves than yours. My gran'pa had two hundred
wives and a thousand slaves."
"Who said so?" Julie demanded.
"Don't you squint yo' eyes and firm yo' mouth at me," Hattie
ordered. "Who said so? My pappy said so, and his pappy said so,
and his

Suddenly a cyclone lifted her, squriming and wriggling and
screaming, from the swing and deposited her on the ground; and
the same power (concentrated in Hattie's strong arms), set me in
her place. Before Julie could raise herself from the ground Hattie
had me high in the air.
"Next time," Hattie said to Julie, "you act polite. Yo' ma told
me to teach you all courtesy and politeness; and I'm going to
teach you courtesy and politeness."
If Hattie had not showed so much inventiveness about games
most probably Julie would have continued the struggle begun
that morning. But though she never learned to say "please," she
showed a certain respect for Hattie, and allowed her to lead us.
The retired clergyman who had taught our mother had been,
to a limited extent, a follower of Rousseau, and our mother was
also. She allowed us to be "natural" to the extent of giving us
permission, when spring had fully arrived, to run about barefoot
so long as there were no guests at Mars Hill; and when Hattie
was free from school we had whole long lazy days together. We
walked in the pasture where we found bluets like stars scattered
in the grass; and in the woods nearby we made a wigwam of tree
branches, and bows and arrows of hickory, and climbed trees to
look out for the "enemy." Sometimes on a path we met young
Negro children, and while one of the boys played his mouth or-
gan Julie and I, barefoot like the rest, learned to "shuffle."
[215]But always Hattie's favorite game was "hospital." During her
stay in a ward at the Johns Hopkins she had learned big words
which probably she did not use correctly, but which satisfied and
impressed us. Julie and I were always the patients and Hattie the
doctor. Even our dolls were laid out in rows in a ward. But Hat-
tie's greatest pleasure came when one of us skinned a knee or cut
a finger. Then she rushed into the house for bandages and iodine
and her clever, gentle hands with their long fingers bound our
wounds while her warm voice eased our pain. She dosed us with
watered down molasses and with something else she called
"nasty herbs," made from the bark of sassafras trees and mashed
green persimmons.
It could be said that Hattie grew into us and became part of
us as a twig becomes part of the fruit tree upon which it has been
grafted. And because our mother always treated her with respect
(for isn't she a child of God? our mother asked us), and with a
sense of justice which made no difference between Hattie and
her own children when there was a quarrel, Hattie, who accepted
love as a gift, began to love our mother with a consciousness of
what love means that was not yet possible to Julie and myself,
who accepted love as a right.
Hattie was with us when we had our lessons, and at the same
time our mother helped her with her own and with her diction.
When our mother read to us Hattie was there, listening. Some-
how my mother understood Hattie when she said, "I'm going to
be a nurse," or "I'm going to Africa." I, for one, did not under-
stand, even when we were older, why Hattie insisted on the latter.
"Why do you want to go to Africa?" I demanded, though what I
meant was, "Why do you want to leave us?" "It's where I come
from, ain't it?" Hattie answered; and we looked at her in amaze-
ment for most of the people of her race that we knew were
ashamed of that heritage.
With my mother's help Hattie saved her money during the
four years she was with us, and when she was sixteen, my father
and mother added something to the amount saved, Hattie went
away to the Institute. I wept when she left us. But she returned
for visits, and each time astonished us by the difference in her ap-
pearance and her manner. She was growing into a poised, con-
[216]fident young woman. But never did she lose her simplicity, her
naturalness. She was Hattie. Not a week went by that my mother
did not have a letter from her. The rhythm of the music of her
life was based on the tonic, dominant, and subdominant chords
that were my mother, nursing, and Africa. Sometimes one chord
beat out the rhythm and sometimes another. Even after she left
the Institute and went to Johns Hopkins to work and study in the
diet kitchen the same rhythm continued.
From 1910 to 1921 she was in New York practicing her profes-
sion and saving money for Africa. It was during that time, in
1914, that my mother died, and Hattie came home to Mars Hill
for the funeral. From that time she transferred her devotion (or
what part of it did not belong eternally to my mother) to me; and
though her letters did not come regularly, I often received one
from her and answered it; and when, in 1918 she announced her
marriage to Pvt. Count Randolph, Julie and I sent a wedding
present.
All those years, from 1914, I had not seen Hattie when one
day, in 1921, she appeared at the Rectory. I was appalled at the
change in her. She sat before me, misery in her face and in the
very droop of her body. And as we talked I noticed that except
for one thing-the way she had been used to bend down and rub
her leg between the knee and ankle, where it had been broken in
the train wreck, as if it still pained her—all the familiar and
loved touches of personality were gone; especially the way, when
she was excited over some project, she would wave her long fin-
gers, her beautiful hands, as if conducting an invisible orchestra.
This too was gone, as well as the warm laughter and the ineffable
heartwarming smile. She was like a bombed-out area which peo-
ple have called home, but which retains nothing of the individu-
ality of that home except the foundation-and that covered
over with rubble.
"I just had to come home, Miss Ann," she said miserably. “I
just had to come home."
"You are," I assured her, and not knowing why, I wept. "You
are home.”
She cried bitterly, and I put my arms about her. "Is it as bad
as that?" I asked.
[217]"I feel so old and done for," she said.
"But you're only thirty-one!" I exclaimed.
"It ain't the years, Miss Ann, it ain't the years," she said, wip-
ing her eyes and using the contraction of "is not" that made what
she said more meaningful, more emphatic.
Thinking to cheer her I asked if she would like to see our child
who was then twenty months old. She followed me upstairs and
we bent over the crib where the baby lay sprawled out, sleeping.
Hattie lifted a lock of Arnie's hair with one of her long fingers
and touched lightly the dark heavy lashes that lay on the child's
cheeks. "What color eyes?" she asked.
"Brown," I said. “Like her father's."
Still bending over the crib Hattie asked, "Who takes. . . do
take care of her, Miss Ann?”

Hattie straightened the covers over the child. She looked
me and I saw something pitifully beaten in that glance, as if all
confidence in herself was gone.
"I could be cook and nurse," she said, offering herself.
"Oh no," I protested. "We couldn't afford you." For I knew
from her letters how much Hattie received for a day's work.
"Why, compared to us, you are rich. Think of all the money you
have in the bank." For long ago she had written me, proudly, of
the money she put in the bank each week, saving for Africa.
"All gone, Miss Ann, all gone," she said, gripping the side of
the crib with her long fingers and not looking at me.
"But how? But why?" I asked in astonishment.
Something strangely ashamed and confused came in her face.
"Don't ask me why. Just let me stay and pay what you can. I'm
lost, Miss Ann, I'm lost. Help me!" she cried out.
The baby stirred restlessly and opened her eyes wide. Hattie
reached down and took the child in her arms. Then for the first
time she looked at me; for the first time she smiled. "Your ma
[218]would have loved her," she said, and as she struck that chord, the
chord that vibrated on the strings of my heart and looked at me
and smiled, I knew that we must keep Hattie with us.
From that time on she was our cook and Arnie's nurse. And
because we wished Arnie to show proper respect for her, and
though Hattie was only thirty-one, we taught Arnie to call her
Mum Hattie, and all in our household fell into the way of calling
her so.
It was a happy time, for Hattie's forlorn drab appearance
sloughed off like a dead skin, and she put on weight. She flew
about the house and the kitchen, getting everything done in
order and well done, and never was Arnie neglected.
Then her husband came. I believe I have not often been so
astonished as when I saw him for the first time. What I had im-
agined, I think, was a handsome, tall, strong Negro man. What
I saw was a thin little person, much shorter than Hattie, with a
cunning look on his face, colored a washed out black and having
eyes that dodged here and there. At once I did not like this per-
son, who dressed with such sharp and flamboyant elegance;
and for a moment anger came in me against Hattie because she
had allowed him to come to our home and sit there as if he be-
longed in our kitchen, as if he not only belonged but were master
there.
Hattie rose from her chair, but the man remained seated.
"This is my husband, Count Randolph, Miss Ann," Hattie
said and in her face, agonizingly apparent, was the unexpressed
misery that was in her soul.
And yet from that day until her husband, much later, was
killed in a brawl in Harlem, she never spoke to me about him;
never told me how she had met him; why she had loved him; nor
did she ever say what had become of the money she had saved.
Even when the regrettable thing about the silver happened,
she never spoke. My father had given my husband and myself a
chest of flat silver when we were married. This silver was not
used every day, and about once a month I gave it a cleaning. One
day I lifted the chest from its compartment in the sideboard and
felt immediately that something was wrong. It was almost weight-
less. Taking it to the pantry where I had laid out the polish, the
[219]flannel cloths, and the chamois I used in cleaning, I opened it and
found it empty.
“Oh, Mum Hattie," I called, "where did you put the silver?"
She came into the pantry. "I didn't put it anywhere, Miss
Ann," she said.
"It isn't here," I told her. "Look."
She came to the chest and lifted the trays. Nothing was there.
I saw a stricken look come on her face. She sat down suddenly as
if her knees would not hold her, and began slowly to massage her
leg, up and down, up and down. The heavy, pleading silence
was more than I could bear, and I left her.
From that day she went about the house in a stoic silence. She
became thin again and all confidence left her. A wall came be-
tween us, Mum Hattie in her quiet pain on one side of it, and
myself miserable on the other. From Julie, who had the news by
grapevine (that is, from Sep), we learned that Count Randolph
had been keeping a girl in the Negro section of Bethel—a pat-
tern that must have been established in Harlem. Whether it was
the silver that paid for a "honeymoon" I do not know, but soon
Julie telephoned that Count Randolph and the girl had left
on a train for New York. Not long afterwards the same grapevine
notified us that Mum Hattie's husband had been killed, and
where and how.
When it was Mum Hattie began going to church, I do not re-
member. Perhaps it was soon after her husband went away. For
it was then, as she worked about the house or put Arnie to sleep,
I heard little snatches of song. At first they were quavering and
trembling attempts, bringing to mind the way a person who has
been ill trembles on uncertain knees when taking his first con-
valescent steps.
Then one day I went toward the kitchen to ask something and
halted, astonished, outside the open door. For through it came a
deep, a glorious voice, singing:
"Were the whole realm of nature mine
That were an offering far too small.
Love so amazing, so divine,
Demands my life, my love, my all.
[220]"Why, Mum Hattie!" I cried out, for her voice sounded as if it
could never express all the happiness that was welling up through
that strong, that energetic, that powerful being.
I went closer and looked in through the door. It was just as if
I had interrupted someone at prayer. "I didn't know," I stam-
mered, "I never dreamed you had such a beautiful voice."
"It isn't the voice, Miss Ann," Mum Hattie said, giving me that
loving, that ineffable smile. “It's grace you hear. I am redeemed,
Miss Ann, I am redeemed."
The words were not said in a shout of joy or emotion. They
were quiet, still, confident words. And so was the face I saw be-
fore me, confident, joyful, and unashamed.
Before Mum Hattie went again to New York—and the matter
was never mentioned between us-most of the silver, a few pieces
at a time, was restored to the chest.
[221]Chapter XXVII
Early in 1924 we said good-by to Mum Hattie. My five-year-old
Arnie wept bitterly, and there were tears in my own eyes as the
train pulled out of the old station, and Mum Hattie's long arm
came out of a window in a last gesture of farewell.
After her first letter sometimes months went by without any
word at all. Then a letter would come: "Good news, Miss Ann,
I've got my home." It was a home she had established in coopera-
tion with her church for orphaned babies and children, and
which she helped to support with the money she earned as a pro-
fessional nurse. But the home for babies in Harlem was only one
project outlined for herself by that big frame, that realistic mind,
that generous heart.
Back and forth she went from New York to Africa, where, in
the interior she had established a home for orphaned children
and babies, similar to the one in Harlem. Then back to New
York to earn and raise more money for both. After three years in
Africa she wrote:
You'd be surprised. I am myself. But things have been done. Neither
chiefs, neither witch doctors can put anything over on Hattie. But I
love them; though I've got no sentimental feelings like the white
people have that their customs are "more natural, more beautiful."
The customs of my people are brutal and savage and cruel, as cruel
as you and Julie when you were six and jumped about and screamed
with excited pleasure in the back yard at Mars Hill after the cook
had wrung the neck of a chicken and its body, dripping blood, flopped
high in the air. And yet this cruelty is different, for there is something
old and deliberate and sophisticated about it.
[222]Some of them still sell their own people as slaves to the East. Also I
hear, but don't know from my own eyes, as I do about the slaves, that
in some places there is cannibalism. These chiefs and witch doctors
can't scare Hattie. I tell them my great grandpa lived here and he
had two hundred wives and a thousand slaves. I tell them we're a
thousand years behind the white people and we've got to catch up.
But not in the white man's way. In our own way. I don't tell them,
but the truth is I don't know what that way is. But I've got faith God
will show us if we put Him first, step by step, day by day. White
people have forgotten to put God first. Put Him first and “all these
things shall be added."
I've got fifteen babies, from one day old to eight years. With them I
have learned to speak their language. With me they have learned
about Jesus Christ who said “Suffer little children to come unto me,
and forbid them not.” I feed them good food, but there is another
kind of food. What good for me to be here to make a lot of husky
meat, like hogs being fattened? No, I don't feed them bread alone. I
don't aim to send children out into the world full of vitamins in the
flesh and anemia in the spirit. These babies have got roots. They are
rooted and grounded in Christ.
This letter came in 1936, at the time when my husband was
getting ready to leave the church, and I showed it to David hop-
ing the phrase "putting God first" would help bring him back.
He had been fond of Mum Hattie and I was surprised at his re-
ception of her letter; for he ridiculed it as if it had been a per-
sonal affront to his ego.
And it was the same with Arnie when Mum Hattie returned
from Africa after the war and she and Arnie had, in our flat in
New York, a royal battle, arising from Mum Hattie's experience
with people of our convictions on the other continent, and going
headlong into a discussion of God.
"Oh, you fool!" Arnie said impatiently to Mum Hattie. "Oh,
you fool! Take just one thing. What about suffering? What does
your God do about suffering?"
"What about His promises?" Mum Hattie said, waving her
hands, conducting that invisible orchestra. "God has never prom-
ised we wouldn't suffer. In the Old Testament, in the New Testa-
[223]ment, He has only, always, promised us Himself. Can I promise
you myself? And keep the promise? Always somehow, somewhere,
I let you down. Always there is something I hold back. Always at
some point I will fail you, as you will fail me. But God never
lets you down. He gives Himself, complete and whole, perfect
love and perfect mercy and perfect wisdom. But only if I invite
Him-only if I make room for Him. He won't force Himself,
Child. He's got to be invited. A broken and a contrite heart-
that's what's needed -a humble heart...
"What about my father?" Arnie interrupted. “He found God
was not enough."
For a little Mum Hattie seemed to be bested. Down went
her hand, the palm going up and down, to her ankle, to her knee.
At first she seemed embarrassed. Then she looked up with that
ineffable smile, that goodness shining on her face. “If you will
excuse me for saying so,” she said, “your pa was just a little stiff;
just a little stiff in the intellectual regions." The smile widened.
"God likes backbone," she said, "but I think he don't care much
for bone in the head."
"Oh, you fool!" Arnie exclaimed impatiently, "You poor fool!"
We did not see Mum Hattie again. But I always had a card at
Christmas, sometimes from Harlem, sometimes from Africa. And
it was because the latest, the most recent card had come from
Harlem, that on the evening of my interview with Masha I called
Mum Hattie.
I was not sure she would be there; and even if she were it was
possible she could not be persuaded to come and help with Arnie.
My child had not been "polite and courteous" at that last inter-
view, and I knew that Mum Hattie felt that these qualities were
important.
But the voice that answered me when I called on the telephone
was just as warm, just as "big" with loving as it had always been.
"Oh!" she exclaimed. "Thank God to hear your voice."
I explained my predicament, not giving the details of Arnie's
illness (that would come later), but asking if she could go with
us to Bethel.
"I am not yet certain the doctors will let her go,” I said. “But I
[224]believe they will. And Mum Hattie. . . ." But I could not go
on. Quite clearly I could hear Masha's voice: "I would take her
away, Mrs. Braxton . . . for you know that even here we have
power you know we have power even here." And I
knew this was so.

"The truth is,” I said, and my voice trembled, “I . . . I am
