Thee. Thou knowest we are weak; we are helpless. But Thou art
the Creator. Teach us to pray what is right for this thy child.
Teach us what to do. I pray for all children in the world, for all
mothers and fathers; for this loving and anxious mother here.
For Jesus' sake and in His Name. Amen."
I looked up and saw their shining faces. Then Mum Hattie
reached to the table beside her cot and turned the pages of the
Bible she had taken from it. She gave the Book to Saul and said,
"Read it now-about the woman of Samaria."
Very simply, without any hypocritical protestations, he ac-
cepted the open Book from her. Then I understood why she had
asked him instead of reading the passage herself. For his beauti-
ful diction, his deep voice that was warm without imparting
any sentimentality to what he was reading, that was even without
any "expression" at all, gave the words-clear, lovely, serene-a
proper
and ordered cadence as he read the passage to us:
Then cometh he to a city of Samaria, which is called Sychar, near to
the parcel of ground that Jacob gave to his son Joseph. Now Jacob's
well was there. Jesus therefore, being wearied with his journey, sat
thus on the well; and it was about the sixth hour. There cometh a
woman of Samaria to draw water.
Jesus saith unto her, Give me to drink. (For his disciples were gone
away unto the city to buy meat.)
Then saith the woman of Samaria unto him; How is it that thou,
being a Jew, askest drink of me, which am a woman of Samaria? For
the Jews have no dealings with the Samaritans.
Saul de Leon looked up at us. "The rector of the church in
which I was baptized and confirmed," he said, "once preached a
sermon on this passage. He pointed out that the woman of Sa-
maria, like many of us when we are confronted with a living
Lord, tried to side-track Jesus on to a social or political question
in order to hide her own sins of which, in His presence, she had
become aware. But Jesus would not be diverted.”
[270]He continued reading:
Jesus answered and said unto her: If thou knewest the gift of God,
and who it is that saith to thee, Give me to drink; thou wouldst have
asked of him and he would have given thee living water.
The woman saith unto him: Sir, thou hast nothing to draw with, and
the well is deep. From whence then hast thou that living water? Art
thou greater than our father Jacob, which gave us the well, and drank
thereof himself, and his children, and his cattle?
Jesus answered and said unto her: Whosoever drinketh of this water
shall thirst again; But whosoever drinketh of the water that I shall
give him shall never thirst; but the water that I shall give him shall
be in him a well of water springing up into everlasting life.
The woman saith unto him: Sir, give me this water, that I thirst not,
neither come hither to draw.
Jesus saith unto her: Go, call thy husband, and come hither.
The woman answered and said: I have no husband.
Jesus saith unto her: Thou hast well said, I have no husband. For
thou hast had five husbands; and he whom thou now hast is not thy
husband; in that saidst thou truly.
"Look!" Mum Hattie whispered. I looked; we all looked, and
saw my child's head that had not moved from that position since
I had brought her home months before, begin to lift, as if she
wished to hear more clearly what was being read. I could not con-
trol the pounding of my heart, but with all my will forced myself
to turn my eyes away, knowing that by focusing them on her I
might arrest that slight movement, interrupt the delicate balance,
the faint stirring of life. Saul de Leon also diverted his glance
from her and began reading again just as if nothing had hap-
pened. But I noticed that his voice, that had been so without
emotion, trembled at first as he went on reading:
The woman saith unto him: Sir, I perceive that thou art a prophet.
Our fathers worshiped in this mountain; and ye say that in Jerusalem
is the place where men ought to worship.
Jesus saith unto her: Woman, believe me, the hour cometh when ye
shall neither in this mountain, nor yet in Jerusalem, worship the
[271]Father. Ye worship ye know not what; we know what we worship;
for salvation is of the Jews. But the hour cometh, and now is, when
the true worshipers shall worship the Father in spirit and in truth;
for the Father seeketh such to worship him. God is a spirit; and they
that worship him must worship him in spirit and in truth.
The woman saith unto him: I know that Messiah cometh, which is
called Christ; when he is come, he will tell us all things.
Jesus saith unto her: I that speak unto thee am he.
Softly he closed the Book. Fearfully I looked at my child.
Tremblingly I watched, trying not to do so openly. I was con-
scious of a slight movement that pulsed through her body. Her
hand lifted toward her cheek as if to brush back the hair that
covered her face. I felt myself in that hand, lifting it.
Then the telephone out in the hall rang sharply, breaking into
our concentration. My child's hand fell into her lap, her body
into the old rigid, sorrowful resignation, the unknowing, the
death in life; and I went out into the hall to answer the insistent
ringing and to say to Mr. Bland, the rector, that I would expect
him at four o'clock.
[272]Chapter XXXVI
Early that Sunday afternoon Saul de Leon drove Mum Hattie to
the station for her train. After saying good-by to them I turned
back into a house made even more desolate than it had been be-
fore, by the contrast to the stirring of life, of hope, that had
seemed to be there when they were within its walls.
The visit of the rector later on only accentuated my feeling of
hopelessness. At the early service that morning I had not noticed
his appearance, only his voice, which had a whining intonation
that made it sound as if he were continually complaining about
something. So when Mr. Bland arrived promptly at four o'clock,
and we introduced ourselves to each other, I was immediately
aware again of the impression his voice had made upon me.
He was a comparatively young man; that is, I judged, about
forty or so years of age; and as he seated himself in the living
room where I had taken him I saw that his face would have
been handsome and attractive if it had possessed any strength of
character. His brown eyes, slightly protuberant, gazed widely and
innocently on the world; and his square jaw, with the curved
mouth of a woman, smiled without sincerity. Yet, I thought, it is
possible, even probable, that I am unjust to him because Julie
has sent him; and I endeavored not to feel on the defensive or
to let "stubbornness" prevent me from listening with an open
mind to what he had to say.
"Your sister, Mrs. Guthrie, asked me to come," he said politely.
"Yes, I know," and in spite of my resolution my voice became
childishly defiant. "She wishes you to persuade me to allow my
child to be taken to a sanitarium."
[273]"Now, Mrs. Braxton," he reproved as we sat across from each
other in the living room, "no one is going to persuade you to do
anything you don't want to do. But sending your daughter to a
sanitarium would be no more than if she had an illness and you
allowed the doctors to take her to a hospital for an operation."
"But it is different," I said, emphasizing the word “is.” “For I
am the only one who understands why she is in this condition.
She needs love and care in her own home, and with someone who
understands."
A triumphant look came on his face. "You speak of love,” he
said, "but don't you ever hate your daughter?"
"Hate her?" I asked bewildered.
"Mrs. Braxton," his whining voice said, "I have three children,
and I can assure you, often I hate them. I see you are surprised.
But the psychiatrists tell us we must not repress these emotions.
So I say frankly, I hate them often, in fact most of the time."
“Then all I can say," I answered angrily, “if you hate them, it
is because you love yourself inordinately. Have the psychiatrists
ever told you that?"
He considered what I had said, and looked on my pityingly.
"We young people," he assured me, "have a more modern out-
look." Stretching out his arm as if to give himself "elbow room,"
he brought up his hand and smoothed the light brown hair that
came to a point on his forehead between two balding spots on
each side of it. "We are honest about our emotions," he said com-
placently.
Mum Hattie had said to me that I would go all around "Robin
Hood's Barn" to evade a quarrel, but she would not have
thought so could she have seen me that day. There was some-
thing about Mr. Bland, his smiling self-satisfaction, his complain-
ing voice (or perhaps it was simply my own agitated condition),
that made me wish to argue this point to the bitter end of it, if
an argument, which is never more than a contest of wills, ever
has any ending.
I did not even invite him, as I had intended to do, to have tea
or coffee, but our argument continued; he insisting that one rea-
son I did not wish to allow my child to be taken to a sanitarium
[274]was first, possessiveness, and then a sense of guilt because of my
unacknowledged hate for her. And I, unable to speak of the rea-
sons that had brought her to the state she was in, could only de-
fend myself and my love for my child.
But the argument at least served a purpose: that he did not
see Arnie. For he became more impatient with me as we contin-
ued and finally he rose, and as if holding himself away from
something that was unclean, he took up his hat and coat; and
without stopping to shake hands, and in a manner that was more
feminine than masculine, he "flounced" out of the door.
Yet after he had gone and I was alone in the kitchen prepar-
ing Arnie's supper, his accusations came back to me, and in my
confusion I wondered if he might have been correct in his ap-
praisal of the situation, and even if Julie could be so.
Did I hate my child? Did our mother hate Julie and myself?
Was all her selfless devotion spurious; her concern, not only for
our physical welfare but for our spiritual welfare, a calculated
hypocrisy? Was her grief for the baby who had died, and whom
she spoke about on his birthday as if he had been living, only a
false sentimentality? If all this was so then my life had no
meaning. Did I love my child? And if I did would I then allow
Julie to take over and do what was "best" for her?
All this confusion continued into the evening, and as I sat in
my child's room after helping her to bed and saw that her con-
dition was unchanged, the heavy nothingness that was in the
room canceled out the hope that had come that morning when
we had seen, during the reading of the story of the woman of
Samaria, an indication of life and consciousness in her. And de-
spair took possession of me.
In that house I had often experienced loneliness; I had often
known despair. But always I had been conscious of a past or of a
future. That evening it was just as if the dark night outside had
blotted out the past and absorbed the future, and had left me in
a void of nothingness. Loneliness may be relieved by tears of self-
pity. But mine was a dry despair that had no tears. Even the con-
sciousness of my child, sleeping in the bed near me, receded. I
was alone and helpless.
[275]In that condition and lifting up my arms, and without know-
ing at all that I was going to do so, I cried aloud, “Oh God, if
there is a God, please help me!"
Instantly, and I say instantly because it was immediate, a sense
of ineffable peace entered the room. It was within me and yet
without. Love took possession of me so that the whole room was
filled with it—a greater love than I had ever known, greater and
more secure than ever my mother's love had been. I was bathed
in it. All confusion was swept away by its power. The negative,
evil presence that had been for so long a heavy burden in the
room was gone. My body became relaxed, absorbing in every
atom the sweetness, the joy, the peace and the love that was in
me and that surrounded me. Just as I was I lay down on Mum
Hattie's cot and went to sleep.
[276]Chapter XXXVII
For the first time in months I slept that night as easily, as relaxed
as a baby. I was even smiling when I woke. And then an agony
of apprehension came in me as I remembered that at the hour of
eleven Julie would appear to take Arnie away.
And yet something was different. Though I said to myself that
the experience of the evening before might well have been the
product of hysteria brought on by my confused and agitated
state of mind, I could not quite believe that this was so. For it was
still present, like a remembered joy. I longed nostalgically for it
to come again. I felt a homesickness for it, and knew that ever
thereafter my life would be a longing, a searching until that love
I had experienced possessed me again. And as I made up the
beds I said to my reason, to my skepticism, "That Presence was as
real as this pillow in my hand."
The house was not yet done when I looked at the clock and
found that I had little time before Julie might be expected, and
my thoughts turned that way. It had always been natural for me
to give in to my sister and to continue to love and admire her.
It would be simple enough, now, and bring a kind of peace, to
let her have her way. Should I do so? Once, during the morning,
an impulse came to call Saul de Leon, but I knew he would be in
his classes and even if he had not been, there was still the fact
that I must meet what Mum Hattie called this "mortal combat"
alone.
Just before eleven the doorbell rang and Julie pushed open
the door and came in. We met and embraced and I, wondering,
knew that I loved my sister.
[277]"Leave the door open," she said as she removed her coat and
we went into the living room. "The doctor should be here any
moment."
"You haven't changed at all," I said almost gaily. "If anything
you're younger than when I saw you last." For it was true. She
moved energetically, like a very young person, and the smile on
her face, the short upper lip giving it, as always, a special fascina-
tion, was eager and assured.
"How is Arnie?" she asked.
"About the same," I answered.
She looked at me critically. "But what has happened to you?"
she demanded.
"Why . . ." I said, "nothing."
"There's something different about you," she said uneasily, for
she was always impatient with what she could not at once under-
stand. “You look as if you were in love,” she laughed. For a mo-
ment she was thoughtful, still gazing at me, and then she said,
"Have you heard from David?"
"David!" I exclaimed in astonishment. "I haven't heard from
him in fifteen years. Why do you ask?"
“Oh, nothing. I wondered. You look so different," she com-
plained.
At that moment the doctor arrived and Julie hurried out into
the hall to greet him. We had not sat down and when the doctor
came into the living room all remained standing. He looked at
me curiously and I knew that Julie must have confided in him
her difficulties with me and my "stubbornness." As I returned his
gaze I accepted the fact that he must have been given a biased
interpretation of the situation, but was agreeably surprised to
find him quite friendly. He was somewhere in his fifties, with
gray hair cut short, and his blue eyes did not probe me for hidden
hates as Mr. Bland's had done, but twinkled cheerfully; and his
amiable smile seemed real and not assumed for the occasion.
"Well, Dr. Fraser," Julie said briskly, “we know you haven't
much time, so we can go in at once to my niece. I have given you
all the facts."
"If you will show me," he said courteously to me. "And I be-
[278]lieve," he added, speaking to Julie, “it would be better if I see
her alone."
“Oh,” Julie said as if astonished, and then, recovering, she took
his arm and led him toward the door across the hall. He stepped
back, however, to let me go ahead, and I went before them and
opened the door to that room. I was certain he could feel, as I
felt, the pressure of the silence that came on us as soon as the
door was opened. And as I looked at my child, bent over, unheed-
ing, and glanced at the doctor's face as he concentrated his gaze
on that still figure in the chair beside the bed, the old hopeless-
ness came over me, for I saw her with his eyes.
Julie took my arm and we crossed the hall together into the
living room. I said that I would make coffee and went into the
kitchen. "Ah," I told myself bitterly as I prepared the percolator,
"how firm you have been. How righteously you have defended
your child and your home." I did not even know I had spoken
aloud until Julie came to the door and asked, "What did you
say?"
"Will you help me, please?" I answered. "Bring in the silver
tray from the dining room." She did so and presently we had the
coffee with bread and butter slices on the tea table in the living
room. I had just poured out my sister's cup of coffee when the
doctor came in. I gave him a cup after he had sat down; and with
hands that I was surprised to find did not tremble, served my
own.
Julie glanced at the doctor inquiringly. “Well, Doctor?" she
asked.
"Yes," he said, "I agree with you. Absolutely."
"You see, Ann," Julie said eagerly. "Now when can Arnie go?
I have my car here. We can take her at once."
There was a heavy silence. Both Julie and the doctor were
looking at me expectantly.
"I am sorry," I said quietly, not looking at Julie, not looking
at the doctor, "she is not going."
"But I assure you, Mrs. Braxton.
•
the doctor began.
"Nonsense, Ann." Julie set her coffee cup with a clatter on the
table. "Of course she is going."
[279]I turned to the doctor. "Doctor," I said, "I am this girl's
mother. Isn't it true that you cannot take her without my con-
sent?"
"Yes, Mrs. Braxton, that is true, for in her present condition
she cannot make that decision for herself. But I assure you. . .
"And," I said firmly, "I mean it when I say she is not to go."
“Oh, Ann,” Julie protested, "can't you understand? It is for
her own good. Do you want her to be well? Or do you want to
keep her here, possessing her . . . never willing to let her
go.
99
"She is not to go,” I repeated stubbornly. But as I did so a pain-
ful, agonizing hurt came in me, the mortal pain of separation, of
being alone, and of fearing to be alone. Before this it had always
been Julie who had separated herself from me. Now it was I who,
in full consciousness of what I was doing, with clarity and preci-
sion, must cut myself off from her. I clutched the handle of the
percolator as if that could help sustain me, as a person in some
kind of physical agony grasps any solid that will aid him to bear
it.
Again I said to Julie and the doctor, "I am sorry. This is my
decision."
After the doctor had gone Julie, who had seen him to the door,
returned and sat down beside me. I looked at her and looking,
saw the rather cruel expression that in the past I had known so
well (and to which I had always submitted), as the preliminary
to conscious spite come on her face and in her eyes.
"There is something,” she said in the soft, caressing voice I had
always dreaded, "I hadn't meant to tell you. But I think I should.
Did you know that Greta has left David?"
I shook my head, voiceless, staring into her face.
"Oh yes," she went on, still in that soft, confiding voice. "Some
time ago. She is at Mars Hill now. She is my secretary. She came
back from England to get a divorce."
"A divorce.
... from David?" I repeated dully.
"Yes. Another man. After all she is twenty years younger than
David."
"Eighteen," I corrected automatically.
Julie shrugged. "Eighteen, then," she said. "Greta has done
[280]all she could. She has even given David cause for divorce to make
it easy for him."
"You mean," I asked, "she is sleeping with the other man?"
"Oh, Ann," Julie said reproachfully, “there you go with that
puritanical attitude. There is a new morality in the world today
-new ethics. All the old ideals are outworn, discarded by people
who are really progressive. You are like David," she said. "He
hasn't been decent or civilized at all about it. He was quite un-
pleasant. And then about a year ago he began drinking heavily
-getting progressively worse. I thought I should bring Greta
away. She was almost ill with his reproaches and his drinking.
He even followed her to this country and insisted on seeing her
in New York. But I put a stop to that. Where he is now, or what
he is doing no one knows. What a mess you have made of
other people's lives, Ann," she added.
•
• •
"Yes," I agreed calmly, "I suppose I have."
My words were passive, even humble. But I scarcely knew what
I was saying. I was seeing my husband and Greta that far-off
morning in the study of the rectory saying to me that they wished
to be married. And as it came I found the remembrance of that
time as immediately real, as tenaciously present, as if it had hap-
pened yesterday. And the hurt I thought had been forgotten, or
at least covered over successfully by new experiences and by the
years, was still alive-terribly, outrageously alive. Also I was con-
scious of a bitter satisfaction, a saying to David in my thoughts:
Ah, now you know. Now it has happened to you.
[281]Chapter XXXVIII
From that first Sunday in February when Saul de Leon had
brought me from the early service he became a regular visitor
in our home. Never had there been anyone, except Mum Hattie,
more welcome. He possessed the natural benevolence of the best
of his race. And this benevolence, this love for his fellow man,
widened and deepened and leavened by his love and loyalty to
"Messiah," had produced a singularly endearing personality.
Having known many other intellectuals it was difficult for me to
remember that once he had been one of those complex and com-
plicated individuals; for now, without losing any of the wisdom
of sophistication or the wit that made for laughter, he appeared
to have crossed over to a place beyond smartness and arrogance
to the simplicity of sincerity and truth.
What particularly impressed me was his realistic approach to
the nature of man. He was neither gullible, for he had experi-
enced too much of human nature to deceive himself, nor was he
bitter about human failings. He saw a continual and everlasting
struggle between good and evil occurring within the hearts of
men, in nations and in the world. (He believed in the reality of
a devil.) And this evil included, as he said, his own more "respect-
able" sins such as pride and what Juliana of Norwich called “un-
charity." As his visits became more frequent a favorite quotation
of my father's came often into my mind: Mercy and Truth are
met together; Righteousness and Peace have kissed each other.
For this young man seemed to embody in himself these excellent
qualities.
There were many evenings of rich exchange of thought as we
[282]sat in my child's room. And I could not refrain from contrasting
these evenings with those of more than twelve years before when
some inner monitor had withheld its full approbation of the con-
clusions to which Arnie and I had come, and as I followed Arnie's
leading. Now there was a conviction that I was in the process of
discovering a truth which was singularly my own. And this truth
did not depend upon the finite whim or whimsies of some human
being; could not be contained, bounded, or defined by a social
statistic, but was eternal; so that there was no end to the possibili-
ties of variety, of the charm of unexpectedness, of growth and
joy, here and hereafter. There was a "romantic" promise, a sense
of adventure in the realization of a pilgrim's progress that had
begun, with me, in the discovery of a small history book in a sec-
ond-hand bookshop; and which was continued on the morning
I attended church and met Saul de Leon there.
Whenever he came to supper we had our meal on trays in the
room with Arnie. At first I would not have it so, and would give
my child her meal before he arrived. For I could not bear for an
outsider to see the manner in which she conveyed the food from
her plate to her mouth (though even this was a sign of progress),
so that it spilled over and I was always obliged to clean her after-
ward. Then one evening I expressed my gratitude for the
strength and comfort he had given us. "But," I added, "you must
not let us impose on you."
For the first time since I had known him Saul appeared to be
embarrassed and confused. A flush came up in his cheeks. He
glanced at Arnie.
"I thought you knew," he said. "I have not tried to hide it."
I looked at him enquiringly.
"The first day I met you," he said, "I told you I had been in
love with your daughter."
"Yes," I agreed, "I remember that you did." A painful stab of
regret came in me as I looked at him, so eager, strong and alive,
and then at my child: pitifully thin, emaciated, and rigid in that
death in life.
He saw that my eyes were centered on her and the pain I felt
must have showed on my face, for he said protestingly, "I don't
see her like that, Mrs. Braxton. I see her as the fascinating girl I
[283]loved . . . that I love.
...
This is not some romantic notion—
a memory of first love. Never could I forget her. Never. Certainly
at times . . . and I have been no saint. Only . do not feel
grateful. . . ."
.. .
It was an awkward confession, and yet so sincere, so real, noth-
ing could have kept the thankful tears from springing to my eyes.
But in the next moment I was able to laugh at them, and we
were both comforted. From that time on I began to look upon
him not as an outsider, but as a person who never for one mo-
ment stood aside in criticism, but who as Mum Hattie said, “put
his shoulder under the burden.”
Often after that he would drive down and stay with Arnie
early on a Sunday morning so that I might attend the first serv-
ice. Then we would breakfast together and he would attend the
later one.
I was outspoken in my opinion of the rector and he agreed
with me that Mr. Bland's personality and his voice were not
such as to make the church service more beautiful and real. Nev-
ertheless, he said, these qualities in a priest did not in the least
detract from the beauty and privilege contained in a service to
the worship of God. He also said that in the Thirty-nine Articles
this contingency was provided for, and he read to me that section
which is headed, "Of the Unworthiness of the Minister, which
Hinders not the Effect of the Sacraments." I said that previous
to that evening I had never read the Thirty-nine Articles. He
laughed and answered that I was like those people who had al-
ways lived in New York, but had never visited its most famous
places; while he was like one who, coming from a distance,
after a time was able to tell those born there more about their
city than they knew themselves.
On those evenings when he was with us he would, before he
left, read some of the Family Prayers and then say a spontaneous
prayer for my child's health. I envied the apparent ease with
which he spoke so simply and trustingly to God, and though I
longed to do so I could not. To call myself a "miserable offender"
along with the congregation, in the beautiful prayers of the
Church, was one thing; but in my early years, without anyone
[284]exactly stating it, I had absorbed a feeling that it was not consist-
ent with our reserve and dignity to pray spontaneously or, out-
side the convention of the Church's prayers, to admit myself a
sinner. And though I knew that to many persons, such as my
mother and father, those prayers were a burning affirmation of
faith and truth, I was just as certain that under the circum-
stances, because I had been a prodigal and had wasted my in-
heritance for many years, for me it was necessary to state my posi-
tion as a sinner in a spontaneous acknowledgment. And one eve-
ning after Saul had prayed my bowed head sank lower, and I
stammered the words said by the prodigal son: "Father, I have
sinned against heaven and before Thee. . . ." After that I seemed
to be released and was able to express (as if speaking to a loving
Father) all my anxieties aloud and to ask forgiveness.
There were evenings when Saul and I talked of other things.
He, also, had read Alexander Hertzen's memoirs, and I brought
out the six small volumes so that he might read passages from
them while we sat with Arnie in her room.
"Ignorance, ignorance alone," he read from Hertzen's letters
written in the year 1850 to Father Pitcherin, “is the cause of pau-
perism and slavery . . . science, nothing but science can correct
that. ." and much else in the same vein. And the priest's
•
answer:
What will happen when your culture gains the day? For you science is
everything, alpha and omega. Not that broad science which embraces
all the faculties of man, everything visible and invisible . . . but a
limited narrow science which analyses and dissects matter, and knows
nothing else. . . Now they will drag us men of prayer into the pub-
lic market place and there ask: "Why do you flee from our society?
Go make speeches in the market place; go and preach political
economy. Paradise is here on earth-let us eat and drink for tomor-
row we die." That is what moves me to horror. For where are we to
find refuge from the tyranny of matter which is gaining more and
more mastery of all?
He was an ardent student of history. He said that when the
Greeks abandoned their spiritual heritage, the worship of the
gods, which was all they knew, and had made democracy a god,
[285]their civilization had perished. He had made an especially pro-
found study of the life of this country both in its more evil as-
pects (when he was eager to find any criticism), and later.
I told him about discovering the little history book on Fourth
Avenue in New York and how beautiful the words "created" and
"Creator" had appeared to me. As I had become, at that mo-
men of clearness of vision, he also had become convinced that
this country was founded upon a belief in God, and that the
Founding Fathers had no thought that this basis would ever be
discarded in favor of another. That by freedom of religion they
had meant no more than that no citizen should ever be penalized
because of lack of belief or for religious convictions. That the
Christian religion was the basis upon which this country was
founded he felt there was no question; that our form of govern-
ment was a by-product of that belief; and that only as we put the
Creator first might we expect to be politically creative. He re-
minded me of the meaning of the great seal of our nation, a pyra-
mid representing society with the eye of God at its apex; above
it the promise that God watches over us and below “A new or-
der of the centuries." He even took a dollar bill from his pocket
and showed me the great seal, something I had not known about
before. And then the words: In God We Trust.
By this time I had become accustomed to the notebook Saul
carried about in the capacious pocket of his beautiful but worn
tweed jacket, and when he would bring it out at some point in
our talks I always knew that from the pages of that book I would
receive, like a flash of lightning showing all the surroundings
but centering in one place, and like thunder in my ears, a new in-
sight, a fresh illumination. So from this notebook he read to me
a quotation from Thomas Jefferson: "The God who gave us life
gave us liberty. . . ." And another. When Jefferson was dying he
pointed to the Bible which rested on the table beside his bed and
said to his physician: "This book, sir, is the rock upon which our
Republic rests." And there were many more.
He felt that neither Jefferson nor Washington, nor Lincoln
believed in the "infallibility" of the people. That neither Jeffer-
son nor Washington would have been so gullible and lacking in
knowledge of human nature as to issue certain opportunistic
[286]statements, as one president had done, which gave to people the
idea that human rights are free for the grabbing. That the
Founding Fathers had looked very realistically upon the possibil-
ities in each of us of love for power for its own sake, and so had
prepared certain checks within the Constitution. Out came the
worn notebook and he read an excerpt from Washington's Fare-
well Address:
A just estimate of the love of power and proneness to abuse it, which
predominates in the human heart is sufficient to satisfy us of the .
necessity of reciprocal checks in the exercise of political power; by
dividing and distributing it in different despositories, and constituting
each the guardian of the public weal against invasion by the others.
One evening I told him of the plans (when the "correct his-
toric moment" should arrive), to abolish all forms of religion in
the public life of this country; plans that had already been suc-
cessful to the extent of banishing the worship of God from the
public schools. Next, singly, easily, so that the main body of citi-
zens would not be aware of what had been done, and with the
compliance of the Supreme Court, would come abolition of all
other religious practices.
Again the notebook (which had become a source of amuse-
ment to us, as well as a source of inspiration to me), was brought
out and again the excerpt was from the Farewell Address:
Of all dispositions and habits which lead to political prosperity, Re-
ligion and Morality are indispensable supports. In vain would that
man claim the tribute of patriotism, who would labor to subvert these
great pillars of human happiness, these firmest props of the duties of
men and citizens. A volume could not trace all their connexions with
private and public felicity. Let it simply be asked, where is the se-
curity for property, for reputation, for life, if the sense of religious
obligation desert the oaths which are the instruments of investigation
in Courts of Justice? And let us with caution indulge the supposition,
that morality can be maintained without religion. Reason and experi-
ence both forbid us to expect that national morality can prevail in
exclusion of religious principle.
"I believe," Saul commented, "there is not one of those who
prefer what they call the 'historic challenge' of Socialism who
has recognized the manifold but often invisible protections
[287]which a Christian nation has thrown about its citizens, nor the
liberty of which so many have taken advantage even while they
ridicule its Source."
He said he had heard people speak of "our way of life" and
give that statement various interpretations. But he had come to
the conclusion that God's eternal order, set against man-made
confusion both within the individual and within the nation, is
our way of life. This, he said, is its greatness and its challenge;
and just as Sir Wilfred Grenfell said about Christianity, it is not
easy to practice. Real men and women are needed for it. Always
before in our history there had been a core of such men and
women all over the country who are unafraid to state what they
believed; who were humble before God and, trusting in His wis-
dom, were positive toward man; who were stable in their faith,
secure in their standards, and confident in the principles by
which they lived; and that this faith must be forever preserved
in the public life of the nation (and in its international rela-
tions), or we would indeed become lost in chaos and confusion.
"Our forefathers recognized the fact," he said, "that a political
body, the nation, has a soul, a spirit, as well as the individual
body."
As he said this I remembered a directive a foreign member of
Arnie's Apparatus had passed on to her, and that she had re-
peated to me: "First," he told her, "we corrupt the soul of a na-
tion. After that the body is no problem."
A trembling shook me as I realized (and perhaps much more
than Saul could), how near we were approaching that chaos and
confusion about which he had spoken; for I knew that for the
past twelve years one of our duties had been to help to create
that confusion, and that in order to escape it people would wel-
come any tyranny, or any group that promised to bring order to
a bewildered and Godless nation.
I understood more clearly how a nation could become Godless
as I reviewed my own experience of the past years when I had,
one by one, abandoned practices and habits of thought and of
belief, and had accepted others in their place. Now I found that
each day I must travel that road again, back the way I had come,
submitting to the Creator all the ideas that had possessed my
[288]mind for so long. It was not easy to do this. It was painful to re-
linquish each treasured Socialist belief and set another in its
place; to love God with my mind as well as with my heart and
soul-for the mind is the last fortress man will surrender to his
Creator. During this process I understood, as I had not done be-
fore, what Mum Hattie had meant when she said we had sold
our birthright at San Francisco; and understood also an expres-
sion I had heard so many times it had become dulled in its mean-
ing, and not comprehended until now: Eternal vigilance is the
price of liberty.
As my life and thoughts became more ordered, more centered
in God, I began to experience an inner freedom I had never
known before; and with it hope for my child began to grow, as
if from roots firmly fixed in the dark earth a tree was sending up
a shoot filled with healthy, life-giving sustenance.
[289]Chapter XXXIX
For a time I felt a great happiness. Then one evening in March
I was alone when I heard the old bell on the front door give its
preliminary whirr of distress and age, and then its faint ring. I
was surprised because the only visitor who could be expected was
Saul de Leon and he had told me there was a school meeting.
As I went calmly to the door, however, it occurred to me that for
some reason the meeting had not been held, and it was Saul's
face I expected to see.
But standing before me, with the porch light I had turned on
shining upon him, making shadows about him and a shaft of
light across his face, I saw David, my former husband. When I
had last seen him, at the hearing for our divorce, he had been
impeccably clothed. Then he was forty-three years old; now he
was fifty-eight. But it was not the years I saw, though these were
evident. Lines of bitterness tugged at his nostrils, pulling them
down toward a mouth that had once been clearly and cleanly out-
lined and firm with character, but which now spread in a slack
sensuality without shape or character at all. The clothes he wore
hung on him so loosely they seemed not his own, and they were
unclean. Thick, uncut hair met a stubble, almost a growth, of
beard on his cheeks. His eyes were partly closed against the light
I had turned on; and as he wavered and put out a hand against
the side of the doorway to support himself, a would-be ingrati-
ating smile came on his face as he said, “Well, Ann, the prodigal
has returned."
Still with that hand supporting him he said with a pitiful at-
tempt at dignity, "I have come to visit my daughter." He glanced
[290]up at me (for he was not able to hold himself erect), from red-
rimmed eyes. "Our daughter," he amended. And then he
laughed.
I believe it was his laughter, though it was probably the laugh-
ter of one who is trying to placate another, that roused such bit-
ter anger in me. It took possession of me physically as if some
outer force I could not control was pounding upon my heart and
lungs, on every organ of my body, and in my veins.
Certainly I should have known this might happen and ex-
pected it. As soon as it was made plain to David in New York
that he could not see his present wife there he would come on to
Bethel, where it would be easier to impose himself on her than
in a large city. But even if I had faced this fact-that he would
inevitably come-it would not have occurred to me that the situ-
ation at Mars Hill had anything to do with me or with Arnie.
Bitterness rose in me as I realized that Julie and Greta must
have turned him out of Mars Hill and that he had come to me,
relying on his remembrance of my easy nature, my dislike of a
quarrel.
"You did not come to see your daughter," I said to the un-
kempt stranger who had laughed. "You came to Bethel to find
your wife and persuade her to return to you. And be certain of
this. You will never see Arnie. Never." I was telling myself: He
is Greta's responsibility. She is his wife; let her deal with him.
And I tried to close the door. But he put out his hand and held
the door open forcibly, pressing his body against it. Tears of self-
pity came in his eyes. "I have nowhere else to go, Ann," he said.
"You have a wife," I reminded him, holding fast to the door;
and immediately became aware of the ridiculousness of the situa-
tion, and ashamed of it. Grudgingly I said, "Well, come in," and
loosed my hold on the door so that he could enter. "I'll give you
coffee,” I said, “and that is all I can do."
He followed me to the dining room where he sat down at the
table, resting his elbows on it as if utterly weary, with his face in
his hands. I brought in the coffee and placed a cup before him
and poured it full, leaving it black and strong. He did not raise
his face from his hands, and I wondered if he were asleep. But
when the telephone rang he raised his head.
[291]"That's your sister calling," he muttered. "Your she-devil sis-
ter." And as he spoke, running the words together and confusing
the aspirates, this lack of control pointed up more shockingly
than anything else his deterioration, for he had always been
proud (as I had been) of the beauty of his voice and his clear
enunciation.
In the hallway I put the receiver to my ear. “Hello, Ann,”
Julie's voice said.
I had known that she was in town, but had not expected to
hear from her. For since the day when I had frustrated her well-
intentioned desire to take Arnie to a sanitarium, Julie had not
communicated with me except by one card from New York.
"You certainly come and go," I said briefly, the anger I felt to-
ward David, toward Julie, toward Greta, harsh in my voice.
"I told you Greta is establishing residence here for her di-
vorce," she said impatiently.
"No, you didn't tell me," I answered.
"I must have done so," she insisted. "We have been back and
forth, flying to New York or Washington. But this is our base.
Now, Ann, I must tell you. David has been here this evening.
He was impossible as usual. But he turned sentimental when
Greta refused to see him and said he had come down from New
York, not to see Greta but to visit Arnie. I told him she is ill and
tried to prevent him. Has he come?"
I closed my mouth stubbornly, refusing to answer.
"I asked you, has he come?" Julie's voice persisted.
"Yes," I said, and would not say more.
"Then get him out of the house immediately," she ordered.
"He will only make trouble for you and all of us. And if you
can't make him go,” she added, “telephone me and I will come.”
Perhaps it was the stubbornness about which Julie had com-
plained so often that made me, as I put down the receiver, re-
solve that for that night at least, I would not put David out. And
when I returned to the dining room and found him asleep with
his head on the table a reluctant compassion came in me.
That head bowed so low on his arms, the uncombed hair with
streaks of gray in it, made me feel as if I were standing at the for-
gotten grave, the neglected and weed-grown grave, of a person
[292]once loved and cherished. He had always been so strong, so
sure, never hesitating over a decision. I remembered something
Mum Hattie had said to Arnie: "Your pa was just a little stiff."
And I wondered if in getting away from what he called the "for-
mality" of the Church he had only been trying to get away from
himself.
With the resolve that had come in me to allow him to remain
for that night, came the necessity for action. The folding cot on
which Mum Hattie had slept in Arnie's room had been moved
back to the storeroom. In the storeroom I unfolded this cot and
brought sheets and a pillowcase from the linen closet and made
up the bed. As I did so I glanced up toward the shelf on which
was the wicker basket that held the figures of the crèche that
David had brought so long ago back from Bavaria, and under it
the black suitcase that contained his surplice and stole and the
articles for vesting a table for private Communion. Quickly I
averted my eyes from these reminders of the past and went stol-
idly about preparing the place so that he might occupy it for that
one night. I did not plan for tomorrow. It was enough to force
myself to do what had to be done, and looking neither toward
the past nor toward the future, set my attention on what was
immediately at hand.
In the dining room I found David still asleep and had diffi-
culty rousing him. He leaned so heavily on me as I supported
him into the storeroom I thought it would be impossible for me
to bear up under that burden. Also the odors of stale whiskey and
filth were almost overpowering.
During our life together he had never been seriously ill, but
there had been times when he was sick with a cold and I had
cared for him with anxiety, but also with a kind of joy because
he needed me. Now, as he lay on the cot and I removed his shoes
and outer clothing and got him between the sheets there was
no anxiety, no joy, only heaviness and disgust; and as I closed
the door of the storeroom and, after looking at my sleeping child,
prepared myself for bed, rebellion came in my heart; for it
seemed a bitter reward from the God I had begun to know and
love that He had allowed this added burden to be placed upon
me.
[293]A dozen times during the night I rose and tried to read, but
like Jacob could only wrestle with my angel. At last, and without
making a light, I sat beside the open window. The day before
(as I always did about this time of the year), I had hired a man
to plow up the garden. Faintly through the window there came
to me the smell of newly turned earth. I could picture in my
mind the large square of grass at the back of the house from
which steps led up into the garden, and where I had seen that
day a few crocuses in bloom and the sturdy spikes of daffodils
just thrusting themselves out of the earth. The daffodils sur-
rounding the grass plot and also in the garden, and hundreds in
the fields beyond, were one of my greatest joys in the spring; and
as I sat by the window I seemed to see them already in bloom in
all their glory. The wisteria and jessamine were on the other
side of the house, but I thought their scent also reached me. Out-
side my window was the outline of the large pomegranate bush
that I knew was just beginning to put out tiny green leaves. Its
branches moved in a breeze that had come up with the pre-dawn
movement of the air, and it seemed to me I could feel, even
hear, the movement of growing things.
When we were bringing Arnie from New York to Bethel Mum
Hattie had said to me, "I think you'll have to take your hands
off Arnie," and I asked, "You mean take her back to the hos-
pital?”
"No," she said. "I mean like Abraham took his hands off
Isaac."
I then told her that Abraham had always seemed to me to be a
fool. Over the more recent past he had been the very symbol of
my rebellion against the idea of a God who would demand such
a needless sacrifice. When I said that she looked at me a long mo-
ment. "Not needless," she said. "God had to know Abraham
trusted."
At the time this meant nothing to me. Now, as I sat at the win-
dow, with the scent of flowers and of the turned earth brought to
me on a soft breeze that cooled my cheeks, what she had meant
came with full force into my mind, and I wondered I had not un-
derstood it before. The emphasis was not upon Abraham, nor
upon Isaac. It lay in the word trust. Abraham trusted.
[294]I laid
my head on my arms on the window sill and wept until
the bitter rebellion had somewhat abated. And then, though I
was not able to say it with the full consent of my deepest con
sciousness, nevertheless I repeated: "Not my will. Thy will. Not
my will. Because I trust. I trust." And a kind of peace, like the
broken peace of the dawn outside, came upon me.
[295]Chapter XL
On the morning after David had come, because of lack of sleep
the night before I rose later than usual, and opened the door of
the storeroom expecting to find him; but he was not there. I
wondered if he had gone to Mars Hill, but could only wait to
find out.
Later as I was having breakfast with my child and saying to
God the Father "I put her in thy hands, I trust," and with, I be-
lieve, a fuller inner consent than I had the night before, I heard
a sound in the dining room and went across the hall.
David must have gone in the early morning to the station and
brought his bag to the house. For in the dining room I saw him
with a handsome but worn English traveling bag open beside
him on the floor. From under soiled shirts and other clothing he
was taking some bottles and setting them carefully in one section
of the sideboard. From the last bottle, which was half full, he
pulled the cork and drank from it.
Then he glanced up and saw me standing in the doorway. A
shamed look came on his face at first. But in a moment the look
changed and he waved the bottle toward me, and miserably I
heard him speak with the cocky assurance that had been so much
an indication of his changed attitude toward everything during
the time when he was preparing to leave the Church.
"You've got a nice place here, Ann," he said. “Nice place.
Who'd have thought when I left Bethel I'd ever come back and
call this home. I'll tell you one thing, though," he said confiden-
tially, “never did understand why your father left Mars Hill and
[296]all that money to Julie." He shook his head deprecatingly.
"Never could understand."
I did not remind him that when my father made his will I was
married and Julie was not, and that he had left me a small in-
come as well as the house in which I was now living. He knew all
this, and I felt he was only trying to "get on the good side of
me," and I said coldly, “I think you should go to the bathroom
and shave."
“Oh, certainly, certainly," he said with an ingratiating smile
that shamed me more than his other attitude had done. He be-
gan to scramble about, stuffing the clothes he had pulled out of
the suitcase back into it and trying to gather all up into his arms.
And all the while he was smiling that dreadful, humiliating, in-
gratiating smile and saying, “Certainly, certainly, certainly. . .
Then he set the traveling bag down again and began to search
in it for his shaving kit. While he was doing this I hurried to
lock Arnie's door that led into the hall and the door between
her room and the bath. For I was determined that he should
never be allowed to see Arnie. If she had been well it would have
been a different matter; she could then have made her own deci-
sion. As I closed and locked the doors I felt a secret pleasure in
the thought that though he had moved in on us-into the house
he so confidently called home, but which was not his home—I
could at least prevent him from seeing my child.
Returning to the dining room I showed David where to go and
he left with his shaving kit. Bending down I took the soiled shirts
and underwear from his suitcase and carried them to the back
porch where, the evening before, I had set the soiled laundry
that was to go out that day. Then I lifted the bag and left it in
the storeroom, and made up the bed.
It was Saul de Leon who persuaded me to allow David to re-
main. We had been so peaceful there and this intrusion terrified
and confused me. I wanted him gone.
"What good can come of it?" I said miserably.
Saul looked at me with compassion; and in his face I saw pain
that was not his pain, but my own misery and bitterness mirrored
in it, so that I did not feel alone.
"Is that for us to decide?" he asked.
[297]"It is for me to decide," I said indignantly.
He thought a moment and said, "Yes, it is for you to decide. I
should not urge you against your will."
Then I remembered the words I had said aloud not long be-
fore: "Not my will . . . I trust. . . ." Also I had learned enough
of Saul's character, of the way his mind worked, as well as his
heart, to be confident that he did not act carelessly, or sentimen-
tally, or even from good nature primarily. For some reason
known only to himself he felt it was right for David to remain.
Suddenly our argument about it became not a contest of wills, as
it had been before; but something extra, some illumination had
come into the situation that lightened it for me. I felt that a
prayer had been said, though no word had been spoken.
"Well then," I agreed. "But he shall not see Arnie."
In the days that followed whenever he could be in the house
Saul helped me with David. But neither Saul nor I was able to
curb his love for the whiskey with which somehow he managed
to keep himself supplied. He did not change the habit of drink-
ing that began early in the morning and continued through the
day until toward night he was usually an unknowing, sodden
wreck. Nor did he cease to disturb me by his manner, which at
times was bitter and reproachful (as when he said I had taught
Arnie to hate him and was now preventing him from seeing her),
or was humiliatingly ingratiating, or full of a cocky assurance.
In one drunken mood he confidently asserted that Arnie must
be sent to a sanitarium. "You might not like to think so," he said
aggressively, "but I have a father's authority and I can use it.
Julie will help. Julie is a bitch, but she's right about Arnie."
I don't know what, all this while, he was thinking (or doing),
about Greta-who, with much more firmness than I seemed to
possess, apparently refused to see him. But after he had asserted
his authority about Arnie I became truly frightened; and a bit-
ter, spiteful struggle arose about the doors to Arnie's room,
which I continued to keep closed against him. I would hear him
turning the knobs of the doors which I had carefully locked
while it was necessary for me to be in the kitchen, and would feel
a malicious satisfaction over his frustrated attempts to reach Ar-
nie. Once when I thought him asleep in the storeroom he slipped
[298]past me, and I reached the door I had inadvertently left unlocked
just in time to prevent him from going in. For several days I even
stayed away from the garden, fearing that if I left the house he
might find a way into my child's room.
Then another factor entered the situation and made it more
complicated and confusing for me. On the evenings when he
could be there, Saul began to sit in the living room or wherever
David had settled down with his bottle and they would talk.
What they discussed I do not know. Often after Saul had gone I
would find that he had put David to bed, so that I would not
have to do so. In this I felt his never-failing thoughtfulness. But
in another direction there was a growing conviction that I was
being left alone to bear this burden. I had not told Saul anything
of the struggle over the doors to Arnie's room, feeling justified
in my position. Especially I could not speak of it because as the
days went on there was, growing up between Saul and David, an
unmistakable understanding, a sort of comradeship it was diffi-
cult for me to accept. It seemed to me that Saul was being neither
fair nor loyal to me, nor, as a matter of fact, to Arnie, as he re-
turned David's hearty greeting with an arm about his shoulder,
and they went off together to what David (delighting in it) called
their “Bull sessions." And for the first time I resented Saul's pres-
ence in the house.
I wondered how it would all end; wondered, also, why I al-
lowed David to remain. I owed him nothing. And each day he
was becoming more difficult, behaving as if the house were in-
deed his own home. I could not forget that he had a wife and
that this woman of forty, this Greta, was the reason he had come
to Bethel; and though I knew she was more often in New York
or Washington with Julie than she was here, in my mind I de-
manded of her that she take the responsibility for her husband,
not I. Also, I thought, David had never by one word hinted at
any regret or remorse for the sorrow he had brought upon us
fifteen years before.
All these thoughts were being reviewed in my mind (and not
for the first time), one evening when Saul was away at a meeting,
David in the living room, and I alone with Arnie, leaving the
door unlocked as I always did when I was with her. I was be-
[299]coming more righteously indignant as the lonely evening went
on, and heard no sound outside the door into the hall, when sud-
denly it was kicked open so violently it swung back with a loud
bang against the wall. I saw my child start at the sound of the
crash, but had no time to consider that she had never, except
once, reacted to any sound before. For her father stood in the
doorway glaring at me with hatred on his face.
What he had intended to say or what he had intended to do I
have never known. We stared at each other, he in drunken anger
and I in offended defense of my child.
It was the first time since he had come that I had looked di-
rectly into David's eyes. Once they had been like Arnie's, with
beautifully formed lids and full of intelligence, and expressive of
whatever thought or emotion lay behind them. Now his eyes were
red-rimmed, the lids swollen and with pouches of flesh beneath
them. But as I looked into his eyes, at first indignantly, I saw, not
the spite and hate I had imagined in him all these days, or the
bitterness of an enemy who would use his authority to give Arnie
over to Julie, but misery and a stark loneliness. Sorrow
and shame took possession of me, sorrow and shame for us all. I
could have wept.
For hours, it seemed, we stared at each other. Then, without a
word he turned and went slowly away.
For what happened next I can give no rational explanation. It
was neither according to reason nor to logic that I should com-
pletely reverse every thought that had consumed me for days—
that David was an enemy and that this enemy must be fought
with all the power I possessed. As if in a dream I rose from my
chair and followed that bowed figure into the dining room where
he was already stooping to the sideboard.
"David," I said, and realized it was the first time I had called
him by his name.
He rose to his feet and faced me, straightening his shoulders
and lifting his head as if preparing himself for a battle.
"Would you like to see Arnie?" I asked.
He only nodded and wordlessly we made our way across the
hall and into her room.
[300]I said, “Arnie, here is your father. He has come to see you. He
has come home.”
Certainly I had not expected her to make any sign of recogni-
tion and she did not. But from that time on I left her doors un-
locked and sometimes during the day her father would go in and
sit with her when I was not there. In the evening, also, he would
come and remain in the room with us, subside into a drunken
stupor, then wake and go quietly away. On other occasions he
would bring her a flower, a crocus that had come up and
bloomed; or he would put in her hands the closed bud of a daf-
fodil he had picked and let it lie there until it died. But outside
of her room he remained the same, with varying moods that were
difficult to bear.
[301]Chapter XLI
Most of these things, as they happened, I wrote to Mum Hattie,
and though I wished for her I could not, after what she had said
about standing on my own feet, and knowing she had commit-
ments in New York, urge her to come. Nevertheless one day she
arrived at the house; that scrubbed, shining look on her face, that
radiant goodness, that heaven on which she depended and with-
out which she was lost, in her eyes.
Ours was now a curious household. There was Mum Hattie
who was sleeping on another cot, procured for us by Saul de
Leon, and that we had set up in Arnie's room. There was Arnie's
father in the storeroom. There was Arnie. There was myself. And
as much a part of the household as if he lived in it, there was Saul.
Everything was quite ordinary that evening when we were all
gathered after supper in Arnie's room. That is, it was ordinary
in the way that what is extraordinary has become ordinary by
acceptance and custom.
Arnie's room was almost as it had been when she was a child,
because I had, when she was first brought home, perhaps through
sentimentality, perhaps because I wished her to be in a place
where she would feel at ease, returned to the room her books,
including her schoolbooks, that had been on the built-in shelves
at each side of her bed since she was fifteen. Her old walnut dress-
ing table, with a comfortable cushioned bench and large mirror,
stood between the two windows. Her desk, which had been
against the other wall, had been relegated to the storeroom to
make way for Mum Hattie's cot, but the photograph of her father
in a silver frame had been taken from the desk and set on the
[302]mantel above the fireplace. I had even returned to the walls the
pictures Arnie had selected for them: a Renoir, a very good re-
production of the "blue girl" which I had always thought re-
sembled Arnie; several beautifully pulled proofs of etchings of
flowers I had given her; and a picture she had kept because it
had been presented to her by her father when she was confirmed,
a painting of Christ with a lantern knocking at a door and below
it the verse it illustrated:
Behold I stand at the door and knock.
If any man hear my voice,
And open the door,
I will come in to him
And will sup with him,
And he with me.
Always endeavoring to have everything in that room "normal,"
I made it my custom until the hour when Arnie was put to bed
to keep all the lights on so that there were no shadowy corners.
Each of us therefore was distinctly visible, although two were not
conscious of the presence of others. For Arnie, sitting in a chair
beside her bed, was as usual bent over, her hair, escaped from the
ribbon I had put on that morning, covering her face; hands
clasped together in her lap. Near her in the upholstered chair
sprawled her father, sodden from the whiskey he had taken that
day, unshaved, his soiled, rumpled shirt open at the neck; and
his heavy breathing, like snores, sounding loud in the room. Near
the opposite wall Mum Hattie and I were seated on her cot, and
at my left, on the bench before the dressing table, was Saul de
Leon.
Since the evening when I had invited David into Arnie's room
all my confidence in Saul had returned and I knew without any
doubt that the time he had spent with David had not been
wasted, but that he had established in his own way, and for his
own reasons, a friendship, a comradeship with that poor wreck
who was Arnie's father. And even before Mum Hattie came we
had resumed the discussions that meant so much to me; but with
one difference, that David was usually present, though most of
the time he was too sodden to understand what was being said.
A few days before Saul had brought to my attention, more be-
[303]cause of its general interest than because it had any reference to
Arnie, a service that had been used in the Church in the begin-
ning of the Fourth Century. It was a service for exorcising de-
mons, and the possessed persons were called "energumens." After
an exhortation by the Bishop, beginning, "Pray, ye energumens
who are vexed with unclean spirits. . . ." and certain prayers, the
Bishop, in the Name of the Trinity, boldly ordered the demons
to leave the possessed bodies.
I asked Saul to tell Mum Hattie about this service and he will-
ingly did so. But even then it was more a matter of interest and
was not connected, consciously in my mind, with Arnie, until
Mum Hattie said, "Why not have that service here? Why not for
Arnie?"
I laughed uneasily. "Mum Hattie," I said, “are you wanting
us to become witch doctors?"
She looked at me as if asking: Are you laughing with me or
at me? Then she said sternly, "The foolishness of God is wiser
than the wisdom of men. . . . Witch doctors propitiate devils,
Miss Ann. God commands them."
I turned to look at Saul to see how Mum Hattie's suggestion
affected him. He was leaning back, relaxed against the dressing
table, one elbow resting on it and supporting his head. There
was the beginning of a smile on his mouth and I noticed again,
as I had before, how the "aristocratic," clear-cut nose, while it
prevented him from being called handsome, gave his whole face
character; and that this impression of character was not only a
surface manifestation, but as in a portrait painted by a master
(and the large mirror behind his head emphasized the effect of a
frame canvas), there was in that "portrait" the suggestion of un-
guessed depths of form and content and color whose purpose
only the artist, the creator who had painted it, could know.
Then my gaze went to the other side of the room where were
those other two who appeared beyond all help. Arnie's father had
been particularly trying that day, and as a result of the irritation
he had aroused in me I had allowed myself again to lose that
hope with which I had recently so often been sustained, and
which I knew, if I opened my heart to it, would come again.
But I did not wish to do so. I was in the same condition of mind
[304]and heart in which my child had been when at six years of age
she had said, “But, Mama, I want to be bad." That is, I wished
to allow my own moods, desires, and impulses to rule me as they
might.
"There is now a service of anointing with oil for healing,"
Saul said in answer to Mum Hattie's question and my objection.
"The manner of it is left to the discretion of the priest. This serv-
ice might be used."
"But we have no priest," I objected.
"Why not you?" Mum Hattie asked Saul, leaning across me
and placing her hand on his knee. "You could do it."
He shook his head and I said penitently (but with some hy-
pocrisy) that I should have been more friendly when Julie sent
the rector to me that Sunday afternoon. "I should have been
willing for Mr. Bland to see Arnie,” I told them. "Even now it is
not too late. We could ask him to come here and hold that serv-
ice. You see," I went on, speaking to Saul, “I have taken to heart
that section of the Thirty-nine Articles that says the grace of
God is not diminished because the priest may be evil or because
I do not happen to like him. Still I do not like him," I said stub-
bornly.
A mischievous, challenging smile came on Saul's face. He said
to me, and indicating David with his head, "There is an ordained
priest, here in this room."
Though he spoke quietly his words were like an explosion that
shocked and terrified.
"Oh no!" I protested, staring in anguish at Arnie's father,
whose body was sprawled grotesquely in the chair, his head sunk
on his chest, his thick hair that had always been unruly now un-
combed and disordered; and hearing, even more dreadful and
hopeless, the sound of his heavy breathing, like snores, making
at regular intervals a sort of derisive comment on Saul's im-
plied suggestion.
I turned miserably back to the others and saw a glance of
understanding pass between Mum Hattie and Saul. And in Mum
Hattie's brown, loved face, also, I saw that gleam of something
like excitement, but which was not; which was more like mis-
chief, but was not exactly mischief, that was on Saul's face; and
[305]I felt a presentiment of something about to happen in the room
over which I had no control, and over which I did not wish to
have any control.
"Why not try?" Saul asked, and both he and Mum Hattie
looked at me, waiting for me to speak.
Suddenly I could feel, as if it were a contagion, the same smile
that was on their faces trembling on my own mouth, and I
nodded.
"I'll rouse him," Mum Hattie said and she went over to David
and in her best authoritative nurse's manner began to waken
him.
"We might use the Prayer of Humble Access?" Saul said to me
questioningly, and again I nodded, unable to speak. "And
then...." he said, but was interrupted by Mum Hattie, who
called him in a low voice to help her lift David from the chair.
As they passed me, on the way to the bathroom, supporting
David who staggered between them, almost a dead weight, Mum
Hattie said, "Will you make coffee, Miss Ann?" There was the
sound of water rushing into the basin and sputtering protests
and
groans. Then I went to the kitchen.
I came back with the coffee to find David seated in the up-
holstered chair in which he had been before, but now he was
awake and at least partly conscious of what was going on about
him. Mum Hattie took the coffee and with that same nurse's
authority in her voice ordered David to drink it. He did so, but
the cup clattered in its saucer as he set it there with shaking fin-
gers.
As soon as he had finished Saul moved the dressing table bench
to within a foot or two of the chair in which David was sitting
and where, himself seated, he could look directly into the older
man's face.
"Mr. Braxton," he said, leaning forward and addressing Arnie's
father with a peculiar accent of respect, as if the human wreck
before him had been indeed a priest of the Church who merited
such respect, "we believe you can be the instrument of your
daughter's healing."
"What's that you say?" David lifted his head and stared at the
young man's face so near his own.
[306]"Do you wish to see Arnie a normal, healthy, beautiful girl
again?" Saul asked in a voice that was harsh as he tried to reach
the other man's consciousness, but which contained some other,
unexpressed, feeling.
David sat up straighter in the chair. "Certainly. Certainly I
do," he answered. "Give me another cup of that coffee," he said
to Mum Hattie. He drank it down. "What do you have in mind?”
he asked Saul.
"It's very simple," Saul explained, and now his voice was casual,
almost indifferent. "Only that you say some prayers for your
daughter and anoint her with oil. You are an ordained priest,
you know."
I saw David's mouth widen in a grin. "There are those who
would not agree with you," he said.
Apparently unaffected by David's words or by his manner,
Saul explained what was required. "All you need do," he said
respectfully, "is say a prayer, and using the oil anoint her with it
on her forehead . he hesitated a moment and went on firmly,
"with the sign of the cross."
It was surprising the effect this request had on David. His ego
seemed to expand enormously, and he took on a sort of spurious
dignity and poise and condescension, as if he said to Saul, “I
know more about all this than you do, my dear boy."
"Do you think you would remember the Prayer of Humble
Access?" Saul asked diffidently.
"Oh," David answered in a cocky, self-assured manner, a con-
tinuation of the behavior that had disturbed me all day, "I sup-
pose once started, I could rattle it off."
Mum Hattie came from the kitchen with a small unopened
bottle of olive oil and gave it to Saul who rose and looked about
him.
"What is it you want?" I asked.
"Something-a container for the oil," he said. "A cup would
do."
Then for the first time a keen interest in what was happening
took possession of me. I said I would find something, and did not
notice, then, that Mum Hattie was pouring another cup of coffee
for David, or that Saul was clearing the dressing table of all the
[307]articles on it. My whole thought was concerned with carrying out
the idea that had come to me when Saul had asked for a con-
tainer. For I remembered then the black suitcase that lay on a
shelf of the storeroom. For within it was the leather case and
within that the tiny silver chalice.
As soon as I had brought the suitcase into the room and we
set it on the bench and opened it, David leaned from his chair;
and when he recognized the cassock and surplice he had worn so
many times and the stole, he said wonderingly, "You've kept
them all these years." Then as if ashamed of his momentary
weakness, and forcing himself to assume his previous manner,
he said in a way that made the whole situation ludicrous, “Why,
Ann, I didn't know you cared."
Silently I went on with what I was doing. I lifted out the cas-
sock and surplice and stole and laid them on Arnie's bed. On the
surface of the dressing table I spread (in place of a fair linen),
the unfolded corporal. Setting the tiny chalice, so beautiful in its
grand simplicity, on the corporal, I filled it with oil from the
bottle, saying to myself: Never mind David; it is for Arnie. Never
mind the unworthiness of the minister; it is for Arnie.
Once begun, all these preparations did not seem alien to me,
but it was as if I had been carried back through the centuries to
the time when the customs and traditions had been in their primi-
tive state—the very beginning of the Church. All seemed natural
and simple, and while one part of my mind looked on at that
"cocky" person who had been my husband, who had been a priest
of the Church, and was disgusted and frightened, another part
Iwas certain that what had been initiated in that room would be
carried out to its end-whatever that end might be.
I saw with an appreciation of the fitness of what he was doing
that Saul had lifted the cassock and surplice and the stole from
Arnie's bed and that very respectfully he asked David if he would
put them on.
"Why not if it gives you pleasure?" David said and shrugged.
"I am indifferent to religion and its formalities. They mean noth-
ing to me one way or another.”
He placed his hands on the arms of the chair to raise himself
[308]from it. But he was trembling and it was necessary for Mum
Hattie to help him before he could get himself on his feet. After
that he submitted passively while, with agony in my heart, I
helped Saul clothe him in the office of his former calling-the
cassock and surplice and the white stole.
"This is the oil," Saul said to him, indicating the dressing table
and the chalice on it. "We will use the Prayer of Humble Access,”
he said. "I will prompt you if you forget."
"Oh, I'll remember it," David said complacently. Following an
orderly habit of long ago, and apparently unconscious of what he
was doing, with a hand on each side of the stole he straightened
it about his neck, even glancing down to see that the fringes were
even.
He took the few steps to the dressing table. Surer now on his
feet, he walked with confidence. Then I saw an expression of un-
certainty and dismay come on his face as he looked into the large
mirror, and he backed away from it abruptly. As he did so we
were able to see what he had been seeing within the boundaries
made by its frame: a face with lines of bitterness on it, loose, char-
acterless lips, red-rimmed eyes, graying, unkempt hair, and cheeks
heavy with a growth of beard. The cassock had not been buttoned
to its top so that above the white surplice and the white stole
we could see his filthy, rumpled shirt. We saw, also, that he be-
gan awkwardly to remove the stole from his neck, bending his
head and fumbling at it with trembling hands.
Quickly Saul stepped to him and laid his arm across David's
shoulders, holding the stole in place. "It is for your daughter,
remember," he said firmly and yet pleadingly. "You are doing
this for Arnie."
"No," David protested, struggling to remove the stole. "What
are you thinking?" he said with a kind of horror in his voice.
"You know I am not fit look at me.

I could not bear it. I whispered to Mum Hattie, "Is it any use
-this pain and suffering-this humiliation?" She came to me
and reaching out, gripped my hand, but said nothing. I looked
at her and saw that her magnificent head was bent and that her
lips moved.
[309]"Who is worthy?" Saul asked. "Who is worthy? Not one of us,"
he said. "The prayer acknowledges this; and that means all of us,
priest and people.
"But you must kneel," he said to David as if speaking to a child.
"It is the Prayer of Humble Access," he said, emphasizing the last
two words. "Will you kneel and say the words after me? Then you
will remember."
His hand gripped David's shoulder and under the pressure of
his hand the weak knees bent, the disordered head bowed low.
There was a silence-all of us waiting. And in the silence I be-
gan to feel, penetrating the atmosphere of the room like a be-
nign influence, the same love and peace I had known on the eve-
ning when I said, "Oh God, if there is a God. . . ." It filled the
room, that love and that deep peace. It was in us and all about us.
It was indeed the peace that passes understanding. But it was also
a Power.
"Now begin the prayer," Saul said at last. "You remember: We
do not presume.

"We do not presume.
David repeated, but was inter-
rupted by a fit of coughing that seemed to wrack his whole frame.
My body was taut and yet at the same time relaxed. Every
part of it seemed still. There was a stillness of body, of mind, of
spirit, and this stillness permeated every part of my being so that
I felt whole, complete, fulfilled; and as if all my life had been
only the preparation, a leading toward this moment, when for the
first time I understood the words "abundant life." I felt neither
responsibility nor anxiety. The responsibility was on Someone
greater than myself, and that Someone I could trust to carry out
what had been so imperfectly begun.
I heard Saul say to David, “. . . to come. We do not presume
to come...." he said, emphasizing the word "presume."
"Trusting in our own righteousness. . . ." the kneeling figure
added. "We are not worthy. . . ." Again he was interrupted by
a fit of coughing. But after it was over he went on without any
prompting. "We are not worthy so much as to . . . to gather
the crumbs under thy table. .
"But thou art the same Lord. . . ." Saul continued.
"Yes, yes, you don't need to prompt me," David muttered.
[310]•
•
"Thou art the same Lord whose property is always is al-
ways always to have mercy... to have mercy. his
voice faltered.
•
Suddenly he started up, struggling to rise and throw off the
stole, even trying to tear the surplice from his body. "No," he
said wildly and angrily to Saul, “you should know better than
this... than to allow
me.
...
""
But the young man's hand remained firmly on the older man's
shoulder, pressing it down. Under the strong pressure David knelt
again with his arms outflung across the table. But he could not
go on with the prayer because he was weeping in strong, heavy,
groaning sobs. I heard Saul's quiet voice steadily completing
the prayer: "That our sinful bodies may be made clean . . . and
our souls washed . . . and that we may evermore dwell in Him
and He in us.
·
...
"Have mercy!" David groaned and then “Eternal God!” He
said the words hoarsely, and it seemed as if they were torn from
his throat. "Forgive! Forgive!"
Mum Hattie's long fingers tightened painfully about my own
and I heard her jubilant whisper, "Hallelujah! The very angels
in heaven rejoice!"
And then through David's sobs I heard another voice, a voice
that had been silent for more than a year—a voice that had been
dead. And this voice, so near and dear, said, "Papa." And then
"Don't cry. You mustn't cry."
I looked up and saw a hand reach out. It was my child's
hand and it was laid on the unkempt, graying head bent over on
the spread-out arms on the table. "Don't cry," she said again. But
I saw that the tears that had been in those drowned eyes had be-
gun to spill over and were mingling with her father's as she bent
toward him from her chair; and that young Saul stood near them,
looking on them with joy.
And it seemed to me I heard a voice saying (and it might have
been Mum Hattie's, or it might have been Saul's, or another's),
"For this my son was dead, and is alive again; was lost, and is
found."
Then Arnie, wiping the tears away with her arm and pushing
her hair back from her thin face, turned her great brown honest
[311]97
eyes, now intelligent, keen, alive and with all the power of love
in them, on me.
“Mama,” she said joyfully and eagerly; and following that in a
practical "everyday” voice, “What time of year is it, Mama?"
"Why. ." I said wonderingly, “it is spring." And surprising
myself, in the simplest, most ordinary way I said, "The daffodils
are blooming. You must go out tomorrow and see them.”
"I will," she promised.
Mum Hattie, taking her hand from mine, gave me a little push,
almost playful, as if she said, "Go to them."
Then for the first time I moved. I went to David-and to our
child.
